### [1] [Quantum-echo Markov process for combinatorial optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38943)
*Tatsuhiko Shirai*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a quantum-echo Markov process for combinatorial optimization, where QA/QAOA-based dynamics engineer a transition kernel that delocalizes in Hamming space while localizing in energy space, governed by their interplay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish quantum-echo dynamics as a framework for engineering structured transition kernels and to use finite-resource quantum many-body dynamics as a computational primitive for iterative optimization.

Method: A quantum-echo Markov process is constructed in which quantum dynamics from quantum annealing (QA) or the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) defines the transition kernel. Longer annealing times or more QAOA layers enable transitions to distant configurations while suppressing large energy changes. Hamming-space delocalization arises from operator spreading, and energy-space localization from a dynamically generated correlation. Quantum-echo local optimization is proposed both with and without greedy descent.

Result: Performance is governed by the interplay between Hamming-space nonlocality and energy-space locality; excessive energy-space localization can degrade optimization performance. Incorporating greedy descent substantially improves performance.

Conclusion: The work highlights the complementary roles of quantum dynamics for exploration and greedy descent for exploitation, and provides a route to using finite-resource quantum many-body dynamics as a computational primitive for iterative optimization.

Abstract: We introduce a quantum-echo Markov process for combinatorial optimization. Quantum dynamics based on quantum annealing (QA) or the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) is used to engineer the transition kernel. Increasing the annealing time in QA or the number of layers in QAOA enables transitions to explore distant configurations while suppressing large energy changes. The Hamming-space delocalization originates from operator spreading, whereas the energy-space localization arises from a dynamically generated correlation. For optimization, we propose quantum-echo local optimization with and without greedy descent. We find that its performance is governed by the interplay between Hamming-space nonlocality and energy-space locality, and that excessive energy-space localization can degrade optimization performance. Incorporating greedy descent substantially improves the performance, highlighting the complementary roles of quantum dynamics for exploration and greedy descent for exploitation. These results establish quantum-echo dynamics as a framework for engineering structured transition kernels and provide a route to using finite-resource quantum many-body dynamics as a computational primitive for iterative optimization.

</details>


### [2] [Gibbs Sampling in the Shattered Phase by Decoded Quantum Interferometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40345)
*Leo Zhou,Noah Shutty,Mark Sellke,Stephen P. Jordan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper applies Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) to Gibbs sampling of classical Ising spin Hamiltonians, showing the problem reduces to quantum decoding and that DQI can overcome topological (shattering/disorder-chaos) barriers that obstruct stable and local algorithms, sampling beyond the dynamical phase transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gibbs sampling of classical Ising spin glasses is a hard problem: below the dynamical phase transition, shattering and disorder chaos create a topological barrier that defeats many algorithms, including Glauber dynamics and any algorithm whose output distribution is stable under input perturbations. The paper asks whether a quantum approach can break this barrier.

Method: The authors apply DQI to sample from Gibbs measures of classical Ising spin Hamiltonians, showing the Gibbs sampling problem reduces to a quantum decoding problem whose achievable temperature is set by the performance of decoding algorithms. They then study Ising k-spin glasses (Max-k-XORSAT) on random Erdős-Rényi hypergraphs with average degree D ≥ k in the temperature range starting asymptotically at the predicted dynamical transition β_dyn(k,D) = √((2 ln k)/D)·[1+o(1)]. They analyze the topological barrier and prove it can be overcome by (i) a classical algorithm based on Prange's method and (ii) DQI equipped with a quantum decoder.

Result: Shattering and disorder chaos form a topological barrier obstructing many algorithms, including Glauber dynamics and any input-stable algorithm. In contrast, both Prange's algorithm and DQI break this barrier. For D = αk with fixed α > 1, both Prange's algorithm and DQI can sample at any inverse temperature β < tanh⁻¹(1/α) for sufficiently large k, well beyond the dynamical threshold β_dyn ~ √(2 ln k/(αk)).

Conclusion: DQI can overcome topological barriers that obstruct stable algorithms, providing a quantum approach to Gibbs sampling well beyond the dynamical phase transition of classical Ising spin glasses.

Abstract: We apply Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) to sample from the Gibbs measures of classical Ising spin Hamiltonians. We show that this Gibbs sampling problem reduces to a quantum decoding problem, and the temperature achievable by DQI is determined by the performance of decoding algorithms. We then focus on the task of Gibbs sampling for classical Ising $k$-spin glasses (or Max-$k$-XORSAT) on random Erdős-Rényi hypergraphs with average degree $D\ge k$. In a temperature range beginning asymptotically at the predicted dynamical phase transition, $β_{\rm dyn}(k,D) = \sqrt{(2\ln k)/D}\times [1+o_{k\to\infty}(1)]$, we show that shattering and disorder chaos form a topological barrier that obstructs many algorithms, including Glauber dynamics and any algorithm whose output distribution is "stable" under perturbations of the input. In contrast, we prove that this barrier can be broken both by a classical algorithm based on Prange's method, and by DQI equipped with a quantum decoder. For example, when $D=αk$ with fixed $α>1$, both Prange's algorithm and DQI can sample at any inverse temperature $β< \tanh^{-1}(1/α)$ for sufficiently large $k$, well beyond the dynamical threshold $β_{\rm dyn} \sim \sqrt{2\ln k / (αk)}$. Therefore, our results show that DQI can overcome topological barriers that obstruct stable algorithms.

</details>


### [3] [Exact Operator Complexity Measures from Random Matchgate Unitaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38300)
*Gregory A. L. White,Jens Eisert,Neil Dowling*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Random matchgate (free-fermionic) ensembles are used to show that Heisenberg-picture complexity measures—operator entanglement, operator stabiliser entropy, and OTOCs—can saturate volume laws and become exponentially small despite the circuits being classically simulable, decoupling magic-like resources from simulation hardness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Matchgate circuits are classically tractable yet display features of complex quantum systems, including high magic. This raises the question of whether typical complexity measures are saturated even under trivial (classically simulable) dynamics, and how quantum resources relate to simulation complexity.

Method: The authors develop a free-fermionic unitary Weingarten calculus formulated entirely in the Majorana basis, applicable to random matchgate ensembles and tailored to computing operator moments. The calculation reduces to a combinatorics problem governed by boundary conditions set by a given operator functional. This framework yields closed-form expressions for typical local-operator entanglement, operator stabiliser entropy, and higher-order out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOCs).

Result: For extensive initial Majorana strings, both operator entanglement and stabiliser entropy obey volume laws, even though the underlying circuits remain classically simulable. OTOCs can likewise become exponentially small, so their late-time saturation value alone does not witness computational hardness, providing a classically tractable example of emergent free independence. Operator entanglement beyond the Gaussian baseline is shown to lower-bound the number of non-Gaussian gates in doped matchgate circuits.

Conclusion: The work provides a practical toolbox for computing exact averages over random matchgates and clarifies the distinction between quantum resources and simulation complexity, including their dependence on the representation being exploited. It demonstrates that saturation of complexity measures does not imply hard-to-simulate dynamics.

Abstract: Matchgate circuits describe classically tractable dynamics in a physically relevant setting while nonetheless exhibiting features characteristic of complex quantum systems, such as high amounts of magic. This invites the broader question: to what extent are typical complexity measures saturated under trivial dynamics? Here, we address this by studying the behaviour of different Heisenberg-picture complexity measures in random matchgate ensembles. Our main technical contribution is a tractable and generalisible free-fermionic unitary Weingarten calculus formulated entirely in the Majorana basis. This method is tailored to computing moments of operators, and simplifies to a combinatorics problem which depends on the boundary conditions prescribed by a given operator functional. Using this framework, we derive closed-form expressions for the typical local-operator entanglement, operator stabiliser entropy, and higher-order out-of-time-ordered correlators. For extensive initial Majorana strings, both operator entanglement and stabiliser entropy obey volume laws, even though the underlying circuits remain classically simulable. Out-of-time-ordered correlators can likewise become exponentially small, showing that their late-time saturation value alone does not witness computational hardness and constituting a classically tractable example of emergent free independence. Finally, we relate these separations to operator resource theories, showing how operator entanglement beyond the Gaussian baseline lower-bounds the number of non-Gaussian gates in doped matchgate circuits. Our results present a practical toolbox for computing exact averages over random matchgates, while clarifying the distinction between quantum resources versus simulation complexity, and their dependence on the representation being exploited.

</details>


### [4] [Making the most of leftovers: Improved privacy amplification for quantum key distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38306)
*Matthew Simon Tan,Bartosz Regula,Marco Tomamichel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Refined privacy amplification bounds from the Regula–Tomamichel leftover hash lemma are integrated into entropic-uncertainty-relation QKD security analysis, boosting finite-block secret key rates without protocol changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In finite-data quantum key distribution, conservative security bounds force users to discard a substantial fraction of the potentially extractable secret key, limiting practical key rates.

Method: Refine and extend the privacy amplification bounds achievable via the recent leftover hash lemma of Regula and Tomamichel, then incorporate these bounds into a QKD security analysis based on entropic uncertainty relations.

Result: The approach improves state-of-the-art key rates in finite-block regimes and outperforms techniques based on entropy accumulation, certifying more secret key from the same experimental data.

Conclusion: Sharper mathematical estimates can directly increase the usable output of a quantum communication system, yielding higher certified key rates without altering the protocol.

Abstract: The amount of secret key that can be obtained from a quantum key distribution run depends on both the physically observed error rates and the mathematical bounds used to certify security. For finite datasets, conservative bounds force users to discard a substantial fraction of the potentially available key. Here we further refine and extend the privacy amplification bounds achievable through the recent leftover hash lemma of Regula and Tomamichel [arXiv:2603.04493] and incorporate them into the security analysis of quantum key distribution based on entropic uncertainty relations. This improves on state-of-the-art key rates in finite-block regimes, certifying more secret key from the same experimental data without changes to the protocol, and outperforming techniques based on entropy accumulation. The results illustrate how sharper mathematical estimates can directly increase the usable output of a quantum communication system.

</details>


### [5] [When Symmetry Suppresses Magic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38319)
*A. de Oliveira Junior,Jake Xuereb,Rafael A. Macêdo,Jonatan Bohr Brask,Rafael Chaves*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that the Robustness of Magic (RoM) for N-qubit X-states is bounded by √3, revealing a symmetry-based constraint on magic. This enables an efficient lower-bounding method for many-body states and an analytic derivation of the critical temperature for magic emergence in X-state equilibrium Hamiltonians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating nonstabilizerness (magic), especially for mixed states, requires superexponentially many samples in system size, making it NP-hard. Although symmetries can reduce this complexity, it remained unclear whether symmetries also restrict the amount of achievable magic. The authors aim to clarify this by exhibiting an explicit symmetry that constrains magic.

Method: The authors prove an upper bound of √3 on the RoM of N-qubit X-states. Using this symmetry constraint, they develop a computationally efficient technique to lower-bound the RoM of arbitrary many-body states. They apply this method to ground states of a spin-1/2 Hamiltonian at system sizes beyond exact evaluation, and analytically derive the critical temperature for magic emergence in Hamiltonians whose equilibrium states are X-states.

Result: RoM for N-qubit X-states is at most √3. The proposed lower-bounding method yields nontrivial RoM bounds for large spin-1/2 ground states inaccessible to exact computation. For X-state equilibrium Hamiltonians, the critical temperature for magic onset is derived analytically.

Conclusion: The work explains why certain symmetries constrain magic while others do not, provides a scalable lower-bounding method for many-body nonstabilizerness, and identifies a nontrivial analytically solvable regime of many-body magic.

Abstract: Nonstabilizerness is a critical resource for quantum advantage, but evaluating it, especially for mixed states, requires superexponentially many samples in system size, making the problem NP-hard. While symmetries are known to reduce this complexity, it is unclear whether they also restrict the amount of magic. In this work, we provide an explicit example of such a symmetry by proving that the Robustness of Magic (RoM) for N-qubit X-states is at most $\sqrt{3}$. Leveraging this symmetry constraint, we introduce a computationally efficient method to lower-bound the RoM of arbitrary many-body states, demonstrating its utility on the ground states of a spin-1/2 Hamiltonian with system sizes well beyond the reach of exact evaluation. Furthermore, for Hamiltonians whose equilibrium states are X-states, we analytically derive the critical temperature at which magic emerges. Our work shows why certain symmetries constrain magic while others do not, provides a scalable lower-bounding method, and identifies a nontrivial regime where many-body nonstabilizerness is analytically solvable.

</details>


### [6] [Exponential strong converse for blind quantum data compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38328)
*Kohdai Kuroiwa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes exponential strong converse theorems for blind quantum data compression of finite-dimensional mixed-state sources, both with and without entanglement assistance, resolving whether a strong converse holds at the optimal rate in general.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong converses for data compression were known for pure-state sources, but for mixed-state sources the optimal compression rate depends on a structural decomposition of quantum states and can change substantially under small perturbations. This sensitivity makes the rate-error trade-off hard to analyze quantitatively, and whether a strong converse holds at the optimal rate had remained unresolved in general.

Method: The authors prove exponential strong converse bounds for blind compression without restrictions on protocols. For unassisted compression, they show accuracy decays exponentially with block length at every rate below the optimum for general mixed-state sources. They also prove an exponential strong converse for blind compression with unlimited entanglement assistance at its optimal rate. The proof introduces a new overlap quantity based on the structure of quantum states, connecting preservation of source information to the dimension of the transmitted system.

Result: At every rate below the optimum, accuracy decays exponentially with block length for unassisted compression of general mixed-state sources. An exponential strong converse also holds for blind compression with unlimited entanglement assistance at its optimal rate. Combining both bounds yields exponential decay at every compression-entanglement rate pair outside the complete achievable rate region.

Conclusion: The strong converse question for blind quantum data compression of mixed-state sources is resolved affirmatively, both with and without entanglement assistance. The new overlap quantity is expected to provide useful tools for converse analyses of other quantum information-processing tasks involving mixed states.

Abstract: We establish exponential strong converse theorems for blind quantum data compression of finite-dimensional mixed-state sources, with and without entanglement assistance. Blind compression is a fundamental quantum information processing task in which the sender aims to transmit quantum states without direct access to the classical label. Strong converse is a notable aspect of data compression: the accuracy of the compression abruptly drops down to zero once the compression rate goes below a threshold. Strong converses for data compression have been established for pure-state sources; however, for mixed-state sources, the optimal compression rate is determined by a structural decomposition of quantum states and can change substantially under small perturbations. This sensitivity makes it difficult to quantitatively analyze the trade-off between compression rate and error. Despite recent progress, whether a strong converse holds at the optimal rate has remained unresolved in general. In this work, we resolve this question affirmatively. For unassisted compression, we show that at every rate below the optimum, the accuracy decays exponentially with the block length for general mixed-state sources without restrictions on protocols. We also prove exponential strong converse for blind compression with unlimited entanglement assistance at its optimal rate. Combining the two strong converse bounds yields exponential decay at every compression-entanglement rate pair outside the complete achievable rate region. Our proof introduces a new overlap quantity based on the structure of quantum states; this quantity connects preservation of the source information to the dimension of the transmitted system. We expect that this approach provides useful tools for converse analyses of other quantum information-processing tasks involving mixed states.

</details>


### [7] [Collective Dynamics in Spin Chains with Constrained Dissipation: Classically Fragile but Quantum Robust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38333)
*Pietro Brighi,Gianluca Frazzei,Igor Lesanovsky,Juan P. Garrahan,Alberto Biella*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies classical-quantum competition in the dissipative far-East spin chain. Quantum fluctuations stabilize phase coexistence, while classical fluctuations suppress it at long times. The steady-state phase diagram and dynamics reveal dynamical heterogeneity in bistable phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collective non-equilibrium phenomena, especially near phase transitions, are strongly affected by fluctuations. The interplay between classical and quantum fluctuations and how they modify cooperative behavior in kinetically constrained systems is not fully understood.

Method: The authors investigate a kinetically constrained spin chain, the dissipative far-East model. They map the steady-state phase diagram using a cluster mean-field approach and characterize the dynamics with stochastic tensor-network methods.

Result: Without fluctuations, the system shows emergent collective dynamics with phase coexistence. This is robust to quantum fluctuations, which further stabilize it, whereas classical fluctuations suppress phase coexistence at long times. Within the bistable phase, signatures of dynamical heterogeneity appear in both local magnetization and entanglement dynamics.

Conclusion: Competing quantum fluctuations and classical kinetic constraints shape the relaxation dynamics and phase structure of many-body systems, highlighting the distinct roles of quantum versus classical fluctuations in non-equilibrium phase coexistence.

Abstract: Collective non-equilibrium phenomena in many-body systems are strongly influenced by fluctuations, particularly in the vicinity of phase transitions, where the interplay between classical and quantum effects can alter cooperative behavior. Here, we investigate this classical-quantum competition in a kinetically constrained spin chain, the dissipative far-East model. In absence of fluctuations, the system exhibits emergent collective dynamics, resulting in phase coexistence. We find that this phenomenon is robust to quantum fluctuations, which further stabilize it, whereas classical fluctuations suppress phase coexistence at long times. We map out the steady-state phase diagram using a cluster mean-field approach and characterize the dynamics with stochastic tensor-network methods. Within the bistable phase we identify signatures of dynamical heterogeneity in both the local magnetization and the entanglement dynamics. This highlights how competing quantum fluctuations and classical kinetic constraints shape the relaxation dynamics and the phase structure of many-body systems.

</details>


### [8] [Interferometric Readout of Momentum-Space Topology in a Programmable Dissipative Photonic Circuit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38352)
*Andrea Cataldo,Emil J. Bergholtz,Daniel Leykam,Jun Gao,Ali W. Elshaari*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A programmable photonic integrated circuit implements dissipative non-Hermitian dynamics via unitary dilation in synthetic momentum space, recovering bulk-evolution phase through phase-shifted interferometry to measure Zak phases and a coherence winding induced by an exceptional point, and, on a synthetic torus driven by a Rice-Mele pump, the first Chern number for trivial and non-trivial cycles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In non-Hermitian dynamics, topology is encoded in the phase of the bulk evolution; however, strong dissipation suppresses the amplitudes that carry this phase information, making the topological signal difficult to access.

Method: A programmable photonic integrated circuit realizes the non-Hermitian dynamics in synthetic momentum space using unitary dilation, with the phase recovered by phase-shifted interferometry. The platform is applied to the non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger model and extended to a synthetic torus via a Rice-Mele pump.

Result: For the non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger model, the approach distinguishes trivial and non-trivial Zak phases and yields a coherence winding q = ±1 induced by an exceptional point. On the synthetic torus, the Rice-Mele pump gives a first Chern number Ch1 = 0 for trivial cycles and Ch1 = 1 for non-trivial cycles.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a programmable route to momentum-space topology under strongly dissipative non-Hermitian dynamics, enabling phase-based topological characterization that survives strong dissipation.

Abstract: Topology under non-Hermitian dynamics is encoded in the phase of the bulk evolution, yet strong dissipation suppresses the amplitudes carrying it. We resolve this using a programmable photonic integrated circuit that implements such dynamics in synthetic momentum space through unitary dilation, with the phase recovered by phase-shifted interferometry. For the non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger model, the method distinguishes trivial and non-trivial Zak phases and yields a coherence winding $q=\pm 1$ induced by an exceptional point. Extending to a synthetic torus via a Rice-Mele pump gives the first Chern number $\mathrm{Ch}_1=0,1$ for trivial and non-trivial cycles, showing a programmable route to momentum-space topology under strongly dissipative non-Hermitian dynamics.

</details>


### [9] [Time-Integrated Leakage as a Dynamical Benchmark for Multi-Mode Superconducting-Qubit Reset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38354)
*Sushant Sharma,Aswath Babu H*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces time-integrated leakage as a complementary metric to endpoint residual population for evaluating superconducting qubit reset, showing that the two metrics can favor different reset configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional reset performance is summarized only by the final residual population at the end of the pulse. This single number does not capture the trajectory during reset: a protocol can achieve a small final residual while leaving the qubit in a leakage state for a comparatively long time. Since leakage during repeated quantum-error-correction cycles is harmful, a time-resolved metric is needed.

Method: The authors use time-integrated leakage together with endpoint population to study a multi-mode dissipative reset model under flux-frequency control. They examine finite thermal occupation, parameter sweeps, frequency disorder, an auxiliary-chain ablation, and a literature-based rate benchmark.

Result: At the representative ablation point, adding the auxiliary chain reduces the time-integrated leakage Lf from 8.56 to 0.78 ns and shifts the sustained Pf < 10^-2 crossing from 39.5 to 9.8 ns. However, the configuration without the chain still reaches the smaller long-time residual, so the two metrics favor different configurations. For the same 300 ns window, an effective rate model based on Zhou et al.'s f-ket and e-ket decay rates gives Lf = 108.0 ns versus 0.783 ns for the complete simulated configuration (used as a kinetic reference only). Across simulations, thermal occupation is the dominant degradation source, while frequency disorder has a much weaker effect.

Conclusion: Time-integrated leakage is a useful quantity to report alongside endpoint residuals, particularly when reset is part of repeated quantum-error-correction cycles, because it captures trajectory-level leakage that endpoint population alone misses.

Abstract: Reset performance in superconducting qubits is often summarized by the population that remains at the end of the pulse. This is an important number, but it does not describe what happens during the reset itself. A protocol may reach a small final residual while keeping the qubit in a leakage state for a comparatively long part of the trajectory. In this work, we therefore use time-integrated leakage, together with the usual endpoint population, to study a multi-mode dissipative reset model under flux-frequency control. We examine finite thermal occupation, parameter sweeps, frequency disorder, an auxiliary-chain ablation, and a literature-based rate benchmark. At the representative ablation point, adding the auxiliary chain reduces Lf from 8.56 to 0.78 ns and shifts the sustained Pf less-than- 10-2 crossing from 39.5 to 9.8 ns. Interestingly, the configuration without the chain still reaches the smaller long-time residual. The two metrics can therefore favor different reset configurations. For the same 300 ns analysis window, an effective rate model built from the f-ket and e-ket decay rates reported by Zhou et al. gives Lf is 108.0 ns, while the complete simulated configuration gives 0.783 ns. We use this only as a kinetic reference and not as a reproduction of the experiment. In the wider set of simulations, thermal occupation is the main source of degradation, while the frequency disorder considered here has a much weaker effect. The results suggest that time-integrated leakage is a useful quantity to report alongside endpoint residuals, especially when reset is part of repeated quantum-error-correction cycles.

</details>


### [10] [On Removing Interaction from Quantum Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38387)
*Nicholas Spooner,Max Tromanhauser*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper provides formal evidence that generic Fiat-Shamir-like compilation of quantum (Ξ-)protocols into publicly-verifiable NIZKs in the QROM is impossible unless QMA = BQP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An important open question in quantum cryptography is constructing publicly-verifiable NIZKs for QMA. Classically, NIZKs for NP are often obtained by compiling an HVZK Σ-protocol via the Fiat-Shamir transformation. Broadbent and Grilo introduced a quantum analog (Ξ-protocol), where the prover's first message is quantum, and showed HVZK Ξ-protocols exist for QMA. But it is unclear how to compile these into NIZKs in the (Q)ROM, as Fiat-Shamir seems incompatible with quantum messages. The paper addresses whether such a generic compilation is possible.

Method: The authors analyze generic "Fiat-Shamir-like" compilers for quantum protocols in the QROM, assuming small completeness and soundness error, and study the consequences for the existence of publicly-verifiable NIZKs for QMA.

Result: The paper shows that if such generic Fiat-Shamir-like compilers for quantum protocols exist in the QROM (with small completeness and soundness error), then QMA = BQP.

Conclusion: The incompatibility between Fiat-Shamir and quantum messages is essentially inherent: generic compilation of HVZK Ξ-protocols into publicly-verifiable NIZKs in the QROM would collapse QMA to BQP, giving formal evidence that no such generic approach can work.

Abstract: An important open question in quantum cryptography is the construction of publicly-verifiable NIZKs for QMA. Classically, one can construct NIZKs for NP in the random oracle model (and sometimes in the standard model) by compiling an honest-verifier ZK (HVZK) $Σ$-protocol for NP using the Fiat-Shamir transformation. Broadbent and Grilo introduced a quantum analog of a $Σ$-protocol (which they call a $Ξ$-protocol) in which the prover's first message is quantum, and show that HVZK $Ξ$-protocols exist for QMA. However, it is not clear how to compile such protocols into NIZKs in the (Q)ROM, because the Fiat-Shamir transformation seems to be incompatible with quantum messages. In this work we give formal evidence that this is indeed the case: we show that if generic "Fiat-Shamir-like" compilers for quantum protocols exist in the QROM (with small completeness and soundness error) then QMA = BQP.

</details>


### [11] [Advantage of Sample Complexity in Quantum PAC Learning Requires Inverse Access to State-Preparation Unitaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38403)
*Natsuto Isogai,Satoshi Yoshida,Mio Murao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Forward-only access to quantum data provides no asymptotic query-complexity advantage over classical data or quantum state copies: optimal query complexities are Θ((d+log(1/δ))/ε) for realizable and Θ((d+log(1/δ))/ε²) for agnostic learning, matching classical/quantum-copy sample complexities and establishing the essential role of inverse access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A fundamental question in quantum machine learning is whether quantum computation can reduce the data sampled from an unknown distribution needed to learn a prediction rule. Prior work showed quantum PAC learning with only copies of quantum data has optimal worst-case sample complexity asymptotically matching classical PAC learning, while access to both a state-preparation unitary and its inverse can improve query-complexity dependence on accuracy in realizable learning. It remained unclear whether forward-only access alone allows such an improvement.

Method: Taking the worst case over compatible state-preparation unitaries and their finite ambient dimensions, the authors analyze optimal forward-only query complexities for realizable and agnostic learning. They prove these bounds by establishing a reduction that uses q copies of the prepared state to approximate the Haar-averaged output of any q-query forward-only algorithm.

Result: The optimal forward-only query complexities of realizable and agnostic learning are Θ((d+log(1/δ))/ε) and Θ((d+log(1/δ))/ε²), respectively, where d is the VC dimension, ε the accuracy parameter, and δ the failure probability. These match the optimal sample complexities with classical data or quantum data copies.

Conclusion: Forward-only access cannot provide an asymptotic query-complexity advantage over learning from classical data or quantum data copies in this worst-case setting. The results establish the essential role of inverse access in the known realizable-setting improvement. The reduction also provides a new framework for analyzing limitations of forward state-preparation access via state-copy lower bounds.

Abstract: Whether quantum computation can reduce the amount of data sampled from an unknown probability distribution required to learn a prediction rule is a fundamental question in quantum machine learning. Quantum PAC learning studies this question using quantum data as a quantum state whose squared amplitudes encode the unknown distribution from which classical learning data are sampled. With only copies of such quantum data, the optimal worst-case sample complexity asymptotically matches that of classical PAC learning. In contrast, access to both a state-preparation unitary for this state and its inverse can improve the query-complexity dependence on the accuracy parameter in realizable learning. However, it has remained unclear whether forward-only access allows such an improvement.
  In this work, taking the worst case over compatible state-preparation unitaries and their finite ambient dimensions, we show that the optimal forward-only query complexities of realizable and agnostic learning are, respectively, $Θ((d+\log(1/δ))/\varepsilon)$ and $Θ((d+\log(1/δ))/\varepsilon^2)$, where $d$ is the VC dimension of the concept class, $\varepsilon$ the accuracy parameter, and $δ$ the failure probability. These bounds match the optimal sample complexities with classical data or quantum data copies. To prove them, we establish a reduction using $q$ copies of the prepared state to approximate the Haar-averaged output of any $q$-query forward-only algorithm.
  These results show that forward-only access cannot provide an asymptotic query-complexity advantage over learning from classical data or quantum data copies in this worst-case setting, and establish the essential role of inverse access in the known realizable-setting improvement. Our reduction also provides a new framework for analyzing limitations of forward state-preparation access via state-copy lower bounds.

</details>


### [12] [Classical Verifier Position Verification from Non-Local Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38441)
*Wen Yu Kon,Fatih Kaleoglu,Kaushik Chakraborty*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a general compiler turning any complete-support non-local game with quantum advantage into a multi-prover classical-verifier position verification (CVPV) protocol, achieving entirely classical verifier-prover communication with quantum resources confined to pre-shared entanglement and local measurements, and proves finite-size security in the QROM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Secure position verification is impossible classically against colluding adversaries, while existing quantum schemes suffer from photon loss over long distances and classical-verifier schemes rely on complex quantum processes unsuitable for near-term hardware. The work aims to overcome both the impractical long-distance transmission of quantum states and the hardware demands of certifiable randomness generation.

Method: A general compiler maps any complete-support non-local game possessing the required quantum advantage into a multi-prover CVPV protocol, with all quantum resources restricted to shared entanglement and local measurements on prover devices and purely classical verifier-prover communication. Instantiating the compiler with the CHSH game yields a near-term implementable protocol; finite-size security is proven in the quantum random oracle model for both simultaneous verification of multiple provers and verification of a single prover using multiple prover devices.

Result: The compiler yields CVPV protocols with classical verifier-prover communication and quantum resources confined to entanglement and local measurements, enabling near-term implementation via the CHSH instantiation on existing experimental platforms. Finite-size security is established in the QROM for multi-prover and single-prover-multi-device settings. Security in the multi-prover setting is shown to be governed by certified blind local randomness, critically dependent on the spatial arrangement of provers and verifiers, rather than by certified randomness of the joint provers' outputs.

Conclusion: By reducing CVPV to standard non-local games under a general compiler, the work removes the need for transmitting quantum states or complex randomness-generating quantum processes, placing position verification within reach of near-term hardware. A key conceptual insight is that multi-prover security stems from certified blind local randomness tied to the geometric configuration of provers and verifiers, not from joint output randomness.

Abstract: Secure position verification certifies that a remote prover occupies a claimed location, a task that is provably impossible with classical resources alone against colluding adversaries. Most existing quantum position verification schemes require transmitting quantum states between verifiers and provers, and the resulting photon loss makes long-distance verification impractical. Classical verifier position verification (CVPV) schemes with classical verifier-prover communication exist, but rely on complex quantum processes that generate certifiable randomness, placing them outside the reach of near-term hardware. Here we introduce a general compiler that maps any complete-support non-local game with the required quantum advantage into a multi-prover CVPV protocol. This addresses both limitations, with entirely classical verifier-prover communication and all quantum resources confined to shared entanglement and local measurements on the prover devices. Instantiating with the CHSH game enables near-term implementation on existing experimental platforms. We prove finite-size security in the quantum random oracle model for both simultaneous verification of multiple provers and verification of a single prover with multiple prover devices. Notably, security in the multi-prover setting is governed not by the certified randomness of the joint provers' outputs, but by certified blind local randomness that depends critically on the spatial arrangement of provers and verifiers.

</details>


### [13] [Quantum Secure Non-Interactive Reductions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38452)
*Maxwell Gold,Sarah Hagen,Daniel Alabi,Eric Chitambar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces quantum secure non-interactive reductions (QSNIR), extending SNIR to bipartite quantum states, and shows that QSNIR privacy is exactly computable via an SDP pair tied to a simulation-based cheating advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient secure computation models rely on offline preprocessed correlations enabling private online computation. SNIR formalizes non-interactive transformation of one classical correlation into another with information-theoretic simulation-based privacy. This work extends that framework to the quantum setting.

Method: The authors define QSNIR, where source and target resources are bipartite quantum states. For classical target resources, the framework analyzes disseminating private correlations from entanglement and classical resources. They formulate QSNIR privacy via a semidefinite program (SDP) pair quantifying an error rooted in simulation-based cheating advantage, and relate QSNIR privacy to minimum-error state discrimination (MED).

Result: QSNIR privacy is computed exactly via the SDP pair and is lower-bounded by a decision problem related to MED. Exact one-shot privacy errors are computed for universal two-party computation (2PC) preprocessing correlations.

Conclusion: QSNIR provides a natural quantum extension of SNIR with exact, operationally grounded privacy quantification, enabling analysis of private correlation dissemination from entanglement and exact one-shot privacy errors for universal 2PC preprocessing correlations.

Abstract: Efficient models for secure computation often rely on offline preprocessed correlations, which subsequently enable private online computation from minimal assumptions. Within these models, the study of secure non-interactive reductions (SNIR) formalizes when one classical correlation can be non-interactively transformed into another, while guaranteeing information-theoretic simulation-based privacy. In this work, we introduce quantum secure non-interactive reductions (QSNIR), a natural extension of SNIR, in which the source and target resource are each a bipartite quantum state. Specifically, when the target resource is classical, our framework enables us to analyze the problem of disseminating private correlations from entanglement, in addition to classical resources. Our primary technical result is that QSNIR privacy in this context can be computed exactly via a semidefinite program (SDP) pair that quantifies an error operationally rooted in a simulation-based cheating advantage. We prove that QSNIR privacy is lower-bounded by a decision problem related to minimum-error state discrimination (MED), and use both definitions to compute exact one-shot privacy errors for universal two-party computation (2PC) preprocessing correlations.

</details>


### [14] [Q-SPARSE: Quantum Subspace Projection for Near-Field Angle-Range Spectrum Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38500)
*Mostafizur Rahaman Laskar,Sajad Daei,Gabor Fodor,Mikael Skoglund*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Q-SPARSE is a quantum subspace-projection algorithm that performs near-field super-resolution angle-range localization by encoding each spherical steering hypothesis as a quantum state and using quantum phase estimation, avoiding full classical eigenstate preparation and preserving the classical near-field MUSIC maximizer under ideal signal-noise partitioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Near-field wavefront curvature enables joint angle-range localization, but exploiting it requires repeated covariance-eigenspace tests over a two-dimensional spherical manifold. Existing quantum variants of MUSIC rely on quantum phase estimation, whose main bottleneck is the full classical preparation of eigenstates.

Method: Q-SPARSE prepares each spherical steering hypothesis as a quantum state, applies quantum phase estimation to covariance-generated evolution, and converts the resolved spectral mass into a localization spectrum with a MUSIC-like spectral signature. It avoids full classical eigenstate preparation. For a finite number q of qubit phase registers, the score is derived from the complete QPE kernel, and a nearest-bin selection scheme with soft-thresholding based on Marchenko-Pastur-calibrated logistic weighting decision criteria is proposed.

Result: Under an ideal signal-noise partition, the Q-SPARSE score equals the classical signal-subspace overlap exactly and thus preserves the maximizer of normalized near-field MUSIC. Numerical results are demonstrated on IBM's QISKIT software platform for near-field array processing.

Conclusion: Q-SPARSE provides a quantum subspace-projection approach for super-resolution near-field parameter estimation that matches the classical near-field MUSIC maximizer under ideal conditions, with practical finite-register handling via nearest-bin selection and calibration, validated numerically on QISKIT.

Abstract: Near-field wavefront curvature enables joint angle-range localization, but exploiting it entails repeated covariance-eigenspace tests over a two-dimensional spherical manifold. We propose a quantum subspace-projection algorithm (Q-SPARSE) for super-resolution estimation of parameters in the near field, employing spectral signature similar to MUSIC. Q-SPARSE prepares each spherical steering hypothesis as a quantum state, applies quantum phase estimation (QPE) to covariance-generated evolution, and converts the resolved spectral mass into a localization spectrum. The proposed method does not need the full classical preparation of the eigenstates, which is a major bottleneck of the QPE algorithm, the main subroutine in quantum variants of MUSIC algorithms. Under an ideal signal-noise partition, its score equals the classical signal-subspace overlap exactly and thus preserves the maximizer of normalized near-field MUSIC. For a finite q number of qubit phase registers, we derive the score from the complete QPE kernel, and propose a nearest-bin selection scheme with soft-thresholding based on Marchenko-Pastur-calibrated logistic weighting decision criteria. Numerical results on IBM's QISKIT software platform are demonstrated for near-field array processing.

</details>


### [15] [Copy-scarce learning of ground states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38513)
*Kaito Wada,Nobuyuki Yoshioka*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes optimal trade-offs between the number of ground-state copies and Hamiltonian-evolution time needed to estimate observables or reconstruct a gapped ground state, and constructs catalytic protocols that nearly preserve all copies while achieving these bounds up to poly-logarithmic factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probing multiple properties of ground states is central to understanding quantum matter, but conventional readout is prohibitively costly in copy-scarce regimes because it consumes many independently prepared copies. There is a need to understand the fundamental limits and optimal strategies when copies are scarce and dynamics can be used instead.

Method: The authors derive a unifying inequality quantifying how well ground states can be distinguished using initial copies and controlled dynamics, even when all copies may be consumed. This yields worst-case lower bounds on elapsed Hamiltonian time for estimating multiple observables or reconstructing a full classical description of a unique gapped ground state. They then construct parallel catalytic readout protocols that return all initial copies nearly unchanged.

Result: The protocols attain the worst-case lower bounds up to poly-logarithmic factors, require no coherent preparation circuit or its inverse, and their elapsed Hamiltonian time depends only logarithmically on the inverse of the desired return error. Numerical benchmarks show substantial reductions in end-to-end elapsed Hamiltonian time relative to a task-specialized copy-consuming protocol, even under an optimistic preparation-cost model.

Conclusion: Optimal copy-dynamics trade-offs can be attained together with catalytic return. The results reveal a copy-dynamics resource landscape connecting copy-scarce learning to copy-only endpoints realized by known protocols for shadow tomography and pure-state tomography.

Abstract: Probing multiple properties of ground states is central to understanding quantum matter, yet conventional readout can be prohibitively costly because it consumes many independently prepared copies. Here, in copy-scarce regimes, we establish optimal trade-offs between the number of initial ground-state copies and elapsed Hamiltonian-evolution time required for estimating multiple observables or reconstructing a full classical description of a unique gapped ground state. We derive a unifying inequality that quantifies how well ground states can be distinguished using initial copies and controlled dynamics, even when all copies may be consumed. It yields worst-case lower bounds on elapsed Hamiltonian time that any protocol must incur to perform either task. We construct parallel catalytic readouts that return all initial copies nearly unchanged while attaining these bounds up to poly-logarithmic factors. Our protocols require no coherent preparation circuit or its inverse, and their elapsed Hamiltonian time depends only logarithmically on the inverse of the desired return error. Thus, optimal copy--dynamics trade-offs can be attained together with catalytic return. Numerical benchmarks further show substantial reductions in end-to-end elapsed Hamiltonian time relative to a task-specialized copy-consuming protocol, even under an optimistic preparation-cost model. Our results reveal a copy--dynamics resource landscape connecting copy-scarce learning to copy-only endpoints realized by known protocols for shadow tomography and pure-state tomography.

</details>


### [16] [Input-output formulation of quantum light spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38533)
*Liwen Ko,Robert L. Cook,K. Birgitta Whaley*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops an input-output formulation of quantum light spectroscopy by merging quantum optics' input-output theory with the perturbative expansion method from chemical physics, enabling non-perturbative and perturbative evaluation of spectroscopic signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum light spectroscopy requires a framework that connects the quantum optical description of photon fields with the perturbative nonlinear spectroscopy methods used in chemical physics. Existing approaches lack a formulation that provides both physical intuition for how matter alters the photon input field and a practical computational scheme.

Method: The authors combine input-output theory (quantum optics) with perturbative expansion (nonlinear spectroscopy). The input-output relation describes how the matter system modifies the photon input field. They apply this to linear spectroscopy, fluorescence, intra-beam G^{(2)}, and Hong-Ou-Mandel correlation, evaluating signal expectation values non-perturbatively for certain initial photon states and perturbatively via expansion of the input-output relation for general photon states.

Result: For certain initial photon states, signal expectation values can be evaluated non-perturbatively. For general initial photon states, signals are computed via perturbative expansion of the input-output relation. The input-output relation reduces the work needed to perturbatively expand the optical signal.

Conclusion: The input-output formulation of quantum light spectroscopy enables cleaner derivations and clearer physical interpretations, providing a unified framework for computing spectroscopic signals under quantum light.

Abstract: We develop an input-output formulation of quantum light spectroscopy (QLS) by combining the input-output theory, commonly used in quantum optics, with the perturbative expansion method for nonlinear spectroscopy, commonly used in chemical physics. The use of the input-output relation provides physical intuition for how matter systems alter the photon input field. As examples, we consider linear spectroscopy, fluorescence, intra-beam $G^{(2)}$, and Hong-Ou-Mandel correlation. For certain initial photon states, we show that signal expectation values can be evaluated non-perturbatively. For general initial photon states, the signals can be computed via a perturbative expansion of the input-output relation. The input-output relation reduces the work needed to perturbatively expand the optical signal, allowing for cleaner derivations and clearer physical interpretations.

</details>


### [17] [Proposal for matter-wave interferometry with a rare-earth-doped microparticle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38564)
*Chris Overstreet*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes matter-wave interferometry of a microparticle embedding a rare-earth ion, using optical transitions to split the center-of-mass wavefunction in a time-symmetric geometry that cancels initial-condition sensitivity. External decoherence rates do not grow with mass, so sufficiently large particles evade decoherence; a moderate-vacuum apparatus could achieve up to 10^3 photons per beam splitter and millisecond coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Matter-wave interferometers are sensitive probes of low-energy physics for testing gravity and quantum mechanics, and heavier interfering particles would improve these tests. However, interfering a large particle is difficult: one must control its initial state and avoid decoherence, which typically worsens with increasing mass.

Method: The authors propose a microparticle with an embedded rare-earth ion whose optical transitions impart momentum to the microparticle center of mass. A time-symmetric interferometer geometry combined with a rare-earth ion state of angular momentum J = 1/2 removes sensitivity to the microparticle's initial conditions. They analyze the scaling of all relevant external decoherence mechanisms with particle mass.

Result: All relevant external decoherence rates either decrease or remain constant as microparticle mass increases, so decoherence can be avoided with sufficiently large particles. An apparatus at moderate vacuum levels supports interferometry with up to 10^3 photons per beam splitter and millisecond coherence time.

Conclusion: This demonstration would establish microparticle interferometry as a new quantum sensing platform, improving near-term searches for minimal modifications of quantum mechanics by up to four orders of magnitude and providing a foundation for future gravitational tests.

Abstract: Matter-wave interferometers are sensitive probes of low energy physics and have been used for precise tests of gravity and quantum mechanics. These applications would benefit from interfering particles of higher mass, but observing the interference of a large particle is challenging due to the need to control its initial state and to avoid decoherence. Here we propose to demonstrate matter-wave interference of a microparticle with an embedded rare-earth ion. Optical transitions of the rare-earth ion will impart momentum to the microparticle's center of mass. The use of a time-symmetric interferometer geometry and a rare-earth ion state with angular momentum $J = 1/2$ will eliminate sensitivity to the initial conditions of the microparticle. We show that the rates of all relevant external decoherence mechanisms either decrease or remain constant as the microparticle mass is increased, allowing decoherence to be avoided by using sufficiently large particles. An apparatus at moderate vacuum levels will support microparticle interferometry with up to $10^3$ photons per beam splitter and millisecond coherence time. This demonstration will establish microparticle interferometry as a new platform for quantum sensing, improving searches for minimal modifications of quantum mechanics by up to four orders of magnitude in the near term and laying the foundation for future gravitational tests.

</details>


### [18] [Normal-ordered perturbative expansion for matter systems interacting with continuous-mode quantum photon fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38576)
*Liwen Ko,Robert L. Cook,K. Birgitta Whaley*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a normal-ordered perturbative expansion to compute the reduced dynamics of a matter system coupled to a general continuous-mode photon field, showing faster convergence and exact truncation for Fock-state inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional perturbative expansions for matter-light interaction involve non-normal-ordered field correlation functions whose physical meaning is unclear, and they converge slowly at intermediate coupling strengths, limiting their applicability.

Method: A normal-ordered perturbative expansion is developed in which all field correlation functions are automatically normal-ordered and the free evolution is modified by spontaneous emission. The expansion is compared with the conventional one, analyzed for truncation behavior under Fock-state inputs, and checked for consistency with master equations for special input states. Numerical demonstration is performed for a two-level system with coherent, m-photon Fock, and cat state inputs.

Result: The normal-ordered expansion clarifies the physical meaning of non-normal-ordered correlation terms, converges significantly faster than the conventional expansion at intermediate coupling strengths, extends perturbative applicability to larger coupling strengths, and truncates exactly at the 2m-th order for an m-photon Fock state input. It is consistent with the corresponding master equations for special classes of input states.

Conclusion: The normal-ordered expansion provides an improved and physically transparent perturbative framework for matter-light dynamics, with enhanced convergence and exact truncation properties, verified numerically for several input states.

Abstract: We develop a normal-ordered perturbative expansion method to compute the reduced dynamics of a matter system interacting with a general continuous-mode photon field state. In this expansion, all field correlation functions are automatically normal-ordered, while the free evolution is modified by spontaneous emission. Our normal-ordered expansion elucidates the physical meaning of non-normal-ordered correlation terms in the conventional expansion. Furthermore, under intermediate coupling strengths, the normal-ordered expansion converges significantly faster than the conventional expansion, allowing one to extend the applicability of perturbative expansion to much larger matter-light coupling strengths. Interestingly, given an $m$-photon Fock state input, the normal-ordered expansion truncates exactly at the $2m$-th order. For special classes of input states, we show the normal-ordered expansion is consistent with the corresponding master equations. We demonstrate the normal-ordered expansion numerically for a two-level system interacting with coherent state, $m$-photon Fock state, and cat state inputs.

</details>


### [19] [Algebraic Characterization of Biphoton Spatial-Mode Entanglement in Higher-Order Laguerre--Gaussian-Pumped SPDC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38580)
*Takumi Jinushi,Hirokazu Kobayashi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops an algebraic framework for biphoton spatial-mode entanglement in LG-pumped SPDC by factoring it into a spatial-mode beam-splitter followed by two-mode squeezing, yielding a natural modal basis, conservation laws, and an analytic Schmidt number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional spatial entanglement from SPDC is a powerful resource for quantum information processing, but its mode structure becomes complex when the pump occupies a higher-order Laguerre-Gaussian mode. A unified, tractable characterization of this mode structure was lacking.

Method: The authors factorize LG-pumped SPDC into a spatial-mode beam-splitter transformation followed by spatial two-mode squeezing. They choose the biphoton LG basis radius as the geometric mean of the pump-beam radius and the crystal-induced correlation width, giving a natural modal basis where the two operations can be treated separately. They also derive an analytical expression for the Schmidt number for arbitrary LG pump modes and squeezing strengths.

Result: When the pump-beam radius matches the correlation width, spatial squeezing vanishes, and the two circular-mode components of the pump are independently conserved across signal and idler, conserving both orbital angular momentum and the total spatial mode number. The analytic Schmidt number reveals how radial and azimuthal pump indices govern the dimensionality of the spatial entanglement.

Conclusion: The algebraic characterization provides a unified description of the mode structure, conservation laws, and entanglement dimensionality of higher-order-LG-pumped SPDC.

Abstract: High-dimensional spatial entanglement generated via spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) provides a powerful resource for quantum information processing, yet its mode structure becomes increasingly complex when the pump occupies a higher-order Laguerre--Gaussian (LG) mode. Here, we develop an algebraic framework for characterizing biphoton spatial-mode entanglement by factorizing LG-pumped SPDC into a spatial-mode beam-splitter transformation followed by spatial two-mode squeezing. By choosing the biphoton LG basis radius as the geometric mean of the pump-beam radius and the crystal-induced correlation width, we obtain a natural modal basis in which the two operations can be treated separately. When the pump-beam radius matches the correlation width, spatial squeezing vanishes, and the two circular-mode components of the pump are independently conserved across the signal and idler photons, leading to conservation of both orbital angular momentum and the total spatial mode number. We further derive an analytical expression for the Schmidt number for arbitrary LG pump modes and squeezing strengths, revealing how the radial and azimuthal pump indices govern the dimensionality of the spatial entanglement. This algebraic characterization provides a unified description of the mode structure, conservation laws, and entanglement dimensionality of higher-order-LG-pumped SPDC.

</details>


### [20] [Passive realism in the presence of open system dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38631)
*James Fullwood,Boyu Yang,Weixiang Ye*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "passive realism" and tests whether open-system dynamics can restore the no-signalling in time (NSIT) condition. It proves that environmental masking is possible only if the initial state is maximally mixed or the channel is discard-and-prepare; otherwise binary projective measurements violating NSIT always exist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The scale-independent assumption that a system's pre-existing properties can be passively observed without altering its later dynamics underlies passive realism. Closed quantum systems generally violate this via measurement back action. The authors ask whether coupling to an environment can dissipate this disturbance and restore NSIT, which would rescue passive realism for open systems.

Method: They formalize passive realism and identify NSIT as its necessary condition: a measurement outcome must be independent of whether a prior measurement was performed. They model the dynamics between two sequential measurements as an arbitrary quantum channel and analyze when NSIT can hold. They derive the conditions for environmental masking and give a constructive procedure to find NSIT-violating binary projective measurements, applying it to depolarizing and dephasing channels.

Result: Environmental masking of measurement disturbance is possible only in two special cases: when the initial state is maximally mixed, or when the channel is a discard-and-prepare channel. Outside these cases there always exist binary projective measurements violating NSIT and hence passive realism. The constructive procedure identifies these violating measurements for depolarizing and dephasing channels.

Conclusion: Except for the maximally mixed initial state and discard-and-prepare channel, system-environment interactions cannot erase the statistical footprint of a quantum measurement for all measurement choices. Passive realism therefore fails generically in open quantum systems, as the measurement's effect remains detectable.

Abstract: We introduce the terminology \emph{passive realism} to encapsulate the scale-independent assumption that a physical system's pre-existing properties can be passively observed without altering its subsequent dynamics. A necessary condition for passive realism is provided by the no-signalling in time (NSIT) condition, which states that a measurement outcome is independent of whether or not a prior measurement was performed. While closed quantum systems generically violate NSIT due to measurement back action, we investigate whether open-system dynamics can dissipate this disturbance to the environment and restore NSIT. By modeling the time evolution between sequential measurements as an arbitrary quantum channel, we show that such environmental masking is possible only in two special cases. Specifically, we show that if the initial state of a two-time sequential measurement scenario is not maximally mixed, and if the channel governing the dynamics between measurements is not a discard-and-prepare channel, then there necessarily exist binary projective measurements violating NSIT, hence violating passive realism. By providing a constructive procedure for determining these violating measurements---applied here to depolarizing and dephasing channels---we demonstrate that, outside these two cases, system-environment interactions cannot erase the statistical footprint of a quantum measurement for all measurement choices.

</details>


### [21] [Towards noble gas quantum optical magnetometry using direct ultraviolet detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38633)
*James Maldaner,Gil Porat*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A theoretical analysis of a noble-gas magnetometer using an ultraviolet frequency comb for direct nuclear spin optical detection, predicting a record sensitivity of 24 aT/√Hz achievable in 10 minutes with a 10 W laser at -100 dBc/Hz relative intensity noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional quantum magnetic sensing based on nuclear spin precession in noble gases typically requires mixing alkali vapor with the noble gas, which shortens spin relaxation time and forces cryogenic or high-temperature operation. The authors seek a scheme that avoids alkali vapor to preserve long spin relaxation times at room temperature and to potentially surpass state-of-the-art magnetometric performance, including cryogenic magnetometers.

Method: The authors perform a theoretical noise analysis of a sensing scheme in which nuclear spin precession in a noble gas is detected optically via an ultraviolet frequency comb laser, with no alkali vapor present during the sensing stage. They model the contributions of fundamental and technical noise sources, examining the roles of laser power, relative intensity noise (RIN), and beam size on achievable sensitivity and identifying the regimes where each noise type dominates.

Result: The analysis shows that combining high laser power with low laser intensity noise is essential to reach and exceed state-of-the-art magnetometric performance, and that beam-size optimization is critical for optimal sensitivity. Distinct operational regimes are identified where either fundamental or technical noise dominates. A record-breaking sensitivity of 24 aT/√Hz is predicted to be achievable in 10 minutes using a 10 W ultraviolet laser with -100 dBc/Hz RIN.

Conclusion: Alkali-free ultraviolet frequency comb detection of noble-gas nuclear spin precession is a promising route to ultra-sensitive, room-temperature magnetometry, with the design guided by balancing laser power, intensity noise, and beam size. Experimental efforts should target higher laser power or lower RIN depending on whether fundamental or technical noise dominates.

Abstract: We analyze a novel approach for quantum magnetic sensing via optical detection of nuclear spin precession in a noble gas. The detection is carried out with an ultraviolet frequency comb laser, so no alkali vapor is required to be mixed with the noble gas during the sensing stage, allowing for hours-long spin relaxation time at room temperature. Our analysis reveals that combining high laser power and low laser intensity noise is crucial to achieving and exceeding state-of-the-art magnetometric performance, including that of cryogenic magnetometers. We find operational regimes where either fundamental or technical noise dominate, which directs experimental effort towards increasing laser power or lowering laser intensity noise, respectively. We also show that optimizing beam size is key to achieving optimal sensitivity. Our calculations predict that a record-breaking sensitivity of $24~\mathrm{aT}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ is achievable in 10 minutes with a 10 W ultraviolet laser with a relative intensity noise of -100 dBc/Hz.

</details>


### [22] [Exponential separation in sensing continuous signals via squeezing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38678)
*Francesco Anna Mele,Nadine Meister,Haimeng Zhao,Senrui Chen,Hsin-Yuan Huang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes an exponential separation in sensing time for continuously evolving signals based on available squeezing: sensors with squeezing at least ω(√log T) achieve poly(T) sensing time, while those with squeezing at most o(√log T) require exponential sensing time in T.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum sensing traditionally targets fixed signals using squeezing and entanglement for precision gains, but many real applications (e.g., gravitational-wave detection, electromagnetic-field sensing) involve signals that evolve continuously over time and where the learner can adaptively prepare, control, and measure the sensor. The role of squeezing in this time-varying, adaptive regime is not established.

Method: The paper formalizes continuously evolving signals characterized by a pattern size T and analyzes the sensing time achievable as a function of the amount of squeezing available. It derives upper and lower bounds separating sensors by their squeezing strength (ω(√log T) versus o(√log T)).

Result: An exponential separation in sensing time is proven: sensors with squeezing at least ω(√log T) attain poly(T) sensing time, whereas sensors with squeezing at most o(√log T) must use exponential sensing time in T.

Conclusion: Squeezing provides an exponential advantage in sensing time for continuously evolving signals, establishing a sharp threshold at √log T and highlighting the value of quantum resources in time-dependent adaptive sensing tasks.

Abstract: Quantum sensing traditionally focuses on using quantum resources such as squeezing and entanglement to improve precision for sensing fixed signals. However, in many applications such as gravitational-wave detection and electromagnetic-field sensing, the signal evolves continuously and varies through time. Additionally, the learner is free to prepare, control, and measure the sensor at arbitrary times, possibly chosen adaptively. In this work, we establish exponential separation in sensing time for continuously evolving signals due to the available squeezing. The signals we study are characterized by a pattern size $T$, and we find that a sensor with squeezing at least $ω(\sqrt{\log T})$ offers a $\mathrm{poly}(T)$ sensing time, whereas those with squeezing at most $o(\sqrt{\log T})$ must use an exponential sensing time in $T$.

</details>


### [23] [Polarisation-resolved identification of spontaneous four-wave mixing processes in a multimode fused tapered fibre coupler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38687)
*Jefferson Flórez,Chams Baker,Benjamin J. Sussman,Xiaoyi Bao,Jeff S. Lundeen,Lambert Giner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies spontaneous four-wave mixing (SFWM) in a fused tapered-fibre microcoupler, observing multimode photon-pair generation and identifying distinct intermodal SFWM processes via phase-matching, selection rules, and polarisation measurements, with theoretical prediction of entanglement generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrated quantum photonics needs photon-pair sources emitting directly into waveguides for efficient collection and manipulation. Multimode waveguide devices can host multiple SFWM processes yielding similar wavelengths, so wavelength alone cannot identify their physical origin, creating a need for a robust identification strategy.

Method: The authors investigate SFWM in a fused tapered-fibre microcoupler made from two single-mode fibres. The elliptical central region supports six guided modes (three spatial profiles × two polarisations). Pumping near 800 nm, they observe photon pairs at 648/1048 nm and 660/1021 nm. They combine phase-matching calculations and selection rules with two independent polarisation measurements—dependence of coincidence rate on pump polarisation and polarisation tomography of generated photons—to distinguish the processes. They then extend the analysis theoretically beyond the measured operating point.

Result: SFWM photon pairs are observed in this multimode device. Within the geometric model, the 660/1021 nm pair arises from co-polarised pump photons in a process entirely within a single higher-order mode, whereas the 648/1048 nm pair arises from orthogonally polarised pump photons in an intermodal process coupling fundamental and higher-order modes. Signal photons from the two processes have similar output polarisations, while idler photons are nearly orthogonal. Theoretically, pairs of simultaneous SFWM processes driven by a common pump could generate either polarisation entanglement or composite spatial-polarisation entanglement, depending on pump wavelength.

Conclusion: This work provides a practical strategy for identifying intermodal SFWM processes in multimode waveguides when spectral information alone is insufficient, and highlights the potential for generating polarisation and composite spatial-polarisation entanglement in such devices.

Abstract: Integrated quantum photonics benefits from photon-pair sources that generate photons directly in waveguides, where they can be efficiently collected, routed, and manipulated. Here, we investigate spontaneous four-wave mixing (SFWM) in a fused tapered-fibre microcoupler formed from two single-mode fibres, and report four contributions. First, we observe SFWM photon pairs in this multimode device: its elliptical central region supports three spatial mode profiles, each with two polarisations, giving six guided modes, and pumping it near 800 nm yields two photon pairs at 648/1048 nm and 660/1021 nm. Second, because several allowed SFWM processes produce similar wavelengths and wavelength alone does not identify their origin, we distinguish the processes by combining phase-matching calculations and selection rules with two independent polarisation measurements: the dependence of the coincidence rate on pump polarisation and polarisation tomography of the generated photons. Third, within the present geometrical model, we find that the 660/1021 nm pair is generated by co-polarised pump photons in a process occurring entirely within a single higher-order mode, whereas the 648/1048 nm pair is generated by orthogonally polarised pump photons in an intermodal process coupling a fundamental and a higher-order mode; the signal photons from the two processes have similar output polarisations, while the idler photons are nearly orthogonal. Fourth, extending the analysis beyond the measured operating point, we theoretically identify pairs of simultaneous SFWM processes driven by a common pump that could generate either polarisation entanglement or composite spatial-polarisation entanglement, depending on the pump wavelength. More generally, this work provides a practical strategy for identifying intermodal SFWM processes in multimode waveguides when spectral information alone is insufficient.

</details>


### [24] [A Separation Between Types of Quantum Oracle Separations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38704)
*Scott Aaronson,Adam Bouland,Jordan Docter,Barak Nehoran*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "meta-complexity of quantum relativization," classifying which quantum oracle types can separate PostBQP from PreciseBQP, showing they coincide under real or polynomial-dimensional unitary oracles and with inverse/conjugate access, but separate under forward-only complex diagonal unitaries of superpolynomial dimension and under single-qubit state-preparation oracles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed quantum oracles behave subtly: inverse, conjugate, or controlled queries can exponentially change query complexity. The authors generalize this to a meta-level question—which "types" of quantum oracles equalize or separate any two quantum complexity classes—motivated by understanding the subtleties inherent to relativizing quantum complexity classes.

Method: They define "oracle pantheons" (quantum oracle types such as unitary vs. state, polynomial- vs. superpolynomial-dimensional, closed under inverse or not), forming a partially ordered set by separating power. Two pantheons are separated if some complexity-class pair is separated under one but equal under all oracles of the other. They give a complete classification of which pantheons separate PostBQP and PreciseBQP (the exponentially precise variant of BQP); both equal PP unrelativized.

Result: PostBQP = PreciseBQP relative to real and polynomial-dimensional unitary oracles and whenever inverse or conjugate access is available. In contrast, a separation is shown relative to forward-only complex diagonal unitaries of superpolynomial dimension, and relative to single-qubit state-preparation oracles.

Conclusion: This provides a nontrivial test case for the meta-complexity of oracles, demonstrating that the classification of oracle pantheons by separating power yields genuinely nontrivial structure and underscoring the subtlety of relativization for quantum complexity classes.

Abstract: Recent works have demonstrated that quantum oracles have subtle behavior, as access to inverse, conjugate or controlled queries can exponentially change the query complexity of certain tasks. Inspired by these works, we introduce the notion of meta-complexity of quantum relativization. We ask: for any two quantum complexity classes, under which "types" of quantum oracles are they equal or separated? Different pantheons of oracles (or quantum oracle types, e.g. unitary vs state, poly- vs superpoly-dimensional, closed under inverse or not) form a partially ordered set based on their power in separating complexity classes.
