Moreover, two oracle pantheons A and B are separated if there exists a pair of complexity classes that are separated under an oracle from pantheon A but yet the complexity classes are equivalent under all oracles from pantheon B.
  We show that this meta-complexity can be nontrivial by giving a complete classification, within the family of oracle pantheons defined in this paper, of which models can separate the complexity classes $\mathsf{PostBQP}$ and $\mathsf{PreciseBQP}$, the exponentially precise variant of $\mathsf{BQP}$. Both classes equal $\mathsf{PP}$ in the unrelativized setting. Within our taxonomy, they remain equal relative to real or polynomial-dimensional unitary oracles and whenever inverse or conjugate access is supplied. In contrast, we give a separation relative to forward-only complex diagonal unitaries of superpolynomial dimension, as well as a separation relative to single-qubit state-preparation oracles. We view this as a test case for the meta-complexity of oracles which underscores the subtlety inherent to the relativization of quantum complexity classes.

</details>


### [25] [When Error Mitigation Makes Things Worse: Budget-Aware Evaluation, Extrapolation Failure, and the Calibration Trust Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38713)
*Guilin Zhang,Kai Zhao,Xiquan Cui,Henry Heng,Xu Chu,Aletta Johanna Blanken*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that error mitigation, specifically zero-noise extrapolation (ZNE), can amplify rather than reduce errors in near-term quantum pipelines. It connects budget-aware evaluation, tail risk, and calibration integrity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Error mitigation is expected to transform noisy quantum measurements into more useful estimates. The authors challenge this assumption by investigating whether mitigation can actually amplify error under realistic conditions such as miscalibration and unreliable provider-reported calibration metadata.

Method: The study evaluates unconstrained ZNE estimators under control-offset miscalibration in simulation and on a small IBM Heron device. It compares mitigation methods at equal online shot budgets per evaluation, reporting offline training cost separately. A budget-conditioned neural corrector with an ensemble-based disagreement fallback is introduced and assessed against an accuracy-cost frontier. A white-box projected-gradient stress test probes the reliance of the corrector on provider-reported calibration metadata treated as an input not bound to execution-time device state.

Result: Under miscalibration, ZNE estimators were worse than no mitigation on 38-63% of simulated instances and produced extreme tail errors. On the IBM Heron study, performance worsened on 80-88% of 18 instances across two depths, with mean error 3.0-4.1 times the raw error. Median error changed little, so median-only monitoring missed the failures. The neural corrector occupied the low-budget end of the simulated accuracy-cost frontier and fell back to the raw estimate on ensemble disagreement, but hardware transfer remained unsuccessful. The metadata stress test increased the corrector's error 8.1 times within training feature ranges and evaded the disagreement monitor on half of the worsened cases.

Conclusion: Error mitigation can amplify error under miscalibration and unreliable calibration metadata, with failures hidden from median-based monitoring. The work highlights the need for budget-aware evaluation, tail-risk awareness, and calibration integrity in near-term quantum learning pipelines.

Abstract: Error mitigation is expected to turn noisy quantum measurements into more useful estimates. We show that it can instead amplify error. Under control-offset miscalibration, the evaluated unconstrained zero-noise extrapolation (ZNE) estimators are worse than no mitigation on 38-63% of simulated instances and produce extreme tail errors. A small IBM Heron study finds worsening on 80-88% of 18 instances across two depths, with mean error 3.0-4.1 times the raw error. In simulation, the median changes little, so median-only monitoring misses the failures. We then compare methods at the same online shot budget per evaluation and report offline training cost separately. After that cost is amortized, a budget-conditioned neural corrector occupies the low-budget end of the simulated accuracy-cost frontier and falls back to the raw estimate when an ensemble disagrees; its hardware transfer remains unsuccessful. Finally, we treat provider-reported calibration as an input that is not bound to the execution-time device state. A white-box projected-gradient stress test on this metadata increases the corrector's error 8.1 times within the feature ranges used for training and evades the disagreement monitor on half of the worsened cases. Together, the results connect budget-aware evaluation, tail risk, and calibration integrity in near-term quantum learning pipelines.

</details>


### [26] [Does the Readout Bypass Leak the Input? A Feature-Visibility Audit of Hybrid Quantum-Classical Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38720)
*Guilin Zhang,Kai Zhao,Xiquan Cui,Henry Heng,Xu Chu,Aletta Johanna Blanken*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Under single-example gradient sharing, residual hybrid models with a biased first linear layer allow analytic recovery of raw inputs, exposing bypassed coordinates even without inverting the quantum circuit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Readout-side residual hybrids concatenate raw inputs with measured quantum features, a design choice whose privacy implications were not previously audited in a coordinate-aware way.

Method: Two tabular datasets, four architectures, metrics conditioned on feature visibility; iterative gradient matching used to reconstruct inputs from residual and input-only heads versus quantum-only heads, plus a loss-threshold membership attack.

Result: Residual and input-only heads reach median full-record PSNR of 73-96 dB; quantum-only heads score 8-11 dB on the full record but 54-96 dB on the six encoded input coordinates. Membership attack stays near chance.

Conclusion: Omitted coordinates should not be credited as quantum-supplied protection, and near-exact PSNR gaps should not be treated as meaningful privacy rankings; findings apply to individual gradients, not aggregation or multiple local steps.

Abstract: Readout-side residual hybrids concatenate raw inputs with measured quantum features. Under single-example gradient sharing, a biased first linear layer admits standard analytic recovery of its input, so the bypass exposes raw coordinates without requiring inversion of the quantum circuit. We audit this mechanism using two tabular datasets, four architectures, and metrics conditioned on feature visibility. Iterative gradient matching gives median full-record PSNR of 73-96 dB for residual and input-only heads. Quantum-only heads score 8-11 dB on the full record but 54-96 dB on the six input coordinates they actually encode. These are reconstruction results for the tested six-input, six-observable circuits, not a general statement about quantum encodings. A loss-threshold membership attack remains near chance. The contribution is a visibility-conditioned privacy audit: omitted coordinates must not be credited as protection supplied by quantum processing, and near-exact PSNR differences must not be interpreted as meaningful privacy rankings. Our findings concern individual gradients and do not establish leakage under aggregation or multiple local training steps.

</details>


### [27] [Ergotropy from energetic coherence and the third law of thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38724)
*O. S. Yáñez-Soria,G. Guarnieri,B. Çakmak,R. Román-Ancheyta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work derives closed-form expressions for ergotropy and thermodynamic potentials of a qubit battery charged via repeated interactions with thermal ancilla clusters, showing that extractable work originates entirely from steady-state coherence and exhibits universal, statistics-independent behavior at large cluster sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study is motivated by the need to understand how quantum coherence, generated through composite system-bath coupling, governs the extractable work (ergotropy) of quantum batteries, and how bath quantum statistics and the third law constrain charging performance.

Method: A qubit battery is charged by repeated interactions with clusters of thermal ancillas (qubits or oscillators) via a composite system-bath coupling that induces steady-state coherence in the energy basis. A single parameter encoding the bath quantum statistics is introduced to obtain the coherence and ergotropy in closed form for arbitrary cluster size; the ergotropy is then analyzed against equilibrium thermodynamic quantities and extended to multi-qubit batteries.

Result: Coherence and ergotropy are obtained in closed form for any cluster size via one statistics-encoding parameter. For large clusters, ergotropy becomes independent of bath statistics and, at optimal couplings, reaches a universal fraction of the qubit's equilibrium internal energy at bath temperature. Since the charged state has no population inversion, all extractable work comes from coherence. The ergotropy is flat at low temperatures, traced to the third law, and is proportional to the equilibrium enthalpy of the qubit whose slope vanishes with heat capacity. For multi-qubit batteries, coherence grows exponentially with qubit number while ergotropy grows linearly, both remaining nearly constant fractions of their maxima.

Conclusion: The charged state is a non-equilibrium steady state with finite entropy at zero temperature, sustained by the work of switching the collisional interaction on and off. This cost is nonzero whenever the parallel coupling is nonzero and, unlike the ergotropy, depends on the bath statistics.

Abstract: We study the ergotropy and the thermodynamic potentials of a qubit battery charged by repeated interactions with clusters of thermal ancillas, qubits or oscillators, through a composite system-bath coupling that generates steady-state coherence in the energy basis. A single parameter encoding the bath quantum statistics yields the coherence and ergotropy in closed form for any cluster size. For large clusters, the ergotropy becomes independent of the bath statistics and, at optimal couplings, reaches a universal fraction of the equilibrium internal energy of the qubit at the bath temperature. Since the charged state has no population inversion, all extractable work comes from coherence. The ergotropy is flat at low temperatures. We trace this plateau to the third law, and show that the ergotropy is proportional to the equilibrium enthalpy of the qubit, whose slope vanishes with the heat capacity. For a battery composed of multiple qubits, the coherence grows exponentially with the number of qubits while the ergotropy grows linearly, both remaining a nearly constant fraction of their respective maxima. The charged state is a non-equilibrium steady state with finite entropy at zero temperature, sustained by the work of switching the collisional interaction on and off. The cost is nonzero whenever the parallel coupling is nonzero, and, unlike the ergotropy, it depends on the bath statistics.

</details>


### [28] [Exact Diagonal Completion on Reachable Subspaces: Application to QAOA Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38734)
*Owen Friedewald,Ali Shiri Sichani,Chi-Ren Shyu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work optimizes unspecified diagonal operators in QAOA placement circuits via weighted-ℓ₁ exact completion, reducing CX counts in most unused-code geometries under Gray-code synthesis, though gains are compiler-dependent and no end-to-end advantage over classical search is established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unused encoding states in restricted reachable subspaces leave diagonal-operator entries unspecified, offering a degree of freedom to simplify quantum circuits without altering ideal computation, provided the unspecified entries can be completed optimally.

Method: Exact diagonal completion for QAOA placement using permutation-preserving register swaps. Exact Manhattan-distance operators are constructed via weighted-ℓ₁ optimization of Walsh coefficients plus a sparse recurrence needing O(√m) terms on balanced rectangles, avoiding O(m⁴) dense constraint storage.

Result: Across 160 geometries, weighted-ℓ₁ completion lowers CX counts versus four alternative extensions in all 96 unused-code cases under Gray-code synthesis. On an independent 60-case cohort, median reductions versus virtual-coordinate extension are 28.0%, 53.9%, 21.6% at 6/9/12 sites; under generic diagonal synthesis reductions fall to 10.9%, 1.3%, 0.7%. Ancillas reduce mixer serialization but token circuits stay deeper than one-hot baselines. Classical search yields better solution quality. OpenROAD integration runs 72 QAOA and 216 classical placements across six RTL designs with zero overflow.

Conclusion: Diagonal completion improves phase construction and reduces CX counts, but benefits are compiler-dependent and no end-to-end advantage over classical placement is demonstrated; solution quality remains baseline-dependent with classical search performing better.

Abstract: Unused encoding states offer opportunities to simplify quantum circuits. For algorithms restricted to reachable subspaces, unspecified diagonal-operator entries can be optimized without changing ideal computation. We investigate exact diagonal completion for the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) applied to placement, using permutation-preserving register swaps. We construct exact Manhattan-distance operators through weighted-$\ell_1$ optimization of Walsh coefficients and a sparse recurrence requiring $O(\sqrt{m})$ terms on balanced rectangles, avoiding $O(m^4)$ dense constraint storage.
  Across 160 geometries, weighted-$\ell_1$ completion reduces controlled-NOT (CX) counts relative to four alternative extensions in all 96 cases with unused binary codes under Gray-code synthesis. On an independently specified 60-case cohort, median reductions relative to virtual-coordinate extension are 28.0\%, 53.9\%, and 21.6\% at six, nine, and twelve sites. Under generic diagonal synthesis, reductions decrease to 10.9\%, 1.3\%, and 0.7\%, demonstrating compiler dependence. Additional ancillas reduce mixer serialization, but token circuits remain deeper than one-hot baselines. Ideal placement simulations show baseline-dependent solution quality, with classical search performing better. OpenROAD integration takes 72 QAOA and 216 classical placements across six RTL designs through clock-tree synthesis and global routing with zero overflow. Completion improves phase construction; no end-to-end advantage is established.

</details>


### [29] [Quantum Query Complexity for List Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38736)
*Niranka Banerjee,Akinori Kawachi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: In the quantum query complexity model, searching a hidden linked list embedded in an address universe [N] has tight complexity Θ(min{ℓ, (Nℓ)^{1/4}}), showing that address space can provide a genuine quantum speedup over ordinary Θ(ℓ) traversal when N < ℓ³.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classically, memory addresses of list nodes play no role in search cost: one starts at the head and follows successor pointers, giving Θ(ℓ) traversal. The paper asks whether the quantum setting differs, specifically whether the ambient address space from which list vertices are drawn can itself affect query complexity and yield an advantage over standard traversal.

Method: The authors formalize an analogous problem in the query complexity model: input is an address universe [N], a public start symbol s, a successor oracle f whose non-⊥ values trace a hidden simple path s → a_1 → ... → a_ℓ → ⊥, and a marking oracle g marking at most one list vertex; the task is to decide whether the list contains a marked vertex (decision) or find it (search). They prove matching upper and lower bounds on the quantum query complexity for all N ≥ ℓ ≥ 1, and extend the analysis to the natural doubly linked-list version.

Result: Both the decision and search versions have quantum query complexity Θ(min{ℓ, (Nℓ)^{1/4}}) for all N ≥ ℓ ≥ 1. Hence when N < ℓ³ the optimal quantum complexity is (Nℓ)^{1/4}, strictly smaller than the Θ(ℓ) cost of ordinary linked-list traversal. The same tight asymptotic bounds hold for the doubly linked-list version.

Conclusion: The ambient address space yields a genuine quantum advantage for linked-list search, and the paper gives a precise characterization of when this occurs: specifically when N < ℓ³, where the optimal complexity (Nℓ)^{1/4} beats classical Θ(ℓ) traversal. Tight asymptotics are established for both singly and doubly linked lists.

Abstract: Searching in a linked list is one of the most basic problems in classical algorithms. Although the nodes of the list come with memory addresses, classically those addresses play no role in the cost of search: one simply starts at the head and follows successor pointers to search for an element. In this paper, we show that the quantum setting is different. Here, the ambient address space from which the list vertices are drawn can itself affect the query complexity.
  We study the following problem analogous to search in a linked list in the query complexity model: the input consists of an address universe $[N]$, a public start symbol $s$, a successor oracle $f$ whose non-$\perp$ values trace a hidden simple path $s \to a_1 \to a_2 \to \cdots \to a_\ell \to \perp$, and a marking oracle $g$ that marks at most one list vertex. The task is to decide whether the list contains a marked vertex.
  We prove that both the decision and search versions of this problem for all $N \ge \ell \ge 1$ have quantum query complexity $Θ\!\bigl(\min\{\ell,(N\ell)^{1/4}\}\bigr)$. Thus, quite surprisingly, when $N < \ell^3$ the optimal quantum complexity is $(N\ell)^{1/4}$, which is strictly smaller than the $Θ(\ell)$ cost of ordinary linked-list traversal. This gives a precise characterization of when the ambient address space yields a genuine quantum advantage for linked-list search.
  We extend our results and give the same tight asymptotic bounds for the natural double linked-list version as well.

</details>


### [30] [Generating random unitaries by products of conjugated Hamiltonian evolutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38738)
*Oscar Scholin,Apollonas S. Matsoukas-Roubeas,Sathyawageeswar Subramanian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A sequence of evolutions under a fixed Hamiltonian conjugated by a global control operator yields random unitaries converging exponentially to the Haar measure under uniform parameter sampling, provided the generated connected Lie subgroup is closed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random unitaries are a fundamental resource for quantum information processing, but provably generating them with experimentally available controls is difficult. Existing methods require computing higher-order operator expectation values and rely on statistical assumptions about the experimental Hamiltonian's spectrum for tractability.

Method: The authors implement random unitaries via a sequence of evolutions generated by a fixed Hamiltonian conjugated by a global control operator. They provide a geometric argument showing exponential convergence to the uniform random (Haar) measure on the accessible unitary group under uniform parameter sampling, and bound the required sequence-length threshold. The result holds when the connected Lie subgroup generated by the conjugated Hamiltonians is closed.

Result: Exponential convergence to the Haar measure is established under uniform parameter sampling beyond a bounded finite sequence-length threshold, valid whenever the generated connected Lie subgroup is closed.

Conclusion: Native dynamics combined with global control offers a constructive route to uniform randomness, applicable to many experimental platforms including Rydberg atoms, bosons and fermions in optical lattices, transmons, and trapped ions.

Abstract: Random unitary operations are a fundamental resource for quantum information processing, yet provably generating them with experimentally available controls remains challenging. Existing approaches often require calculating higher-order operator expectation values, relying on statistical assumptions about the experimental Hamiltonian's spectrum to make these calculations tractable. We show how to implement random unitaries by a sequence of evolutions generated by a fixed Hamiltonian conjugated by a global control operator. We give a geometric argument that establishes exponential convergence to the uniform random (Haar) measure on the accessible unitary group under uniform parameter sampling, beyond a finite sequence-length threshold that we bound. We show this holds when the connected Lie subgroup generated by the conjugated Hamiltonians is closed. Native dynamics and global control thus provide a constructive route to uniform randomness, with applications to many experimental platforms including Rydberg atoms, bosons and fermions in optical lattices, transmons, and trapped ions.

</details>


### [31] [Geometric estimation of NV charge-state contributions from a low-dimensional spectral representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38739)
*Yuto Yamakawa,Keisuke Oshimi,Keigo Arai*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A geometric, physically-calibrated method extracts NV0/NV- photoluminescence contributions from spectra taken at varying excitation intensities, achieving sub-percentage-point accuracy and high noise robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantifying the respective photoluminescence (PL) contributions of neutral (NV0) and negatively charged (NV-) nitrogen-vacancy centers is essential for charge-state-based measurements, but existing approaches require extra experimental control or lack physical identifiability.

Method: PL spectra acquired at different excitation intensities are mapped into a low-dimensional space via broadband spectral weighting, where varying NV0/NV- contributions trace a one-dimensional trajectory. Zero-phonon-line (ZPL) information from two spectra physically calibrates this trajectory, and geometric projection yields the NV- PL contribution. A three-dimensional CIE XYZ representation is used, and Fisher-information analysis quantifies retained information.

Result: On two bulk single-crystal diamond samples, NV- contributions matched an independent dual-excitation reference-spectrum method with RMS errors of 1.11 and 0.31 percentage points. Under additive spectral noise, variation was over an order of magnitude lower than a ZPL-only method. The CIE XYZ representation retained ~81% of the full-spectrum Fisher information about the NV- contribution.

Conclusion: The method provides a physically calibrated approach to low-dimensional spectral estimation, combining reduced experimental overhead with robust parameter estimation and offering a general framework for spectral sensing governed by few physical degrees of freedom.

Abstract: Nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond exist in neutral (NV0) and negatively charged (NV-) states, and quantifying their respective photoluminescence (PL) contributions is important for charge-state-based measurements. Existing methods either require additional experimental control or may suffer from limited physical identifiability. Here, we introduce a geometric method for determining NV charge-state contributions from PL spectra acquired at different excitation intensities. Each spectrum is mapped into a low-dimensional space through broadband spectral weighting, where changes in the relative NV0 and NV- contributions trace a one-dimensional trajectory. Zero-phonon-line (ZPL) information from two spectra physically calibrates this trajectory, enabling the NV-PL contribution to be determined by geometric projection. Measurements on two bulk single-crystal diamond samples yielded NV- contributions in close agreement with those obtained using an independent dual-excitation reference-spectrum method, with root-mean-square errors of 1.11 and 0.31 percentage points. Under additive spectral noise, the proposed method exhibited more than an order of magnitude less variation than a ZPL-only method. Fisher-information analysis further showed that the three-dimensional CIE XYZ representation retained approximately 81% of the information about the NV- contribution available in the full spectrum. These results establish a physically calibrated approach to low-dimensional spectral estimation that combines reduced experimental overhead with robust parameter estimation and provides a general framework for spectral sensing governed by a small number of physical degrees of freedom.

</details>


### [32] [The Generalized Semi-Clifford Conjecture Holds at Level 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38751)
*Maxwell Marcus,Sathyawageeswar Subramanian,Marcel Dall'Agnol*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves the generalized semi-Clifford conjecture for hierarchy levels k ≤ 4 in any prime dimension d, by extending fixed-point arguments of Pllaha et al. via conjugation groups Γ_1(U), Γ_2(U), ...


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Clifford hierarchy characterizes gates admitting fault-tolerant implementation via gate teleportation, but little is known about C_k for k > 3. The generalized semi-Clifford conjecture (Zeng et al.) asserts every gate in ∪_k C_k is, up to Clifford multiplication, a product of a permutation and a diagonal matrix. Previously it was known only for d=2, k=3 (Beigi–Shor; Pllaha et al.), and progress has been restricted to structural properties of restricted settings (diagonal gates, small dimension d, few qudits).

Method: Extend the fixed-point argument of Pllaha et al., which exploits fixed points of the conjugation map induced by a Clifford correction of U ∈ C_3 on the span of maximal stabilizer subgroups. Introduce and analyze conjugation groups Γ_1(U), Γ_2(U), ... generated by U P U† and beyond, where U ∈ C_k. Use these groups as the central tool, and establish a sufficient condition on such groups for gates at higher levels to be generalized semi-Clifford.

Result: Proves the generalized semi-Clifford conjecture for all k ≤ 4 and any prime dimension d. Establishes a natural sufficient condition on conjugation groups for gates in higher hierarchy levels to be generalized semi-Clifford.

Conclusion: The conjecture holds for k ≤ 4 in prime dimension d. Conjugation groups Γ_1(U), Γ_2(U), ... are expected to be a broadly useful tool for studying the Clifford hierarchy, with the sufficient condition providing a route toward higher levels and the general conjecture.

Abstract: The Clifford hierarchy $\mathsf{C}_1 \subset \mathsf{C}_2 \subset \cdots$ was introduced by Gottesman and Chuang (arXiv:quant-ph/9908010) to characterize gates that admit fault-tolerant implementation by gate teleportation. Yet, despite its rich mathematical structure and the attention it has received in recent years, little is known about $\mathsf{C}_k$ for $k > 3$. Most progress has focused on identifying structural properties of restrictions of the hierarchy, such as diagonal gates and gates on systems of small dimension $d$ or with few qudits.
  The generalized semi-Clifford conjecture, proposed by Zeng et al. (arXiv:0712.2084), states that every gate in $\cup_k \mathsf{C}_k$ is, up to multiplication by Cliffords, the product of a permutation and a diagonal matrix. Beigi and Shor proved the case $d=2$, $k=3$ (arXiv:0810.5108) and Pllaha et al. found an alternative proof by exploiting fixed points of the conjugation map induced by (a Clifford correction of) $U \in \mathsf{C}_3$ on the span of maximal stabilizer subgroups (arXiv:2006.14040). By extending their fixed-point arguments to the group $Γ_1(U)$ generated by $U \mathsf{P} U^\dagger$ and beyond, we prove the conjecture for $k \leq 4$ and any prime dimension $d$.
  Our proof centers on conjugation groups $Γ_1(U), Γ_2(U), \ldots$ of $U \in \mathsf{C}_k$, which we expect to be a useful tool in the study of the Clifford hierarchy more generally. We also show a natural sufficient condition on such groups for gates in higher levels to be generalized semi-Clifford.

</details>


### [33] [Rayleigh-Wood Threshold Controls Nonlinear Photon Correlations in Atomic Arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38770)
*Zai-Cheng Xiao,Li-Ping Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The Rayleigh-Wood anomaly in a 2D atomic array provides a sharp radiative control knob that reshapes linear and nonlinear photon scattering, producing transmission Fano zeros and order-of-magnitude suppression of two-photon correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 2D atomic arrays are promising free-space quantum optical interfaces, but a sharp, tunable mechanism to control both linear and nonlinear photon scattering is needed. The authors propose exploiting the Rayleigh-Wood (radiative diffraction) threshold as such a control principle.

Method: Theoretical analysis of photon scattering from a single atomic layer, studying the collective lattice modes as the lattice spacing crosses the diffraction threshold. They examine specular reflection versus off-axis diffuse scattering, momentum-space Fano interference between incident and collectively scattered fields in transmission, and an integrated nonlinear contrast quantifying the connected two-photon contribution relative to the factorized background.

Result: Crossing the diffraction threshold opens new radiative channels that rapidly broaden dominant collective modes, converting specular reflection into diffuse off-axis scattering. In transmission, Fano interference yields single-photon intensity zeros reshaping the momentum-space correlation pattern. The connected two-photon contribution drops by several orders of magnitude relative to the factorized background in transmission, while remaining close to unity in reflection—a pronounced channel asymmetry.

Conclusion: Radiative diffraction thresholds constitute a sharp control principle for quantum nonlinear optics in single-layer atomic arrays, enabling dramatic, channel-dependent tuning of linear and nonlinear photon scattering.

Abstract: Two-dimensional atomic arrays provide versatile free-space quantum optical interfaces by coupling photons to collective lattice modes. Here we show that the Rayleigh--Wood anomaly provides a sharp control mechanism for both linear and nonlinear photon scattering from a single atomic layer. As the lattice spacing crosses the diffraction threshold, newly opened radiative channels rapidly broaden the dominant collective modes, converting specular reflection into diffuse off-axis scattering. In transmission, momentum-space Fano interference between the incident and collectively scattered fields produces single-photon intensity zeros that strongly reshape the momentum-space correlation pattern. An integrated nonlinear contrast reveals a pronounced channel asymmetry: upon opening the Rayleigh channels, the connected two-photon contribution drops by several orders of magnitude relative to the factorized background in transmission, while remaining close to unity in reflection. These results establish radiative diffraction thresholds as a control principle for quantum nonlinear optics in single-layer atomic arrays.

</details>


### [34] [Experimentally Testable Quantum Advantage in Shallow Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38775)
*Kishor Bharti,Adán Cabello*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper refines a finite-size classical soundness bound for a graph-distributed nonlocal-game construction to scale linearly with the number of players, yielding a two-round single-processor test with arbitrarily small classical success and an explicit 99-qubit experimental proposal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experimental demonstrations of shallow-circuit quantum advantage require explicit classical bounds at finite circuit sizes. Existing finite-size classical soundness bounds for graph-distributed constructions were insufficiently tight or did not scale favorably with player number, limiting practical, experimentally testable separations.

Method: Refine the finite-size classical soundness bound of Aasnaess's graph-distributed construction to depend linearly on the number of players. Combine this with standard disjoint-player repetition: play m copies with disjoint players and teleport each player's register to a uniformly random one of N sites before questions are revealed. Bound classical response locality: each answer bit of a depth-D, fan-in-K classical response with fixed wiring depends on at most K^D question wires. Provide an explicit 99-qubit experimental proposal.

Result: A classical device wins with probability at most γ^m + O(mK^D/N), which vanishes as O(log N / N) for m = ceil(log_{1/γ} N) at fixed D and K. The test is two-round on a single processor for any finite nonlocal game with a finite-dimensional perfect quantum strategy and classical winning probability γ < 1. The quantum implementation has constant depth per round and wins with certainty.

Conclusion: The refined bound enables a practical, experimentally feasible shallow-circuit quantum advantage test with arbitrarily small classical success probability, exemplified by an explicit 99-qubit proposal.

Abstract: Experimental tests of shallow-circuit quantum advantage require explicit classical bounds at finite circuit sizes. We refine the finite-size classical soundness bound of Aasnaess's graph-distributed construction to depend linearly on the number of players. Combined with standard disjoint-player repetition, this gives a two-round test on a single processor for any finite nonlocal game with a finite-dimensional perfect quantum strategy and classical winning probability $γ<1$, with arbitrarily small classical success. The method is based on playing $m$ copies with disjoint players and teleporting each player's register to a uniformly random one of $N$ sites before the questions are revealed. Each answer bit of a depth-$D$, fan-in-$K$ classical response with fixed wiring depends on at most $K^D$ question wires, making cross-player dependencies unlikely when $N$ is large. The quantum implementation has constant depth per round and wins with certainty. A classical device wins with probability at most $γ^m+O(mK^D/N)$, which vanishes as $O(\log N/N)$ for $m=\lceil\log_{1/γ}N\rceil$ at fixed $D$ and $K$. We present an explicit proposal for an experimentally testable quantum advantage with 99 qubits.

</details>


### [35] [Motzkin-Straus Optimization on an Entropy-Computing Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38779)
*PoJen Wang,Sutapa Samanta,Yuntai Song,Mohammad-Ali Miri*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework for combinatorial optimization via sum-constrained continuous quadratic programs solved on QCi's Dirac-3S photonic entropy computer, leveraging the Motzkin-Straus theorem to bridge discrete clique problems and optimization over the probability simplex. Benchmarked on the DIMACS suite, the platform matches or beats two classical baselines on over four-fifths of instances and reaches best known solutions on nearly all structured graph families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a viable pathway for solving combinatorial optimization problems using natively analog unconventional computing platforms, positioning entropy computing as a competitive approach for navigating non-convex landscapes.

Method: A framework for combinatorial optimization using sum-constrained continuous quadratic programs solvable by QCi's Dirac-3S photonic entropy computer, enabled by the Motzkin-Straus theorem that bridges discrete clique problems and optimization over the probability simplex. Constraint satisfaction problems are solved and extensively benchmarked on the DIMACS suite.

Result: The Dirac-3S platform matches or outright leads two independently implemented classical baselines on more than four-fifths of benchmark instances, reaching the best known solution on nearly all structured graph families, and even outperforming both classical solvers on several of the largest instances tested. Well-tuned classical continuous optimizers retain an edge only on the hardest planted-clique instances.

Conclusion: The work establishes a viable pathway for solving combinatorial optimization problems using natively analog unconventional computing platforms, positioning entropy computing as a competitive approach for navigating non-convex landscapes, and provides rigorous baselines for an emerging computational paradigm.

Abstract: We introduce a framework for combinatorial optimization using sum-constrained continuous quadratic programs solvable by QCi's Dirac-3S photonic entropy computer. This is enabled by the Motzkin-Straus theorem which provides a powerful bridge between discrete clique problems and optimization over the probability simplex. We demonstrate this framework's versatility by solving constraint satisfaction problems, providing extensive benchmarks on the DIMACS suite. The Dirac-3S platform matches or outright leads two independently implemented classical baselines on more than four-fifths of the benchmark instances, reaching the best known solution on nearly all structured graph families, even outperforming both classical solvers on several of the largest instances tested. On the other hand, well-tuned classical continuous optimizers retain an edge only on the hardest planted-clique instances. This work establishes a viable pathway for solving combinatorial optimization problems using natively analog unconventional computing platforms, while positioning entropy computing as a competitive approach for navigating non-convex landscapes and providing rigorous baselines for an emerging computational paradigm.

</details>


### [36] [Breaking the Shot-Noise Barrier: Cached Recycled Variance-Reduced Gradients for Quantum Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38800)
*Lam M. Nguyen,Sumanta Mukherjee,Dzung T. Phan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces CRVG, a variance-reduced gradient estimator with center-point caching that reduces inner-loop circuit complexity by 25%, offering a principled bias-throughput trade-off for near-term quantum optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VQAs depend on classical optimization over noisy parameter landscapes. Analytical gradients via the parameter-shift rule require O(p) circuit executions, which is prohibitive for deep circuits. Simultaneous perturbation methods offer O(1) cost but suffer from O(p) spatial variance, impeding convergence. This creates a cost-versus-variance tension the paper aims to resolve.

Method: The authors introduce the Cached Recycled Variance-Reduced Gradient (CRVG) using variance reduction. They formulate two variants: a 3-Circuit (1-Sided) Non-Recursive CRVG and a Recursive CRVG. A center-point caching mechanism recycles quantum measurements to cut inner-loop circuit complexity by 25% in both variants. They provide theoretical separation analysis of the two routing paths to reach an epsilon-squared-stationary point.

Result: Sample complexity: non-recursive variant reaches O(max{p/eps^2, p^{2/3}/eps^{10/3}}); recursive variant improves to O(max{p/eps^2, sqrt(p)/eps^3}). Empirical evaluations on MaxCut and Maximum Independent Set benchmarks validate these findings. Standard simultaneous perturbation remains a robust baseline across depths, while CRVG shows targeted advantages in amortizable regimes (non-recursive on EfficientSU2; both variants on depth-three QAOA), yielding superior energy minimums and tighter output variances.

Conclusion: CRVG establishes a principled bias-throughput trade-off for scaling near-term quantum optimization, with the recursive variant achieving improved sample complexity and empirical gains in specific amortizable regimes.

Abstract: Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) rely heavily on classical optimization routines to navigate high-dimensional, noisy parameter landscapes. However, the evaluation of analytical gradients via the parameter-shift rule requires $\mathcal{O}(p)$ circuit executions, rendering this approach computationally prohibitive for deep circuits. While simultaneous perturbation methods provide an $\mathcal{O}(1)$ alternative, their gradient estimates suffer from severe $\mathcal{O}(p)$ spatial variance, which impedes convergence. To resolve this tension, we introduce the Cached Recycled Variance-Reduced Gradient (CRVG) by leveraging variance reduction techniques. We formulate two variants: a 3-Circuit (1-Sided) Non-Recursive CRVG and a Recursive CRVG. By introducing a center-point caching mechanism, the 3-Circuit CRVG mathematically recycles quantum measurements to reduce the inner-loop circuit complexity by 25\% across both variants. We establish a theoretical separation between the two routing paths to achieve an $ε^2$-stationary point: the non-recursive variant reaches a sample complexity of $\mathcal{O}(\max\{p/ε^2, p^{2/3}/ε^{10/3}\})$, while the recursive variant achieves an improved complexity of $\mathcal{O}(\max\{p/ε^2, \sqrt{p}/ε^3\})$. Empirically, extensive evaluations on MaxCut and Maximum Independent Set (MIS) benchmarks validate these findings. While standard simultaneous perturbation remains a robust baseline across varying depths, CRVG demonstrates targeted advantages in specific amortizable regimes, such as the non-recursive variant on EfficientSU2 circuits and both variants on depth-three QAOA, yielding superior energy minimums and tighter output variances. Ultimately, CRVG establishes a principled bias-throughput trade-off for scaling near-term quantum optimization.

</details>


### [37] [Entangling Atomic Quantum Memories Using High-Order Modulated Coherent States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38804)
*Chaohan Cui,Prajit Dhara,Saikat Guha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: High-order coherent-state modulation with reflective cavity-coupled atomic memories enables multiple heralded ebits per optical mode, requiring a collective midpoint POVM optimized for distillable entanglement. An SRM-inspired 16-QAM receiver achieves 1.75 ebits/mode at 0.5 dB end-to-end loss; a variational POVM improves the 4-QAM SRM rate by 4.0%, with the advantage persisting for per-interface loss <0.2 dB and phase error <0.2π.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reflective cavity-coupled atomic memories can distribute multiple heralded ebits per transmitted optical mode via high-order coherent-state modulation. Realizing this requires a collective midpoint measurement optimized for maximum distillable entanglement rather than minimum-error state discrimination.

Method: Design of a collective midpoint POVM optimized for distillable entanglement. An SRM-inspired 16-QAM receiver is analyzed, and for 4-QAM a variational POVM with more outcomes than constellation points is employed to exceed the standard SRM rate.

Result: The SRM-inspired 16-QAM receiver reaches 1.75 ebits per mode at 0.5 dB end-to-end loss, 3.8 dB below the half-link capacity benchmark. For 4-QAM, the variational POVM improves the SRM rate by 4.0% but remains below capacity, indicating measurement-design headroom. The advantage over single-qubit-per-mode midpoint protocols persists for per-interface loss <0.2 dB and calibrated phase error <0.2π.

Conclusion: High-order coherent-state modulation with distillable-entanglement-optimized midpoint POVMs provides a viable route to multi-ebit-per-mode heralded entanglement distribution, with measurable headroom remaining in measurement design.

Abstract: High-order coherent-state modulation with reflective cavity-coupled atomic memories can distribute multiple heralded ebits per transmitted optical mode, but requires a collective midpoint POVM optimized for maximum distillable entanglement rather than minimum-error state discrimination. An SRM-inspired 16-QAM receiver reaches $1.75$ ebits per mode at $0.5$~dB end-to-end loss, $3.8$~dB below the half-link capacity benchmark. For 4-QAM, a variational POVM with more outcomes than constellation points improves the SRM rate by $4.0\%$ yet remains below capacity, revealing measurement-design headroom. The advantage over single-qubit-per-mode midpoint protocols persists for per-interface loss $<0.2$~dB and calibrated phase error $<0.2π$.

</details>


### [38] [Average-and Last-Iterate Lower Bounds for Optimistic Matrix Mirror-Prox in Quantum Zero-Sum Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38835)
*Yiheng Su,Emmanouil-Vasileios Vlatakis-Gkaragkounis,Pucheng Xiong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes tightness of the O(1/ε) average-iterate guarantee for Optimistic Matrix Mirror-Prox in quantum zero-sum games and shows that geometric last-iterate convergence cannot be guaranteed in general.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OMMP achieves an O(1/ε) average-iterate guarantee for computing ε-approximate Nash equilibria in quantum zero-sum games, but it is unclear whether this accuracy dependence is optimal and whether faster (geometric) last-iterate convergence is achievable.

Method: The authors analyze explicit one-qubit-per-player quantum games, proving an Ω(1/ε) lower bound for uniform-average output including the maximally mixed initial state (independent of regularizer and step size), and construct fixed games to study last-iterate behavior of OGDA and OMMWU.

Result: An Ω(1/ε) lower bound is proven. For fixed games, OGDA from the maximally mixed state yields last-iterate Frobenius distance Θ(1/t) and duality gap Θ(1/t^3); arbitrarily long delays occur for reducing initial error by a constant factor; and OMMWU on a game with a unique strictly complementary equilibrium converges only polynomially, with Frobenius distance and quantum relative entropy Θ(1/t) and duality gap Θ(1/t^2).

Conclusion: The O(1/ε) average-iterate rate of OMMP is tight, and geometric last-iterate convergence cannot be guaranteed—only polynomial convergence is achievable in the constructed instances.

Abstract: Optimistic matrix mirror-prox (OMMP) computes $ε$-approximate Nash equilibria in quantum zero-sum games with an $O(1/\varepsilon)$ average-iterate guarantee [arXiv:2311.10859]. We investigate whether this dependence on accuracy is tight and whether geometric last-iterate convergence can be guaranteed. We study these questions through explicit games with one qubit per player. First, we prove an $Ω(1/\varepsilon)$ lower bound for the uniform-average output that includes the maximally mixed initial state, independently of the regularizer and step size. Second, we construct a fixed game on which optimistic gradient descent-ascent (OGDA), initialized at the maximally mixed state, has last-iterate Frobenius distance to equilibrium $Θ(1/t)$ and duality gap $Θ(1/t^3)$ for every sufficiently small fixed step size. A separate fixed game exhibits arbitrarily long delays in reducing the initial error by a constant factor across a family of initial states. Finally, we give a fixed game with a unique, strictly complementary equilibrium on which optimistic matrix multiplicative weights updates (OMMWU) converge only polynomially from the maximally mixed state for every fixed positive step size. The last-iterate Frobenius distance and quantum relative entropy from the equilibrium to the iterates decay as $Θ(1/t)$, while the duality gap decays as $Θ(1/t^2)$.

</details>


### [39] [Joint measurability of coplanar POVMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38836)
*Taro Yoshino,Koichi Tojo,Kei Hagihara,Akinori Tanaka,Takuki Tomita*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: For qubit POVMs with coplanar Bloch vectors, joint measurability is equivalent to the perimeter of the convex closure of the Bloch vectors being ≤ 4, which resolves a conjecture on POVMs with co-circular Bloch vectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Joint measurability of quantum measurements is a fundamental concept, and qubit POVMs admit a convenient characterization via Bloch vectors. However, a simple geometric criterion for joint measurability of POVMs with coplanar Bloch vectors was lacking, and a conjecture regarding POVMs whose Bloch vectors share a common circle remained open.

Method: Unbiased qubit POVMs are uniquely expressed in terms of Bloch vectors. The authors analyze the geometry of coplanar Bloch vectors and characterize joint measurability through the perimeter of the convex closure of these vectors, proving that joint measurability holds if and only if this perimeter is at most 4.

Result: Joint measurability of coplanar-Bloch-vector POVMs is exactly equivalent to the perimeter of the convex closure of the Bloch vectors being ≤ 4. As a corollary, the conjecture of Andrejic-Kunjwal (2020) concerning the existence of a joint device for POVMs whose Bloch vectors lie on the same circle is affirmatively resolved.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a clean geometric criterion—perimeter ≤ 4 of the convex closure of coplanar Bloch vectors—for joint measurability of qubit POVMs, and confirms the Andrejic-Kunjwal conjecture for co-circular Bloch vectors.

Abstract: An unbiased qubit positive operator-valued measure (POVM) can be uniquely expressed in terms of the Bloch vector. We prove that for POVMs with coplanar Bloch vectors, they are jointly measurable if and only if the perimeter of the convex closure of Bloch vectors is less than or equal to 4. Moreover, as a corollary, we give an affirmative answer to the conjecture suggested by [Andrejic-Kunjwal 2020] on the existence of a joint device for POVMs whose Bloch vectors lie on the same circle.

</details>


### [40] [Classical Algorithms for Function Computation in Gaussian Boson Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38882)
*Ruoting Dou,Hao Zhan,Shengjun Wu,Lijian Zhang,Penghui Yao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Sampling hardness of Gaussian boson sampling (GBS) does not imply hardness of computing functions on mode-resolved photon-number outcomes. The authors show that the average-case expectation value of any such function over passive linear-optical networks can be classically evaluated under finite squeezing, and give a classical estimator with inverse-polynomial additive error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many proposed GBS applications compute classical functions of mode-resolved photon-number outcomes via experimental postprocessing, implicitly assuming that sampling hardness transfers to computational hardness. This assumption is unjustified, and the paper aims to determine whether such function-computation tasks are actually classically tractable.

Method: The authors analyze the irreducible decomposition of fixed-photon-number operator spaces to compute the average-case expectation value (over random passive linear-optical networks) of arbitrary functions applied to mode-resolved photon-number outcomes, for inputs with finite squeezing strength. They also construct a classical algorithm estimating this expectation value to inverse-polynomial additive error.

Result: The average-case expectation value of every function of mode-resolved photon-number outcomes is classically evaluable for finite squeezing, and a classical algorithm achieves inverse-polynomial additive-error estimation. This weakens the link between GBS sampling hardness and the hardness of function-computation applications, clarifying the origin of existing GBS hardness evidence.

Conclusion: Sampling hardness alone does not confer quantum advantage on GBS-based function computation. The results provide new theoretical tools for linear-optical quantum systems, clarify current GBS hardness evidence, and motivate new GBS applications with genuine quantum advantages.

Abstract: Gaussian boson sampling (GBS) seeks quantum advantage by sampling photon-number patterns generated with squeezed inputs and passive linear optics. Many proposed GBS applications instead target function computation by applying functions to mode-resolved photon-number outcomes---a natural form of experimental postprocessing that produces classical outputs. Sampling hardness alone, however, does not determine the complexity of these tasks. By analyzing the irreducible decomposition of fixed-photon-number operator spaces, we prove that the expectation value of every such function in the average case over passive linear-optical networks can be classically evaluated for inputs with finite squeezing strength. We also provide a classical algorithm that estimates this value to inverse-polynomial additive error. The result provides new theoretical tools for analyzing linear-optical quantum systems, helps clarify the origin of current GBS hardness evidence, and inspires new applications of GBS with genuine quantum advantages.

</details>


### [41] [Optical studies of negative g-factor dopants in an antiferromagnet: Nd:GdVO$_4$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38892)
*Lachlan W. Cronin,Masaya Hiraishi,Luke S. Trainor,Jevon J. Longdell*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Below the 2.5 K Néel temperature, Nd:GdVO₄ exhibits narrow optical transitions despite disordered Gd spins, revealing negative magnetic g-factors and establishing a spectroscopic foundation for its use in hybrid quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nd:GdVO₄ is a well-established room-temperature laser material but has seen limited quantum-technology use because disordered gadolinium spins in the host are expected to cause linebroadening. The work aims to test whether low-temperature spectroscopy can overcome this limitation and to characterize the material's optical and magnetic properties.

Method: Optical spectroscopy of Nd:GdVO₄ at temperatures below its 2.5 K Néel temperature, analyzing narrow optical transitions and the interplay between exchange and Zeeman interactions of rare-earth ions in the magnetic host.

Result: Narrow optical transitions are observed below 2.5 K. The exchange–Zeeman interplay provides clear evidence of negative magnetic g-factors, showing that rare-earth ions in magnetic hosts offer a new route to observing the signs of magnetic g-factors.

Conclusion: The results provide a detailed spectroscopic foundation for Nd:GdVO₄ and expand understanding of its candidacy as a material for hybrid quantum systems.

Abstract: Neodymium-doped gadolinium vanadate (Nd:GdVO$_4$) is a popular laser material at room temperature. It has received limited attention for quantum technologies because of the linebroadening effects of disordered gadolinium spins in the host. Here we investigate the optical properties at temperatures below the 2.5 K Néel temperature, revealing narrow optical transitions. We demonstrate that rare earth ions in magnetic hosts give us a new way of observing the signs of magnetic g-factors. The interplay between the exchange and Zeeman interactions means that we see clear evidence of negative g-factors. These results provide a detailed spectroscopic foundation for Nd:GdVO$_4$ and expand the understanding of its candidacy as a material for hybrid quantum systems.

</details>


### [42] [Quantum Time-Lock Puzzles in the Quantum Random Oracle Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38894)
*Prabhanjan Ananth,Yao-Ting Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work constructs quantum time-lock puzzles in the quantum random oracle model, achieving one-round generation, at-most-T-round solving, and security against polynomial-width quantum adversaries of depth o(T), thereby resolving an open problem of Mahmoody, Moran, and Vadhan (2011).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-lock puzzles enable timed-release encryption, sealed-bid auctions, electronic voting, fair contract signing, coin flipping, and Byzantine consensus. However, they are known to be impossible in the classical random oracle model, creating a fundamental barrier.

Method: The authors consider quantum time-lock puzzles, where the puzzle itself is a quantum state, and construct a scheme in the quantum random oracle model.

Result: The construction achieves generation in one oracle round, solving in at most T oracle rounds, and security against polynomial-width quantum adversaries of depth o(T) for every polynomially bounded delay T = T(λ).

Conclusion: The work overcomes the classical impossibility barrier and resolves the open problem posed by Mahmoody, Moran, and Vadhan (2011).

Abstract: A time-lock puzzle allows a sender to hide a message in a puzzle such that recovering the message requires substantially more sequential computation than the time required to generate the puzzle, even when parallel computation is allowed. Applications of time-lock puzzles include timed-release encryption, sealed-bid auctions, electronic voting, fair contract signing, coin flipping, and Byzantine consensus. However, time-lock puzzles are known to be impossible in the classical random oracle model.
  To overcome the classical barrier, in this work we consider quantum time-lock puzzles, in which the puzzle itself is a quantum state. Our construction in the quantum random oracle model achieves generation in one oracle round, solving in at most $T$ oracle rounds, and security against polynomial-width quantum adversaries of depth $o(T)$ for every polynomially bounded delay $T=T(λ)$, resolving an open problem posed by Mahmoody, Moran, and Vadhan (2011).

</details>


### [43] [Boundary-rank obstructions and measurement-assisted recovery in dissipative flat-band preparation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38933)
*Mingdi Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Number-conserving cooling can fail to prepare an interacting flat-band target because Pauli exclusion blocks transfer destinations. The authors characterize this failure via the rank of a cut block of the flat-orbital Gram matrix, construct stationary dark states and wrong-state overlaps on decorated graphs, and show that dephasing compact bright modes restores global attraction to the ferromagnetic target under stated conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Number-conserving cooling protocols are expected to prepare flat-band targets, but Pauli exclusion can block the transfer destinations, causing cooling to fail. The paper seeks to understand and quantify this failure and to identify conditions under which global attraction to the target can be restored.

Method: The analysis is built on the flat-orbital Gram matrix and the rank of a cut block of it, which bounds the number of residual source directions that can remain flat. The authors study vertex-edge decorated graphs at one particle per flat orbital, considering sublinear-range cooling without a Hamiltonian term and a chain with fixed finite cooling range. They construct explicit physical Fock initial states, bound overlaps with wrong dark states, and analyze higher-dimensional cases via boundary-area decay and local boundary rotations. Dephasing of compact bright-mode occupations and the strong-dephasing compressed dynamics are analyzed, with the Liouvillian gap examined. Numerical checks include Gram calculations, finite-system dynamics, and Liouvillian spectra.

Result: For sublinear-range cooling without a Hamiltonian term, separated spin domains on periodic decorated hypercubic lattices support exponentially many stationary states with nonzero bright-particle density. For a chain with fixed finite cooling range, a physical Fock initial state has overlap with a wrong dark state independent of system size, yielding fidelity and bright-density bounds valid at all times. In higher dimensions, the bare overlap decays with boundary area, while local boundary rotations prepare finite-failure-weight states in constant circuit depth. Dephasing all compact bright-mode occupations restores global attraction to the ferromagnetic target when graph and cooling destinations satisfy the stated conditions, allowing Hamiltonians that preserve the target. The compressed strong-dephasing dynamics remains attractive with a positive gap at each fixed size. Weak-dephasing and particle-transport bounds constrain preparation time despite eventual convergence.

Conclusion: Number-conserving cooling can fail to reach interacting flat-band targets due to Pauli-blocked transfer destinations, and this failure is governed by the rank structure of the flat-orbital Gram matrix. Dephasing compact bright modes provides a route to restore global attraction to the ferromagnetic target under specified conditions, while weak-dephasing and transport bounds limit preparation time despite eventual convergence.

Abstract: Number-conserving cooling can fail to prepare an interacting flat-band target when Pauli exclusion blocks its transfer destinations. We study this failure on vertex-edge decorated graphs at one particle per flat orbital. The rank of a cut block of the flat-orbital Gram matrix bounds the number of residual source directions that can remain flat. For sublinear-range cooling with no Hamiltonian term, separated spin domains on periodic decorated hypercubic lattices support exponentially many stationary states with a nonzero bright-particle density. For a chain with fixed finite cooling range, we construct a physical Fock initial state whose overlap with a wrong dark state is independent of system size. This gives fidelity and bright-density bounds valid at every time. In higher dimensions, the bare overlap decays with boundary area, while local boundary rotations prepare states with a finite failure weight in constant circuit depth. Dephasing the occupations of all compact bright modes restores global attraction to the ferromagnetic target when the graph and cooling destinations satisfy the stated conditions. The result allows Hamiltonians that preserve the target. The compressed dynamics in the strong-dephasing limit also remains attractive and has a positive gap at each fixed size. Gram calculations, finite-system dynamics, and Liouvillian spectra test the analytic results. Weak-dephasing and particle-transport bounds constrain the preparation time despite eventual convergence.

</details>


### [44] [Defect-Aware Parallel Atom Reloading Protocol for Neutral-Atom Quantum Computers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38939)
*Koki Aoyama,Fumihiko Ino*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a defect-aware parallel atom reloading protocol combining coherent reloading operations with a defect-aware planner to maximize atom filling rates in neutral-atom quantum computers while meeting real-time planning requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neutral-atom quantum computers require continuous reloading of atoms into defect (atom-loss) positions for sustained operation. Existing approaches lack efficiency in replenishing these defects, limiting achievable atom filling rates.

Method: The protocol comprises two components: (1) a coherent atom reloading operation that replenishes defects while preserving quantum information, enabling adaptive strategy selection based on current defect configuration; and (2) a defect-aware planner that determines efficient atom reloading patterns exploiting both parallelism and defect awareness.

Result: In low-noise numerical experiments on a 36 x 90 atom array, the average atom filling rate improved from 98.61% to 99.94%, matching a mixed integer programming solver within 0.001 percentage points, with each reload planned in under 0.1 ms.

Conclusion: Defect-aware parallel reloading achieves and sustains high atom filling rates, and the defect-aware planner satisfies the real-time requirements of neutral-atom quantum computers.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a defect-aware parallel atom reloading protocol that reloads atoms based on defect information to increase the atom filling rate. The proposed protocol is useful for neutral-atom quantum computers, where reloading atoms into the defects, or atom-loss positions, is mandatory for continuous operation against atom loss. Our protocol takes advantage of both parallelism and defect awareness to improve atom-reloading efficiency. The proposed protocol consists of the coherent atom reloading operation and the defect-aware planner. The former replenishes the defects with atoms preserving the quantum information coherently. This coherence allows us to adaptively choose the reloading strategy based on the current defect configuration. The latter determines the atom reloading pattern to efficiently replenish the defects. In numerical experiments assuming a low-noise environment, we confirmed that the average atom filling rate improved from 98.61% to 99.94% for a 36 x 90 atom array, matching the filling rate obtained with a mixed integer programming solver to within 0.001 percentage points while planning each reload in under 0.1 ms. These results demonstrate that defect-aware parallel reloading can achieve and sustain high atom filling rates while the performance of the defect-aware planner meets the real-time requirements of neutral-atom quantum computers.

</details>


### [45] [Tight Parallel Repetition for Private-Coin Arguments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38941)
*Zvika Brakerski,Andrew Huang,Yael Tauman Kalai,Nicholas Spooner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Under the assumption of (quantum) homomorphic encryption, parallel repetition of interactive arguments (run under homomorphic encryption) reduces soundness error at a tight exponential rate even in the post-quantum setting, and extends to threshold verifiers, yielding the first constant-round succinct argument for QMA with negligible errors under quantum homomorphic encryption alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior results on parallel repetition reducing soundness error tightly were only known for classical cheating provers; achieving tight bounds and handling quantum (post-quantum) provers remained open, and no tight quantum-secure parallel repetition theorem was known.

Method: Run all interactive arguments under homomorphic encryption, then apply parallel repetition; prove that this reduces the soundness error at a tight exponential rate even against quantum provers. Generalize the analysis to threshold verifiers, where the repeated verifier accepts iff at least t executions are accepted.

Result: A tight exponential reduction of soundness error for parallel repetition of interactive arguments is established in the post-quantum setting under homomorphic encryption, and generalized to threshold verifiers; as a corollary, the first constant-round succinct argument for QMA with negligible completeness and soundness errors is constructed assuming only quantum homomorphic encryption.

Conclusion: Assuming homomorphic encryption, parallel repetition of interactive arguments achieves tight exponential soundness-error reduction even against quantum provers and extends to threshold verifiers, implying a constant-round succinct QMA argument with negligible errors from quantum homomorphic encryption alone.

Abstract: We show that assuming the existence of homomorphic encryption, parallel repetition of all interactive arguments (after being run under homomorphic encryption) reduces the soundness error at a tight exponential rate even in the post-quantum setting. Moreover, we generalize this result to hold for threshold verifiers, where the parallel repeated verifier accepts if and only if at least $t$ of the executions are accepted (for some threshold $t$). Prior to this work, these results were known only when the cheating prover was assumed to be classical, and it was not known how to achieve tight bounds.
  As a corollary, we construct the first constant-round succinct argument for $\mathsf{QMA}$ with negligible completeness and soundness errors assuming only the existence of quantum homomorphic encryption.

</details>


### [46] [Certified Randomness with Optimal Rate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38945)
*Siddhartha Jain,Saachi Mutreja,Bhaskar Roberts*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work constructs a protocol that certifies nearly uniform (rate ~1) randomness with no trusted seed, secure unconditionally in the quantum random oracle model, and introduces a proof of conditional min-entropy with applications to randomness beacons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Certified randomness generation is a near-term quantum computing application, but uses like randomness beacons and CRS generation need nearly uniform randomness (rate ~1). Existing protocols either achieve only weakly random strings (rate o(1)) or require a trusted random seed. The paper aims to eliminate the trusted seed while achieving optimal rate, improving on Coladangelo et al. and answering a question of Aaronson and Hung.

Method: The authors design a protocol that certifies randomness at optimal rate ~1 without any trusted randomness from the verifier, proven secure unconditionally in the quantum random oracle model (QROM). They also define and construct a proof of conditional min-entropy, which certifies optimal min-entropy even conditioned on an adversarially chosen transcript.

Result: The protocol certifies randomness with optimal rate ~1, with no trusted seed, secure unconditionally in the QROM. The proof of conditional min-entropy certifies optimal min-entropy conditioned on an adversarial transcript. This primitive is applied to randomness beacons, ensuring each pulse has high min-entropy conditioned on all previous messages.

Conclusion: The work improves on Coladangelo et al.'s unconditional QROM certified randomness and resolves Aaronson and Hung's question, achieving near-uniform certified randomness without a trusted seed. The conditional min-entropy proof provides a useful primitive, notably enabling randomness beacons with per-pulse conditional min-entropy guarantees.

Abstract: The generation of certified random bits is an emerging near-term application of quantum computers. Potential applications, such as randomness beacons and CRS generation, require nearly uniform randomness, whose rate (the ratio of min-entropy to bitlength) is ~ 1. However, existing protocols for certified randomness either produce weakly random strings with rate o(1), or they require a trusted random seed.
  We show how to certify randomness with the optimal rate ~ 1 without requiring any trusted randomness from the verifier. Our protocol is secure unconditionally in the quantum random oracle model (QROM). This improves the result of Coladangelo et al., who certified weakly random strings unconditionally in the QROM, and answers a question posed by Aaronson and Hung.
  We also define and construct a proof of conditional min-entropy, which certifies optimal min-entropy even conditioned on an adversarially chosen transcript. We show an application of this primitive to randomness beacons, where each pulse should have high min-entropy conditioned on all previous messages.

</details>


### [47] [The Commuting Local Hamiltonian Problem: Relativized Evidence Against BQP-Hardness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38957)
*Itay Shalit,Mark Zhandry*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives a classical oracle O such that BQP^O ⊄ QIMA^O, providing relativized evidence that the general Commuting Local-Hamiltonian (CLH) problem is not BQP-hard and hence not QMA-complete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CLH is a restriction of the local-Hamiltonian problem where Hamiltonian terms pairwise commute. While it is known to lie in NP for certain families, no formal evidence ruled out that the general CLH problem is QMA-complete. Since CLH is complete for QIMA (quantum verifiers with commuting local gates), CLH is QMA-complete iff QIMA = QMA. The paper seeks evidence separating these classes or refuting hardness of CLH.

Method: The authors define a classical-oracle analogue QIMA^O of the class QIMA and construct an explicit classical oracle O separating it from BQP^O, i.e., BQP^O ⊄ QIMA^O, using relativization techniques.

Result: For the constructed classical oracle O, BQP^O ⊄ QIMA^O. Since BQP^O ⊆ QMA^O for any classical oracle, this yields QIMA^O ≠ QMA^O for that oracle.

Conclusion: The separation provides relativized evidence against the general CLH problem being BQP-hard, and consequently against it being QMA-complete. This supports the view that CLH is unlikely to capture full QMA power in the general commuting case.

Abstract: The commuting local-Hamiltonian (CLH) problem is a restriction of the local-Hamiltonian problem, in which the terms of the Hamiltonian are required to pairwise commute. A long line of work has shown that the problem lies in $\mathsf{NP}$ for certain families of commuting local Hamiltonians. Nevertheless, there has been no formal evidence against the possibility that the general CLH problem is $\mathsf{QMA}$-complete.
  CLH is complete for the complexity class $\mathsf{QIMA}$, defined through quantum verifiers whose local gates commute. Therefore, CLH is $\mathsf{QMA}$-complete if and only if $\mathsf{QIMA}=\mathsf{QMA}$. In this work, we introduce a classical-oracle analogue $\mathsf{QIMA}^{\mathcal{O}}$ and construct a classical oracle $\mathcal{O}$ such that $\mathsf{BQP}^{\mathcal{O}}\not\subseteq\mathsf{QIMA}^{\mathcal{O}}$.
  Since $\mathsf{BQP}^{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq\mathsf{QMA}^{\mathcal{O}}$ for any classical oracle $\mathcal{O}$, this implies $\mathsf{QIMA}^{\mathcal{O}}\neq\mathsf{QMA}^{\mathcal{O}}$ for our constructed oracle. Thus, our result provides relativized evidence against the possibility that the general commuting local-Hamiltonian problem is $\mathsf{BQP}$-hard, and hence also against the possibility that it is $\mathsf{QMA}$-complete.

</details>


### [48] [Quantum Pseudorandom Error-Correcting Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38986)
*Min-Hsiu Hsieh,Shogo Yamada*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper initiates the study of quantum pseudorandom error-correcting codes (QPRCs) and constructs them under an LPN hardness assumption, including pseudorandom isometric error-correcting codes (PRICs) and a direct quantum analogue of classical PRCs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical pseudorandom error-correcting codes (PRCs) were introduced by Christ and Gunn; the authors aim to develop quantum analogues. This requires defining QPRCs and constructing codes whose encodings are computationally indistinguishable from Haar-random isometries or the completely depolarizing channel, while remaining robust to local quantum noise.

Method: Assuming LPN hardness against 2^{O(sqrt(n))}-time quantum algorithms, the authors construct PRICs (encodings indistinguishable from Haar-random isometries) and QPRCs indistinguishable from the completely depolarizing channel. They introduce pseudorandom functional error-correcting codes (PRFCs) and develop an efficient decoding procedure within the codeword-stabilized (CWS) framework.

Result: PRICs are robust to all o(n log log n / log n)-local quantum noise for n physical qubits. The second class of QPRCs is robust to all alpha*n-local quantum noise for some constant alpha>0, giving a direct quantum analogue of classical PRCs. The work also resolves an open problem on efficiently decoding CWS codes based on nonlinear classical codes.

Conclusion: The paper establishes quantum pseudorandom error-correcting codes under the LPN assumption, achieving robustness against local quantum noise and introducing PRFCs plus a general efficient decoding method for CWS codes built from nonlinear classical codes.

Abstract: Pseudorandom error-correcting codes (PRCs), introduced by Christ and Gunn [CRYPTO 2024], are classical error-correcting codes whose codewords are computationally indistinguishable from uniformly random strings. We initiate the study of quantum analogues of PRCs. We define quantum pseudorandom error-correcting codes (QPRCs). Assuming that Learning Parity with Noise (LPN) is hard for $2^{O(\sqrt{n})}$-time quantum algorithms, we construct QPRCs whose encodings are indistinguishable from Haar-random isometries. We call these pseudorandom isometric error-correcting codes (PRICs), and our construction is robust to all $o(n \frac{\log \log n}{\log n})$-local quantum noise, where $n$ is the number of physical qubits. Under the same assumption, we also construct QPRCs whose encodings are indistinguishable from the completely depolarizing channel and that are robust to all $αn$-local quantum noise for some constant $α>0$. The latter QPRCs give a direct quantum analogue of classical PRCs.
  To construct PRICs, we develop two technical ingredients of independent interest. First, we introduce a new classical cryptographic primitive which we call pseudorandom functional error-correcting codes (PRFCs) and construct them under the same LPN assumption. Second, we develop a new efficient decoding procedure within the codeword-stabilized (CWS) framework introduced in [Cross, Smith, Smolin, and Zeng, IEEE ISIT 2008], which is a generic framework for constructing quantum error-correcting codes by combining (possibly nonlinear) classical error-correcting codes and graphs. This resolves the open problem of finding a general method for efficiently decoding such CWS codes based on nonlinear classical codes, raised in [Li, Dumer, Grassl, and Pryadko, Physical Review A 2010].

</details>


### [49] [Constant Rate Codes with Fully Addressable Transversal T: Good Codes, Sparse Checks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38996)
*Alexander Meiburg*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs asymptotically good CSS codes with fully addressable transversal T gates (selective logical control), plus Pauli-LDPC codes with transversal T and growing distance at vanishing rate, a protected-subsystem construction, and generalizations to every fixed finite-order one-qubit gate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work by Wills built codes with constant rate and transversal T, but did not offer selective control over which logical qubits receive the gate (addressability), and the broader question of achieving fault-tolerant transversal non-Clifford gates with good parameters and sparse checks remained open.

Method: Extend Wills' line of work by constructing asymptotically good CSS codes admitting a fully addressable transversal T; a protected-subsystem construction with constant protected rate, distance Ω(√n), and sparse non-Pauli checks; and generalized asymptotically good binary codes with full transversal addressability for every fixed finite-order one-qubit gate, keeping CSS realizations for dyadic phase gates such as √T and using generally nonadditive codes for rotations such as R_z(π/7).

Result: Asymptotically good CSS codes with fully addressable transversal T are obtained; Pauli-LDPC codes with transversal T achieve growing distance at vanishing rate; the protected-subsystem construction attains constant protected rate, distance Ω(√n), and sparse non-Pauli checks; full transversal addressability holds for all fixed finite-order one-qubit gates.

Conclusion: Fully addressable transversal gates are achievable at asymptotically good parameters across a wide gate class. The open challenge is constructing asymptotically good Pauli-LDPC codes with fully addressable transversal non-Clifford one-qubit gates.

Abstract: Recent work by Wills constructed codes with constant rate and transversal $T$.
