Extending this line of work, we construct asymptotically good CSS codes with \textbf{fully addressable} transversal $T$, permitting selective control over which logical qubits receive a $T$ gate. We also give Pauli-LDPC codes with transversal $T$ and growing distance at vanishing rate; and a protected-subsystem construction with fully addressable transversal $T$, constant protected rate, distance $Ω(\sqrt n)$, and sparse (non-Pauli) checks. More generally, we obtain asymptotically good binary codes with full transversal addressability for every fixed finite-order one-qubit gate, retaining CSS realizations for dyadic phase gates such as $\sqrt T$ and using generally nonadditive codes for rotations such as $R_z(π/7)$. The remaining challenge is to construct asymptotically good Pauli-LDPC codes with fully addressable transversal non-Clifford one-qubit gates.

</details>


### [50] [Entropy threshold: A simple proxy for performance of quantum error correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39016)
*Diego Ruiz,Aleksander Kubica*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a simple entropy-based proxy that predicts quantum error-correction thresholds across various protocols, including Clifford-deformed surface codes with biased Pauli/erasure noise and circuit-level noise models of surface/color codes with flag qubits, achieving good agreement with numerical simulations despite neglecting correlations and degeneracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field of quantum error correction has generated extensive numerical data but lacks simple heuristics for understanding and predicting protocol performance. Computationally intensive simulations are the norm, and there is a need for phenomenology enabling quick, back-of-the-envelope estimates of QEC thresholds.

Method: The authors develop an entropy-based proxy that estimates the threshold by locally comparing the noise entropy with the error information gained via stabilizer measurements. In circuit-level settings, this comparison incorporates spacetime detectors and flag outcomes. The proxy neglects correlations between stabilizer outcomes and code degeneracy contributions.

Result: The proxy captures the main trends across diverse QEC settings and yields threshold estimates in good agreement with numerical results, spanning from code-capacity settings of Clifford-deformed surface codes with biased Pauli or erasure noise to circuit-level noise models of surface and color codes with flag qubits.

Conclusion: The work provides much-needed phenomenology that enables simple estimates of QEC performance, recovering and explaining results of computationally intensive detailed simulations.

Abstract: The rapidly growing landscape of quantum error-correction (QEC) protocols has produced a wealth of numerical data, but comparatively few heuristics for understanding and predicting their performance. Here, we develop a simple entropy-based proxy that predicts the thresholds of a variety of QEC protocols, ranging from the code-capacity setting of Clifford-deformed surface codes with biased Pauli or erasure noise to the circuit-level noise model of the surface or color codes with flag qubits. Our proxy estimates the threshold by locally comparing the noise entropy with the error information gained via stabilizer measurements (or spacetime detectors and flag outcomes in the circuit-level settings). Despite neglecting correlations between stabilizer outcomes and the contribution from code degeneracy, the proxy captures the main trends across diverse settings and yields threshold estimates in good agreement with numerical results. Our work develops much-needed phenomenology that enables simple back-of-the-envelope estimates of QEC performance, recovering and providing an explanation for the results of computationally intensive detailed simulations.

</details>


### [51] [Provable Classical and Quantum Local Algorithms for Max-$k$-Cut and Quantum Advantage at Moderate Girth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39042)
*Anuj Apte,Abid Khan,Edward Farhi,Kunal Marwaha,Sami Boulebnane,Ruslan Shaydulin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper compares local classical and quantum algorithms for Max-$k$-Cut on $d$-regular graphs of girth $g$, improving a classical vector algorithm (TPM-based) and developing tensor network methods for QAOA. QAOA provably achieves a better cut-fraction guarantee for depths $p\geq 9$ (girth $g\geq 20$), indicating an apparent quantum advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending quantum optimization from binary to $k$-element alphabets is seen as a path to potential quantum advantage. A valid advantage claim for approximate optimization requires proving, under identical assumptions, that an efficient quantum algorithm outperforms the best provable guarantee of the best known efficient classical algorithms. Max-$k$-Cut on $d$-regular girth-$g$ graphs provides a controlled setting for such a comparison.

Method: The authors develop a local vector classical algorithm based on the explicit vector construction of Thompson, Parekh, and Marwaha (TPM), yielding the best provable cut-fraction guarantee among known efficient classical algorithms on regular graphs for $k\geq3$. For QAOA under the same girth and regularity assumptions, they develop tensor network techniques for general $d$ and $k\geq2$, and compute QAOA performance in the infinite-degree limit via an equivalence to a coupled qudit--boson system.

Result: The improved classical algorithm provides the strongest known provable cut-fraction guarantee for $k\geq3$ regular graphs. The tensor network and infinite-degree techniques give provable QAOA performance guarantees on large graphs. Despite the classical improvements, QAOA achieves a better cut-fraction guarantee at depths $p\geq 9$, corresponding to girth $g\geq 20$, in both finite- and infinite-degree regimes.

Conclusion: Applying QAOA to Max-$k$-Cut yields an apparent quantum advantage: under matched assumptions of regularity and girth, QAOA provably surpasses the best provable guarantee of the improved classical algorithm at sufficiently large depth.

Abstract: Broadening the study of quantum optimization algorithms from binary to $k$-element alphabets has been shown to open new avenues for potential quantum advantage. A quantum advantage claim for approximate optimization requires showing that, under the same assumptions, an efficient quantum algorithm provably achieves a better performance than can be proven for the best known efficient classical algorithms. We study local classical and quantum algorithms for Max-$k$-Cut on $d$-regular graphs of girth $g$. We advance classical algorithms for this problem by developing a local vector algorithm based on the explicit vector construction of Thompson, Parekh, and Marwaha (TPM). Our algorithm gives the best provable cut fraction guarantee among known efficient classical algorithms on regular graphs for $k\geq3$. To evaluate the performance of QAOA under identical assumptions of girth and regularity, we develop tensor network techniques for general $d$ and $k \geq 2$. In addition, using an equivalence to a coupled qudit--boson system, we compute the QAOA performance in the infinite-degree limit. Together, these techniques give provable guarantees on QAOA performance on large graphs. Despite the improvements we introduce to the classical algorithm, QAOA achieves a better cut fraction guarantee for depths $p\geq 9$, corresponding to girth $g\geq 20$, for both finite- and infinite-degree regimes. Thus, we obtain an apparent quantum advantage from applying QAOA to the Max-$k$-Cut problem.

</details>


### [52] [Fixed-Point Bifurcations and Path-Information Bounds in Nonlinear Exceptional-Point Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39046)
*Xue-Hao Yu,Cong-Feng Qiao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that nonlinear exceptional points (NEPs) cannot deliver divergent SNR enhancement: any singular stationary response entails a fixed-point bifurcation, and responsivity and frequency uncertainty share the same critical relaxation rate, causing exact SNR cancellation. A general path-information bound caps long-time SNR enhancement, and this ceiling is attainable in the local fluctuation regime, making such frequency readout information-optimal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear exceptional-point sensors suffer from noise–responsivity cancellation that limits their performance. Nonlinear exceptional points (NEPs) were proposed to circumvent this limit because the stationary frequency response can be singular while the Hamiltonian Petermann factor stays finite, hinting at a genuine SNR enhancement. However, prior model-specific analyses of nonlinear fluctuations have produced conflicting conclusions, leaving the fundamental sensing limits of NEPs unresolved.

Method: The authors provide a general theoretical proof rather than a model-specific analysis. They show that any singular stationary frequency response necessarily requires a fixed-point bifurcation of the nonlinear dynamics. They then analyze local fluctuation regimes, comparing the critical scaling of responsivity and long-time frequency uncertainty, and derive a path-information bound valid in both local and nonlinear fluctuation regimes.

Result: Singular stationary frequency response implies a fixed-point bifurcation. In local fluctuation regimes, both the responsivity singularity and the long-time frequency uncertainty are governed by the same critical relaxation rate, producing exact cancellation in the SNR and precluding divergent enhancement. A path-information bound imposes a finite ceiling on long-time SNR enhancement, and this bound is attainable in the local fluctuation regime.

Conclusion: NEPs do not provide divergent SNR enhancement; the responsivity gain is exactly cancelled by enhanced frequency uncertainty near the bifurcation. Nevertheless, the attainable path-information bound indicates that frequency readout in the local fluctuation regime is information-optimal, clarifying and reconciling conflicting prior results on nonlinear fluctuation limits.

Abstract: Nonlinear exceptional points (NEPs) were proposed to circumvent the noise--responsivity cancellation that limits linear exceptional-point sensors. At an NEP, the stationary frequency response can be singular while the Hamiltonian Petermann factor remains finite, suggesting a genuine signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) enhancement. Yet recent model-specific analyses of nonlinear fluctuations have reached conflicting conclusions. We prove that any singular stationary frequency response requires a fixed-point bifurcation of the nonlinear dynamics. In local fluctuation regimes, the singularities in responsivity and long-time frequency uncertainty are both set by the critical relaxation rate, leading to exact cancellation in the SNR and precluding any divergent enhancement. Furthermore, we derive a path-information bound that applies across both local and nonlinear fluctuation regimes and places a finite ceiling on the long-time SNR enhancement. Notably, this upper bound is attainable in the local fluctuation regime, implying that frequency readout under such parameter settings is information-optimal.

</details>


### [53] [Engineered Dissipation for Thermal-State Tracking Across Quantum Criticality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39084)
*Saikat Mistry,Dibyajyoti Sahu,Suhas Gangadharaiah*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A Markovian dissipation-engineering framework based on engineered population currents is developed for finite-time thermal-state tracking in the transverse-field Ising model, suppressing thermal excitation deviations and yielding steeper residual scaling than Kibble-Zurek behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissipation engineering complements coherent control for quantum state preparation, but finite-time driving across a quantum critical point produces nonadiabatic excitations and coherences. Existing approaches mostly characterize how a fixed environment modifies critical dynamics rather than actively steering the system toward a prescribed thermal trajectory.

Method: The authors develop a Markovian dissipative framework for finite-time thermal-state tracking based on engineered population currents with non-negative transition rates, and apply it to the transverse-field Ising model driven across its quantum critical point, using a free common current Q_k to control coherence damping.

Result: Compared with unitary dynamics and a natural thermal bath, the engineered dynamics strongly suppresses thermal excitation deviations and provides additional control over coherence damping. It exhibits a substantially steeper residual scaling, δn_ex^th ~ (v/v_ch)^2, than the Kibble-Zurek behavior recovered for unitary evolution.

Conclusion: These results indicate that engineered dissipation offers a promising route for controlling thermal-state preparation in driven quantum critical systems.

Abstract: Engineering dissipation has emerged as a powerful strategy for controlling quantum many-body dynamics, complementing purely coherent control for state preparation. However, finite-time driving across a quantum critical point generates nonadiabatic excitations and coherences, and existing dissipation-engineering approaches have largely characterized how a fixed environment modifies critical dynamics rather than actively steering a system toward a prescribed thermal trajectory. Here we develop a Markovian dissipative framework for finite-time thermal-state tracking based on engineered population currents with non-negative transition rates, and apply it to the transverse-field Ising model driven across its quantum critical point. Compared with unitary dynamics and a natural thermal bath, the engineered dynamics strongly suppresses thermal excitation deviations and provides additional control over coherence damping through a free common current $Q_k$, while exhibiting a substantially steeper residual scaling, $δn_{\mathrm{ex}}^{\mathrm{th}}\sim(v/v_{\mathrm{ch}})^2$, than the Kibble--Zurek behavior recovered for unitary evolution. These results point toward engineered dissipation as a route for controlling thermal-state preparation in driven quantum critical systems.

</details>


### [54] [Quantum circuit compilation with constant overhead](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39092)
*Chenyi Zhang,Xinyu Tan,Robin Kothari,David Gosset,Craig Gidney*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Adaptivity eliminates the logarithmic overhead in compiling continuous gate sets to the discrete universal gate set {H, T, CNOT}, achieving optimality for polynomial-sized circuits with inverse-polynomial target error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compiling a continuous gate set into a discrete universal gate set (e.g., {H, T, CNOT}) typically incurs an overhead: each arbitrary gate is replaced by O(log(G/ε)) elementary gates, where G is the number of gates and ε the approximation error.

Method: The authors use adaptivity—allowing later operations to depend on earlier measurement outcomes—to compile continuous-gate circuits into the discrete universal gate set.

Result: The standard O(log(G/ε)) overhead per gate is avoided entirely for polynomial-sized circuits with inverse-polynomial target error.

Conclusion: Adaptive compilation removes the compilation overhead, and this is optimal for the considered regime of polynomial-sized circuits with inverse-polynomial error.

Abstract: Compiling a continuous gate set to a discrete universal gate set generally incurs an overhead. A circuit composed of $G$ arbitrary one- and two-qubit gates can be $ε$-approximated by a sequence of gates from $\{H,T,\mathrm{CNOT}\}$ by replacing each original gate by a sequence of $O(\log(G/ε))$ elementary gates. We show that this overhead can be avoided using adaptivity for polynomial-sized circuits with inverse-polynomial target error, which is optimal.

</details>


### [55] [Optimal testing of fermionic and bosonic Gaussian states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39113)
*Vishnu Iyer,Bryan O'Gorman,Ojas Parekh,Kevin Thompson,Andrew Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes a tight sample complexity of Θ(1/ε²) for property testing five classes of Gaussian quantum states, showing size-independent testing is possible, via testers that project onto the top irrep of two or three copies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed some classes require sample complexity scaling with system size, while existing testers for Gaussian classes only gave upper bounds polynomial in the number of modes. The paper aims to identify classes admitting size-independent sample complexity and to characterize their optimal testing cost.

Method: The tester is the projection onto the "top" irreducible representation (irrep) of two or three copies of the state. Constant sample complexity is obtained by lower bounding how fast the rejection probability grows with distance from the class; the key technical component is bounding the spectral gap of the symmetric extension of the test projector. Matching lower bounds are derived from binary state discrimination.

Result: A tight sample complexity of Θ(1/ε²) is proven for five classes: (1) fermionic Gaussian states, (2) Slater determinants, (3) bosonic Gaussian states, (4) zero-mean bosonic Gaussian states, and (5) bosonic coherent states. Results also extend to the tolerant testing setting where ψ may be O(ε)-close to C rather than exactly inside.

Conclusion: Testing these five Gaussian state classes requires only a size-independent number of samples, with optimal Θ(1/ε²) sample complexity, and the testers and optimality claims extend to tolerant testing.

Abstract: We study the problem of property testing quantum states: given a pure state $|ψ\rangle$ that either (A) belongs to some class $C$ of pure states, or (B) is $\varepsilon$-far in trace distance from all states in $C$, determine which is the case with high probability. It is known that there exists classes $C$ for which the sample complexity of property testing necessarily scales with the size of the system. In this work, we identify classes for which testing only requires a size-independent number of samples. We prove a tight sample complexity of $Θ(1/\varepsilon^2)$ for the property testing of five important classes of Gaussian quantum states: 1) Fermionic Gaussian states, 2) Slater determinants, 3) bosonic Gaussian states, 4) zero-mean bosonic Gaussian states, and 5) bosonic coherent states.
  While property testers for some of these classes have been studied previously, those analyses only gave upper bounds scaling polynomially in the number of modes. In each case, our tester is the projection onto the ``top'' irrep of two or three copies. We get to constant sample complexity by lower bounding how quickly the rejection probability increases with distance from the class; the main technical component involves bounding the spectral gap of symmetric extension of the test projector. We then establish optimality by proving matching lower bounds based on binary state discrimination for each class. Our testers and optimality claims also extend to the tolerant setting, wherein $ψ$ may be $O(\varepsilon)$-close to $C$ in case (A), rather than lying exactly inside.

</details>


### [56] [Complete parameterization and parameter-space topology of discrete Wigner representations on $d \times d$ phase space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39119)
*Lucky K. Antonopoulos,Nicolas C. Menicucci*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper classifies all valid toroidal discrete Wigner functions for a single qudit by solving projected-stencil admissibility conditions, yielding explicit parameter spaces for odd and even d, and showing how canonical marginals and displacement-operator order requirements reduce this freedom.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Toroidal DWFs for finite-dimensional systems are non-unique, and the freedom inherent to their validity was not clearly separated from freedom selected by additional physical requirements such as marginals or displacement-algebra properties.

Method: Using a previously established stencil theorem, the family is expressed as descents of a doubled 2d x 2d parent. The projected-stencil admissibility conditions are solved explicitly in the symplectic-Fourier representation, fixing the modulus and reducing freedom to phase data on a d x d base cell obeying a parity-dependent twisted-oddness relation.

Result: For odd d the parameter space is (S^1)^((d^2-1)/2); for even d it is (S^1)^((d^2-4)/2) x Z_2^3. Canonical horizontal/vertical marginals reduce these to (S^1)^((d-1)^2/2) and (S^1)^(d(d-2)/2) x Z_2. Requiring doubled Weyl-Heisenberg displacement operators to have order dividing d replaces each S^1 factor by Z_d. Symplectic-Fourier characteristic functions have pointwise magnitudes independent of the valid stencil convention.

Conclusion: The classification cleanly separates DWF-validity freedom from freedom selected by marginal and displacement-algebra requirements, and exposes the structural distinction between odd and even dimensions.

Abstract: Toroidal discrete Wigner functions (DWFs) for finite-dimensional systems are non-unique. We classify the labeled single-qudit ${d\times d}$ representations whose phase-point operators are Hermitian, unit-trace, Hilbert-Schmidt orthogonal, and Weyl-Heisenberg covariant. Our previously established stencil theorem expresses this family as descents of a doubled ${2d\times2d}$ parent. Here, we solve its projected-stencil admissibility conditions explicitly. In the symplectic-Fourier representation, admissibility fixes the modulus, leaving phase data on a ${d\times d}$ base cell satisfying a parity-dependent twisted-oddness relation. This gives the parameter space ${(S^1)^{(d^2-1)/2}}$ for odd ${d}$ and ${(S^1)^{(d^2-4)/2}\times\mathbb{Z}_2^3}$ for even ${d}$. Canonical horizontal and vertical marginals reduce these spaces to ${(S^1)^{(d-1)^2/2}}$ and ${(S^1)^{d(d-2)/2}\times\mathbb{Z}_2}$, respectively. The same phase data parameterize validity-preserving rephasings of the doubled Weyl-Heisenberg displacement operators. Requiring these operators to have order dividing the Hilbert-space dimension ${d}$ replaces each ${S^1}$ factor by a discrete ${\mathbb{Z}_d}$ factor. The associated symplectic-Fourier characteristic functions encode the same operator information, with pointwise magnitudes independent of the valid stencil convention. This classification separates the freedom intrinsic to DWF validity from that selected by marginal and displacement-algebra requirements and makes explicit the structural distinction between odd and even dimensions.

</details>


### [57] [Bivariate Bicycle Codes and Metachecks: Syndrome Repair, Measurement-Fault Ambiguity, and Logical Obstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39121)
*Mohammad Rowshan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes when the inherent parity-constraint redundancy in bivariate bicycle (BB) codes can be used as metachecks to repair faulty syndrome measurements, characterizing this via quotient algebras and showing that metasyndrome-only repair fundamentally fails for large block-length families while specific small codes behave differently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Faulty syndrome measurements can corrupt an otherwise correct quantum-error correction step. BB codes contain dependent stabilizer checks, so their measured syndromes obey parity constraints usable as metachecks. The paper asks when this built-in redundancy actually supports syndrome repair and how it interacts with the code's logical structure.

Method: Annihilator quotients are used to identify single-block logical classes and detect when mixed-block search is unavoidable. For syndrome repair, the metasyndrome is shown equivalent to reduction modulo the valid-syndrome ideal. The translation group is mapped into the unit group of a quotient algebra of dimension k/2, with kernel K_M, yielding a distance-two characterization, a unit-group bound on distinguishable single faults, and a family-level obstruction. Exact calculations are performed for standard examples, supported by sustained phenomenological experiments.

Result: For bounded-k BB families with growing block length, metasyndrome-only exact repair fails with probability tending to one at any fixed measurement-error rate. The orbit count also gives the minimum partial second measurement needed to remove every single-fault ambiguity (absent intervening data faults). Exact calculations separate standard examples: the [[72,12,6]] code has syndrome distance three and minimum-weight repair corrects every single measurement fault, whereas the Gross [[144,12,12]] code has 36 indistinguishable single-fault pairs. Experiments show the same qualitative contrast and favor joint data-measurement decoding over a separated repair stage for codes with stronger ambiguity.

Conclusion: The analysis extends the earlier coprime-period treatment to general two-block BB codes and separates measurement ambiguity from the data error it can induce. Metasyndrome-only repair is fundamentally limited for growing block length, and joint data-measurement decoding is preferable when ambiguity is high.

Abstract: Faulty syndrome measurements can corrupt an otherwise correct quantum-error correction step. Bivariate bicycle (BB) codes contain dependent stabilizer checks, so their measured syndromes obey parity constraints that can be used as metachecks. We study when this built-in redundancy actually supports syndrome repair and how it interacts with the logical structure of the code. Annihilator quotients identify single-block logical classes and show when a mixed-block search is unavoidable. For syndrome repair, the metasyndrome is equivalent to reduction modulo the valid-syndrome ideal. The translation group therefore maps into the unit group of a quotient algebra of dimension $k/2$, with kernel $K_M$. This gives a distance-two characterization, a unit-group bound on distinguishable single faults, and a family-level obstruction: for bounded-$k$ BB families with growing block length, metasyndrome-only exact repair fails with probability tending to one at any fixed measurement-error rate. With no intervening data fault, the same orbit count also gives the minimum partial second measurement needed to remove every single-fault ambiguity. Exact calculations separate the standard examples. The $[[72,12,6]]$ code has syndrome distance three and minimum-weight repair corrects every single measurement fault, whereas Gross $[[144,12,12]]$ has 36 indistinguishable single-fault pairs. Sustained phenomenological experiments show the same qualitative contrast and favor joint data--measurement decoding over a separated repair stage on the codes with stronger ambiguity. The analysis extends our earlier coprime-period treatment to general two-block BB codes and separates measurement ambiguity from the data error it can induce.

</details>


### [58] [An uncertainty principle for entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39126)
*Mitali Nanda,Daochen Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a quantitative entropic uncertainty principle between two orthonormal bases with weakly and strongly entangled states, bounding the sum of Shannon entropies of a state's coefficient distributions by the difference of entanglement Rényi entropies of the basis states, with applications to Hamiltonian ground states and the SYK model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty principles relate localization in complementary bases, but their quantitative entanglement-dependent form for bipartite bases of differing entanglement strength is underexplored. The authors aim to formalize how being localized in a weakly entangled basis forces delocalization in a strongly entangled basis.

Method: They derive new lower and upper bounds on the entanglement of superpositions and use them to prove a lower bound on the sum of Shannon entropies of any state's coefficient distributions in the two bases, expressed via the difference between the entanglement Rényi entropies of the respective basis states.

Result: A quantitative uncertainty relation is established: the entropy sum is bounded below by the difference of entanglement Rényi entropies. The superposition entanglement bounds are of independent interest, and the principle limits the ability of weakly entangled states to represent ground states of Hamiltonians with strongly entangled eigenstates, demonstrated numerically for the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev model.

Conclusion: The work provides a quantitative entropic uncertainty principle linking basis entanglement to state delocalization, along with generally useful entanglement bounds for superpositions, and shows concrete implications for representing Hamiltonian ground states, illustrated in the SYK model.

Abstract: Consider two orthonormal bases of a bipartite Hilbert space such that states in one basis are weakly entangled and states in the other are strongly entangled. We establish a quantitative version of the following uncertainty principle: every state that is localized in one basis must be delocalized in the other. More specifically, we lower bound the sum of the Shannon entropies of any state's coefficient distributions in the two bases by the difference between the entanglement Rényi entropies of their basis states. The result follows from new lower and upper bounds on the entanglement of superpositions that may be of independent interest. As an application, our result limits the power of weakly-entangled states in representing ground states of Hamiltonians with strongly-entangled energy eigenstates. We illustrate this application numerically for the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev model.

</details>


### [59] [Geometric Characterization and Feasible Region Analysis of Bipartite Qutrit Bound Entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39141)
*Mazhar Ali*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides an exact analytical and geometric characterization of bound entanglement in a three-parameter bipartite qutrit family, combining the PPT criterion with a structural non-completely positive map, and identifies the exact threshold separating PPT bound entangled states within the PPT region.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bound entanglement in bipartite qutrit systems is difficult to characterize precisely; the authors aim to give an exact, analytical partitioning of the physical state space and to pinpoint the PPT bound entangled sub-region, generalizing known Horodecki bound entangled states.

Method: The authors combine the Positive Partial Transpose (PPT) criterion with a structural, non-completely positive map and analyze a comprehensive three-parameter bipartite qutrit family ρ_{a,b,c}, deriving closed-form boundaries of the PPT region and the bound entangled threshold.

Result: They obtain a closed-form quadratic boundary governing the leaf-like PPT region (a²+ab+b²−b ≤ 0) over 0 < a ≤ 1/3, and an exact linear threshold (a > c) for the PPT bound entangled sub-region. The structural map certifies bound entanglement throughout the threshold region, accounting for 14.76% of the total PPT leaf area.

Conclusion: The proposed framework yields an exact analytical and geometric decomposition of the qutrit state space, strictly generalizing the Horodecki bound entangled states (a single slice of the three-dimensional leaf) and quantifying the certified bound entangled portion of the PPT region.

Abstract: We characterize bound entanglement in bipartite qutrit systems by combining the Positive Partial Transpose (PPT) criterion with a structural, non-completely positive map. Focusing on a comprehensive three-parameter bipartite qutrit family $ρ_{a,b,c}$, we present an exact analytical and geometric partitioning of the physical state space simplex. We derive the closed-form quadratic boundary governing the leaf-like PPT region $(a^2+ab+b^2-b \leq 0)$ over the domain $0 < a \leq \frac{1}{3}$, and identify the exact linear threshold $(a > c)$ that characterizes the PPT bound entangled sub-region. This family strictly generalizes the well-known Horodecki bound entangled states, which appear as a single slice of the three-dimensional leaf. Applying the structural map to this decomposition, we show that it certifies bound entanglement throughout the entire threshold region, accounting for $14.76\%$ of the total PPT leaf area.

</details>


### [60] [A Width-Matched Comparison of Hybrid Quantum-Classical Self-Supervised Learning for Fingerprint Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39172)
*Maria S. Edwards,Kidwell Dlamini,Pin-An Lin,Wen-Hsien Hsu,Wen-Chieh Fang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Inserting a QuFeX quantum feature-extraction module into three SSL frameworks (SimCLR, MoCo v2, BYOL) and comparing against classical counterparts at matched width on SOCOFing shows hybrid gains for contrastive objectives but no reliable gain for BYOL, indicating objective-dependent benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised fingerprint recognition needs large labeled enrollment sets; SSL removes this labeling requirement, and hybrid quantum-classical models have been proposed to enrich learned representations. However, prior quantum SSL studies use only a single contrastive objective, leaving it unclear whether reported benefits stem from the objective or from the quantum circuit itself.

Method: The QuFeX quantum feature-extraction module is inserted into three SSL frameworks: contrastive SimCLR and MoCo v2, and non-contrastive BYOL. Each hybrid is compared with its classical counterpart at matched representation width (8 features, equal to 8 qubits) on the SOCOFing fingerprint dataset with a CIFAR-10 control, evaluated via k-nearest-neighbor identification on encoder features. A hardware-efficient QNet circuit is also tested, and the authors probe whether gains arise from circuit architecture, trainable parameter count, nonlinearity, or the classical simulability of 8-qubit circuits.

Result: In single-run experiments the hybrid scores clearly higher for both contrastive objectives, whereas multi-seed analysis shows no reliable difference for BYOL. A hardware-efficient QNet circuit does not show the same gain.

Conclusion: Any benefit of the hybrid quantum-classical module depends on the SSL objective rather than being a general property of the quantum circuit, and alternative circuit designs do not reproduce the gains; the authors analyze possible attributions including architecture, parameter count, nonlinearity, and classical simulability.

Abstract: Fingerprint recognition is a widely deployed biometric, but supervised training requires large labeled enrollment sets. Self-supervised learning (SSL) removes this requirement, and hybrid quantum-classical models have been proposed to enrich the learned representations. Prior quantum SSL studies consider a single contrastive objective, so it is unclear whether reported benefits depend on the objective or can be attributed to the quantum circuit. We insert the QuFeX quantum feature-extraction module into three SSL frameworks, the contrastive SimCLR and MoCo v2 and the non-contrastive BYOL, and compare each hybrid with its classical counterpart at matched representation width (8 features, equal to 8 qubits) on the SOCOFing fingerprint dataset, with a CIFAR-10 control, using k-nearest-neighbor identification on encoder features. In single-run experiments the hybrid scores clearly higher for both contrastive objectives, whereas for BYOL a multi-seed analysis shows no reliable difference, suggesting that any benefit depends on the SSL objective. A hardware-efficient circuit (QNet) does not show the same gain. We examine whether the gains can be attributed to the quantum circuit, considering circuit architecture, trainable parameter count, nonlinearity, and the classical simulability of 8-qubit circuits.

</details>


### [61] [Metrological Benchmarking of Random Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39226)
*Simone Cavazzoni,Changhun Oh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a metrological benchmark for random circuit sampling based on quantum Fisher information (QFI), avoiding costly ideal output probability calculations, and links QFI to out-of-time-order correlators to probe dynamical properties and noise degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random circuit sampling demonstrates quantum computational advantage, but benchmarking noisy implementations via linear cross-entropy requires expensive computations of ideal output probabilities. There is a need for a cheaper, physically grounded benchmark.

Method: The authors use a metrological benchmark based on the response to controlled perturbations, characterized by QFI. They establish a relation between QFI and out-of-time-order correlators, enabling local and global control protocols. They analyze Haar-random circuits, Clifford circuits, and a butterfly protocol that exploits system size, using single-qubit readout. For noisy implementations, they derive an exact relation between noisy and ideal QFI within a global white-noise model.

Result: For Haar-random circuits, average QFI approaches its maximal value in the local protocol and grows linearly with qubit number under collective control. Clifford circuits yield zero QFI despite extensive operator spreading. The butterfly protocol yields mean inverse sensitivity proportional to qubit number with single-qubit readout, and its fluctuations distinguish Haar from Clifford ensembles despite identical mean responses. The exact noisy-ideal QFI relation provides a quantitative reference for benchmark degradation.

Conclusion: These protocols enable metrological benchmarking of noisy random quantum circuits without computing ideal output probabilities, and reveal dynamical properties beyond operator spreading, with a quantitative handle on noise-induced degradation.

Abstract: Random circuit sampling is a leading approach to demonstrating quantum computational advantage, but benchmarking noisy implementations through linear cross-entropy requires costly calculations of ideal output probabilities. We propose a metrological benchmark based on the response to controlled perturbations, characterized by quantum Fisher information (QFI). A relation between QFI and out-of-time-order correlators enables protocols with local or global control. For Haar-random circuits, the average QFI approaches its maximal value in the local protocol and grows linearly with the number of qubits under collective control. In contrast, Clifford circuits yield zero QFI despite extensive operator spreading, showing that the response probes dynamical properties beyond spreading alone. A butterfly protocol further uses system size to enhance sensitivity, yielding a mean inverse sensitivity proportional to the number of qubits with single-qubit readout. Its fluctuations distinguish the Haar and Clifford ensembles despite their identical mean responses. For noisy implementations, we derive an exact relation between the noisy and ideal QFI within a global white-noise model, providing a quantitative reference for the degradation of the benchmark. These protocols enable metrological benchmarking of noisy random quantum circuits without computing ideal output probabilities.

</details>


### [62] [Breaking the Bounded Entanglement Barrier for Quantum Position Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39234)
*Amit Behera,Vipul Goyal*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs a BB84-based Quantum Position Verification protocol that beats the known LOCC-attack barrier by exploiting timing precision, achieving an arbitrary polynomial resource gap between honest and adversarial parties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical position verification is impossible, and even QPV admits LOCC attacks whenever the adversary holds exponentially large preshared entanglement. Prior protocols required honest-party resources to be at least as large as adversarial entanglement, so a way to overcome this barrier was needed.

Method: The authors work in an idealized continuous-time model where time is a real-valued parameter and challenge messages are sent at a uniformly sampled time from a real interval. They give a BB84-based protocol secure against any finite coalition of LOCC adversaries with arbitrary finite (possibly exponential) quantum storage and entanglement. The construction is also instantiated in the discrete-time model.

Result: The BB84-based protocol remains secure against finite coalitions of LOCC adversaries with arbitrary finite quantum storage and entanglement in the continuous-time model. In the discrete-time instantiation, an information-theoretic QPV protocol is obtained in which honest parties' total communication and storage can be significantly smaller than the adversarial resource bound, achieving any desired polynomial gap, unlike all previous protocols.

Conclusion: The resource gap between honest and adversarial parties depends on how precisely time can be measured: as the best-known clock precision improves, the adversarial resource bound increases while honest resources stay largely the same. The authors call this the "I sleep, you work" paradigm.

Abstract: Position verification, introduced by Chandran et al. (SIAM J. Computing 2014), allows verifiers to test a prover's claimed position by an interactive protocol. Classical position verification is impossible. Even for Quantum Position Verification (QPV), there always exists an LOCC (Local Operations and Classical Communication) attack if the adversary can hold an exponentially large amount of preshared entanglement.
  Somewhat surprisingly, we show that we can circumvent this barrier in the idealized continuous-time model, where time is represented by a real-valued parameter and challenge messages can be sent at a time sampled uniformly from a real interval. In this model, we give a BB84-based protocol that remains secure against any finite coalition of LOCC adversaries with arbitrary finite (possibly exponential) quantum storage and entanglement.
  Our construction can also be instantiated in the discrete-time model. Even though the previous impossibility results apply, we are able to obtain an information-theoretic QPV protocol in which the honest parties' total resources (communication and storage) can be significantly smaller than the adversarial resource bound. In fact, we can achieve any desired polynomial gap between honest parties and adversarial resources. To our knowledge, all previous protocols in the literature required resources of the honest parties to be at least as large as the adversarial entanglement.
  Interestingly, the resource gap between the honest and adversarial party resources in our protocol depends on how precisely time can be measured. As the precision of the best-known clock improves with further research, the resource gap in our protocol keeps increasing. In particular, the adversarial resource bound keeps increasing while the honest parties' resources remain largely the same. We call this the "I sleep, you work" paradigm.

</details>


### [63] [Optimal learning of covariant quantum states and channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39280)
*Satoshi Yoshida,Kazuki Okigami,Pietro M. Posta,Dmitry Grinko*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops collective tomography protocols for quantum states and channels with known symmetries, achieving optimal copy/query complexity via random purification and dilation that reduce learning to pure-state estimation, and provides gate-efficient circuits for the Hayashi measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tomography of quantum states and channels is fundamental, but general protocols suffer high sample complexity. Known symmetries (compact-group representations, covariance) should be exploitable to reduce the cost of learning. Prior work lacked optimal collective protocols and efficient circuits, notably for the optimal Hayashi measurement in pure-state estimation.

Method: Random purification and dilation reduce learning symmetric states and channels to pure-state estimation. For states commuting with compact-group representations with multiplicities m_λ, the optimal copy complexity is characterized. For G-covariant channels with finite-dimensional unitary representations, parallel queries achieve diamond-distance scaling governed by D_G, the count of real parameters of covariant Choi operators before trace preservation. The Hayashi measurement is approximated by encoding symmetric-subspace states into bosonic occupation modes, realizing the measurement via heterodyne detection and normalization, and approximating it on qubits using the quantum Hermite transform, combined with symmetry-compatible purification and dilation circuits.

Result: Optimal copy complexity Θ((Σ_λ m_λ² + log η^{-1})/ε²) for symmetric states when Σ_λ m_λ² > 1. For G-covariant channels, parallel Õ(D_G/ε²) queries achieve diamond-distance error ε at success probability 2/3. For permutation-covariant channels on k qudits of fixed local dimension d, optimal scalings Θ_d(k^{d⁴−1}) (diamond distance) and Θ_d(k^{d⁴−d²}) (Choi trace distance) are obtained. Gate-efficient learners of permutation-covariant states and channels achieve gate complexity O_d(poly(k, ε^{-1}, log η^{-1})).

Conclusion: Symmetry-aware collective tomography attains optimal sample/query complexity for states and channels, and the previously open problem of efficiently realizing the Hayashi measurement is resolved, yielding query-optimal and gate-efficient learners for permutation-covariant states and channels.

Abstract: We give collective tomography protocols for quantum states and channels with known symmetries, using random purification and dilation to reduce learning to pure-state estimation. For states commuting with a compact-group representation with multiplicities $m_λ$, the optimal copy complexity is $Θ((\sum_λm_λ^2+\logη^{-1})/\varepsilon^2)$ for sufficiently small trace-distance error $\varepsilon$ and failure probability $η$ for the nontrivial case $\sum_λm_λ^2>1$. For $G$-covariant channels with finite-dimensional unitary representations of a compact group $G$, parallel $\widetilde{O}(D_G/\varepsilon^2)$ queries achieve a diamond-distance error $\varepsilon $ at fixed success probability $2/3$, where $D_G$ counts the real parameters of covariant Choi operators before imposing trace preservation. For permutation-covariant channels on $k$ qudits of fixed local dimension $d$, at sufficiently small fixed error and fixed success probability $2/3$, we obtain the optimal query scalings $Θ_d(k^{d^4-1})$ for diamond distance and $Θ_d(k^{d^4-d^2})$ for Choi trace distance. Furthermore, we resolve an open problem in quantum tomography of constructing efficient quantum circuits that approximate the Hayashi measurement for optimal pure-state estimation. We encode states in the symmetric subspace into bosonic occupation modes, realize the measurement by heterodyne detection and normalization, and approximate this procedure on qubits using the quantum Hermite transform. Combined with our symmetry-compatible purification and dilation circuits, this yields query-optimal and gate-efficient learners of permutation-covariant states and channels with gate complexity $O_d(\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},\logη^{-1}))$.

</details>


### [64] [Bounds on adiabatic path geometry from the width class of the gap profile](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39286)
*Mancheon Han,Sangkook Choi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper tightens bounds on the geometric length \(L\) and total curvature \(K\) of adiabatic ground-state paths using a gap-profile "width class" \(p\), replacing the loose traditional \(\mathcal{O}(\Delta_*^{-1/2})\) and \(\mathcal{O}(\Delta_*^{-1})\) scaling with logarithmic (for \(p=1\)) or improved power-law (for \(p>1\)) bounds, and derives corresponding evolution-time schedules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In adiabatic evolution, the runtime \(T\) is governed by the path length \(L\), curvature \(K\), and minimum gap \(\Delta_*\). The traditional bounds \(L=\mathcal{O}(\Delta_*^{-1/2})\) and \(K=\mathcal{O}(\Delta_*^{-1})\) are loose in many physical settings, so tighter, profile-dependent geometric bounds are needed to better predict and optimize adiabatic evolution times.

Method: The authors characterize the gap profile by \(\mu_<(\gamma)\), the total width in \(s\) where the gap is below \(\gamma\), and define width class \(p\) by \(\mu_<(\gamma)\lesssim \gamma^{1/p}\). They use this to bound \(L\) and \(K\) in terms of \(\Delta_*\), and analyze evolution time under (i) a constant-rate schedule and (ii) a constant-geometric-speed schedule. They prove bounds for twice continuously differentiable \(H(s)\) (for \(L\)) and affine \(H(s)\) (for \(K\)), and establish tightness of the \(L\) scaling for every integer \(p\ge1\) and of the \(K\) scaling at \(p=1\).

Result: For a typical avoided crossing in width class \(p=1\), \(L=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log \Delta_*^{-1}})\) and \(K=\mathcal{O}(\log \Delta_*^{-1})\). For \(p>1\), power laws arise with exponents \((p-1)/(2p)\) for \(L\) and \((p-1)/p\) for \(K\). At \(p=1\), \(T=\mathcal{O}(\Delta_*^{-2})\) with a constant-rate schedule and \(T=\mathcal{O}(\Delta_*^{-1})\) up to polylogarithmic correction with a constant-geometric-speed schedule. Tightness of the scaling in \(\Delta_*\) is proven for \(L\) for every integer \(p\ge1\) and for \(K\) at \(p=1\). The bounds are demonstrated on adiabatic Grover search, the XXZ spin chain, and molecular electronic Hamiltonians.

Conclusion: The gap-profile width class provides a refined framework that substantially improves traditional adiabatic path-geometry and runtime bounds, yielding logarithmic scaling for typical avoided crossings and improved power laws otherwise. The results are tight in the proven regimes and are validated on Grover search, the XXZ chain, and molecular electronic Hamiltonians.

Abstract: In an adiabatic evolution, the wave function follows the path of ground states $|Φ_0(s)\rangle$ of a Hamiltonian $H(s)$ as $s$ is tuned from $0$ to $1$. The geometry of this adiabatic path is described by quantities such as its length $L=\int_0^1 \|\partial_s |Φ_0(s)\rangle\|\,ds$ and its total curvature $K$. This geometry affects the evolution time $T$. For instance, $T$ is at least of order $L/Δ_*$, where $Δ_*$ is the minimum energy gap over $s\in[0,1]$. The traditional approach gives $L=\mathcal{O}(Δ_*^{-1/2})$ and $K=\mathcal{O}(Δ_*^{-1})$, which are loose in many cases. We improve these bounds by using $μ_<(γ)$, the width of the region in $s$ on which the gap is below $γ$. The gap profile is in width class $p$ when $μ_<(γ)$ falls at least as fast as $γ^{1/p}$. A typical avoided crossing is in width class $p=1$, which gives $L=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log Δ_*^{-1}})$ and $K=\mathcal{O}(\log Δ_*^{-1})$. A profile in width class $p>1$ gives power laws with exponents $(p-1)/(2p)$ for $L$ and $(p-1)/p$ for $K$. The width class also bounds the evolution time. At $p=1$ we have $T=\mathcal{O}(Δ_*^{-2})$ with a schedule that advances $s$ at a constant rate, and $T=\mathcal{O}(Δ_*^{-1})$ up to a polylogarithmic correction with a schedule that traverses the path at a constant geometric speed. The bounds on $L$ hold for any twice continuously differentiable $H(s)$, and the bounds on $K$ for affine $H(s)$. We also prove the tightness of scaling of $L$ in $Δ_*$ for every integer $p\ge1$, and the scaling of $K$ at $p=1$. We demonstrate the bounds on the adiabatic Grover search, the XXZ spin chain, and molecular electronic Hamiltonians.

</details>


### [65] [Resource-Tunable Quantum Circuit Implementation of Nonlinear Element-Wise Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39295)
*Chunlin Yang,Yuqi Li,Hongmei Yao,Zhaobing Fan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A resource-tunable quantum framework implements nonlinear transformations by factorizing a high-degree polynomial into m lower-degree factors, enabling a trade-off between circuit depth, query complexity, gate complexity, ancilla count, and normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear transformations are crucial in quantum machine learning and quantum numerical applications, reducible to element-wise polynomial implementation via polynomial approximation. Prior works achieve feasibility and improve query or auxiliary-space efficiency, but circuit depth still scales linearly with polynomial degree, which is a bottleneck.

Method: Decompose a (2^d−1)-degree polynomial into m lower-degree factors, with m controlling the degree of parallelism. Varying m from 1 to 2^d−1 yields a spectrum of implementations from a binary-tree construction to an O(n+d)-depth construction with highly parallel oracle queries matching the query lower bound. Analyze polynomial approximation and implementation error effects.

Result: The framework provides a flexible trade-off among query complexity, additional gate complexity, ancilla count, and normalization. The O(n+d)-depth variant achieves highly parallel oracle queries that match the query lower bound. Demonstrated on nonlinear activation functions (sigmoid, tanh) and pixel-wise image transformations.

Conclusion: The proposed resource-tunable framework overcomes the linear-depth limitation of prior quantum polynomial implementations by exposing a tunable parallelism parameter, enabling efficient and adaptable implementations for nonlinear transformations in quantum machine learning and numerical applications.

Abstract: Nonlinear transformations are important in quantum machine learning and other quantum numerical applications, which can be reduced to the implementation of element-wise polynomials through polynomial approximation. Existing works establish the feasibility of transformations and improve query or auxiliary-space efficiency, but circuit depth remains linear in scale with degree. In this work, we develop a resource-tunable quantum framework that decomposes a $(2^d-1)$-degree polynomial into $m$ lower-degree factors, with $m$ controlling the degree of parallelism. By varying $m$ from $1$ to $2^d-1$, the framework provides a trade-off among query complexity, additional gate complexity, ancilla count, and normalization, ranging from a binary-tree implementation to a $\mathcal{O}(n+d)$-depth construction with highly parallel oracle queries that match the query lower bound. The effects of polynomial approximation and implementation errors are further analyzed. We demonstrate the framework for nonlinear activation functions such as sigmoid and tanh, as well as pixel-wise image transformations.

</details>


### [66] [SQD-Agent: LLM-driven agentic framework for Quantum Chemistry workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39302)
*Kislaya Tiwari,Anupama Ray*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: SQD Agent is an LLM-based agentic framework that converts natural-language intent into executable hybrid quantum-classical workflows for Quantum Chemistry using Sample-Based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) algorithms, lowering expertise and configuration barriers for application researchers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain researchers face a significant barrier translating domain-specific problems into executable hybrid quantum-classical workflows due to required expertise in quantum algorithms, quantum programming nuances, and hardware-aware integration. Meanwhile, LLM-based agents are increasingly capable of interpreting intent, reasoning over complex workflows, and generating executable code and pipelines.

Method: The authors introduce SQD Agent, a modular and extensible LLM-based agentic framework with seamless integration of heterogeneous quantum backends, classical solvers, and workflow components. It provides interactive capabilities for on-demand profiling, bottleneck analysis, resource optimization, intelligent result caching, and convergence visualization, plus error mitigation on real quantum hardware and analysis of candidate mitigation schemes.

Result: The framework automates the natural-language-to-workflow translation for Quantum Chemistry with SQD-family algorithms, enabling error mitigation on real hardware and analysis of mitigation schemes in terms of error-recovery behavior and computational budget, exposing practical trade-offs.

Conclusion: SQD Agent reduces required human expertise and configuration overhead, simplifying hybrid quantum-classical experimentation for application researchers new to quantum, while remaining adaptable to a rapidly evolving quantum ecosystem.

Abstract: Quantum algorithms and quantum hardware are advancing towards a promising paradigm for scientific applications. However, translating domain-specific problems into executable hybrid quantum-classical workflows remains a significant barrier for application researchers due to the required expertise in quantum algorithms, nuances in quantum programming, and hardware-aware system integration. At the same time, AI and primarily LLM based agents are increasingly capable of interpreting natural-language intent, reasoning over complex workflows, and translating high-level objectives into executable code and building computational pipelines. In this work, we introduce SQD Agent, an LLM-based agentic framework that translates natural-language user intent into executable workflows for Quantum Chemistry applications where algorithms from the Sample-Based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) family are used. By automating this translation, SQD Agent reduces the level of human expertise and configuration overhead required, thereby simplifying experimentation in hybrid quantum-classical settings for application researchers new to quantum. SQD Agent adopts a modular and extensible architecture that supports seamless integration of heterogeneous quantum backends, classical solvers, and workflow components, ensuring adaptability to rapidly evolving quantum ecosystems. The framework further incorporates interactive capabilities for on-demand profiling, bottleneck analysis, resource optimization, intelligent result caching, and convergence visualization. Key features include quantum chemistry experiments, error mitigation on real quantum hardware, together with analysis of candidate mitigation schemes in terms of their potential error-recovery behavior and computational budget, helping users understand their practical trade-offs and decide which strategies to explore in subsequent experiments.

</details>


### [67] [A Dichotomy for MIP* in the Presence of Unital Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39318)
*Yangjing Dong,Zhenyu Jiang,Minglong Qin,Penghui Yao,Haigang Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper removes the constant-answer restriction on noisy two-prover one-round proof systems with unital noise and establishes a complete complexity dichotomy for MIP^ψ[poly,poly], showing it equals NEXP when ψ is separable or has quantum maximal correlation < 1, and RE when ψ is entangled with ρmax(ψ) = 1. Two new techniques are introduced: a positivity-preserving low-degree approximation for quantum measurements and a Pauli folding dimension-reduction method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work established a dichotomy only for constant-answer systems MIP^ψ[poly,O(1)], where noisy EPR states with ρmax(ψ) < 1 yield NEXP, while the noiseless EPR case gives RE. However, not all noise reduces quantum maximal correlation; dephasing noise is a basic counterexample that retains ρmax = 1. The restriction to constant answers leaves open whether the classification extends to polynomially many answers, and importantly the earlier techniques could not handle dephased states with maximally mixed marginals but full maximal correlation.

Method: The authors remove the constant-answer restriction and prove a full dichotomy for MIP^ψ[poly,poly] with arbitrarily many copies of EPR pairs under unital noise (equivalently, fixed two-qubit states with maximally mixed marginals). The proof develops two techniques: (1) a positivity-preserving low-degree approximation framework for quantum measurements, which approximates suitable square-root factorizations rather than truncating positive operators directly in the Pauli basis, achieving low Pauli degree, positivity, and global control over an entire POVM simultaneously; and (2) a dimension-reduction method called Pauli folding for low-degree positive operators, whose key ingredient is an almost-multiplicativity theorem for randomized Pauli hashing, allowing noncommutative products, positivity, and normalization to survive compression with controlled error.

Result: The paper proves the complete dichotomy: MIP^ψ[poly,poly] = MIP = NEXP if ψ is separable or ρmax(ψ) < 1; and MIP^ψ[poly,poly] = MIP^ψ[poly,O(1)] = RE if ψ is entangled and ρmax(ψ) = 1. This extends the earlier classification from constant-answer to polynomial-answer systems and covers dephasing-type noise previously unaddressed.

Conclusion: The complexity classification is fully resolved for unital noise with arbitrary answer length. Beyond the classification, the two introduced techniques—positivity-preserving low-degree approximation of POVMs via square-root factorizations, and Pauli folding with almost-multiplicativity of randomized Pauli hashing—are expected to be broadly useful in quantum information and quantum complexity theory.

Abstract: A recent work studied quantum two-prover one-round proof systems $\mathsf{MIP}^ψ[\text{poly},O(1)]$. It showed that when $ψ$ is a noisy EPR state with quantum maximal correlation $ρ_{\max}(ψ)<1$, the resulting complexity class is equivalent to $\mathsf{NEXP}=\mathsf{MIP}$. In contrast, $\mathsf{MIP}^{\text{EPR}}[\text{poly},O(1)]=\mathsf{RE}$. Not all noise, however, decreases quantum maximal correlation. A basic example is dephasing noise: a dephased EPR pair retain quantum maximal correlation $1$. In this work, we remove the constant-answer restriction and establish a complete dichotomy for $\mathsf{MIP}^ψ[\text{poly},\text{poly}]$, where the provers share arbitrarily many copies of EPR pairs subject to unital noise. Equivalently, let $ψ$ be a fixed two-qubit state with maximally mixed marginals. We prove that \[\mathsf{MIP}^ψ[\text{poly},\text{poly}]=\mathsf{MIP} =\mathsf{NEXP},~\text{if $ψ$ is separable or $ρ_{\max}(ψ)<1$};\]\[\mathsf{MIP}^ψ[\text{poly},\text{poly}]=\mathsf{MIP}^ψ[\text{poly},O(1)]=\mathsf{RE},\text{if $ψ$ is entangled and $ρ_{\max}(ψ)=1$.}\] Beyond the complexity classification, our proof develops two techniques that may be useful more broadly in quantum information and quantum complexity. First, we introduce a positivity-preserving low-degree approximation framework for quantum measurements: instead of truncating positive operators directly in the Pauli basis, we approximate suitable square-root factorizations, simultaneously achieving low Pauli degree, positivity, and global control over an entire POVM. Second, we develop a new dimension-reduction method, called Pauli folding, for low-degree positive operators. Its key ingredient is an almost-multiplicativity theorem for randomized Pauli hashing, which allows noncommutative products, positivity, and normalization to survive compression with controlled error.

</details>


### [68] [Information Potential: A Variational Approach to Quantum Information Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39328)
*Penghui Yao,Yifan Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a variational "information potential" approach to analyze quantum information complexity (QIC), enabling quantum communication lower bounds. Using this, it proves optimal QIC lower bounds for AND, optimal quantum communication bounds for r-round Set-Disjointness, a direct-sum theorem, and a nearly tight asymmetric tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QIC is a powerful method for proving quantum communication complexity and equals amortized quantum communication complexity, but it is generally hard to analyze because it is a sum of quantum conditional mutual information terms that are difficult to estimate. This motivates the need for a more tractable framework for analyzing QIC and deriving quantum communication lower bounds.

Method: The authors introduce a new variational approach called the information potential, inspired by the resolvent representation for quantum relative entropy. This approach connects the QIC of individual messages to a quadratic form, making it more amenable to analysis. It then bounds the cumulative positive increments of the potential throughout an interactive quantum protocol by its QIC, so lower bounds on the growth of the potential translate into lower bounds on both QIC and quantum communication complexity.

Result: The paper proves an optimal Ω(1/r) lower bound on the QIC of the two-bit AND function and an optimal Ω(n/r) lower bound on the quantum communication complexity of r-round Set-Disjointness, resolving an open problem. It also proves a direct-sum theorem for bounded-round quantum communication complexity of Set-Disjointness and establishes a nearly tight asymmetric tradeoff (q_A+1)(q_B+1)=Ω(n) for Set-Disjointness.

Conclusion: The information potential provides a tractable variational technique for analyzing QIC, yielding optimal communication lower bounds and tradeoffs for fundamental problems such as AND and Set-Disjointness, and resolving previously open questions in quantum communication complexity.

Abstract: Quantum information complexity (QIC), introduced by Touchette [Touchette, STOC 2015], has been shown to be one of the most powerful methods for proving quantum communication complexity and has also been shown to be equal to amortized quantum communication complexity. Unfortunately, QIC is generally hard to analyze because it is a sum of quantum conditional mutual information terms, which are difficult to estimate. In this work, we introduce a new variational approach, the information potential, for analyzing QIC and proving quantum communication lower bounds inspired by the resolvent representation for quantum relative entropy. This approach connects the QIC of individual messages to a quadratic form, making it more amenable and thus enables us to bound the cumulative positive increments of the potential throughout an interactive quantum protocol by its QIC. Lower bounds on the growth of the potential therefore translate into lower bounds on both QIC and quantum communication complexity.
  As an application, we give an optimal $Ω(1/r)$ lower bound on the QIC of the two-bit $\mathsf{AND}$ function as well as an optimal $Ω(n/r)$ lower bound on the quantum communication complexity of $r$-round Set-Disjointness, answering an open problem in~[Braverman, Garg, Ko, Mao, Touchette FOCS 2015]. Moreover, we further prove a direct-sum theorem for bounded-round quantum communication complexity of Set Disjointness. With the tight bound on the QIC of $\mathsf{AND}$ function, we further establish a nearly tight tradeoff for the asymmetric quantum communication complexity of $\mathsf{Set} \mathsf{Disjointness}$: $(q_A+1)(q_B+1)=Ω(n)$, where $q_A$ and $q_B$ denote the total numbers of qubits sent by Alice and Bob, respectively.

</details>


### [69] [Complete product-state contact set and optimality of the canonical three-qubit Shifts witness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39332)
*Bing-Dong Wan,Yu-Chen Guo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper fully characterizes the equality set of the three-qubit Shifts UPB projector, showing it consists of exactly eight product rays; this complete classification proves the associated projector witness is optimal and gives the exact white-noise entanglement-detection threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Only four attaining product states were previously known for the minimum product-state expectation value of the three-qubit Shifts UPB projector. Whether these four constitute the entire equality set—and hence whether the associated witness is optimal—was unresolved. Since the four known rays fail to span the full Hilbert space, the spanning criterion for optimality could not be certified.

Method: Exact phase analysis combined with a Gröbner-basis classification of all 28 finite real stationary points, plus a complete treatment of the projective boundaries of the complex product-state manifold.

Result: The complete equality set over all complex product states consists of exactly eight product rays: two rays invariant under cyclic permutation of the three qubits and two cyclic orbits of size three. These eight contact rays span the full three-qubit Hilbert space, unlike the four previously known rays.

Conclusion: The complete equality classification shows the Shifts projector witness satisfies the spanning criterion and is optimal—no nonzero positive semidefinite operator can be subtracted while preserving block positivity. Consequently, for the white-noise family ρ(p)=(1-p)ρ_Shifts+p I/8, the witness detects entanglement for p<2-3√6/4≈16.29%.

Abstract: For the three-qubit Shifts unextendible product basis (UPB), the minimum product-state expectation value $λ_{\mathrm{Shifts}}=1-3\sqrt6/8$ of the UPB projector is known, and four attaining product states were exhibited previously. We determine the complete equality set over all complex product states and prove that it consists of exactly eight product rays: two rays invariant under cyclic permutation of the three qubits and two cyclic orbits of size three. The proof combines exact phase analysis, a Gröbner-basis classification of all 28 finite real stationary points, and a complete treatment of the projective boundaries. Crucially, the eight contact rays span the full three-qubit Hilbert space, whereas the four previously known rays do not. The complete equality classification therefore establishes that the associated projector witness satisfies the spanning criterion and is optimal in the sense that no nonzero positive semidefinite operator can be subtracted while preserving block positivity. As a corollary, for the white-noise family $ρ(p)=(1-p)ρ_{\mathrm{Shifts}}+p\mathbb I/8$, where $ρ_{\mathrm{Shifts}}$ is the complementary-projector Shifts state, the same witness detects entanglement for $p<2-3\sqrt6/4\approx16.29\%$.

</details>


### [70] [Constant-Overhead Injection into Quantum Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39345)
*Louis Golowich,Venkatesan Guruswami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs the first fault-tolerant scheme for injecting and ejecting states in constant-rate quantum LDPC codes with constant space and time overhead, resilient to circuit-level locally stochastic noise, with single-shot gadgets and linear qubit scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault-tolerant state injection into quantum error-correcting codes typically incurs high overhead. The paper aims to achieve constant space and time overhead for injection/ejection, error correction, and state preparation, under circuit-level locally stochastic noise.

Method: They build constant-rate quantum codes via a high-dimensional hypergraph product of classical LDPC codes, derived from a simplified version of Spielman's linear-time encodable codes. Since the resulting product codes resist only non-uniform physical noise, they concatenate with inner codes of various sizes to achieve fault-tolerance against uniform noise.

Result: The scheme fault-tolerantly injects bare physical qubits into a code block and ejects code states back to bare qubits, succeeding under circuit-level locally stochastic noise with only a small constant per-qubit corruption probability. Error correction and code-state preparation are also fault-tolerant. All gadgets are single-shot (constant circuit depth), with physical qubits growing linearly in logical qubits, given polynomial-sized noiseless classical side circuits.

Conclusion: Constant-rate quantum codes admit fault-tolerant injection, ejection, error correction, and state preparation with constant space and time overhead, using hypergraph products of classical LDPC codes and concatenation with inner codes to handle uniform noise.

Abstract: We construct the first known fault-tolerant scheme for injecting states into quantum error-correcting codes with constant space and time overhead. That is, we construct a family of constant-rate quantum error-correcting codes for which a set of bare physical qubits can be injected, i.e. fault-tolerantly encoded, into a code block. Similarly, a code state can be ejected, i.e. fault-tolerantly decoded, back into bare physical qubits. We show that these injection and ejection procedures succeed under circuit-level locally stochastic noise, while incurring just a small constant probability of corrupting each qubit, which is unavoidable for bare physical qubits. We also show how to perform fault-tolerant error correction and code-state preparation under locally stochastic noise. All of our gadgets can be implemented with constant quantum circuit depth (i.e. are single-shot), and with a number of physical qubits growing linearly with the number of logical qubits, assuming the ability to run polynomial-sized noiseless classical circuits on the side.
  We construct our quantum codes by taking a high-dimensional hypergraph product of classical LDPC codes, which in turn are a simplified version of Spielman's linear-time encodable codes (STOC'95). As our resulting product codes are only resilient to physical errors occurring with non-uniform probabilities across qubits, we then show how to concatenate with inner codes of various sizes to obtain fault-tolerance against uniform noise.

</details>


### [71] [Efficient Learning of Fermionic Magic States under Free-Fermion Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39372)
*Jiwon Heo,Myeongjin Shin,Changhun Oh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives an efficient algorithm for learning fermionic magic states under unknown number-conserving free-fermion evolution, exploiting particle reduced density matrix (RDM) spectral structure to recover hidden block decompositions with polynomial sample and computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning quantum states under unknown dynamics is challenging, especially for non-Gaussian (magic) resources that enable quantum advantage. Fermionic magic states undergoing unknown free-fermion evolution lack a scalable reconstruction method, and it is unclear whether an extensive number of non-Gaussian blocks can still be learned efficiently.

Method: The inputs are definite-particle-number blocks that are superpositions of Fock states with disjoint occupied mode sets, both within and across blocks. The algorithm exploits the spectral structure of particle RDMs to separate single-block contributions from multi-block contributions, reconstructing the hidden block structure. It uses single-copy measurements and requires only RDMs up to a fixed particle-number bound for reconstruction, achieving polynomial sample and classical computational complexity without prior knowledge of the block decomposition or evolution.

Result: The algorithm recovers a compact description of the state with prescribed fidelity and high probability in polynomial time and samples. The required RDM order (fixed particle-number bound) is proven necessary in general: there exist two orthogonal states in the family with identical RDMs at every lower order.

Conclusion: An extensive number of non-Gaussian blocks can be compatible with efficient state learning, and the RDM order used is both sufficient and, in general, necessary, establishing tightness of the approach.

Abstract: We establish efficient learning for a family of fermionic magic states under unknown number-conserving free-fermion evolution. Each input block has a definite particle number and is a superposition of Fock states, with occupied mode sets disjoint both within and across blocks. The key idea is to exploit the spectral structure of particle reduced density matrices (RDMs) to separate contributions from individual blocks from those involving several blocks, allowing us to reconstruct the hidden block structure. For a fixed upper bound on the particle number per block, our algorithm uses single-copy measurements and polynomial sample and classical computational complexity to recover a compact description of the state with prescribed fidelity and high probability, without prior knowledge of the block decomposition or the evolution. RDMs up to this upper bound suffice for reconstruction. We further show that this RDM order is necessary in general: two orthogonal states in the family can have identical RDMs at every lower order. These results show that an extensive number of non-Gaussian blocks can be compatible with efficient state learning.

</details>


### [72] [Spectrally Selective Charging of an Interacting Quantum Battery via an Anharmonic Mediator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39393)
*Sujay Mondal,Anumita Mukhopadhyay,Siddhartha Dutta,Abhijit Bandyopadhyay*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A finite-time quantum-battery charging scheme uses a driven two-level working system and an anharmonic three-level mediator to transfer coherence and ergotropy into an interacting two-qubit battery, enabling spectrally selective single- and two-excitation charging channels with cycle-level energy balance closure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-time (non-adiabatic) driving generates coherence and ergotropy, while excitation-number conservation organizes transfer into distinct dynamical sectors. The mediator then provides spectrally selective charging channels: its lower transition enables nearly complete single-excitation transfer with negligible residual mediator energy, whereas initial mediator loading activates a cross-resonant two-excitation cascade toward the doubly excited battery state.

Method: Finite-time driving generates coherence and ergotropy, while excitation-number conservation organizes the transfer into distinct dynamical sectors. Detuning scans quantify the spectral tolerance, and the full mixed-state dynamics reveals distinct energy- and ergotropy-transfer profiles. Finite-time compression, thermal reset, and switching work close the cycle-level energy balance to numerical precision.

Result: The mediator provides spectrally selective charging channels: its lower transition enables nearly complete single-excitation transfer with negligible residual mediator energy, whereas initial mediator loading activates a cross-resonant two-excitation cascade toward the doubly excited battery state. The latter exhibits a modest reduction in transfer efficiency due to the unequal collective matrix elements of the effective three-state chain.

Conclusion: The resulting scheme combines nonequilibrium resource generation, spectral selectivity, coherent multilevel charging, and thermodynamic cycle closure within a single architecture.

Abstract: We investigate a finite-time quantum-battery charging architecture in which a driven two-level working system transfers nonequilibrium resources to an interacting two-qubit battery through a weakly anharmonic three-level mediator. Finite-time driving generates coherence and ergotropy, while excitation-number conservation organizes the transfer into distinct dynamical sectors.
