The mediator then provides spectrally selective charging channels: its lower transition enables nearly complete single-excitation transfer with negligible residual mediator energy, whereas initial mediator loading activates a cross-resonant two-excitation cascade toward the doubly excited battery state. The latter exhibits a modest reduction in transfer efficiency due to the unequal collective matrix elements of the effective three-state chain. Detuning scans quantify the spectral tolerance, and the full mixed-state dynamics reveals distinct energy- and ergotropy-transfer profiles. Finite-time compression, thermal reset, and switching work close the cycle-level energy balance to numerical precision. The resulting scheme combines nonequilibrium resource generation, spectral selectivity, coherent multilevel charging, and thermodynamic cycle closure within a single architecture.

</details>


### [73] [Logical Operator Decomposition for Distance Analysis of Bivariate Bicycle Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39395)
*Mohammad Rowshan,Simon Devitt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives an algebraic short exact sequence for the Z-logical quotient of bivariate bicycle (BB) quantum codes, yielding an explicit logical basis, a dimension formula, and a componentwise distance identity d_Z=min(d_A,d_C). It proves the annihilator and colon components each contribute k/2 to dimension, establishes lower-bound conditions, and uses a translation-anchored cluster search to determine and enumerate minimum-weight logical operators for six standard BB codes, revealing [[108,8,10]] as the only one with distance attained in a single component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BB codes are a leading finite-length family of quantum LDPC codes, but their minimum distance is typically obtained by numerical search rather than from the defining polynomials. The paper aims to give a structural algebraic understanding of the Z-logical space and its distance, providing provable tools instead of purely numerical determination.

Method: Studies the Z-logical quotient K/S over F_2[x,y]/(x^l-1,y^m-1) and embeds it in a short exact sequence with an annihilator quotient as kernel and a colon quotient as cokernel. Uses the Frobenius structure of the finite group algebra to analyze component dimensions. Introduces a componentwise distance identity and lower-bound conditions based on the nonzero-syndrome property for proper subsets of minimum-weight logical operators. Implements a translation-anchored cluster search to compute distances and enumerate minimum-weight logical operators.

Result: The short exact sequence yields an explicit logical basis, a dimension formula, and the identity d_Z=min(d_A,d_C). For every BB code, including repeated-root cases, r_A=r_C=k/2. The annihilator and colon components can differ in support shape. The search proves distances 4,6,10,10,12,18 for the six standard BB codes of lengths 18 to 288 and enumerates all minimum-weight logical operators; [[108,8,10]] is the only one attaining its distance in one component, with d_C=10 and d_A=12.

Conclusion: The Z-logical quotient of BB codes admits a clean algebraic decomposition into annihilator and colon components with balanced dimensions k/2 each and componentwise distance minimum. This framework replaces numerical distance estimation with provable structure and enables exact enumeration of minimum-weight logical operators, clarifying the geometry of logical operators across standard BB codes.

Abstract: Bivariate bicycle (BB) quantum codes are a prominent finite-length family of quantum low-density parity-check codes, but their minimum distance is usually established numerically rather than read from the defining polynomials. We study the $Z$-logical quotient $K/S$ over $\mathbb F_2[x,y]/(x^\ell-1,y^m-1)$ and show that it fits into a short exact sequence with an annihilator quotient as kernel and a colon quotient as cokernel. The sequence gives an explicit logical basis, a dimension formula, and a componentwise distance identity $d_Z=\min(d_{\mathcal A},d_{\mathcal C})$. Using the Frobenius structure of the finite group algebra, we prove $r_{\mathcal A}=r_{\mathcal C}=k/2$ for every BB code, including repeated-root cases. The algebraic component of a logical class is distinct from the support shape of its lightest representatives: an annihilator class can have a lighter two-block representative, and a colon class can have a one-sided minimum. For lower bounds we show that every proper subset of a minimum-weight logical operator has nonzero syndrome, and that this property persists inside the colon component but not inside the annihilator component. A translation-anchored cluster search built on it proves the distances $4,6,10,10,12,18$ of the six standard BB codes of lengths $18$ to $288$ and enumerates every minimum-weight logical operator. The resulting census shows that $[[108,8,10]]$ is the only one of the six whose distance is attained in a single component, with $d_{\mathcal C}=10$ and $d_{\mathcal A}=12$.

</details>


### [74] [Genuine Multipartite Nonlocality Is Fermionic Magic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39411)
*Jamal Slim*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Free fermions, measured as fermionic qubits, carry at most the Bell nonlocality of a single entangled pair regardless of the number of parties, due to fermion parity; genuine multipartite nonlocality is therefore the resource distinguishing universal fermionic quantum computation from classically simulable free-fermion dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free-fermion systems are classically simulable, whereas adding a single non-Gaussian resource yields universal quantum computation. The paper asks whether the same boundary also demarcates quantum nonlocality, i.e., whether free fermions can exhibit multipartite nonlocal correlations.

Method: Fermionic qubits are used as the measurement framework to quantify Bell nonlocality of free-fermion states, with fermion parity identified as the structural constraint. Certification is performed on 48 qubits of IBM quantum processors.

Result: Free fermions carry no more than the Bell nonlocality of one entangled pair, however many parties share them, and are never genuinely multipartite nonlocal. Genuine multipartite nonlocality is experimentally certified on 48 qubits of IBM processors.

Conclusion: Genuine multipartite nonlocality is the resource of universal fermionic computation, cannot arise from any mean-field state, and is experimentally certified on 48 qubits.

Abstract: A system of free fermions can be simulated on a classical computer, and a single non-Gaussian state makes it a universal quantum computer. We show that the same line bounds quantum nonlocality. Measured as fermionic qubits, free fermions carry the Bell nonlocality of one entangled pair, no more, however many parties share them, and are never genuinely multipartite nonlocal. Fermion parity is the reason. Genuine multipartite nonlocality is thus the resource of universal fermionic computation, cannot arise from any mean-field state, and we certify it on $48$ qubits of IBM processors.

</details>


### [75] [Reservoir- and Measurement-free Microwave Initialization of Semiconductor Spin Qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39415)
*Leon C. Camenzind,Ik Kyeong Jin,Akito Noiri,Kenta Takeda,Takashi Nakajima,Takashi Kobayashi,Seigo Tarucha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors demonstrate reservoir-free and measurement-free initialization of a silicon spin-qubit pair in an industrial Si/SiGe quantum-dot device using a fixed microwave and baseband pulse sequence, reaching 99.4% median singlet-preparation probability in ~12 μs, with projections below 1 μs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalable quantum processors need repeated qubit initialization across large arrays, but fast initialization in semiconductor spin qubits typically relies on local reservoir access or measurement-based feedback, requiring dedicated infrastructure that is hard to distribute at scale. A fixed-sequence, infrastructure-light initialization primitive is therefore needed.

Method: Using an industrially fabricated Si/SiGe quantum-dot device, they initialize a spin-qubit pair with a fixed sequence of microwave and baseband pulses, without a reservoir or measurement feedback. Odd spin-parity states relax to the singlet charge state; blocked even spin-parity states are microwave-driven through the triplet manifold and converted to the singlet via singlet-triplet mixing and charge hybridization. Exchange spectroscopy verifies mapping to the target |↑↓⟩ operational state, while microwave spectroscopy and time-domain measurements identify the dark-state-limited single-cycle transfer and the ensuing blockade-lifting dynamics that set the initialization timescale.

Result: Repeated cycles yield the singlet-associated charge outcome with 99.4% median probability across sampled preparation states. The sequence consumes approximately 12 μs of microwave bursts and mixing dwells, and sub-microsecond initialization is projected under improved device conditions.

Conclusion: Fixed-sequence microwave initialization is established as a scalable control primitive for semiconductor spin-qubit processors, grounded in singlet-triplet physics that can be adapted to platforms possessing suitable Pauli-blockade transitions.

Abstract: Scalable quantum processors require repeated qubit initialization throughout large arrays. In semiconductor spin qubits, fast initialization commonly relies on local reservoir access or measurement-based feedback, requiring dedicated infrastructure that becomes increasingly difficult to distribute as processors scale. Here, we demonstrate reservoir- and measurement-free initialization of a silicon spin-qubit pair in an industrially fabricated Si/SiGe quantum-dot device using a fixed sequence of microwave and baseband pulses. Odd spin-parity states relax to the singlet charge state, whereas blocked even spin-parity states are microwave-driven through the triplet manifold and subsequently converted to the singlet by singlet-triplet mixing and charge hybridization. Repeated cycles produce the singlet-associated charge outcome with a median probability of 99.4% across the sampled preparation states, while exchange spectroscopy independently verifies mapping to the target $|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle$ operational state. Microwave spectroscopy and time-domain measurements identify the dark-state-limited single-cycle transfer and the subsequent blockade-lifting dynamics that set the initialization time scale. The demonstrated pumping sequence uses approximately $12\,μ\mathrm{s}$ of microwave bursts and mixing dwells, while we project sub-microsecond initialization under improved device conditions. These results establish fixed-sequence microwave initialization as a scalable control primitive for semiconductor spin-qubit processors, based on singlet-triplet physics that can be adapted to platforms with suitable Pauli-blockade transitions.

</details>


### [76] [Parallel classical simulation of noisy shallow circuits: no quantum advantage in 1D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39448)
*Robert Koenig,Marco Tomamichel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Noisy constant-depth nearest-neighbor quantum circuits on a line can be efficiently sampled classically and reproduced by randomized AC^0 circuits, ruling out quantum advantage in this one-dimensional setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Noisy shallow quantum circuits in two and three dimensions are known to provide unconditional quantum advantage over classical circuits of the same gate set. The paper asks whether an analogous advantage survives in one dimension, where depolarizing noise may sever long-range correlations.

Method: A randomized parallel sampling algorithm exploiting the fact that depolarizing noise cuts a one-dimensional circuit into independent pieces of logarithmic width, each of which can be sampled exactly in parallel; the approach is also adapted to give an exact sampler in the noiseless case.

Result: The sampler achieves total variation error δ with parallel runtime 2^{O(d)} log log(n/δ) and n 2^{O(d)} real-arithmetic operations; without noise it gives an exact sampler with parallel runtime O(log n). For constant depth, the noisy circuit's input/output behavior is reproduced up to constant error by a randomized AC^0 circuit, so every relation problem solved by a noisy constant-depth one-dimensional quantum circuit is also solved, with essentially the same success probability, by a randomized AC^0 circuit.

Conclusion: For noisy one-dimensional circuits, the unconditional quantum advantage established for noisy shallow circuits in two and three dimensions does not hold; the result sharply delineates the dimensional threshold at which noisy shallow quantum advantage exists.

Abstract: We consider quantum circuits consisting of $d$ layers of nearest-neighbor two-qubit gates acting on $n$ qubits arranged on a line, where every qubit is independently depolarized with a constant probability before each layer. We describe a randomized parallel algorithm which samples from the output distribution of any such circuit to within total variation error $δ$, with parallel runtime $2^{O(d)}\log\log(n/δ)$ and $n 2^{O(d)}$ elementary real-arithmetic operations. Without noise, the same approach gives an exact sampler with parallel runtime $O(\log n)$. For constant depth, we further show that the input/output behavior of the noisy quantum circuit is reproduced up to a constant error by a randomized $\mathsf{AC}^0$-circuit, that is, a Boolean circuit of polynomial size and constant depth with unbounded fan-in AND and OR gates and NOT gates. Consequently, every relation problem solved by a noisy constant-depth quantum circuit in one dimension is also solved, with essentially the same success probability, by a randomized $\mathsf{AC}^0$-circuit. This rules out, for noisy circuits in one dimension, the unconditional quantum advantage established for noisy shallow circuits in two and three dimensions, where polynomial-size classical circuits over the same gate set require depth $Ω(\log n/\log\log n)$. Our algorithm exploits the fact that depolarizing noise cuts a one-dimensional circuit into independent pieces of logarithmic width, each of which can be sampled exactly in parallel.

</details>


### [77] [From Wavefunction Regularity to Eigenvector Conditioning: Accuracy--Cost Trade-offs of Quantum Algorithms for Non-Hermitian Transcorrelated Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39452)
*Cheng-Lin Hong,Emiel Koridon,Paul K. Faehrmann,Thomas D. Kühne,Stefano Polla,Werner Dobrautz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work analyzes how the transcorrelated (TC) method improves basis convergence by incorporating electron-electron Coulomb cusp behavior into the Hamiltonian, and examines the resulting non-Hermitian, non-normal operators' impact on quantum algorithm complexity, using both a solvable model and second-quantized electronic-structure Hamiltonians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The electron-electron Coulomb singularity produces cusp structure in electronic wavefunctions, limiting wavefunction regularity and slowing convergence of expansions in smooth bases, thus requiring large basis sets. The transcorrelated method improves transformed-wavefunction regularity and accelerates convergence, but its resulting Hamiltonian is non-Hermitian and non-normal, restricting standard quantum algorithms whose complexity depends on eigenvector conditioning and eigenvalue sensitivity.

Method: The authors proceed in two stages: (1) study a solvable model to isolate how transcorrelation affects basis convergence and eigenvector conditioning, investigating two distinct one-parameter Jastrow correlators and deriving asymptotic energy-error scaling; (2) extend the analysis to second-quantized electronic-structure Hamiltonians, using particle-number-sector diagnostics to distinguish target-sector from auxiliary-sector conditioning.

Result: Derived asymptotic energy-error scaling: N^{-1/2} for the bare projection and N^{-3/2} for both TC correlators at generic fixed parameters (N = basis size). Also showed that this faster convergence can yield a polynomial improvement in the asymptotic query-complexity upper bound.

Conclusion: The work provides a framework for understanding the central accuracy-cost trade-off in TC Hamiltonians, linking cusp incorporation, basis convergence, eigenvector conditioning, and quantum algorithmic complexity.

Abstract: The electron--electron Coulomb singularity forces cusp structure in electronic wavefunctions. This cusp structure limits wavefunction regularity and slows the convergence of expansions in smooth bases, requiring large basis sets to reach a target accuracy. The transcorrelated (TC) method incorporates this short-range behavior into the Hamiltonian, thereby improving the regularity of the transformed wavefunction and accelerating convergence toward the complete-basis-set limit. The resulting Hamiltonian is non-Hermitian and non-normal, which limits the direct applicability of standard quantum algorithms. For such non-normal operators, the complexity of several recent quantum algorithms depends explicitly on quantities related to eigenvector conditioning and eigenvalue sensitivity. Understanding these quantities is essential for assessing both algorithmic complexity and quantum resource requirements. In this work, we address these issues in two stages. We first study a solvable model, which provides a controlled setting for isolating how transcorrelation affects basis convergence and eigenvector conditioning. Within this setting, we investigate two distinct one-parameter Jastrow correlators. We derive the asymptotic energy-error scaling: $N^{-1/2}$ for the bare projection and $N^{-3/2}$ for both TC correlators at generic fixed parameters, with $N$denoting the basis size. Furthermore, we show that this faster convergence can yield a polynomial improvement in the asymptotic query-complexity upper bound. We then extend the analysis to second-quantized electronic-structure Hamiltonians and use particle-number-sector diagnostics to distinguish target-sector from auxiliary-sector conditioning. Our work provides a framework for understanding the central accuracy--cost trade-off in TC Hamiltonians.

</details>


### [78] [Optimal Scaling of Unitary Design Formation in $U(1)$-Symmetric Random Circuits: A Bottleneck Slower than Charge Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39458)
*Toshihiro Yada*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work determines the optimal scaling of unitary k-design formation rate in U(1)-symmetric random circuits (k ≤ O(√log N)) across lattices, expander graphs, and all-to-all geometries. It shows the rate is governed by a two-particle encounter rate—parametrically slower than single-particle transport—disproving the prior conjecture, and shows the bottleneck can be bypassed with symmetry-breaking gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding randomness formation under conservation laws matters for characterizing chaos in isolated quantum systems and for symmetry-constrained quantum information processing. U(1)-symmetric random circuits offer a minimal tractable setting, where the design formation rate was conjectured to be governed by charge transport. However, whether charge transport sets the slowest relaxation mode remained open because deriving a tight lower bound on the symmetric design formation rate was difficult.

Method: The authors determine the optimal scaling of the unitary k-design formation rate for k ≤ O(√log N) across circuit geometries including lattices, expander graphs, and all-to-all interactions. They identify the governing mechanism as a two-particle encounter rate (rate at which two random walkers meet), and prove it is the actual bottleneck by deriving a matching lower bound using a novel proof technique based on an auxiliary subgroup ensemble.

Result: For k ≥ 2, the design formation rate is governed by the two-particle encounter rate, which is parametrically smaller than the single-particle transport rate in many geometries, disproving the previous conjecture. Matching lower bounds confirm this encounter mechanism sets the rate. The bottleneck is robust for U(1)-symmetric circuits but can be circumvented using symmetry-breaking local gates, for which an explicit faster protocol is provided.

Conclusion: The results establish a new picture of randomness formation under conservation laws—governed by multiparticle encounter dynamics rather than simple charge transport—and provide guiding principles for characterizing and efficiently generating symmetry-constrained randomness.

Abstract: Understanding randomness formation under conservation laws is important both for characterizing chaotic phenomena in isolated quantum systems and for symmetry-constrained quantum information processing. $U(1)$-symmetric random circuits on an $N$-qubit system provide a minimal tractable setting for this problem, in which the formation rate of unitary designs was conjectured to be governed by charge transport in the underlying circuit geometry. However, whether charge transport indeed sets the slowest relaxation mode has remained an open problem, due to the difficulty in deriving a tight lower bound on the symmetric design formation rate. In this work, we resolve this problem by determining the optimal scaling of the unitary $k$-design formation rate for $k\leq O(\sqrt{\log N})$ across a wide range of circuit geometries, including lattices, expander graphs, and all-to-all interactions. In particular, for $k\geq 2$, we find that the formation rate is governed by a two-particle encounter rate---the rate at which two random walkers encounter each other in a given circuit geometry. This encounter rate is parametrically smaller than the single-particle transport rate in many geometries, thereby disproving the previous conjecture. We further establish that this encounter mechanism indeed sets the design formation rate by deriving its matching lower bound with a novel proof technique based on an auxiliary subgroup ensemble. Although this encounter mode imposes a robust bottleneck on design formation in $U(1)$-symmetric random circuits, we also show that this bottleneck can be circumvented by using symmetry-breaking local gates and provide an explicit protocol that achieves faster design formation. These results establish a new picture of randomness formation under conservation laws and provide guiding principles for characterizing and efficiently generating symmetry-constrained randomness.

</details>


### [79] [Excitation gap of a blockade structure with $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topological order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39475)
*Simon Fell,Tobias F. Maier,Hans Peter Büchler,Nicolai Lang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves the existence of a finite excitation (spectral) gap for a Rydberg-motivated two-body blockade Hamiltonian with a topologically ordered toric code ground state, and extends the result to a broader class of blockade Hamiltonians realizing non-Abelian quantum double phases, using gap stability results and local symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematically rigorous proofs of spectral gaps for quantum many-body systems in the thermodynamic limit are notoriously difficult, yet they are fundamentally important for classifying quantum phases of matter. The studied Hamiltonians are experimentally motivated (Rydberg platform) and host topologically ordered ground states, making a rigorous gap statement highly desirable.

Method: The proof builds on known gap stability results and exploits the local symmetry of the studied models.

Result: A finite excitation gap is proven to exist for the particular two-body blockade Hamiltonian proposed by Maier et al. (PRX Quantum 6, 030340 (2025)), whose ground state is topologically ordered in the toric code phase. The result also applies to a broader class of blockade Hamiltonians realizing non-Abelian quantum double phases, proposed by Büchler et al. (Phys. Rev. B 114, 065113 (2026)).

Conclusion: The finite spectral gap is rigorously established (in the thermodynamic limit) for the Rydberg-motivated blockade Hamiltonian and, more generally, for blockade Hamiltonians hosting non-Abelian quantum double phases, providing a rigorous contribution to the classification of quantum phases of matter.

Abstract: Mathematically rigorous statements on the spectral gap of quantum many-body systems in the thermodynamic limit are notoriously difficult to prove -- yet they are of fundamental importance for classifying quantum phases of matter. Here we prove the existence of a finite excitation gap for a particular Hamiltonian which was proposed in [T. F. Maier et al., PRX Quantum 6, 030340 (2025)] and is motivated by the Rydberg platform. The Hamiltonian exhibits only two-body blockade interactions between two-level systems and has a topologically ordered ground state in the toric code phase. We show that our result also applies to a broader class of blockade Hamiltonians which realize non-Abelian quantum double phases and were proposed in [H. P. Büchler et al., Phys. Rev. B 114, 065113 (2026)]. The proof builds on known gap stability results and exploits the local symmetry of the studied models.

</details>


### [80] [Generation of large-amplitude squeezed cat states with near-unity efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39482)
*Simon K. Yung,Matthew S. Winnel,Timothy C. Ralph,Jie Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: An all-optical scheme using quantum non-demolition interactions and photon-number measurements prepares large-amplitude cat states with near-unity probability, requiring no postselection and remaining robust to photon loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill encoding is a leading candidate for fault-tolerant continuous-variable quantum computation. In photonic architectures, the main obstacle is preparing high-quality resource states, which can be deterministically synthesized from many large-amplitude cat states. However, experimentally prepared optical cat states remain modest in size, and existing methods are highly probabilistic.

Method: The authors propose an all-optical scheme that utilizes quantum non-demolition (QND) interactions and photon-number measurements to prepare large-amplitude cat states. Crucially, no particular photon-number outcome is postselected: all outcomes contribute to the accumulated photon number, and the protocol is repeated until a required threshold is reached.

Result: The scheme achieves near-unity probability for preparing large-amplitude cat states and is demonstrated to be robust against realistic levels of photon loss, the dominant error source in optical systems.

Conclusion: The results highlight the power of active Gaussian operations for state preparation and pave the way for efficient quantum error correction using bosonic codes.

Abstract: The Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill encoding has emerged as a leading candidate for fault-tolerant quantum computation with continuous variables. For photonic architectures, the major challenge is preparing high-quality resource states, which can be deterministically synthesised from many large-amplitude cat states. Thus far, only modest-sized optical cat states have been prepared experimentally, and the implemented methods are highly probabilistic. We propose an all-optical scheme utilising quantum non-demolition interactions and photon-number measurements to prepare large-amplitude cat states with near-unity probability. Importantly, no particular photon-number outcome is postselected: all outcomes contribute to the accumulated photon number, with the protocol repeated until a required threshold is reached. We demonstrate that the scheme is robust to realistic levels of photon-loss, the dominant source of error in optical systems. Our results highlight the power of active Gaussian operations for state preparation and pave the way for efficient quantum error correction using bosonic codes.

</details>


### [81] [On quantum interactive proofs with a laconic prover](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39495)
*Zihan Hu,Yupan Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies two-message quantum interactive proof systems with a laconic prover, introducing the class QIP_{ℓ-bit}(2), characterizing it via Multi-State Distinguishability (with QSD as QIP_bit-complete), identifying regimes that collapse to QSZK (including an improved polarization resolving an open problem of Sahai-Vadhan), and showing that quantum public coins with O(√log n) messages and constant gap yield no advantage (collapse to BQP).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactive proofs with laconic provers capture problems verifiable with logarithmic prover communication classically (Goldreich-Vadhan-Wigderson). For two-message quantum analogs, even a single-bit prover response contains QSZK (Watrous), yet restricting the verifier's question to classical public coins collapses the class to BQP (Beigi-Shor-Watrous). The paper aims to map out the intermediate landscape between these extremes and understand when limited quantum prover responses and quantum public coins add power.

Method: Introduces the class QIP_{ℓ-bit}(2), parameterized by the length ℓ of the prover's response in two-message quantum interactive proofs. Establishes a complete characterization via Multi-State Distinguishability (showing QSD is QIP_bit-complete). Proves collapse of QSD[a,b] (and QIP_bit[a,b]) to QSZK when a(n)-b(n) ≥ 1/O(log n). Combines an answer-compression from QIP_{ℓ-bit}[2,c,s] to QIP_bit to derive another easy regime when 2c > (1+2^{ℓ/2})s. Develops an improved polarization technique applicable to SD and SZK. Analyzes qc-QAM[ℓ], a subclass where the verifier's question is exactly halves of EPR pairs.

Result: QSD is QIP_bit-complete, placing QIP_{ℓ-bit}(2) for ℓ≥2 "just above" QSZK (since QSD is QSZK-hard). QSD[a,b] and QIP_bit[a,b] lie in QSZK when a(n)-b(n) ≥ 1/O(log n); another easy regime holds when 2c > (1+2^{ℓ/2})s. The improved polarization resolves an open problem of Sahai and Vadhan for SD and SZK. Quantum public coins make interaction useless: qc-QAM[O(√log n)] with constant gap is contained in BQP.

Conclusion: Two-message quantum interactive proofs with a laconic prover are completely characterized by Multi-State Distinguishability, with QSD as the single-bit complete problem. The class QIP_{ℓ-bit}(2) sits just above QSZK, and several natural parameter regimes collapse to QSZK, yielding an improved polarization that resolves a longstanding open problem. Quantum public coins provably destroy the power of interaction, collapsing qc-QAM with O(√log n) messages to BQP.

Abstract: Interactive proof systems with a laconic prover, studied by Goldreich, Vadhan, and Wigderson (CC, 2002), capture problems verifiable with logarithmic prover communication in the classical setting. For two-message quantum analogs, even a single-bit prover response contains quantum statistical zero-knowledge ($\sf QSZK$), introduced by Watrous (FOCS 2002). However, restricting the verifier's question to classical public coins collapses the corresponding class to $\sf BQP$, as shown by Beigi, Shor, and Watrous (ToC, 2011).
  We further study two-message quantum interactive proof systems with a laconic prover. To this end, we introduce the class ${\sf QIP}_{\ell\text{-}{\rm bit}}(2)$, where $\ell$ is the length of the prover's response, and establish:
  1. A natural complete characterization of ${\sf QIP}_{\ell\text{-}{\rm bit}}(2)$ by Multi-State Distinguishability. In particular, Quantum State Distinguishability (QSD) is ${\sf QIP}_{\rm bit}$-complete. Since QSD is $\sf QSZK$-hard, our result places ${\sf QIP}_{\ell\text{-}{\rm bit}}(2)$, for $\ell\geq 2$, in a landscape "just above" $\sf QSZK$.
  2. Easy regimes for ${\sf QIP}_{\ell\text{-}{\rm bit}}(2)$ collapsing to $\sf QSZK$. We prove that QSD$[a,b]$ (and thus ${\sf QIP}_{\rm bit}[a,b]$) is in $\sf QSZK$ when $a(n)-b(n)\geq 1/O(\log n)$, and combine this with an answer compression from ${\sf QIP}_{\ell\text{-}{\rm bit}}[2,c,s]$ to ${\sf QIP}_{\rm bit}$ to obtain another easy regime when $2c>(1+2^{\ell/2})s$. Remarkably, our improved polarization applies to SD and $\sf SZK$, resolving an open problem in Sahai and Vadhan (JACM, 2003).
  3. Quantum public coins also make the interaction useless: ${\sf qc}\text{-}{\sf QAM}[O(\sqrt{\log{n}})]$ with constant gap is in $\sf BQP$, where ${\sf qc}\text{-}{\sf QAM}[\ell]$ is a subclass of ${\sf QIP}_{\ell\text{-}{\rm bit}}(2)$ in which the verifier's question is exactly halves of EPR pairs.

</details>


### [82] [Quantum double lock-in detection via sequential orthogonal quantum mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39498)
*Min Zhuang,Sijie Chen,Jiahao Huang,Chaohong Lee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a protocol that achieves quantum double lock-in detection using only a single quantum interferometry with sequential orthogonal periodic multipulse sequences, avoiding the resource cost of two separate interferometries while extracting complete amplitude, frequency, and initial phase information. With a GHZ input state and two interaction-based operations, the measurement precisions for all three parameters can approach the Heisenberg limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-precision measurement of oscillating signals is important in fundamental science and practical technology. Quantum double lock-in detection can extract complete amplitude, frequency, and initial phase information when the initial phase is unknown, but conventional implementations require two separate quantum interferometries, each needing state preparation and readout. Since preparation and readout times are non-negligible (preparation often longer than sensing), reducing experimental resource consumption is a key challenge.

Method: The authors present a general protocol for quantum double lock-in detection using a single quantum interferometry under sequential orthogonal periodic multipulse sequences. Specifically, a GHZ input state is used and two interaction-based operations are applied during the interferometry, enabling extraction of amplitude, frequency, and initial phase simultaneously.

Result: The single-interferometry protocol extracts complete information on amplitude, frequency, and initial phase. With a GHZ input state and two interaction-based operations, the measurement precisions for frequency, amplitude, and initial phase can each approach the Heisenberg limit.

Conclusion: The work provides a new route for measuring oscillating signals via a single quantum interferometry and offers a feasible method for Heisenberg-limited detection of alternating signals, saving experimental resources compared with conventional two-interferometry schemes.

Abstract: High-precision measurement of oscillating signal is a ubiquitous issue in fundamental science and a critical task in practical technologies. In quantum metrology, quantum lock-in detection provide an efficient method for measuring such signal. In general, when the initial phase of the oscillating signal is unknown,quantum double lock-in detection can effectively extract complete information about the signal's amplitude, frequency, and initial phase. Conventional quantum double lock-in detection requires two individual quantum interferometry, each of which must undergo state preparation and readout. However, the time for state preparation and readout need not be negligible in practical experiments. In particular, the time for state preparation is longer than the time for sensing. To save experimental resources, it is challenging to achieve quantum double lock-in detection just via a single quantum interferometry while still extracting complete information about the signal's amplitude, frequency, and initial phase. Here, we present a general protocol for achieving a quantum double lock-in detection just via a single quantum interferometry under a sequential orthogonal periodic multipulse sequences. In particular, if the input state is a Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger state and two interaction-based operations are applied during interferometry, the measurement precisions for frequency, amplitude, and initial phase can both approach the Heisenberg limit. Our study paves a new way for measuring oscillating signals with a single quantum interferometry, and provides a feasible method for achieving Heisenberg-limited detection of alternating signals.

</details>


### [83] [Layer codes as quantum memories: syndrome extraction, thresholds and idle robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39522)
*Zhu Sun,Zhenyu Cai*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work evaluates layer codes—3D local CSS codes built by quasi-concatenating a CSS input code with surface-code patches—as active memories under circuit-level noise, introducing a decoder-free syndrome-extraction scheduler and comparing them to surface codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior knowledge of layer codes characterizes the code itself, not the circuit that measures it. To assess them as active memories under circuit-level noise, a syndrome extraction circuit is needed, but the junction stabilizers coupling patches preclude reusing a surface code patch's CNOT schedule.

Method: A decoder-free scheduler that constrains hook propagation first and compresses depth afterwards; circuits generated for eighteen layer codes across four input codes; benchmarked against same-distance surface codes simulated and decoded identically; modeling of beyond-2D cross-plane CNOT costs.

Result: No distance loss to hook errors on [[4,2,2]]-input codes where a circuit-distance proof is affordable; lower logical error rate than a general-purpose CSS scheduler at roughly half its CNOT depth per round; primary [[4,2,2]]-input family reaches circuit-level threshold 5.06(7)×10⁻³; 1.7–3.0× longer tolerable idle interval than rotated surface code at equal distance, at the cost of 1.7–5.0× more operations per unit time and ~5× more physical qubits per logical qubit; cross-plane CNOT speed matters far more than fidelity.

Conclusion: Layer codes are viable active memories under circuit-level noise, outperforming rotated surface codes in idle robustness at equal distance, though at substantially higher operational and qubit overhead; their practical viability hinges primarily on the speed rather than the fidelity of the required beyond-2D cross-plane CNOTs.

Abstract: Layer codes are three-dimensional local CSS codes of check weight at most six, obtained by quasi-concatenating a CSS input code with unrotated surface code patches. What is established about them describes the code, not the circuit that measures it. In this work, we evaluate them as active memories under circuit-level noise. Doing so first requires a syndrome extraction circuit, and the junction stabilizers that couple the patches rule out borrowing a surface code patch's CNOT schedule. We present a decoder-free scheduler that constrains hook propagation first and compresses depth afterwards, generate circuits for eighteen layer codes across four input codes, and compare them with surface codes of the same distance, simulated and decoded in the same way. The circuits lose no distance to hook errors on [[4,2,2]]-input codes wherever a circuit-distance proof is affordable, and reach a lower logical error rate than those of a general-purpose scheduler for CSS codes at roughly half its CNOT depth per round. Our primary family built from a [[4,2,2]] input code reaches a circuit-level threshold of $5.06(7)\times 10^{-3}$. The layer code outperforms the surface code in terms of idle robustness: at the same distance it tolerates an interval between syndrome extraction rounds 1.7-3.0 times longer than the rotated surface code. This comes at the cost of 1.7-5.0 times more operations per unit time and approximately five times more physical qubits, both per logical qubit. Finally, we model the cost of the beyond-2D cross-plane CNOTs that the construction demands, and find that their speed matters far more than their fidelity.

</details>


### [84] [Optimal local recovery cannot alter critical orthogonality exponents in quantum spin environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39528)
*Zhixuan Zhao,Jun Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves local amplitude-ratio bounds and reverse fidelity comparisons for ground states under erasure, with an exact lattice result (amplitude exponent 1/8) for a critical Ising chain that is robust under optimal control on fixed spin sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize how much local, finite-region information about positive ground states can be recovered or bounded under erasure, and to test whether stronger projected-state inferences hold.

Method: Analytical proofs of a local amplitude-ratio bound and an explicit reverse fidelity comparison; an exact lattice calculation for a connected critical Ising chain; derivation of the finite Cauchy product and uniform strip asymptotics; exact two-spin optimization and an interferometric recovery-measurement protocol; a finite-mediator theorem for local compression with an exact environment decoder.

Result: Amplitude exponent 1/8, preserved by arbitrary optimal control on any fixed spin set and, for logarithmic exponents, on o(log N) spins; exact two-spin optimization results; a finite-mediator compression/decoder theorem; a ground-preparation Ramsey bound; counterexamples to stronger projected-state inferences; independent full-Hamiltonian calculations.

Conclusion: Local ground-state recoverability obeys sharp amplitude-ratio and fidelity bounds, with the Ising exponent 1/8 stable under optimal control, while stronger projected-state claims fail and are corroborated by exact full-Hamiltonian calculations.

Abstract: We prove a local amplitude-ratio bound for positive ground states and an explicit reverse comparison for fidelity under finite-region erasure. An exact lattice calculation for a connected critical Ising chain gives amplitude exponent $1/8$, preserved by arbitrary optimal control on any fixed spin set and, at the level of logarithmic exponents, on $o(\log N)$ spins. We derive the finite Cauchy product and its uniform strip asymptotics, and document exact two-spin optimization and an interferometric protocol for measuring the recovery. A separate finite-mediator theorem quantifies local compression and a decoder while retaining the rest of the environment exactly. We also give a ground-preparation Ramsey bound, counterexamples to stronger projected-state inferences, and independent full-Hamiltonian calculations.

</details>


### [85] [Boron vacancies in bulk h-BN created by high-energy He+ irradiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39535)
*P. G. Vilyuzhanina,S. V. Bolshedvorskii,R. Isayev,A. S. Doroshkevich,A. A. Tatarinova,V. V. Soshenko,I. S. Cojocaru,A. M. Kozodaev,S. M. Drofa,A. Chernyavskiy,N. I. Salangin,A. N. Smolyaninov,A. Y. Kuntsevich,A. V. Akimov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Negatively charged boron vacancy color centers in bulk h-BN are fabricated via ~1 MeV helium ion implantation, confirmed by photoluminescence and ODMR with a 180 MHz resonance linewidth and expected Zeeman shift; measured center depth agrees with SRIM predictions within uncertainties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Color centers in 2D materials are rapidly developing with promise for high-tech applications, but reliable creation of boron vacancies in hexagonal boron nitride (h-BN)—a center already showing magnetometry potential—remains challenging.

Method: Implantation of helium ions at ~1 MeV into bulk h-BN to fabricate negatively charged boron vacancy color centers, characterized by photoluminescence and optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR); center depth along the c-axis was measured and compared with Stopping and Range of Ions in Matter (SRIM) modeling.

Result: Characteristic photoluminescence and ODMR confirm the color centers; the resonance has a width of 180 MHz and exhibits the expected Zeeman shift of the resonance lines. The measured depth and modeled prediction are consistent within reported uncertainties (overlapping 1σ intervals), though the experiment shows a greater depth and wider distribution.

Conclusion: Helium ion implantation at ~1 MeV reliably creates negatively charged boron vacancy color centers in bulk h-BN, with depth consistent with SRIM predictions, supporting their use for applications such as magnetometry.

Abstract: While color centers in diamond and other three-dimensional crystals are nowadays key elements of several types of sensors, color centers in two-dimensional materials are rapidly developing and promise high-tech applications. One of the interesting centers in 2D materials is the negatively charged boron vacancy in hexagonal boron nitride (h-BN), which has already shown some potential for magnetometry, but the reliable creation of boron vacancies remains challenging. Here, we demonstrate the fabrication of negatively charged boron vacancy color centers in bulk h-BN via implantation of helium ions at ~1 MeV, as confirmed by characteristic photoluminescence and optically detected magnetic resonance. The resonance has a width of 180 MHz and exhibits the expected Zeeman shift of the resonance lines. The depth of the color center along the c-axis of the h-BN crystal was measured and compared with predictions from Stopping and Range of Ions in Matter modeling. The experimental value and the modeled prediction are consistent within the reported uncertainties, as their 1σ intervals overlap, although the experiment shows a greater depth and a wider distribution.

</details>


### [86] [Exact Maximum Likelihood Decoding beyond Treewidth via Rank-Decomposition Dynamic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39556)
*Bin Cheng,Feng Pan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Rank DP, a rank-decomposition dynamic-programming decoder for maximum-likelihood quantum error correction that exploits algebraic structure to achieve polynomial arithmetic complexity in the Tanner graph's rank-width, outperforming standard tensor-network contraction on punctured Reed-Muller and Steane-concatenated codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maximum-likelihood decoding is optimal for quantum error correction under stochastic Pauli noise, but computing logical-class probabilities is hard. The leading exact tensor-network contraction method scales exponentially in treewidth, making exact decoding intractable for many codes. This motivates seeking alternative algorithms that exploit algebraic structure for greater efficiency.

Method: The authors express decoding partition functions, with independent local fault factors, as quadratic sums of powers, then apply rank-decomposition dynamic programming (Rank DP). This yields an exact evaluator whose arithmetic complexity is polynomial in input size and exponential in the Tanner graph's rank-width relative to the fault partition, including decomposition construction cost. A nonnegative realization provides relative floating-point error bounds under explicit arithmetic assumptions.

Result: After Gaussian elimination, Rank DP gives polynomial-time decoding for punctured quantum Reed-Muller codes and a Steane-concatenated family with growing distance under independent single-qubit Pauli noise, whereas standard tensor-network contraction requires superpolynomial time. Numerical experiments show runtime advantages over tested tensor-network implementations on selected code-capacity and circuit-level instances, with full likelihood evaluation for Reed-Muller codes up to 1,023 qubits.

Conclusion: Rank DP exploits algebraic structure to enable efficient exact maximum-likelihood quantum decoding and is used to learn circuit noise parameters from syndromes, evaluate rare postselection probabilities, and quantify decoder optimality gaps. The work opens new avenues for exploiting algebraic structure in quantum decoding and noise characterization.

Abstract: Maximum-likelihood decoding provides an optimal decoding strategy for quantum error correction under stochastic Pauli noise. However, computing logical-class probabilities is challenging, and the leading exact tensor-network contraction requires time exponential in treewidth. In this work, we introduce a new decoding algorithm based on rank-decomposition dynamic programming (Rank DP). We express decoding partition functions with independent local fault factors as quadratic sums of powers and apply Rank DP. The resulting exact evaluator has arithmetic complexity polynomial in the input size and exponential in the Tanner graph's rank-width with respect to the fault partition, including decomposition construction. After Gaussian elimination, it gives polynomial-time decoding for punctured quantum Reed-Muller codes and a Steane-concatenated family with growing distance under independent single-qubit Pauli noise. Standard contraction of the corresponding tensor networks requires superpolynomial time. A nonnegative realization gives relative floating-point error bounds under explicit arithmetic assumptions. Numerical experiments demonstrate runtime advantages over the tested tensor-network implementations on selected code-capacity and circuit-level instances, with full likelihood evaluation for Reed-Muller codes up to $1{,}023$ qubits. We further use these likelihoods to learn circuit noise parameters from syndromes, evaluate rare postselection probabilities, and quantify decoder optimality gaps. Our work opens new avenues for exploiting algebraic structure in quantum decoding and noise characterization.

</details>


### [87] [Rate 1/5 Non-Malleable Codes against Entangled Split-State Tampering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39589)
*Naresh Goud Boddu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs efficient information-theoretic non-malleable codes for classical messages, secure against two noncommunicating local quantum tampering operations with arbitrary pre-shared entanglement, achieving constant rate at least 1/5−ξ with perfect correctness and negligible error, thereby resolving the constant-rate question for worst-case classical messages in the entangled two-split-state model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The constant-rate question for worst-case (as opposed to uniformly random) classical messages in the entangled two-split-state non-malleable code model remained open. Prior work by Batra, Boddu, and Jain achieved rate approaching 1/5 only for uniformly random messages, leaving worst-case security unresolved.

Method: The construction retains the permutation-based two-split architecture of Batra, Boddu, and Jain. The uniform message input to the permutation is replaced with a prescribed message concatenated with fresh uniform padding. The main technical contribution is a worst-case security reduction for this modification, yielding a single message-independent simulator per attack.

Result: For every sufficiently small fixed ξ>0 and all sufficiently large first-share lengths n, the codes achieve rate at least 1/5−ξ, perfect correctness, and error 2^{−n^{Ω(1)}}. Security holds for every message, with a single message-independent simulator for each attack.

Conclusion: The work resolves the constant-rate question for worst-case classical messages in the entangled two-split-state model, establishing efficient information-theoretic non-malleable codes at constant rate 1/5 with security against quantum tampering with pre-shared entanglement.

Abstract: We construct efficient information-theoretic non-malleable codes for classical messages that are secure against two noncommunicating local quantum tampering operations with arbitrary pre-shared entanglement. For every sufficiently small fixed $ξ>0$ and all sufficiently large first-share lengths $n$, the codes have rate at least $1/5-ξ$, perfect correctness, and error $2^{-n^{Ω(1)}}$. Security holds for every message, with a single message-independent simulator for each attack. This resolves the constant-rate question for worst-case classical messages in the entangled two-split-state model.
  Our construction builds on the permutation-based two-split construction of Batra, Boddu, and Jain, which achieves rate approaching $1/5$ for uniformly random messages. We retain their architecture but replace the uniform message input to the permutation with a prescribed message concatenated with fresh uniform padding. Our main contribution is a worst-case security reduction for this modification.

</details>


### [88] [The Complexity of Single-Interaction Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39592)
*Elad Hecker*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Single-Interaction Hamiltonians (SIH), where all local terms are identical unweighted interactions, and shows that this restriction retains essentially full Local Hamiltonian complexity via an exact compiler reducing k-LH to SIH_{3k+3}.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Local Hamiltonian problems allow independently chosen local matrices and tunable coupling strengths, leaving open whether this flexibility is necessary for Hamiltonian-complexity phenomena. The authors ask how much complexity survives when every local term is the same fixed, unweighted interaction.

Method: The core technical tool is an exact compiler converting any fixed finite alphabet of local interactions into a single interaction: on a designated auxiliary subspace it reproduces the source Hamiltonian exactly while preserving the complete sub-separation-scale spectrum with multiplicity. This is combined with a finite-alphabet normalization to yield a universal polynomial-time reduction from k-Local Hamiltonian to SIH_{3k+3}, up to a known global energy scale and inverse-polynomial approximation. Sharper constructions are developed for small k, geometric locality, and the stoquastic setting.

Result: QMA-completeness of SIH_3 and of geometrically local SIH_8 with bounded degree and interaction range; StoqMA-completeness of Stoq-SIH_3 and QMA-completeness of 1-Pinned Stoq-SIH_4; hardness results for uniformly system-size-dependent interactions; and single-interaction formulations of the Hamiltonian quantum PCP conjecture, frustration-free, exponentially precise, and guided Local Hamiltonian problems.

Conclusion: Independently chosen local matrices and independently tunable coupling strengths are not necessary for a broad range of Hamiltonian-complexity phenomena: a single fixed unweighted interaction suffices to reproduce them.

Abstract: We study the complexity of Local Hamiltonian problems under the restriction that every local term is an unweighted occurrence of the same fixed interaction. In the resulting Single-Interaction Hamiltonian (SIH) model, once the interaction is fixed, the Hamiltonian is specified entirely by its ordered interaction hypergraph.
  Our main technical tool is an exact compiler that converts any fixed finite alphabet of local interactions into a single interaction. On a designated auxiliary subspace, the compiled Hamiltonian reproduces the source Hamiltonian exactly, while the complete spectrum below a chosen separation scale is preserved with multiplicity. Combined with a finite-alphabet normalization, this gives a universal polynomial-time reduction from $k$-Local Hamiltonian to SIH$_{3k+3}$ up to a known global energy scale and inverse-polynomial approximation.
  Sharper constructions give QMA-completeness of SIH$_3$ and of geometrically local SIH$_8$ with bounded degree and interaction range. In the stoquastic setting, we obtain StoqMA-completeness of Stoq-SIH$_3$ and QMA-completeness of 1-Pinned Stoq-SIH$_4$. We also establish hardness results for uniformly system-size-dependent interactions and derive single-interaction formulations of the Hamiltonian quantum PCP conjecture, frustration-free, exponentially precise, and guided Local Hamiltonian problems. These results show that independently chosen local matrices and independently tunable coupling strengths are not necessary for a broad range of Hamiltonian-complexity phenomena.

</details>


### [89] [Thermodynamics of Ahn--Doherty--Landahl Continuous Quantum Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39597)
*Juan Garcia-Nila,Pedro B. Melo,Lucas Johns*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work analyzes continuous quantum error correction (CQEC) as an information engine, separating system-side and controller-side power. Applied to the ADL one-qubit protocol and the three-qubit repetition code, it shows that stronger feedback improves steady-state fidelity and lowers conditional entropy, while both energetic costs keep growing after fidelity saturates—revealing a tradeoff between logical stabilization and thermodynamic resource cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CQEC replaces discrete syndrome measurements and recovery with continuous syndrome extraction and real-time Hamiltonian feedback, yet the thermodynamic resources this process consumes remain unclear. The paper aims to quantify these resources and identify whether improving logical stabilization carries an unavoidable energetic penalty.

Method: The authors formulate measurement-based CQEC as an information engine, distinguishing system-side power (energy transfer between feedback field and protected system) from controller-side power (associated with the feedback Hamiltonian). The framework is developed first for the one-qubit Ahn-Doherty-Landahl (ADL) protocol and then extended to the three-qubit repetition code under continuous stabilizer monitoring, using numerical simulations.

Result: Increasing feedback strength raises steady-state fidelity and reduces conditional-state entropy. However, both the system-side and controller-side energetic contributions continue to grow in magnitude even after fidelity saturates, indicating diminishing returns in fidelity per unit of energetic cost.

Conclusion: There is a direct tradeoff between logical stabilization and the energetic resources required for continuous feedback, closely related to established energy–precision tradeoffs in quantum measurement and quantum-Zeno stabilization.

Abstract: Continuous quantum error correction (CQEC) replaces discrete syndrome measurements and recovery operations with continuous syndrome extraction and real-time Hamiltonian feedback. Here we investigate the thermodynamic resources required by this process by formulating measurement-based continuous quantum error correction as an information engine. We distinguish the system-side power associated with energy transfer between the feedback field and the protected system from the controller-side power associated with the feedback Hamiltonian. The framework is first developed for the one-qubit Ahn-Doherty-Landahl (ADL) protocol and subsequently extended to the three-qubit repetition code under continuous stabilizer monitoring. Numerical simulations show that increasing the feedback strength improves the steady-state fidelity and reduces the conditional-state entropy, while both energetic contributions continue to increase in magnitude after the fidelity begins to saturate. These results expose a direct tradeoff between logical stabilization and the energetic resources required for continuous feedback, closely related to previously established energy--precision tradeoffs in quantum measurement and quantum-Zeno stabilization.

</details>


### [90] [Warm-Start Iterative QITE for Distribution Network Reconfiguration via Branch-Exchange Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39602)
*Noah Crum,Pete Pritchard,Tanner Rase,Phillip C. Lotshaw,Shaked Regev,Claudio Girotto,Ananth Kaushik,Paul Smith*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A hybrid quantum-classical algorithm for distribution network reconfiguration combines radiality-preserving branch-exchange encoding with iterative warm-start quantum imaginary-time evolution, guided by a surrogate model, to minimize active line losses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distribution network reconfiguration is a combinatorial optimization problem on power distribution networks, and the paper aims to minimize active line losses using a quantum-classical hybrid approach.

Method: The encoding uses a fixed-width binary representation of sequential branch-exchange actions so every register outcome decodes to a radial configuration. The quantum subroutine uses iterative warm-start quantum imaginary-time evolution and is informed by a surrogate model fit to classically-solved ACPF labels. The algorithm returns the lowest-loss configuration after a set budget of ACPF solves and circuit samples. Two methods reduce problem size for larger networks.

Result: Across ten test cases (seven benchmarks and three modified standard systems), the algorithm reaches minimal-loss configurations for six cases and finds configurations 0.2% to 2.18% above the reported reference minimal loss for the other four.

Conclusion: The hybrid quantum-classical approach is effective for distribution network reconfiguration and, with problem-size reduction methods, can be extended to larger networks.

Abstract: We present a hybrid quantum-classical algorithm for distribution network reconfiguration, a combinatorial optimization problem on power distribution networks, that combines a radiality preserving branch-exchange encoding with iterative warm-start quantum imaginary-time evolution to minimize active line losses. The encoding uses a fixed-width binary representation of sequential branch-exchange actions, ensuring that every register outcome decodes to a radial configuration. The quantum subroutine is informed by a surrogate model fit to classically-solved alternating current power flow (ACPF) labels. After a set budget of ACPF solves and circuit samples is exhausted, the algorithm returns the lowest-loss configuration seen. We employ our algorithm on ten test cases, including seven commonly-used benchmark cases and three cases we modified from these and similar standard systems. We include two methods to reduce problem size, which enable extensions of the algorithm to larger networks. We successfully reach minimal-loss configurations for six cases and find configurations with losses from 0.2 percent to 2.18 percent above the reported reference minimal loss for the other four.

</details>


### [91] [Weakly Measured Loops for Quantum Amplitude Amplification: Oracle Savings and Adaptive Search with Unknown Target Probability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39621)
*Wang Fang,Chris Heunen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces quantum amplitude amplification algorithms that interleave standard Grover rotations with tunable weak measurements inside loops. Failed measurements resume from the post-measurement state, and the authors derive leading expected Grover iteration costs for known, bounded, and completely unknown target probabilities, achieving provable oracle savings over standard Grover.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Grover search and its restart-based optimisations incur a fixed constant cost, and it is desirable to reduce the expected number of oracle calls while retaining the Θ(1/√p) scaling. The paper is motivated by the idea that weak measurements can provide a fixed-point control mechanism and provable oracle savings beyond ordinary amplitude amplification.

Method: The authors analyse a family of quantum amplitude amplification algorithms that interleave ordinary Grover rotations with tunable weak measurements in a loop. A successful measurement stops the algorithm at the target state, while a failure resumes from the post-measurement state without restarting. They derive leading expected Grover iteration costs as p→0 for three settings: (i) known target probability p, using an exact weak-measurement-conditioned loop near angle π/4; (ii) a known lower bound 0<p0≤p<1/2, using a discrete Lyapunov equation to obtain exact expected oracle cost and a closed-form optimal measurement strength; and (iii) completely unknown p, using the critical scale κ(t)=Θ(1/t) and analysing schedules κ_b(t)=min{1/2,b/t}.

Result: For known p near angle π/4, the algorithm uses (π/8+1/4+o(1))/√p expected iterations, improving on the standard Grover constant π/4 and optimised restart Grover, and matching the continuous-angle infimum. For a known lower bound p0, the expected Grover iterations are at most (1/(2√2))(1/√p+1/√p0), with the leading constant 1/√2 at the promise boundary p=p0. For completely unknown p, for each fixed b>2 the expected iterations are (C(b)/2+o(1))/√p, where C(b) has a closed-form Gamma-function expression; numerical minimisation gives b≈5.2 and C(b)/2≈1.01.

Conclusion: Weak measurements in amplitude amplification preserve the Θ(1/√p) search scale while adding an explicit fixed-point control mechanism and provable oracle savings. The approach improves the leading constant over standard and optimised restart Grover, and provides rigorous expected-cost expressions for known, bounded, and unknown target probabilities.

Abstract: We present a family of quantum amplitude amplification algorithms that interleave ordinary Grover rotations with tunable weak measurements in loops. A successful measurement yields the target state and stops the algorithm, while after a failure, the loop resumes from the post-measurement state without restarting. We express the leading costs in expected Grover iterations as $p \to 0$. For a known target probability $p$, an exact weak measurement-conditioned loop for states near the angle $π/4$ uses $(π/8+1/4+o(1))/\sqrt{p}$ iterations, improving on the standard Grover constant $π/4$ and on optimised restart Grover, and matching the corresponding infimum in our continuous-angle analysis. When only a lower bound $0 < p_0 \leq p < 1/2$ is available, a discrete Lyapunov equation gives the exact expected oracle cost for every fixed measurement strength and a closed-form optimal strength. Using the strength selected from $p_0$, the expected number of Grover iterations is at most $\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_0}})$, where the expected cost decreases as the actual $p$ increases, and gives the leading constant $1/\sqrt{2}$ at the promise boundary $p = p_0$. For completely unknown $p$, we identify measurement strength $κ(t)=Θ(1/t)$ as the critical scale within the regular schedules considered here and analyse $κ_b(t)=\min\{1/2,b/t\}$. For each fixed $b > 2$, we rigorously derive a closed-form Gamma-function expression $C(b)$ giving $(C(b)/2+o(1))/\sqrt{p}$ expected iterations. Numerical minimisation of this explicit expression gives $b \approx 5.2$ and $C(b)/2 \approx 1.01$. The results show that weak measurements preserve the $Θ(1/\sqrt{p})$ search scale while adding an explicit fixed-point control mechanism and provable oracle savings.

</details>


### [92] [Phase-sensitive avalanche quantum sensing of sub-shot-noise fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39650)
*Nikolai D. Klimkin,Misha Ivanov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Trapped atomic clouds in photonic cavities can amplify sub-shot-noise fluctuations of a cavity field by two orders of magnitude via a non-perturbative avalanche-like mechanism, while preserving the phase information of the original weak light wave.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Avalanche-based detection is standard for sensing small perturbations in precision measurement devices, but extending this principle to sensing light waves below the quantum shot noise limit remains a challenge. Conventional detection protocols lack a broadly tunable amplification mechanism that also preserves phase information, motivating a new approach for quantum state characterization and weak classical signal detection.

Method: The authors numerically model atomic clouds trapped in photonic cavities whose cavity mode is tuned to a parity-prohibited transition. When pumped by a continuous-wave laser, the atomic cloud emits radiation that non-perturbatively amplifies the initially sub-shot-noise cavity field fluctuations.

Result: The atomic cloud amplifies sub-shot-noise fluctuations by two orders of magnitude. The amplification is broadly tunable in frequency and independent of atomic energy structure. Notably, the mechanism preserves the phase imparted on the atoms by the original ultra-weak light wave, a qualitative improvement over conventional protocols.

Conclusion: This cavity-atom avalanche mechanism enables sub-shot-noise sensing with preserved phase information, with implications for both quantum state characterization and detection of weak classical signals.

Abstract: Avalanche-based detection of small perturbations is commonplace in precision measurement devices from Geiger counters to single-photon avalanche detectors. Here, we expand this principle to sensing of light waves below the quantum shot noise limit, demonstrating numerically how atomic clouds trapped in photonic cavities can exhibit non-perturbative sensitivity to changes in the cavity field when the cavity mode is tuned to a parity-prohibited transition. We find that, when pumped by a continuous-wave laser, the atomic cloud creates emissions which amplify the initially sub-shot-noise fluctuation by two orders of magnitude. Crucially, this amplification is broadly tunable with respect to frequency and independent of atomic energy structure. Even more strikingly, our amplification mechanism preserves the phase imparted on the atoms by the original ultra-weak light wave, marking a qualitative improvement over conventional protocols. Our finding has implications for both quantum state characterization and detection of weak classical signals.

</details>


### [93] [QMA(2) with Limited Shared Entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39668)
*Alex Della Schiava,Ranitha Mataraarachchi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The power of QMA(2) with up to O(log n) shared EPR pairs equals plain QMA(2); establishing this for n^ε would settle an open problem, and LOCC-prep variants behave analogously.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aaronson et al. (ToC 2009) introduced QMA(2;h), where two provers may share h EPR pairs, and left open how the EPR budget affects computational power—specifically whether bounded entanglement between provers changes the class and whether equality with QMA(2) can be extended to larger budgets.

Method: The main result is proved by a simulation using four unentangled witnesses, together with the Harrow-Montanaro equality QMA(4)=QMA(2). Monotonicity QMA(2;h)⊆QMA(2;H) for h≤H is shown while preserving completeness and soundness, and input padding is used to relate subpolynomial EPR budgets to all polynomially bounded budgets. The results are extended to a variant where provers may use local operations and classical communication (LOCC) during witness preparation.

Result: QMA(2;h)=QMA(2) for h=O(log n), so logarithmically many shared EPR pairs do not increase the class's power. Monotonicity in the EPR budget holds with preserved completeness and soundness, and combined with input padding, proving QMA(2;n^ε)=QMA(2) for any fixed ε>0 would imply equality for every polynomially bounded budget, resolving Aaronson et al.'s open problem. In the LOCC variant with logarithmic-size witnesses and inverse-polynomial gaps, both models remain equivalent to the unentangled counterpart when h=O(log n); extending this equivalence to any superlogarithmic EPR budget would imply NP⊆BQP.

Conclusion: Logarithmic shared entanglement between provers is powerless in QMA(2) (and in its LOCC-prep variant under restricted witness size and gap), whereas closing the gap for larger budgets carries significant complexity-theoretic consequences—resolving Aaronson et al.'s open problem in the general case, or implying NP⊆BQP in the LOCC case.

Abstract: A $\mathsf{QMA}(2)$ protocol involves two provers submitting unentangled witnesses to a polynomial-time quantum verifier. In The Power of Unentanglement (ToC, 2009), Aaronson et al. proposed $\mathsf{QMA}(2;h)$, a variant of $\mathsf{QMA}(2)$ in which the two provers may share $h$ EPR pairs. Our main result shows that the power of $\mathsf{QMA}(2)$ remains unchanged for up to logarithmically many shared EPR pairs: $\mathsf{QMA}(2;h)=\mathsf{QMA}(2)$ for $h=O(\log n)$, where $n$ is the input length. The result follows from a simulation using four unentangled witnesses, combined with the Harrow-Montanaro equality $\mathsf{QMA}(4)=\mathsf{QMA}(2)$ (FOCS, 2010).
  We also prove monotonicity in the EPR budget: $\mathsf{QMA}(2;h)\subseteq\mathsf{QMA}(2;H)$ for $h\le H$, preserving completeness and soundness. Combined with input padding, this shows that establishing $\mathsf{QMA}(2;n^\varepsilon)=\mathsf{QMA}(2)$ for any fixed $\varepsilon>0$ would imply equality for every polynomially bounded budget, resolving the open problem raised by Aaronson et al.
  Finally, we extend these results to a variant of the model in which the provers may use local operations and classical communication (LOCC) during witness preparation. For logarithmic-size witnesses and inverse-polynomial gaps, both models remain equivalent to their unentangled counterpart when $h=O(\log n)$. We show that extending this equivalence to any superlogarithmic EPR budget in the LOCC model would imply $\mathsf{NP}\subseteq\mathsf{BQP}$.

</details>


### [94] [Separable decompositions of 2xn states with operator Schmidt rank three](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39669)
*Perrine Vantalon,Nicolas Macris*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Every bipartite state of a qubit coupled to an n-level system with operator Schmidt rank three can be decomposed into rank(ρ) pure product states, and into at most n+1 mixed product states, with the latter bound being tight and both decompositions obtained constructively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to determine the minimal number of terms (length) needed to decompose bipartite quantum states into product states, a central problem in entanglement theory and quantum information. Existing results for pure product state decompositions are recovered, while the new framework extends to decompositions into mixed product states, filling a gap in the understanding of such decompositions.

Method: The proof is constructive. It employs eigendecompositions of suitable unitary dilations of an operator that is explicitly determined by the state ρ. This framework yields explicit decomposition procedures for both pure and mixed product states.

Result: For bipartite states of a qubit coupled to an n-level system with operator Schmidt rank three: (1) the state can be written as a mixture of rank(ρ) pure product states; (2) the state can be written as a mixture of at most n+1 mixed product states. The second bound is tight: there exist states that cannot be decomposed with fewer terms. The pure product decomposition obtained differs from previously known ones.

Conclusion: The work provides constructive decompositions of bipartite states with operator Schmidt rank three into pure and mixed product states, recovers known length results for pure product states, establishes a tight bound of n+1 for mixed product states, and offers a unified framework that handles both cases.

Abstract: We prove that every bipartite state $ρ$ of a qubit coupled to an $n$-level system whose operator Schmidt rank equals three can be written as a mixture of $\text{rank}(ρ)$ pure product states, and as a mixture of at most $n+1$ mixed product states in general. The second result is tight in the sense that there exist states that cannot be decomposed with a smaller number of terms. Our proof gives constructive methods to obtain the decompositions. It involves eigendecompositions of suitable unitary dilations of an operator explicitly determined by the state. Our framework recovers previously known results for the length of decompositions into pure product states and is also able to deal with decompositions into mixed states. For pure product states, our decomposition is different from existing ones.

</details>


### [95] [Polylog-depth Quantum Thermal Simulation via Local Recovery Channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39674)
*Hideaki Hakoshima,Atsushi Iwaki,Nobuyuki Yoshioka*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives sufficient conditions for preparing thermal states of noncommuting local Hamiltonians with polylogarithmic-depth circuits in fixed spatial dimension, using locality/stability bounds, a high-temperature condition, coarse classical reference Hamiltonians, and spatially localized Petz recovery maps, achieving N·polylog(N/ε) gate and qubit complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Connecting two fundamental questions: how correlations are organized in thermal equilibrium, and how efficiently such states can be realized by quantum operations. The goal is to give equilibrium locality a constructive computational interpretation and a physically grounded role in quantum algorithm design.

Method: Sufficient conditions are established by combining locality and stability bounds on effective interactions of reduced density matrices of a Gibbs state, a quantitative high-temperature condition, and access to coarse classical reference Hamiltonians. When references can be generated locally, classical preprocessing costs N^{1+o(1)}. The preparation circuit is constructed from spatially localized Petz recovery maps, where quantum corrections from the microscopic Hamiltonian enable accurate recovery while classical references remain coarse.

Result: After preprocessing, the circuit prepares the canonical purification using N·polylog(N/ε) gates and qubits, achieving inverse-polynomial global accuracy, where ε is the preparation error.

Conclusion: The results translate static equilibrium structure into explicit preparation circuits, giving equilibrium locality a constructive computational interpretation and a physically grounded role in quantum algorithm design.

Abstract: We establish sufficient conditions for preparing quantum thermal states of noncommuting local Hamiltonians with polylogarithmic circuit depth in arbitrary fixed spatial dimension. Our conditions combine locality and stability bounds on the effective interactions of reduced density matrices of a Gibbs state with a quantitative high-temperature condition and access to coarse classical reference Hamiltonians. When these references can be generated locally, the classical preprocessing cost is $N^{1+o(1)}$ for $N$ sites at inverse-polynomial global accuracy. We construct the preparation circuit from spatially localized Petz recovery maps. Quantum corrections derived from the microscopic Hamiltonian enable accurate recovery while the classical references remain coarse. After preprocessing, the resulting circuit prepares the canonical purification using $N\operatorname{polylog}(N/\varepsilon)$ gates and qubits, where $\varepsilon$ is the preparation error. These results link two fundamental questions: how correlations are organized in thermal equilibrium, and how efficiently the corresponding states can be realized through operations allowed by quantum mechanics.
