By translating static equilibrium structure into explicit preparation circuits, they give equilibrium locality a constructive computational interpretation and a physically grounded role in quantum algorithm design.

</details>


### [96] [Scaling Laws of Quantum Networks: An Entanglement Transport Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39690)
*Jaemin Kim,Petar Popovski*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "entanglement transport" as a distance-weighted fidelity-constrained metric and a two-layer framework (resource and physical layers) to analyze how entanglement-distribution performance scales with network nodes N and active users M.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As quantum networks move toward multi-user architectures, it remains unclear how entanglement-distribution performance scales with node count N and active users M, because existing studies use inconsistent objectives, resource assumptions, and normalizations that prevent fair comparison.

Method: The authors define entanglement transport, weighting each delivered Bell pair meeting a fixed fidelity threshold by endpoint separation. They propose a two-layer framework: (i) a resource layer measuring capacity per activation slot under different aggregation regimes, and (ii) a physical layer measuring service per second under implementation constraints, assuming matched implementations with a bounded number of activation slots per second.

Result: On expanding honeycomb lattices with one random session, fixed-window entanglement-percolation constructions achieve Θ(N^{-1}) entanglement transport per activation slot below their thresholds and Θ(√N) above them. For homogeneous chains, asymptotic aggregation exceeds fixed-window capacity by a factor Θ(N^2/M), and in spatial expansion a noisy physical implementation achieves Θ(M/N) service per second.

Conclusion: Entanglement-transport scaling depends not only on connectivity but also on resource aggregation and physical implementation. The two-layer framework provides a common basis for establishing quantum-network scaling laws.

Abstract: As quantum networks move toward multi-user architectures, a central question is how the performance of entanglement distribution scales with the numbers of network nodes $N$ and active users $M$, yet existing studies use different objectives, resource assumptions, and normalizations. To account for delivery distance under multi-user demand, we define entanglement transport, which weights each delivered Bell pair meeting a fixed fidelity threshold by its endpoint separation. In a two-layer framework, (i) a resource layer measures capacity per activation slot under different aggregation regimes, and (ii) a physical layer measures service per second under implementation constraints. For matched implementations with a bounded number of activation slots per second, asymptotic-aggregation capacity sets a service ceiling, whereas achievable service must be established separately. On expanding honeycomb lattices with one random session, fixed-window entanglement-percolation constructions achieve $Θ(N^{-1})$ entanglement transport per activation slot below their thresholds and $Θ(\sqrt N)$ above them. For homogeneous chains, asymptotic aggregation exceeds fixed-window capacity by a factor $Θ(N^2/M)$, and in spatial expansion a noisy physical implementation achieves $Θ(M/N)$ service per second. These examples show that entanglement-transport scaling depends not only on connectivity but also on resource aggregation and physical implementation. The framework provides a common basis on which quantum-network scaling laws can be established.

</details>


### [97] [Quantum--classical break-even in electronic dynamics of macrocyclic molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39693)
*Shu Kanno,Kenji Sugisaki,Takashi Imamichi,Toshinari Itoko,Rei Sakuma,Hajime Nakamura,Qi Gao,Naoki Yamamoto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates quantum-classical break-even for chemical dynamics, using a tensor-network circuit compression workflow on IBM QPUs to simulate long-time electronic dynamics of periodic 1D systems (12-qubit benzene, 120-qubit [60]annulene), achieving QPU wall-clock times comparable to estimated classical HPC resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemical dynamics applications require simulating long-time electronic evolution of large systems that are classically hard. The goal is to determine whether quantum hardware can reach a wall-clock-time regime comparable to practical classical computing resources, i.e., quantum-classical break-even for chemically relevant problems.

Method: A quantum workflow for electronic dynamics based on tensor-network circuit compression exploiting spatial locality, extending circuit compression beyond one-dimensional open-boundary systems. Target systems are one-dimensional periodic Hamiltonians: circuits classically optimized only for short-time evolution within a small spatial region are replicated in space and time to build long-time full-system dynamics without classically simulating the large-scale evolution. Applied to rotationally periodic cyclic conjugated macrocycles (12-qubit benzene, 120-qubit [60]annulene) embedded onto loops of IBM quantum processors.

Result: For hole-doped nonequilibrium dynamics in [60]annulene, QPU wall-clock times became comparable to estimated wall-clock times of two classical methods—Majorana propagation and MPS time evolution via the time-dependent variational principle—corresponding to tens to hundreds of compute nodes (36 CPU cores per node) assuming near-ideal parallel scaling. A situation is also shown where a hybrid quantum-classical calculation is reasonable in both accuracy and computational time.

Conclusion: Quantum hardware can become a realistic computational option for chemically relevant electronic dynamics, reaching useful accuracy at wall-clock times comparable to conventional parallel HPC resources.

Abstract: Here we demonstrate quantum--classical break-even for chemical dynamics applications, reaching useful accuracy with a quantum-hardware wall-clock time comparable to that estimated for practical classical computing resources. Specifically, we construct a quantum workflow for electronic dynamics based on tensor-network circuit compression leveraging spatial locality, extending circuit compression beyond one-dimensional open-boundary systems. This enables application of the workflow to problems that are hardware-native but classically hard. The workflow targets one-dimensional periodic Hamiltonians: circuits classically optimized only for short-time evolution within a small spatial region can be replicated in both space and time to construct long-time dynamics of the full system without classically simulating the corresponding large-scale evolution. We apply the workflow to cyclic conjugated macrocycles with rotational periodicity, 12-qubit benzene and 120-qubit [60]annulene, by embedding their electronic structure onto loops of IBM quantum processors. For the annulene, the quantum-processing-unit (QPU) wall-clock times for hole-doped nonequilibrium dynamics became comparable to the estimated wall-clock time of two classical methods, Majorana propagation and matrix-product-state time evolution based on the time-dependent variational principle; this corresponds to tens to hundreds of compute nodes depending on the task (36 CPU cores per node), assuming near-ideal parallel scaling, bringing the quantum calculation into a wall-clock-time regime comparable to conventional parallel high-performance-computing resources. We also show a situation in which a hybrid quantum--classical calculation is a reasonable choice in terms of both accuracy and computational time. These results show that quantum hardware can become a realistic computational option for chemically relevant electronic dynamics.

</details>


### [98] [The Intrinsic Cost of Quantum Syndrome Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39705)
*Chia-Tung Chu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces an intrinsic measure of the interaction required for syndrome extraction derived directly from the stabilizer group, yields hardware-dependent lower bounds on exact syndrome-extraction depth for arbitrary stabilizer codes, and gives a tight space–depth tradeoff for a quantum Tanner family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work (Delfosse–Beverland–Tremblay 2021; Baspin–Fawzi–Shayeghi 2023) established bounds linking syndrome extraction to locality constraints on quantum hardware. However, those bounds depend on the chosen generating set of the stabilizer code rather than the code itself, and in some cases are either incorrect or not tight. A generating-set-independent measure is needed to correctly characterize the fundamental interaction cost of syndrome extraction under given hardware connectivity.

Method: The authors formulate an intrinsic measure of the interaction required for syndrome extraction directly from the stabilizer group, independent of the generating set used to describe the code. Using this measure, they derive hardware-dependent lower bounds on exact syndrome-extraction depth for arbitrary stabilizer codes. They also construct annular surface-code examples with constant-depth local syndrome extraction to test prior results, and analyze a fixed quantum Tanner family to strengthen stated depth bounds and establish an asymptotically tight space–depth tradeoff.

Result: The intrinsic measure yields hardware-dependent lower bounds on exact syndrome-extraction depth for arbitrary stabilizer codes. The annular surface-code construction with constant-depth local syndrome extraction provides a counterexample to Corollary 1 of DBT21. For a fixed quantum Tanner family, the measure strengthens the depth bound explicitly stated in BFS23 and, combined with a matching construction, establishes an asymptotically tight space–depth tradeoff.

Conclusion: Theoretically, the results connect the information required by syndrome extraction to the communication capacity of a device; practically, they offer a way to assess unavoidable circuit costs before committing to a hardware layout.

Abstract: How much time is fundamentally required to extract the syndrome of a stabilizer code on hardware with a given connectivity graph? Previous work by Delfosse--Beverland--Tremblay (2021) and Baspin--Fawzi--Shayeghi (2023) established bounds connecting syndrome extraction to locality constraints on quantum hardware. Here, we address this question by first formulating an intrinsic measure of the interaction required for syndrome extraction directly from the stabilizer group, independent of the generating set used to describe the code. We then show that this measure yields hardware-dependent lower bounds on exact syndrome-extraction depth for arbitrary stabilizer codes. Guided by this measure, we also construct annular surface-code examples with constant-depth local syndrome extraction that provide a counterexample to Corollary 1 of DBT21. Furthermore, for a fixed quantum Tanner family, this measure strengthens the depth bound explicitly stated in BFS23 and, together with our matching construction, establishes an asymptotically tight space--depth tradeoff. In theory, our results connect the information required by syndrome extraction to the communication capacity of a device; while in practice, they provide a way to assess unavoidable circuit costs before committing to a hardware layout.

</details>


### [99] [Complex Quantum Dynamics Versus Classical Simulability of Noisy Random Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39734)
*Anjali Waghmare,Sergii Strelchuk,Sathyawageeswar Subramanian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: In noisy Clifford+T and matchgate+SWAP circuits, dynamical diagnostics (magic, scrambling, anticoncentration, non-Gaussianity) and classical simulability can decouple in both directions, so these diagnostics alone do not prove classical hardness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Claims of quantum advantage rely on the classical hardness of simulating quantum circuits, and diagnostics like magic, operator scrambling, anticoncentration, and non-Gaussianity are standard indicators of complex quantum dynamics. While some diagnostics are rigorously linked to classical simulability for pure states, it is unclear whether they reliably track the limits of efficient classical simulation under noise.

Method: The authors analyze noisy Clifford+T and nearest-neighbour matchgate+SWAP circuits, comparing dynamical diagnostics against classical simulability. They trace the observed mismatch to the distinct statistical properties probed: magic and scrambling depend on fourth-order statistics of the Pauli spectrum, whereas simulation algorithms depend primarily on second-order moments, which local noise suppresses at different rates.

Result: Dynamical diagnostics and classical simulability separate in both directions: diagnostics can remain nontrivial after classical simulation becomes efficient, or become trivial before known efficient classical algorithms apply.

Conclusion: Dynamical diagnostics measured on a noisy quantum device do not by themselves constitute evidence for classical hardness.

Abstract: Claims of quantum advantage rest on the classical hardness of simulating quantum circuits. Magic, operator scrambling, anticoncentration, and non-Gaussianity for fermionic circuits are standard diagnostics of complex quantum dynamics. For pure states, some of these have been rigorously connected to classical simulability. Whether these diagnostics reliably track the limits of efficient classical simulation under noise remains unclear. Here, we show that in noisy Clifford+$T$ and nearest-neighbour matchgate+SWAP circuits, dynamical diagnostics and classical simulability can separate in both directions. In particular, the diagnostics can remain nontrivial after classical simulation becomes efficient, or become trivial before known efficient classical algorithms apply. We trace this mismatch to the different statistical properties they probe: magic and scrambling depend on fourth-order statistics of the Pauli spectrum, whereas the simulation algorithms depend primarily on second-order moments, which local noise suppresses at different rates. Thus, dynamical diagnostics measured on a noisy quantum device do not by themselves constitute evidence for classical hardness.

</details>


### [100] [What makes a causal loop consistent?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39735)
*Hippolyte Dourdent,Andreas Leitherer,Emanuel-Cristian Boghiu,Kyrylo Simonov,Ravi Kunjwal,Antonio Acín*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A deterministic classical process is logically consistent (probability-valid under arbitrary independent local interventions) if and only if it contains no signaling loops—cyclic signaling is forbidden, cyclic causation is allowed—which is equivalent to its list of events being pairwise exclusive and complete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal loops are often dismissed as paradoxical. Previous work sought an intervention-free criterion for consistency but that criterion missed global loops, so a correct, foundation-level characterization of when causal loops yield valid probabilities was needed.

Method: The authors analyze deterministic classical processes under arbitrary independent local interventions and characterize logical consistency in terms of the presence or absence of signaling loops, relating it to event-list structure (pairwise exclusivity and completeness) and to a multipartite form of Specker's principle.

Result: Logical consistency holds exactly when the process contains no signaling loops, equivalently when its event list is pairwise exclusive and complete. This yields the first event-based, intervention-free, non-recursive characterization of consistent causal loops, repairs the earlier intervention-free criterion, and shows that consistency of deterministic classical communication without causal order rests on a multipartite Specker principle: if any two of several questions can be answered jointly, so can all.

Conclusion: Cyclic causation (indefinite causal order) is compatible with logical consistency as long as cyclic signaling is absent, giving an event-based, intervention-free, non-recursive account of consistent causal loops and linking this consistency to a multipartite Specker principle.

Abstract: Causal loops escape paradoxes if they generate valid probabilities under arbitrary independent local interventions. We show that a deterministic classical process is logically consistent if and only if it contains no signaling loops: cyclic signaling is forbidden but cyclic causation is not, allowing indefinite causal order. Logical consistency is then found to hold exactly when the list of events of a process is pairwise exclusive and complete, providing the first event-based, intervention-free, non-recursive characterization of consistent causal loops. Our results repair a previously introduced intervention-free criterion that misses global loops and reveal that what makes deterministic classical communications without causal order consistent is a multipartite form of Specker's principle: if any two of several questions can be answered jointly, so can all of them.

</details>


### [101] [A depolarizing choir sings in Gaussian harmony](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39747)
*Rabsan Galib Ahmed,Sujeet Bhalerao,Sungjai Lee,Felix Leditzky,Debbie Leung,Luke Schaeffer,Graeme Smith*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the noise threshold for positive quantum capacity of the qubit depolarizing channel by relating it, in the asymptotic many-use limit on symmetric subspaces, to an emergent bosonic Gaussian channel. Codes optimized for the simpler Gaussian channel are translated back to the depolarizing channel, yielding improved lower bounds on the threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The noise threshold for positive quantum capacity of the qubit depolarizing channel is a central open problem in quantum information. Improved bounds and a better structural understanding of the relevant symmetric codes are needed.

Method: Analytically examine the action of the qubit depolarizing channel on symmetric subspaces of the input qubits in the limit of asymptotically many channel uses; show the emergence of a bosonic Gaussian channel; translate existing depolarizing-channel codes to the emergent Gaussian channel, optimize them there, then map them back.

Result: The emergent Gaussian channel admits easier code optimization. Translating the optimized codes back to the depolarizing channel produces extremely good input states for the coherent information, yielding new (improved) lower bounds on the noise threshold for positive capacity.

Conclusion: A novel link between depolarizing noise and Gaussian channels is established, leading to improved lower bounds on the threshold and offering a new perspective on the structure of symmetric codes for the depolarizing channel.

Abstract: We study the noise threshold for positive quantum capacity for the qubit depolarizing channel. We explore analytically the action of the qubit depolarizing channel on the symmetric subspaces of the input qubits, in the limit of asymptotically many uses of the channel. We observe the emergence of a bosonic Gaussian channel. Furthermore, the codes previously developed for the depolarizing channel can be translated to codes for the emergent Gaussian channel, and it is easier to further optimize these codes for the simpler emergent Gaussian channel. Translating these codes back to the depolarizing channel leads to extremely good input states for the coherent information of the depolarizing channel producing new lower bounds on the noise threshold for positive capacity. In addition to improved lower bounds on the threshold, this newly found link between depolarizing noise and Gaussian channels offers a novel perspective contributing to our understanding of these symmetric codes.

</details>


### [102] [Theory of spacetime quantum fault tolerance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39758)
*Yijia Xu,Yixu Wang,Zi-Wen Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified algebraic theory of Clifford spacetime quantum fault tolerance using a spacetime chain complex derived from tensor networks, subsuming existing fault-tolerance formalisms and recovering known constructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum fault tolerance is described by several seemingly separate formalisms—spacetime codes, Gottesman's gadget framework, fault complexes, and ZX calculus—and handles fault models (phenomenological vs. circuit-level) in disconnected ways. A unifying algebraic foundation is needed to connect these perspectives and link spacetime distances to gadget-level correctness.

Method: The authors construct a spacetime chain complex from a tensor network representation of a circuit, encoding fault propagation, detector syndromes, and logical actions via the associated gauge and stabilizer structure. Different tensor decompositions and fault assignments yield different fault models within one algebraic framework.

Result: The theory (1) unifies spacetime codes, Gottesman's gadget framework, fault complexes, and ZX calculus; (2) connects spacetime distances to gadget-level correctness properties; (3) accommodates both phenomenological and circuit-level fault models within a common framework; and (4) recovers known constructions and results as special cases, including repeated syndrome extraction, correlated detectors for transversal and fold-transversal gates, Steane- and Knill-type syndrome extraction gadgets, and single-shot error correction.

Conclusion: The proposed spacetime chain complex provides a single algebraic language that unifies diverse formulations of Clifford fault tolerance and recovers established fault-tolerance schemes as special cases, offering a coherent foundation for universal fault-tolerant quantum computation.

Abstract: We present a unified algebraic theory of Clifford spacetime quantum fault tolerance, which underpins universal fault-tolerant quantum computation. We construct a spacetime chain complex from a tensor network representation of a circuit that encodes fault propagation, detector syndromes, and logical actions through the associated gauge and stabilizer structure. We show that this theory (1) unifies various formulations of quantum fault tolerance including spacetime codes, Gottesman's gadget framework, fault complexes, and ZX calculus; (2) connects spacetime distances to the gadget-level correctness properties in Gottesman's formalism; (3) naturally accommodates a range of fault models, in particular phenomenological and circuit-level models, bringing these often separately formulated descriptions into a common algebraic framework through different tensor decompositions and fault assignments; and (4) recovers many known fault tolerance constructions and results as special cases, including repeated syndrome extraction, correlated detectors for transversal and fold-transversal gates, Steane- and Knill-type syndrome extraction gadgets, and single-shot error correction.

</details>


### [103] [Measurement and feedforward circuits from quantum error correcting codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39764)
*Georgios Styliaris,Rahul Trivedi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: All measurement-and-feedforward protocols on shallow circuits correspond to quantum error-correcting codes: the circuit is an encoder, and unitary feedforward removes post-selection iff measurement projectors are detectable errors on the codespace. For stabilizer codes with Pauli measurements, nonstabilizerness comes entirely from the encoder; non-Pauli measurements or non-additive codes enable long-range nonstabilizerness with only single-qubit corrections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Measurements and feedforward are known to enhance shallow quantum circuits, enabling deterministic global unitaries and long-range entangled state preparation, but a unified theoretical understanding of such protocols was lacking. The paper seeks a general framework that characterizes when and how these protocols work, and clarifies the origin of their non-Clifford power.

Method: The authors establish a correspondence between measurement-and-feedforward protocols and quantum error-correcting codes: the pre-measurement circuit is interpreted as an encoder, and unitary feedforward is shown to eliminate post-selection if and only if the measurement projectors are detectable errors on the codespace. This reduces both state preparation and global unitary implementation to a code-design problem. They then analyze stabilizer codes under Pauli measurements, and extend beyond this setting using non-Pauli measurements or non-additive codes.

Result: For stabilizer codes with Pauli measurements, the resulting operation can be non-Clifford, yet its nonstabilizerness is shown to originate entirely from the encoder. Using non-Pauli measurements or non-additive codes, the authors construct protocols that generate long-range nonstabilizerness while requiring only single-qubit unitary corrections.

Conclusion: Measurement-and-feedforward protocols are equivalent to code-design problems via an encoder/detectable-error correspondence. The nonstabilizerness of Pauli-measurement stabilizer protocols is fully attributed to the encoder, a limitation that can be overcome with non-Pauli measurements or non-additive codes, yielding long-range nonstabilizerness with minimal (single-qubit) feedforward corrections.

Abstract: Measurements and feedforward enhance the power of shallow quantum circuits, enabling the deterministic implementation of global unitary operations and the preparation of long-range entangled states. We establish a general correspondence between all such protocols and quantum error-correcting codes: the circuit preceding the measurements acts as an encoder, and unitary feedforward can eliminate post-selection if and only if the measurement projectors are detectable errors on the codespace. This correspondence provides a common framework for state preparation and implementation of global unitaries with measurements and feedforward, turning both into a code-design problem. For stabilizer codes with Pauli measurements, although the resulting operation can be non-Clifford, we show that its nonstabilizerness originates entirely from the encoder. We overcome this restriction using non-Pauli measurements or non-additive codes, constructing protocols that generate long-range nonstabilizerness while requiring only single-qubit unitary corrections.

</details>


### [104] [Double Localization for Quantum Gibbs Sampler Gaps: From an Abstract Framework to Finite-Group Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39802)
*Ryu Hayakawa,Angus Southwell,Caesnan M. G. Leditto,Kuo-Chin Chen,Min-Hsiu Hsieh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "double localization," a framework that proves spectral-gap lower bounds for quantum Gibbs samplers by combining a global estimate on a model-defined subspace (interface localization) with local quantum gap estimates (geometric localization), and applies it to plaquette-free quantum-double Hamiltonians on finite 3-regular graphs of girth ≥ 6, obtaining size-independent gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Proving spectral gaps of quantum Gibbs samplers (e.g., Davies dynamics) is hard because the generator couples global and local behavior: standard approaches often require the "interface" subspace to be invariant under the generator, and existing results typically yield gap guarantees that are either non-constructive or degrade with system size. The authors want a framework that separates global and local analysis to obtain explicit, size-independent lower bounds without invariance assumptions.

Method: Double localization, combining (i) geometric localization—updates supported in small spatial regions—and (ii) interface localization—a model-defined subspace of observables that is geometrically global. An abstract gap theorem merges a global bound on the interface subspace with local quantum gap estimates under quantitative coupling and geometric assembly conditions, yielding an explicit lower bound on the full dynamics' gap without requiring interface invariance. This lets global estimates (including classical comparison) combine with local control of remaining quantum directions. Applied to Hamiltonians from vertex terms of Kitaev's finite-group quantum double construction (no plaquette terms) on finite simple 3-regular graphs with girth ≥ 6, under specified local Davies dynamics.

Result: An unconditional spectral-gap lower bound by a positive constant independent of graph size, for every fixed nontrivial finite group and each fixed inverse temperature β with 0 ≤ βJ < log(5/3), where J > 0 is the coupling strength. For the smallest non-Abelian group S₃, double localization gives a positive lower bound uniform in graph size over the entire interval 0 ≤ βJ ≤ log 3—a guarantee not directly obtainable from existing results.

Conclusion: Splitting the analysis into global (interface) and local (geometric) components enables explicit, size-independent spectral-gap bounds for quantum Gibbs samplers even when the interface is not generator-invariant. Model-specific finite calculations (as for S₃) can extend spectral-gap guarantees for the full quantum dynamics beyond what generic methods provide.

Abstract: We introduce double localization, a framework for proving spectral gaps of quantum Gibbs samplers through two complementary operations: geometric localization, which selects updates supported in small spatial regions, and interface localization, which focuses on a model-defined subspace of observables while remaining geometrically global. Our abstract gap theorem combines a global bound on this subspace, called the interface, with local quantum gap estimates under quantitative coupling and geometric assembly conditions. The resulting explicit lower bound controls the gap of the full dynamics without requiring the interface to be invariant under the generator. This separation allows global estimates, including classical comparison, to be combined with local control of the remaining quantum directions.
  We apply the framework to Hamiltonians built from the vertex terms of Kitaev's finite-group quantum double construction, without plaquette terms, on finite simple three-regular graphs of girth at least six. For the specified local Davies dynamics, we prove an unconditional spectral-gap lower bound by a positive constant independent of graph size for every fixed nontrivial finite group and each fixed inverse temperature $β$ with $0\leβJ<\log(5/3)$, where $J>0$ is the coupling strength. For the smallest non-Abelian group $S_3$, double localization yields a positive lower bound uniform in both graph size and the entire interval $0\leβJ\le\log 3$, providing a guarantee that does not follow directly from existing results. This demonstrates how model-specific finite calculations can extend spectral-gap guarantees for the full quantum dynamics.

</details>


### [105] [Sharp Quantum Entropy Mixing Rates and the Operator Layer Cake Theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39814)
*Alexander Stottmeister*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves the optimal constant c=1 in the matrix inequality conjectured by Mariën–Audenaert–Van Acoleyen–Verstraete, which resolves Bravyi's small incremental mixing conjecture and yields sharp bounds on entropy mixing and entangling rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The von Neumann entropy change rate of a quantum ensemble under Hamiltonian evolution is controlled by Bravyi's small incremental mixing conjecture. Its proof was reduced to a commutator matrix inequality $\|[A,\log B]\|_1 \le c\,h_2(p)$ with conjectured optimal constant c=1, which remained open; a related Lieb–Vershynina conjecture for general ensembles was also at stake.

Method: The author proves the commutator inequality using an exact integral representation of $[A,\log B]$ derived from the operator layer cake theorem (due to Cheng and Liu).

Result: The optimal constant c=1 is established, giving a sharp dimension-independent limit on how fast unitary evolution of one component can change the entropy of a binary quantum ensemble. Consequently, mixing rates satisfy small incremental mixing with the optimal constant, entangling rates of bipartite Hamiltonians are bounded by $(2\log d+1)\|H\|$, and a conjecture of Lieb and Vershynina for mixing rates of general ensembles is corrected.

Conclusion: Bravyi's small incremental mixing conjecture holds with the optimal constant c=1, and the result provides sharp, dimension-independent bounds on entropy mixing and entangling rates, while correcting the Lieb–Vershynina conjecture.

Abstract: At what rate does the von Neumann entropy of an ensemble of quantum states change under Hamiltonian evolution of its constituents? Bravyi proposed the small incremental mixing conjecture controlling the mixing rate of a binary ensemble $\{(1\!-\!p,ρ_1),(p,ρ_2)\}$ by $c\,\|{H}\|h_{2}(p)$ (with the binary entropy $h_{2}$). The proof of Bravyi's conjecture was subsequently reduced to the matrix inequality $\|[A,\log B]\|_{1}\!\leq\! c\,h_{2}(p)$ for positive trace-class operators $A\!\leq\! B$ with $\text{Tr} A\!=\!p$ and $\text{Tr} B\!=\!1$ on separable Hilbert spaces by Mariën, Audenaert, Van Acoleyen, and Verstraete, and they conjectured the optimal constant to be $c\!=\!1$. Here, I prove the latter conjecture using an exact integral representation of the commutator $[A,\log B]$ obtained from the operator layer cake theorem, due to Cheng and Liu. The result gives a sharp dimension-independent limit on how rapidly unitary evolution of one component can change the entropy of a binary quantum ensemble. It follows that mixing rates satisfy small incremental mixing with the optimal constant, and entangling rates of bipartite Hamiltonians are bounded by $(2\log d\!+\!1)\|H\|$. Moreover, the result corrects a conjecture by Lieb and Vershynina for mixing rates of general ensembles.

</details>


### [106] [Learning Random Quantum Circuits and the Emergence of Pseudorandomness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39821)
*Srinivasan Arunachalam,Qizhao Huang,Makrand Sinha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: An efficient algorithm learns brickwork random quantum circuits (depth d, n sites, random 2ℓ-qubit gates) from copies of U|0^n⟩ in poly(n,2^{ℓd}) time, i.e., polynomial when ℓd = O(log n), matching the pseudorandomness threshold in 1D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior circuit-learning approaches suffer from a bottleneck: identifying gates requires learning their entire backward light cones. The paper aims to learn random quantum circuits efficiently and to clarify the ℓd = ω(log n) pseudorandomness threshold for ancilla-free pseudorandom states.

Method: A local correlation criterion identifies gates in the final layer without learning their full backward light cones, avoiding the previous bottleneck. The key technical ingredient is a Carbery-Wright-type anticoncentration inequality for low-degree polynomials of Haar random unitaries, with a small-ball exponent independent of the matrix dimension, which enables handling gates of growing locality.

Result: For a depth-d brickwork circuit on n sites with random 2ℓ-qubit gates, the algorithm recovers U with high probability in poly(n,2^{ℓd}) time for every constant-dimensional lattice, and is polynomial-time whenever ℓd = O(log n). In 1D this reaches the natural pseudorandomness boundary (pseudorandom states need ℓd = ω(log n)); in higher dimensions the same scale plausibly remains the ancilla-free pseudorandomness threshold.

Conclusion: The work gives an efficient learner for brickwork random quantum circuits up to the ℓd = O(log n) regime, matching the pseudorandomness boundary in 1D and clarifying conditions under which pseudorandomness arises. The dimension-independent anticoncentration inequality is of independent interest.

Abstract: We give an efficient algorithm for learning $k$-dimensional brickwork random quantum circuits using only copies of the output state obtained by applying $U$ to the all-zero input. For a depth-$d$ circuit on $n$ sites with random $2\ell$-qubit gates, the algorithm learns the original circuit $U$ with high probability in $\text{poly}(n,2^{\ell d})$ time for every constant dimensional lattice. In particular, the algorithm is polynomial time as long as $\ell d = O(\log n)$. In one dimension, this reaches the natural boundary suggested by pseudorandomness: pseudorandom states require $\ell d=ω(\log n)$, and structured cryptographic constructions suggest that this scale may be achievable from above. In higher dimensions, it remains plausible that the same $\ell d=ω(\log n)$ scale remains the threshold for ancilla-free pseudorandomness, and our results help clarify the conditions under which pseudorandomness can arise in this setting.
  The main idea behind our algorithm is a local correlation criterion that identifies gates in the final layer without learning their entire backward light cones, avoiding a bottleneck in the previous approaches. A key technical ingredient is a Carbery-Wright type anticoncentration inequality for low-degree polynomials of Haar random unitaries whose small-ball exponent is independent of the matrix dimension. The dimension-independent exponent is crucial for handling gates of growing locality. This anticoncentration result may also be of independent interest.

</details>


### [107] [Improved Quantum Random Self-Reduction for Linear Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39823)
*Vahid R. Asadi,Shuichi Hirahara,Nobutaka Shimizu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a uniform quantum random self-reduction for linear maps over finite fields that computes $Mx$ in $\widetilde{O}(nT^{1/3})$ time given a faulty oracle, improving prior $\widetilde{O}(n^{3/2}+T)$ work by avoiding explicit learning of the Bogolyubov--Ruzsa subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior reductions for linear problems with an average-case oracle (Asadi et al., SODA 2024) required explicitly learning the Bogolyubov--Ruzsa subspace, which is computationally expensive. The goal is a faster, uniform reduction that avoids this bottleneck while tolerating a constant fraction of incorrect oracle answers.

Method: Given coherent oracle access to $\mathcal{O}$ (agreeing with $x\mapsto Mx$ on an $\varepsilon$-fraction of inputs) and coherent entry access to $M$, the authors construct a uniform quantum random self-reduction. It leverages the Bogolyubov--Ruzsa subspace from additive combinatorics without explicit learning, decomposes inputs into sparse pieces, and finds a vector outside this subspace via quantum search based on amplitude amplification, yielding a tunable query/verification tradeoff.

Result: The reduction computes $Mx$ on any prescribed input with probability at least $2/3$ in time $\widetilde{O}(nT^{1/3})$ for $n\le T\le n^{3/2}$ (constant field size and $\varepsilon$), where $T$ is the cost of one coherent query. When $T=\widetilde{O}(n)$, it runs in $\widetilde{O}(n^{4/3})$, improving the previous $\widetilde{O}(n^{3/2}+T)$.

Conclusion: The work achieves a faster uniform quantum self-reduction for linear maps over finite fields by sidestepping explicit subspace learning, offering a tunable tradeoff between oracle query cost and matrix-vector verification cost.

Abstract: We study quantum random self-reductions for linear problems over finite fields. Let $M\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}$ be an arbitrary matrix, and let $\mathcal{O}$ be an oracle that agrees with the linear map $x\mapsto Mx$ on an $\varepsilon$-fraction of inputs $x\sim\mathbb{F}^n$. Given coherent access to $\mathcal{O}$ and coherent entry access to $M$, we give a uniform quantum reduction that computes $Mx$ on any prescribed input $x$ with probability at least $2/3$ in time $\widetilde{O}(nT^{1/3})$, for $n\le T\le n^{3/2}$ and constant field size and $\varepsilon$, where $T$ is the cost of one coherent query to $\mathcal{O}$. In particular, when $T=\widetilde{O}(n)$, the reduction runs in time $\widetilde{O}(n^{4/3})$, improving the $\widetilde{O}(n^{3/2}+T)$ reduction of Asadi, Golovnev, Gur, Shinkar, and Subramanian (SODA 2024).
  Our reduction uses the Bogolyubov--Ruzsa subspace guaranteed by additive combinatorics, but it avoids learning this subspace explicitly, which was computationally expensive for the previous reduction; in particular, it does not recover a basis for its orthogonal complement. The main technical step is to decompose the inputs into sparse pieces and find a vector that lies outside the Bogolyubov--Ruzsa subspace via a quantum search based on amplitude amplification. This yields a tunable tradeoff between the cost of querying the average-case oracle and the cost of verifying matrix-vector products.

</details>


### [108] [Dynamic distributed quantum sensing of radio-frequency fields via time-bin entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39831)
*Vedansh Nehra,Richard Birrittella,Benjamin Malia,Nicholas J. Barton,Christopher Tison,James Schneeloch,David Hucul,Benjamin Kyle,Erin Sheridan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a discrete-variable distributed quantum sensing framework that uses time-bin entangled qubits instead of polarization-based probes, exploiting their intrinsic temporal structure as a built-in two-time differential sampling reference for RF field sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing protocols based on polarization Bell states are limited to static measurements and cannot coherently sense dynamic RF phase signals. There is a need for frequency-agile, quantum-enhanced distributed RF sensing in DV photonic quantum networks.

Method: The authors replace traditional polarization-based probes with time-bin entangled qubits. The intrinsic temporal structure of time-bin Bell states serves as a tunable, built-in two-time differential sampling reference. By matching the time-bin separation to the RF or intermediate frequency (IF) half-period (antipodal sampling), they coherently map dynamic RF phase signals onto static quantum optical phases. Performance is validated via Monte Carlo simulations.

Result: The protocol provides a 6 dB sensitivity enhancement over one-sample static polarization encodings while preserving the 3 dB per-pair entanglement advantage over the separable standard quantum limit benchmark.

Conclusion: The proposed approach enables RF sensing in DV photonic quantum networks and offers a path toward quantum-enhanced, frequency-agile distributed sensor arrays and broader use of time-domain quantum probe encodings.

Abstract: We propose a novel framework for discrete-variable (DV) distributed quantum sensing, wherein traditional polarization-based probes are replaced with time-bin entangled qubits. We show that the intrinsic temporal structure of time-bin Bell states can be utilized as a tunable, built-in two-time differential sampling reference for sensing radio-frequency (RF) fields. Unlike existing protocols based on polarization Bell states, which are limited to static measurements, our proposed architecture enables the coherent mapping of dynamic RF phase signals onto static quantum optical phases. By matching the time-bin separation to the RF or intermediate frequency (IF) half-period (antipodal sampling), we theoretically demonstrate that our protocol provides a 6 dB sensitivity enhancement over one-sample static polarization encodings while preserving the 3 dB per-pair entanglement advantage over the separable standard quantum limit benchmark. The performance enhancement is validated with Monte Carlo simulations. This approach provides a solution for RF sensing in DV photonic quantum networks, offering a clear path toward quantum-enhanced and frequency-agile distributed sensor arrays and broader utilization of time-domain quantum probe encodings.

</details>


### [109] [All-Microwave Multiqubit Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39834)
*Guanqi Wang,Yao Song,Peng-Bo Li,Xiu-Hao Deng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: An all-microwave cross-resonance scheme realizes single-step multiqubit (n control, m target) gates on frequency-tunable transmons, achieving >99.9% fidelity three-qubit gates in 60 ns and scalable to a five-qubit CXXXX architecture without tunable couplers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity multiqubit gates are essential for reducing circuit depth in near-term processors and fault-tolerant architectures, but realizing multiqubit interactions and entanglement remains a critical challenge, especially since conventional approaches rely on tunable couplers and sequential two-qubit decompositions.

Method: The authors propose an all-microwave scheme for single-step multiqubit gates with n control and m target qubits on frequency-tunable superconducting transmon networks. Using cross-resonance (CR) drives, they induce effective two-body ZX interactions stronger than conventional resonant regimes, avoiding tunable couplers. The effective Hamiltonian is derived analytically via quad frame rotation and rigorous block diagonalization to identify optimal parameter regimes that enhance target couplings while suppressing parasitic higher-order terms such as ZXX interactions. They then apply numerical simulations and gradient-based pulse-shape optimization.

Result: Numerical simulations and gradient-based pulse-shape optimization yield three-qubit gates with fidelities exceeding 99.9% within a 60 ns gate duration. Scaling the optimization to a five-qubit CXXXX architecture confirms the physical viability of the multi-target driving scheme.

Conclusion: Because these multiqubit operations naturally emulate stabilizer measurements, the architecture offers a hardware-efficient strategy for reducing circuit depth of stabilizer-type operations by replacing sequential two-qubit gate decompositions with single-step multiqubit gates.

Abstract: Implementing high-fidelity multiqubit gates is critical for reducing circuit depth in near-term quantum processors and fault-tolerant architectures. However, realizing multiqubit interactions and entanglement remains a critical challenge that limits the implementation of multiqubit gates. Here we propose an all-microwave scheme to realize single-step multiqubit gates incorporating n control and m target qubits, tailored for frequency-tunable superconducting transmon networks. By leveraging cross-resonance (CR) drives, this approach induces effective two-body ZX interactions that are significantly stronger than those achieved in conventional resonant regimes, circumventing the need for tunable couplers. The system's effective Hamiltonian is analytically derived using both quad frame rotation and rigorous block diagonalization. These combined theoretical methods facilitate the identification of optimal parameter regimes that simultaneously enhance desired target couplings and suppress parasitic higher-order terms, such as ZXX interactions. Through numerical simulations and gradient-based pulse-shape optimization, we demonstrate three-qubit gates achieving fidelities exceeding 99.9% within a 60 ns gate duration. Furthermore, we evaluate the fundamental scalability of this framework by extending the optimization to a five-qubit CXXXX architecture, confirming the physical viability of the multi-target driving scheme. Because these multiqubit operations naturally emulate stabilizer measurements, this architecture provides a hardware-efficient strategy for reducing the circuit depth of stabilizer-type operations by replacing sequential two-qubit gate decompositions with single-step multiqubit gates.

</details>


### [110] [A self-tallying quantum anonymous voting protocol for multiple-selection elections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39835)
*Guang-Bao Xu,Meng-Na Li,Yu-Guang Yang,Dong-Huan Jiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes the first self-tallying quantum anonymous voting protocol that supports multi-selection voting, overcoming the single-selection limitation of all prior protocols while satisfying self-tallying, nonreusability, verifiability, and fairness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-tallying quantum anonymous voting (SQAV) protocols are constrained by design challenges and only accommodate single-selection voting, leaving multi-selection voting functionality unsupported. This paper aims to bridge that gap.

Method: The protocol distributes nm n-particle entangled states equally into n groups. Each of the n voters receives one particle from each entangled state. Every voter sequentially measures all particles belonging to the n different groups to obtain n voting vectors, then encodes voting information into the vector corresponding to a secret index. Entanglement correlation enables each voter to verify ballot correctness by computation, and any participant to derive the vote count for each candidate by computation.

Result: The protocol achieves multi-selection voting functionality and satisfies self-tallying, nonreusability, verifiability, and fairness.

Conclusion: The proposed SQAV protocol extends quantum anonymous voting to multi-selection scenarios, addressing a key limitation of prior work while preserving essential security and correctness properties.

Abstract: Self-tallying quantum anonymous voting (SQAV) has drawn considerable attention since it was proposed. However, constrained by design challenges, all existing protocols only accommodate single-selection voting and cannot support multi-selection voting. In this paper, we propose a SQAV protocol with multi-selection voting functionality. In our protocol, $nm$ $n$-particle entangled states are equally divided into $n$ groups, and the $n$ particles in each entangled state are delivered to $n$ voters, one particle per voter. Each voter obtains $n$ voting vectors by sequentially measuring all his (or her) particles that belong to $n$ different group, and then encodes his or her voting information into the vector corresponding to the secret index. Due to entanglement correlation, each voter can verify whether his or her ballot is correct through computation, while any participant can obtain the vote count for each candidate through computation. Our protocol satisfies self-tallying, nonreusability, verifiability, and fairness.

</details>


### [111] [Natural Barriers to Quantum Extraction: On the Post-Quantum (In)security of (O)EKE and Masny-Rindal OT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39844)
*James Bartusek,Jake Januzelli*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: EKE-style PAKE and Masny-Rindal OT, though UC-secure classically and appealing for post-quantum use, are proven NOT UC-secure against quantum polynomial-time adversaries even with a post-quantum KEM; only game-based security notions are recoverable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EKE (Bellovin-Merritt, 1992) and Masny-Rindal OT (2019) are leading practically-implementable compilers for PAKE and OT: simple, plug-and-play over any KEM, with classical UC-security proofs. Given post-quantum KEM standardization, they are attractive candidates for efficient post-quantum PAKE/OT, motivating the question of whether their UC-security extends to the quantum adversarial model.

Method: The paper analyzes UC-security of a general family of (O)EKE protocols and Masny-Rindal OT under quantum polynomial-time adversaries. It constructs an adversarial strategy that provably prevents the simulator from extracting the secret input (the password for PAKE, the receiver's choice bit for OT), thereby establishing UC-insecurity even when instantiated with a post-quantum KEM. It also proves positive game-based security results for both compilers, deriving an advantage-tight one-way to hiding lemma.

Result: A general family of (O)EKE protocols and Masny-Rindal OT are not UC-secure against quantum polynomial-time adversaries, even with a post-quantum KEM. The simulator cannot be forced to extract the password (PAKE) or the receiver's choice bit (OT). Both compilers nonetheless achieve certain notions of game-based security. A novel advantage-tight one-way to hiding lemma is established.

Conclusion: UC-security of EKE and Masny-Rindal OT does not translate to the quantum adversarial model, so they should not be relied upon for post-quantum PAKE/OT with UC guarantees despite quantum-safe KEMs. However, they retain weaker game-based security, and the new advantage-tight one-way to hiding lemma may be of independent interest.

Abstract: Encrypted key exchange (EKE), introduced by Bellovin and Merritt (IEEE S\&P 1992), and Masny-Rindal OT, introduced by Masny and Rindal (ACM CCS 2019), are highly-efficient methods for compiling essentially any KEM into advanced cryptographic protocols, namely password-authenticated key exchange (PAKE) and oblivious transfer (OT), by relying only on idealized symmetric-key primitives. They have become leading candidates for practically-implementable PAKE and OT due to (1) their simplicity, (2) their plug-and-play nature, allowing for flexibility in the choice of KEM, and (3) existing proofs of UC-security (in the classical adversarial model).
  Due to point (2) above, these compilers yield attractive candidates for efficient \emph{post-quantum} PAKE and OT, especially given the recent post-quantum KEM standardization efforts. This motivates the question of whether the (UC-)security of these compilers translates to the quantum adversarial model. In this work, we show that it does not. In particular, we prove that a general family of (O)EKE protocols, as well as Masny-Rindal OT, are \emph{not} UC-secure against quantum polynomial-time adversaries, even when instantiated with a post-quantum KEM. To establish UC-insecurity, we devise an adversarial strategy that provably thwarts any attempt by the simulator to extract its input (the password in the case of PAKE, and the receiver's choice bit in the case of OT).
  To complement these negative results, we establish that both compilers yield certain notions of \emph{game-based} security. Along the way, we establish a novel ``advantage-tight'' one-way to hiding lemma that may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [112] [Optical measurement is almost quantum: Quantum inspired universal optical bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39857)
*Daniel Ish,Chase T. Ellis*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a general, system-agnostic formalism for optical sensing, inspired by quantum information theory. It derives a universal performance bound for all passive optical systems, shows a unique measurement scheme attains this bound for each quantity, and reveals that the optical formalism is nearly identical to the quantum formalism, including an uncertainty principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing optical sensing analyses are tied to specific optical systems and technologies, lacking a general framework that abstracts away implementation details. The authors are motivated by quantum information results to establish fundamental limits on measurement based solely on the quantity to be measured.

Method: The authors construct a general formalism for optical sensing that abstracts the specifics of optical systems. They derive a universal bound governing the performance of any passive optical system, identify a unique measurement scheme that attains this bound for each quantity of interest, and compare their optical formalism to the quantum formalism.

Result: A universal bound controls the performance of any passive optical system regardless of its construction technology. This bound is attained by a unique measurement scheme for each quantity of interest. The optical formalism is nearly identical to the quantum formalism, sharing essentially the same notions of the state to be measured and the measurement operation, along with an accompanying uncertainty principle.

Conclusion: Optical sensing admits a fundamental, technology-independent bound and an optimal measurement scheme, and its mathematical structure closely mirrors quantum mechanics, suggesting deep connections between optical metrology and quantum information theory.

Abstract: Inspired by results in quantum information, we report the development of a formalism for optical sensing which abstracts the details of specific optical systems and allows us to form general bounds on measurement based only on the quantity to be measured. We find a universal bound that controls the performance of any passive optical system regardless of the technology used to construct it and that this bound is attained by a unique measurement scheme for each quantity of interest. Shockingly, our optical formalism is nearly identical to the quantum formalism, with essentially the same notions of the state to be measured and the operation of the measurement, along with an accompanying uncertainty principle.

</details>


### [113] [Reverse quantum state diffusion from differential geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39861)
*Dinh-Long Vu,Patrick Rebentrost*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives the time reversal of quantum state diffusion (the stochastic unraveling of a Lindblad master equation), obtaining a backward stochastic differential equation on the pure-state manifold that provides a physical nonlinear unravelling of the inverse Lindbladian, and uses it in a score-based generative framework to generate new ensembles close to the original, with error bounds in various settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ensemble of quantum state diffusion trajectories is described by a probability density on the pure-state manifold, which contains far more information than the density matrix (its first moment). Exploiting this richer description motivates a reversal of the diffusion, analogous to score-based generative models, to characterize and reproduce the underlying ensemble.

Method: The reversal of a general diffusion on a manifold is written in Stratonovich form and specialized to the manifold of pure states, yielding a backward stochastic differential equation for the state vector valid for any Hilbert space dimension and any Lindbladian. This backward equation is then used, following score-based generative models, to generate a new ensemble close to the original. For the depolarizing channel, the unravelling is identified as Brownian motion on complex projective space.

Result: The backward stochastic equation provides a physical, nonlinear unravelling of the inverse Lindbladian, valid only for the ensemble from which it was constructed. For the depolarizing channel, the uniform distribution of pure states is the stationary law in any dimension. Starting the backward equation from that prior, the error of the resulting distribution relative to the original is bounded in exact and learned score settings, and in continuous and discrete time.

Conclusion: Time reversal of quantum state diffusion yields a backward stochastic differential equation on the pure-state manifold that physically unravels the inverse Lindbladian and enables score-based generation of quantum ensembles, with provable error bounds under the depolarizing channel prior.

Abstract: Quantum state diffusion unravels a Lindblad master equation into stochastic trajectories of pure states. The ensemble of trajectories is described by a probability density on the manifold of pure states, which contains much more information than the density matrix, its first moment. We derive the time reversal of this diffusion. Writing the reversal of a general diffusion on a manifold in Stratonovich form, and passing through the manifold of pure states, we obtain a backward stochastic differential equation for the state vector, valid for any Hilbert space dimension and any Lindbladian. The backward stochastic equation provides a physical, nonlinear unravelling of the inverse Lindbladian that holds only for the ensemble from which it was constructed. Following score-based generative models, we then use the backward equation to generate a new ensemble close to the original. For the depolarizing channel the unravelling is a Brownian motion on complex projective space, so the uniform distribution of pure states is its stationary law in any dimension. Starting the backward equation from that prior, we bound the error of the resulting distribution compared to the original one in different settings: exact and learned scores, continuous and discrete time.

</details>


### [114] [Reducing the Entanglement Cost of Distributed Bipartite Quantum Computation with Constant Qubit Overhead](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39865)
*Kosuke Matsui,Jun-Yi Wu,Min-Hsiu Hsieh,Mio Murao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that the operator Schmidt rank lower bound on entanglement cost for bipartite unitaries is attainable with constant qubit overhead: every bipartite Clifford unitary can be implemented with at most two auxiliary qubits per QPU at exactly the bound, and Clifford+T unitaries with T-count t need at most 2t extra Bell pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed quantum computation links multiple QPUs via quantum communication to perform large-scale computations, but since each QPU has limited qubits, reducing quantum communication while keeping qubit overhead small is essential. In the entanglement-assisted model, the operator Schmidt rank gives a general lower bound on entanglement cost for exact deterministic implementations when qubit overhead is unrestricted, but it was unclear how closely this bound could be approached under constant qubit overhead.

Method: The authors study an entanglement-assisted model where entanglement consumption measures quantum communication cost, and analyze exact deterministic implementations of bipartite unitaries. They construct explicit implementations attaining the operator Schmidt rank lower bound for every bipartite Clifford unitary using at most two auxiliary qubits per QPU, and extend the construction to non-Clifford unitaries specified by exact Clifford+T decompositions with T-count t.

Result: The operator Schmidt rank lower bound is attainable for every bipartite Clifford unitary with at most two auxiliary qubits per QPU. For non-Clifford unitaries given by exact Clifford+T decompositions with T-count t, the constructed implementations use the same qubit overhead and consume at most 2t Bell pairs above the lower bound.

Conclusion: The operator Schmidt rank bound can be approached with only constant qubit overhead, establishing near-optimal entanglement-assisted distributed implementations for both Clifford and Clifford+T bipartite unitaries.

Abstract: Distributed quantum computation connects multiple quantum processing units (QPUs) through quantum communication to jointly perform large-scale quantum computations. Since the number of qubits available at each QPU is limited, it is important to reduce quantum communication while keeping the qubit overhead small. To this end, we study an entanglement-assisted model, in which entanglement consumption serves as a measure of quantum communication cost. For exact deterministic implementations in this model, the operator Schmidt rank of the target bipartite unitary provides a general lower bound on entanglement cost when qubit overhead is unrestricted. However, it has remained unclear how closely this bound can be approached with constant qubit overhead. In this work, we show that this lower bound is attainable for every bipartite Clifford unitary using at most two auxiliary qubits per QPU. In addition, for non-Clifford unitaries specified by exact Clifford+$T$ decompositions with $T$-count $t$, we construct implementations with the same qubit overhead whose entanglement cost is at most $2t$ Bell pairs above this lower bound.

</details>


### [115] [Parallel algebraic surgery for qLDPC codes with constant shuttling depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39874)
*Tian-Gang Zhou,Boren Gu,Jens Eisert,Chen Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces "algebraic surgery" for lifted product codes, co-designing logical measurements, auxiliary codes, and atom shuttling to enable hardware-efficient Pauli-product measurements with constant shuttling depth on neutral-atom processors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconfigurable atom arrays are a promising platform for high-rate quantum LDPC codes, but their computational advantage hinges on implementing logical operations without prohibitive shuttling and space overheads.

Method: The authors develop algebraic surgery for lifted product codes, jointly designing logical measurements, auxiliary codes, and atom shuttling. The protocols exploit the cyclic-symmetry constraint to provide flexible logical addressability, supporting parallel logical measurements including canonical logical operators with heavily overlapping physical supports and joint measurements within or between code blocks with different lift sizes, achieving constant shuttling depth under explicit layout assumptions.

Result: In a representative hardware-model benchmark, a cycle supporting 33 parallel canonical logical measurements is about 2x faster than compared single-observable graph-based surgery schedules, with competitive qubit overhead. Shuttling and circuit-level simulations validate the approach.

Conclusion: The algebraic structure of quantum LDPC codes can be leveraged not only to reduce space overhead but also to simplify physical implementation and expand the set of efficiently accessible logical operations on neutral-atom processors.

Abstract: Reconfigurable atom arrays provide a promising platform for high-rate quantum low-density parity-check codes, but their computational advantage depends on whether logical operations can be implemented without incurring prohibitive shuttling and space overheads. We introduce algebraic surgery for lifted product codes, co-designing the logical measurements, auxiliary codes, and atom shuttling to enable hardware-efficient Pauli-product measurements. These protocols provide flexible logical addressability subject to the cyclic-symmetry constraint, enabling a broad class of parallel logical measurements, including canonical logical operators with heavily overlapping physical supports and joint measurements within or between code blocks even with different lift sizes, while achieving constant shuttling depth on neutral-atom processors under explicit layout assumptions. In a representative hardware-model benchmark, a cycle supporting 33 parallel canonical logical measurements is about $2\times$ faster than the compared single-observable graph-based surgery schedules, with competitive qubit overhead. These shuttling and circuit-level simulations confirm that the algebraic structure of quantum low-density parity-check codes can be used not only to reduce space overhead, but also to simplify physical implementation and expand the set of efficiently accessible logical operations on neutral-atom processors.

</details>


### [116] [Fault Tolerant Quantum Phases of Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39879)
*Colin V. Coane,Shouzhen Gu,Aleksander Kubica*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper classifies quantum phases of mixed states by their ability to preserve and fault-tolerantly transfer logical information, defining phase equivalence via shallow local channel circuits with measurements and classical communication, and shows fault-tolerance thresholds are phase invariants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing classifications of quantum phases largely target pure states, while realistic quantum information processing involves mixed states and noisy operations. There was a need for a framework that classifies phases of mixed states according to genuinely operational, fault-tolerance-relevant properties rather than state-specific features.

Method: The authors define phase equivalence between sets of states encoding logical quantum information, connected by shallow local channel circuits assisted by local measurements and global classical communication, with all operations required to be noise-robust. They relate this equivalence to recovery thresholds under local noise and analyze subsystem codes without privileging any gauge-subsystem state. They also examine how syndrome structure (extended vs. point-like) affects fault tolerance in measurement-based circuits.

Result: The existence of a recovery threshold for local noise is shown to be a property of an entire phase. Any two sets of states with nonzero thresholds connected by finite-depth local channel circuits belong to the same fault-tolerant phase. The framework yields a classification of subsystem codes and demonstrates phase equivalence between 2D and 3D color codes via dimensional jumps within the 3D gauge color code. Additionally, qudit stabilizer codes with extended syndromes permit single-shot state preparation, whereas shallow circuits measuring point-like syndromes—including 2D topological code preparation and non-Abelian topological orders with solvable anyon theories—are not fault tolerant.

Conclusion: Fault tolerance provides a robust, operational basis for classifying mixed-state quantum phases, unifying subsystem codes and color codes within a single phase-equivalence framework and revealing syndrome structure as a decisive factor for fault-tolerant state preparation.

Abstract: We introduce a classification of quantum phases of mixed states based on the ability to preserve and fault tolerantly transfer information. We formulate phase equivalence between sets of states that encode logical quantum information and are connected by shallow local channel circuits assisted by local measurements and global classical communication of measurement outcomes, where all quantum operations, including measurements, must be robust to noise. We show that the existence of a recovery threshold for local noise is a property of an entire phase. We prove that any two sets of states with nonzero thresholds that are related by finite-depth local channel circuits belong to the same fault tolerant phase. Our approach leads to a classification of subsystem codes without favoring any state of the gauge subsystem, and we demonstrate phase equivalence between 2D and 3D color codes using dimensional jumps within the 3D gauge color code. Lastly, we study how syndrome structure affects fault tolerance in circuits with measurements. We show that qudit stabilizer codes with extended syndromes permit single-shot state preparation, whereas a broad class of shallow circuits measuring point-like syndromes, including state preparation for 2D topological codes and non-Abelian topological orders with solvable anyon theories, is not fault tolerant.

</details>


### [117] [Quantum State Routing and Perfect State Transfer on Signed Graphs under Environmental Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39890)
*Nur Mohammad Sanfui,Supriyo Dutta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a deterministic topological quantum routing architecture using coined Szegedy quantum walks on edge-duplicated signed graphs, achieving exact zero back-scattering and unit-fidelity Perfect State Transfer (PST) at precise arrival times, with analysis under open-system noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Routing unknown quantum information across distributed communication networks requires autonomous, measurement-free mechanisms to avoid wave-function collapse. Conventional Szegedy walks on unweighted graphs suffer from severe back-reflection, spatial dispersion, and channel crosstalk, necessitating a better routing scheme.

Method: The authors introduce a deterministic topological quantum routing architecture based on coined Szegedy quantum walks on edge-duplicated signed graphs. Edge signs are treated as localized phase shifts within a balanced coin reflection, enforcing an exact zero back-scattering condition across routing nodes.

Result: Deterministic Perfect State Transfer with unit fidelity at exact arrival times is demonstrated across fundamental archetypes, including the signed dumbbell switch D_{2m,0,2n} and scalable glued binary trees. Routing performance is further analyzed under realistic open-system amplitude and phase damping noise channels.

Conclusion: The proposed signed-graph Szegedy walk architecture enables deterministic, measurement-free quantum routing with perfect state transfer, and its performance is characterized under realistic noise, supporting its viability for distributed quantum communication networks.

Abstract: Routing unknown quantum information across distributed communication networks requires autonomous, measurement-free mechanisms to prevent wave-function collapse. Szegedy quantum walks provide a mechanism for spatial state transport. The conventional walks on unweighted graphs suffer from severe back-reflection, spatial dispersion, and channel crosstalk. In this paper, we introduce a deterministic topological quantum routing architecture based on coined Szegedy quantum walks on edge-duplicated signed graphs. By treating edge signs as localized phase shifts within a balanced coin reflection, we enforce an exact zero back-scattering condition across routing nodes. We demonstrate deterministic Perfect State Transfer (PST) with unit fidelity at exact arrival times across fundamental archetypes, including the signed dumbbell switch $D_{2m,0,2n}$ and scalable glued binary trees. Furthermore, we analyse routing performance under realistic open-system amplitude and phase damping noise channels.

</details>


### [118] [Quantum Sensing and Hamiltonian Learning under Stochastic Parameter Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39908)
*Kelvin Koor,Patrick Rebentrost*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces stochastically evolving Hamiltonians (SEHs), whose coefficients follow stochastic differential equations, and studies Hamiltonian learning for them by extending a protocol that originally achieved the Heisenberg limit, identifying when this limit can be preserved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Hamiltonian learning typically assumes deterministic coefficients, whereas many realistic settings involve control parameters governed by stochastic dynamics. The authors aim to open the exploration of quantum information processing tasks where control parameters follow SDEs.

Method: The authors formalize the notion of SEHs, develop supporting theoretical aspects, and reanalyze and extend the Huang-Tong-Fang-Su protocol—the first to attain the Heisenberg limit for Hamiltonian coefficient learning—to the SEH setting. They also develop tools in quantum sensing and Hamiltonian simulation applicable to such stochastic settings.

Result: Theoretically delineated regimes in which the Heisenberg limit can and cannot be retained for learning SEHs, alongside auxiliary tools in quantum sensing and Hamiltonian simulation of independent interest.

Conclusion: The work establishes a framework for Hamiltonian learning under stochastically evolving coefficients and characterizes the limits of precision achievable, encouraging further study of quantum information processing settings with SDE-governed control parameters.

Abstract: We introduce the notion of stochastically evolving Hamiltonians (SEHs), which are local Hamiltonians whose coefficients are governed by stochastic differential equations (SDEs). After developing some theoretical aspects of these objects, we consider the task of Hamiltonian learning for SEHs. We reanalyse and make necessary extensions to the Huang-Tong-Fang-Su protocol, which first achieved the Heisenberg limit for Hamiltonian coefficient learning, under these circumstances. We delineate theoretically the regimes where the Heisenberg limit can and cannot be retained. Along the way we develop a few tools in quantum sensing and Hamiltonian simulation that may be of independent interest under similar settings in quantum information processing. The broad aim is to encourage the exploration of various settings in quantum information processing in which the control parameters are governed by SDEs.

</details>


### [119] [Distillation of N-Qubit Stabilizer States on a Star Network Topology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39916)
*Theodore M. Mahaffey,Chaohan Cui,Saikat Guha,Murphy Yuezhen Niu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a scalable entanglement distillation protocol using arbitrary [[n,k,d]] stabilizer codes to convert n noisy N-qubit GHZ copies into k high-fidelity logical copies on a star network topology, and proves generalization to any N-qubit CSS stabilizer state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributing high-fidelity entangled states across quantum processors over noisy channels is a key challenge; existing stabilizer-based Bell pair and GHZ distillation schemes lacked an explicit, scalable formulation and a general working principle.

Method: An explicit entanglement distillation scheme using an arbitrary [[n,k,d]] stabilizer code, formulated on a star network topology with only local operations and minimized impact of errors on idle physical qubits. Numerical experiments use the 5-qubit code, toric code, and [[144,12,12]] bivariate bicycle code against independent single-qubit depolarizing noise. A stabilizer-formalism proof extends the protocol to any N-qubit CSS stabilizer state.

Result: The scheme is efficiently scalable to arbitrary N. Encoding improves final GHZ state fidelity relative to a single bare GHZ state over the same channel for depolarizing rates up to or above 6% for all tested codes. It is proven applicable to any N-qubit CSS stabilizer state, with GHZ as a special case, establishing the working principle of prior stabilizer-based distillation schemes. Some stabilizer codes can distill any stabilizer state.

Conclusion: The protocol provides a scalable, local-operation-based method for high-fidelity entangled logical state distribution among any number of quantum processors, generalizes previous stabilizer-based Bell pair and GHZ distillation schemes beyond traditionally considered states, and shows certain stabilizer codes can distill any stabilizer state.

Abstract: We introduce an entanglement distillation protocol that utilizes an arbitrary $[[n,k,d]]$ stabilizer code to convert $n$ raw copies of an $N$-qubit Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state into $k$ logical copies in the presence of Pauli noise. Our explicit formulation of the scheme on a star network topology is efficiently scalable to arbitrary $N$, requires only local operations, and minimizes the impact of errors on idle physical qubits, enabling the distribution of high-fidelity entangled logical states between any number of quantum processors. We report the results of numerical experiments using the 5-qubit code, toric code, and $[[144,12,12]]$ bivariate bicyle code to protect against independent single-qubit depolarizing noise on all qubits. Our encoding scheme improves the final GHZ state fidelity relative to a single bare GHZ state sent over the same noisy channel for depolarizing rates up to or above $6 \%$ for all codes tested.
