Additionally, we use the stabilizer formalism to prove that our protocol can be applied to any $N$-qubit Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) stabilizer state, from which the GHZ state emerges as a special case. This result explicitly establishes the working principle of previous stabilizer-based Bell pair and GHZ state distillation schemes, and generalizes such protocols beyond the traditionally considered states. We also show that some stabilizer codes can distill any stabilizer state with our protocol.

</details>


### [120] [Verifiable quantum advantage based on polynomials with planted structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39918)
*Markus Bläser,Michael Gullans,Dominik Hangleiter,Yuxuan Liu,Youming Qiao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "simulation secrets" for verifiable quantum advantage, instantiated with IQP circuits from cubic polynomials with planted independent spaces, yielding a polynomial gap between verification and classical spoofing time under two conjectures, with potential for certifiable randomness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A central open question is whether quantum advantage protocols exist that match random circuit sampling's modest resource requirements yet remain verifiable solely from the classical outputs of the quantum computation.

Method: The authors develop the idea of simulation secrets, where a verifier uses a secret to run a cross-entropy test faster than any classical adversary. They instantiate this with IQP circuits described by cubic polynomials with planted independent spaces—the largest independent set in the polynomial's orbit under the general linear group—which induce a low-rank stabilizer decomposition of the corresponding state.

Result: Under two conjectures—that large independent spaces are invisible to computationally bounded adversaries, and a fine-grained complexity conjecture on producing samples passing the cross-entropy test for uniformly random polynomials—the scheme achieves a polynomial gap between verification time and classical adversary time, both exponential. The output distributions have high min-entropy, enabling classically certifiable randomness. The planted polynomial scheme is estimated implementable with 100 logical qubits at logical error rates around 10^-6.

Conclusion: Simulation secrets provide a promising route to verifiable quantum advantage with resource requirements comparable to random circuit sampling, contingent on the stated conjectures, and offer applications to certifiable randomness generation.

Abstract: A central question in the theory of quantum advantage is whether there are quantum advantage protocols with similar resource requirements as random circuit sampling that are also verifiable just from the classical outputs of the quantum computation. Here, we develop the idea of simulation secrets for verifiable advantage. A verifier can use a simulation secret to evaluate a cross-entropy test faster than it would take a classical adversary to pass the test. We instantiate this idea using IQP circuits described by cubic polynomials with planted independent spaces. These correspond to the largest independent set in the orbit of a polynomial under the general linear group and yield a low-rank stabilizer decomposition of the corresponding state. We conjecture that large independent spaces are invisible to a computationally bounded adversary, and therefore they cannot exploit them to pass the protocol. A second conjecture regards the fine-grained complexity of producing samples that pass the cross-entropy test for uniformly random polynomials. Under these conjectures, our scheme results in a polynomial gap between the verification time and the time a classical adversary would need to pass the protocol---both are exponential. It has a potential application to generating classically certifiable randomness, since the output distributions have high min-entropy. We estimate that the planted polynomial scheme is implementable using 100 logical qubits at logical error rates around $10^{-6}$.

</details>


### [121] [Fault-tolerant cost of shallow QAOA on near-symmetric optimization problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39922)
*Jernej Rudi Finžgar,Martin Leib,Elisabeth Wybo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the fault-tolerant cost of depth-one QAOA for near-symmetric constraint satisfaction problems, showing that small-angle Clifford+T rotation synthesis reduces the non-Clifford cost per circuit to Õ(n²) regardless of clause locality ℓ, while also examining whether the mechanism enabling constant QAOA success leaks the planted solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Montanaro and Zhou demonstrated that depth-one QAOA finds the planted solution of certain near-symmetric CSPs with probability Ω(1), with exponential runtimes observed for classical solvers—evidence of an empirical exponential speedup. This motivates asking what such a circuit actually costs to implement fault-tolerantly, since the cost Hamiltonian carries Θ(n^ℓ) clauses.

Method: The authors analyze the fault-tolerant compilation cost of the depth-one QAOA circuit. They exploit that the QAOA success rotation angle shrinks as n^{1-ℓ}, and apply small-angle Clifford+T rotation synthesis. They also investigate the mechanism underlying constant QAOA success, linking it to degree-one Fourier coefficients of the cost whose signs reveal the planted solution, and then design families of instances to test whether this leakage necessarily reveals the solution.

Result: Small-angle Clifford+T rotation synthesis reduces the non-Clifford cost per circuit to Õ(n²) for every clause locality ℓ. The mechanism underlying constant QAOA success fixes the degree-one Fourier coefficients of the cost, whose signs reveal the planted solution. However, this leakage need not reveal the solution: the authors design families in which the quadratic non-Clifford scaling and hard exact optimization coexist.

Conclusion: Fault-tolerant depth-one QAOA can be implemented with only Õ(n²) non-Clifford cost per circuit for all clause localities, despite the Θ(n^ℓ) clause count, by leveraging the shrinking rotation angle. While the QAOA success mechanism does leak information via degree-one Fourier coefficient signs, this leakage does not necessarily make the solution easy to extract, as demonstrated by constructed instance families combining quadratic non-Clifford scaling with hard exact optimization.

Abstract: Montanaro and Zhou proved that depth-one Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) finds the planted solution of certain near-symmetric constraint satisfaction problems with probability $Ω(1)$, and observed exponential runtimes for general-purpose classical solvers on explicit realizations, providing strong evidence of an empirical exponential speedup of low-depth QAOA. We ask what such a circuit costs fault-tolerantly. Although the cost Hamiltonian carries $Θ(n^\ell)$ clauses, the rotation angle at which QAOA succeeds shrinks as $n^{1-\ell}$, and we show that small-angle Clifford$+T$ rotation synthesis reduces the non-Clifford cost per circuit to $\widetilde O(n^2)$ for every clause locality $\ell$. Compiling the circuit, however, requires the explicit clause list. We find that the mechanism underlying constant QAOA success fixes the degree-one Fourier coefficients of the cost, whose signs reveal the planted solution. However, this leakage need not reveal the solution: we design families in which the quadratic non-Clifford scaling and hard exact optimization coexist.

</details>


### [122] [A Few Constrain Many: Correlation-Enhanced Learning of Many-Body Quantum Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39935)
*Matteo Tedde,Davide Girolami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Correlations among quantum observables reduce learning complexity: a polynomial number of measurements can bound exponentially many unmeasured dependent quantities, enabling efficient estimation of entanglement and magic in 100-qubit systems with lower error than shadow tomography and neural-state methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determining properties of many-body quantum systems is hard due to the large number of parameters to determine. The authors aim to exploit correlations among quantum observables as a resource to reduce the complexity of quantum learning.

Method: They show that a polynomial number of selected measurements can bound the values of exponentially many dependent yet unmeasured quantities. Using this, they design correlation-informed learning algorithms that estimate quantum resources such as entanglement and magic from information about a polynomial number of measured Pauli strings, without requiring premeasurement entangling gates.

Result: The protocols estimate key quantum resources in systems of 100 qubits and achieve lower relative error than shadow tomography and neural-state methods, while being readily testable on today's quantum computers without premeasurement entangling gates.

Conclusion: Quantum constraints themselves are a resource for exploring large quantum systems, since observable correlations enable efficient estimation of general functions of quantum states.

Abstract: Discovering properties of many-body quantum systems is challenging because of the large number of parameters to determine. Here, we show that correlations among quantum observables help reduce the complexity of quantum learning. A polynomial number of selected measurements can bound the values of exponentially many dependent yet unmeasured quantities, enabling efficient estimation of general functions of quantum states. We leverage this result to design correlation-informed learning algorithms that estimate key quantum resources, such as entanglement and magic, in systems of 100 qubits. They achieve a lower relative error than shadow tomography and neural-state methods by using information about a polynomial number of measured Pauli strings. These protocols are readily testable with today's quantum computers, as they do not require premeasurement entangling gates. Quantum constraints themselves are therefore a resource for exploring large quantum systems.

</details>


### [123] [Fast and Sure-ious Quantum Process Tomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39941)
*A. Afham,Sayantan Sen,Marco Tomamichel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that the folklore "partial normalization" for enforcing trace preservation in quantum process tomography is exactly the projection onto the set of Choi states of quantum channels in fidelity (equivalently Bures/purified distance). This yields a closed-form, rank-preserving, Kraus-form map that lifts any fidelity-guaranteed QST protocol to a QPT protocol with inherited sample complexity. Lifting two single-copy protocols gives Fidelity Projected Least Squares, achieving infidelity ε with O(1/ε) channel uses and beating state-of-the-art projected least squares in accuracy, time and memory for low Choi-rank channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QPT protocols are typically obtained by lifting QST via the Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism, but this requires an extra trace-preserving (TP) constraint enforcement. The two existing approaches form an unsatisfactory dichotomy: the folklore partial normalization is closed-form but only heuristic, with asymptotic analyses that lose a polynomial factor in dimension; projection-based methods are principled but lack closed forms and scale poorly with dimension. The paper aims to resolve this gap by providing a principled yet closed-form and scalable way to enforce TP.

Method: The authors characterize partial normalization (sandwiching the density estimate between inverse square roots of its input marginal) geometrically, proving it equals the exact projection onto Choi states of quantum channels under fidelity (equivalently Bures or purified distance). This gives a single closed-form congruence map that preserves Choi rank and is computed directly in Kraus form without any eigendecomposition in Choi dimension. Using this projection, they lift two single-copy QST protocols to QPT, notably deriving Fidelity Projected Least Squares (a non-adaptive protocol), and analyze its sample complexity, showing recovery of Choi rank and infidelity ε from a number of channel uses proportional to 1/ε.

Result: The partial normalization is shown to be the exact fidelity-optimal projection, so any fidelity-guaranteed QST algorithm lifts to a QPT algorithm inheriting its sample complexity. The lifted non-adaptive Fidelity Projected Least Squares protocol recovers the Choi rank and reaches infidelity ε using O(1/ε) channel uses. Numerically, for low Choi-rank channels it improves on state-of-the-art projected least squares in accuracy and by orders of magnitude in the time and memory required by the TP-regularization step and by the returned estimate.

Conclusion: The partial normalization is not merely a heuristic but the exact projection onto channel Choi states in fidelity, resolving the dichotomy between closed-form-but-unprincipled and principled-but-expensive TP enforcement. This enables closed-form, rank-preserving, eigendecomposition-free lifting of fidelity-guaranteed QST protocols to QPT protocols, with the resulting Fidelity Projected Least Squares offering provable O(1/ε) sample complexity and substantial practical gains in accuracy and resource efficiency for low Choi-rank channels.

Abstract: Quantum process tomography (QPT) protocols are often designed by lifting quantum state tomography (QST) protocols through the Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism, at the cost of an extra step enforcing the trace-preserving (TP) constraint. Existing approaches fall into two classes. The folklore partial normalization, which sandwiches the density estimate between inverse square roots of its input marginal, has a closed form but is used as a heuristic, its only analyses being asymptotic and losing a factor polynomial in the dimension. Projection-based methods are principled but have no closed forms and scale poorly with dimension. We resolve this dichotomy by showing that the partial normalization is the exact projection onto the Choi states of quantum channels in fidelity (equivalently in Bures or purified distance). Consequently, any QST algorithm with guarantees in fidelity lifts to a QPT algorithm that inherits its sample complexity, through a single closed-form congruence that preserves the Choi rank and is computed in Kraus form without any eigendecomposition of Choi dimension. We lift two single-copy protocols and prove that the non-adaptive one (Fidelity Projected Least Squares) recovers the Choi rank and reaches infidelity $\varepsilon$ from a number of channel uses proportional to $1/\varepsilon$. Numerically, for channels of low Choi rank, it improves on the state-of-the-art projected least squares in accuracy, and by orders of magnitude in the time and memory of the TP-regularization step and of the returned estimate.

</details>


### [124] [Scaling law of variational quantum algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39949)
*Ningfeng Wang,Zhengwei Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Large-scale numerical experiments up to 5000 qubits show variational quantum algorithms, using an O(log n)-depth classically simulable ansatz, converge to energies within 0.5% of benchmark optima for combinatorial optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how variational quantum algorithms scale is key to assessing their practical viability, yet empirical scaling evidence at large qubit counts is scarce, motivating large-scale numerical study.

Method: Large-scale numerical experiments on combinatorial optimization problems using variational quantum algorithms, scaling up to n = 5000 qubits with circuit depth O(log n) and a classically simulable ansatz.

Result: Converged energies consistently differ by no more than 0.5% from the optimal or reference energies reported in the benchmarks.

Conclusion: The variational approach exhibits favorable scaling, achieving near-optimal energies within 0.5% across up to 5000 qubits at logarithmic circuit depth.

Abstract: We study scaling law of variational quantum algorithms by large-scale numerical experiments on combinatorial optimizations. On experiments up to n = 5000 qubits with O( log(n)) circuit depth with a classically simulable ansatz, the converged energies consistently differ by no more than 0.5% from the optimal or reference energies reported in the benchmarks.

</details>


### [125] [Haah's 3D cubic code thermalizes rapidly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39959)
*Sebastian Stengele,Libor Caha,Ángela Capel,Simone Warzel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work rigorously proves that Haah's 3D cubic code thermalizes rapidly at every positive temperature, settling the long-standing question of whether it is a self-correcting quantum memory. It is the first analytic proof of rapid mixing for all positive temperatures for any fracton code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Haah's 3D cubic code was proposed as a candidate self-correcting quantum memory, leveraging the absence of string logical operators to block thermal errors. Whether it truly is self-correcting remained a long-standing open question: numerical studies explored its memory time, but no analytical upper bound was known.

Method: The proof uses the framework of [Commun. Math. Phys. 407, 195 (2026)] to convert a decay-of-correlations condition into a modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality for the Davies generator.

Result: The cubic code is shown to thermalize rapidly at every positive temperature, via the established modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality. This constitutes the first analytic proof of rapid mixing at all positive temperatures for any fracton code.

Conclusion: Haah's 3D cubic code is not a self-correcting quantum memory: it thermalizes rapidly at any positive temperature, rigorously ruling out its candidacy.

Abstract: Haah's 3D cubic code was introduced as a candidate for a self-correcting quantum memory in three dimensions, using the absence of string logical operators to obstruct thermal errors. It has been a long-standing open question whether it is a self-correcting quantum memory. Even though the memory time was explored numerically, no analytical upper bound was known. Here, we settle this question rigorously and show that the cubic code thermalizes rapidly at every positive temperature. Our proof relies on the framework of [Commun. Math. Phys. 407, 195 (2026)] to turn a decay-of-correlations condition into a modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality for the Davies generator. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first analytic proof of rapid mixing for all positive temperatures for any fracton code.

</details>


### [126] [Quantum Algorithms for Computational Fluid Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39962)
*Mario Guillaume Cecile,Nis-Luca van Hülst,Tomohiro Hashizume,Pia Siegl,Abhishek Setty,José Diogo da Costa Jesus,Paul Over,Sergio Bengoechea,Muhammad Umer,Spyros Tserkis,Eleftherios Mastorakis,Tristan Kraft,Francisco Cárdenas-López,Leonardo Scandurra,Thomas Rung,Felix Motzoi,Belda Yesil,Barbara Kraus,Martin Kiffner,Dimitris G. Angelakis,Eugene de Villiers,Dieter Jaksch*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A comprehensive review of quantum approaches for solving CFD-related PDEs, covering fully quantum (QLSA, HHL, QSVT, Hamiltonian simulation, QLBM) and hybrid quantum-classical (QPINN, variational) methods, plus tensor-network encodings, benchmarked on canonical PDE problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFD simulations of PDEs are computationally expensive classically. Quantum computing offers potential speedups, but algorithmic options are fragmented and their applicability to fluid dynamics is unclear. The paper aims to consolidate these approaches, evaluate their mathematical structure and limitations, and assess genuine prospects for quantum advantage under realistic hardware constraints.

Method: The review surveys fully quantum algorithms (QLSAs from HHL to QSVT, Hamiltonian simulation, quantum lattice Boltzmann methods) and hybrid quantum-classical methods (quantum physics-informed neural networks, amplitude-encoded variational PDE solvers). For each, it analyzes mathematical formulation, algorithmic structure, and limitations, emphasizing hardware-agnostic algorithms compatible with noisy and fault-tolerant devices. It also examines tensor-network representations that bridge CFD discretizations and quantum states/operators/circuits via tensor-programmable variational quantum algorithms (TP-VQAs).

Result: The review catalogs benchmark problems (Poisson, reaction, diffusion, and nonlinear model equations) and assesses how well each quantum framework captures key features of fluid dynamics. It finds that potential quantum advantage is highly problem dependent.

Conclusion: Quantum advantage for CFD is governed by condition number, representational complexity, state preparation, and measurement constraints. The review delineates the capabilities, limitations, and open challenges toward scalable quantum algorithms for CFD.

Abstract: We present a comprehensive review of quantum approaches for solving partial differential equations (PDEs) arising in computational fluid dynamics (CFD). We examine fully quantum approaches, including quantum linear system algorithms (QLSAs), ranging from the Harrow--Hassidim--Lloyd (HHL) algorithm to quantum singular value transformation (QSVT), Hamiltonian simulation, and quantum lattice Boltzmann methods (QLBMs), while emphasizing hybrid quantum--classical approaches, including quantum physics-informed neural networks (QPINNs) and amplitude-encoded variational PDE solvers. We focus on hardware-agnostic algorithms compatible with present noisy processors and emerging fault-tolerant architectures. For each framework, we analyze the mathematical formulation, algorithmic structure, and principal limitations. We also examine tensor-network (TN) representations, since CFD fields, differential operators, and geometrical information can often be encoded efficiently in low-rank form. The TN formalism bridges CFD discretizations and quantum states, operators, and circuits, enabling compact representations to be translated into tensor-programmable variational quantum algorithms (TP-VQAs). We further review benchmark problems, including Poisson, reaction, diffusion, and nonlinear model equations, and assess how well quantum algorithms capture key features of fluid dynamics. Our analysis highlights that potential quantum advantage is highly problem dependent and governed by condition number, representational complexity, state preparation, and measurement constraints. We outline capabilities, limitations, and challenges toward scalable quantum algorithms for CFD.

</details>


### [127] [Single-shot state preparation threshold: rigorous theorem and statistical mechanical mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39965)
*Yuanchen Zhao,Yi Yuan,Zhengyi Han,Dong E. Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Linear confinement suffices for a nonzero single-shot state-preparation threshold for CSS quantum LDPC codes, contradicting the belief that soundness is necessary; sublinear soundness may also support a threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-round noisy syndrome measurement can reduce time overhead of fault-tolerant quantum computation, but it remains unclear which structural code properties guarantee a threshold for single-shot state preparation. The prevailing belief holds that soundness is necessary.

Method: Proves that linear confinement implies a nonzero single-shot state-preparation threshold for CSS quantum LDPC codes. For distance at least logarithmic, establishes exponential suppression of logical error probability after a formal ideal final recovery. Under linear soundness, shows residual data error is local stochastic. Develops a statistical mechanical mapping expressing final logical error rate as a classical partition function, and performs Monte Carlo simulations for the three-dimensional toric code.

Result: Linear confinement suffices for a nonzero single-shot threshold, contrary to the soundness-necessity belief. Below nonzero local stochastic readout and data error thresholds, logical error probability is exponentially small and vanishes as code size grows. Linear soundness yields local stochastic residual data error, enabling composition with other fault-tolerant gadgets. Numerical results for the 3D toric code suggest sublinear soundness also supports a threshold.

Conclusion: Linear confinement, rather than soundness, is sufficient for a nonzero single-shot state-preparation threshold in CSS quantum LDPC codes. Linear soundness further ensures composability. Simulations indicate sublinear soundness may also suffice, broadening the class of codes viable for single-shot preparation.

Abstract: Preparing logical codewords with a single round of noisy syndrome measurements can reduce the time overhead of fault-tolerant quantum computation. It remains unclear which structural properties of the code suffice to guarantee a threshold for single-shot state preparation. Here we prove that linear confinement suffices for a nonzero single-shot state-preparation threshold for CSS quantum low-density parity-check codes, in contrast to the common belief that soundness is necessary. When the distance is at least logarithmic, we prove that below nonzero local stochastic readout and data error thresholds, the logical error probability after a formal ideal final recovery is exponentially small and approaches zero when the code size approaches infinity. Under the stronger assumption of linear soundness, we show that the residual data error of the preparation protocol is local stochastic, directly allowing composition with other fault-tolerant gadgets. To investigate single-shot state-preparation threshold beyond the above assumptions, we further develop a statistical mechanical mapping that expresses the final logical error rate in terms of a classical partition function. We perform Monte Carlo simulations of this model for the three-dimensional toric code. The numerical results suggest that sublinear soundness can also support a single-shot state-preparation threshold.

</details>


### [128] [The quantum query complexity of the semigroup product problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39974)
*Troy Lee,Miklos Santha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the quantum query complexity of computing semigroup products, introducing "product breadth" β to characterize this complexity via algebraic structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bounds (e.g., Aaronson–Grier–Schaeffer's √n(N log(nN+2))^{O(N)}) do not reflect the algebraic structure of the semigroup. The authors seek query bounds that are sensitive to structure, motivating the study of product breadth β.

Method: The authors define product breadth β as the smallest bound such that every input word has a subsequence of at most β letters with the same product, and analyze bounded-error quantum query complexity across various algebraic settings: finite commutative aperiodic monoids, monoids with a stable partial order, and arbitrary finite aperiodic semigroups.

Result: For nontrivial finite commutative aperiodic monoids, bounded-error quantum query complexity is Θ(min{n, √(nβ)}), characterized by β; if the monoid has aperiodicity index k, then β = O(k log(|M|+1) log log(|M|+2)). For monoids with a stable partial order (identity minimum), complexity is at most √(n+1)((β+2)log(n+2))^{O(log(β+2))}. For arbitrary finite aperiodic semigroups of order N−1, complexity is at most min{n, √n log^{O((N log(N+2))^{1/3})}(n+2)}. The size dependence is nearly tight via a Dyck lower bound requiring √n 2^{Ω(N^{1/3})} queries.

Conclusion: Product breadth characterizes quantum query complexity for commutative aperiodic monoids, and structure-sensitive bounds extend to ordered monoids and general aperiodic semigroups, improving prior bounds with nearly tight size dependence.

Abstract: We study the quantum query complexity of computing a semigroup product $x_1\cdots x_n$, when one query reveals one input element and the multiplication table is given. For a finite aperiodic semigroup of size $N-1$, the argument of Aaronson, Grier, and Schaeffer gives an upper bound of $\sqrt n\,(N\log(nN+2))^{O(N)}$ queries.
  To obtain query bounds that reflect algebraic structure, we study the product breadth $β$: the smallest bound such that every input word has a subsequence of at most $β$ letters with the same product.
  For nontrivial finite commutative aperiodic monoids, the bounded-error quantum query complexity is $Θ(\min\{n,\sqrt{nβ}\})$, and is thus characterized by product breadth. We further show that if such a monoid $M$ has aperiodicity index $k$ (the least positive integer satisfying $x^k=x^{k+1}$ for every $x\in M$), then $β=O(k\log(|M|+1)\log\log(|M|+2))$.
  For monoids with a stable partial order in which the identity is the minimum element, we prove that the bounded-error quantum query complexity is at most $\sqrt{n+1}((β+2)\log(n+2))^{O(\log(β+2))}$.
  For arbitrary finite aperiodic semigroups of order $N-1$, we improve the bound of Aaronson, Grier, and Schaeffer, obtaining a bounded-error quantum query complexity of at most \[ \min\left\{n,\sqrt n\, \log^{O((N\log(N+2))^{1/3})}(n+2)\right\}. \] The dependence on semigroup size is nearly tight: the bounded-depth Dyck lower bound of Ambainis et al. yields aperiodic monoids requiring $\sqrt n\,2^{Ω(N^{1/3})}$ quantum queries in the relevant parameter range.

</details>


### [129] [Logical information localisation in stabiliser codes via single-qubit measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39980)
*Jelena Mackeprang,Hemant Sharma,Jonas Helsen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents an analytical and computational study of stabiliser path finding (SPF) for localising logical information in stabiliser codes under qubit loss, introducing g-SPF, proving a localisation threshold via percolation theory, and proposing two algorithms (an exact ILP and a fast heuristic) that scale to much larger codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Localising logical information onto target qubits with only one round of single-qubit measurements is valuable for fault-tolerant communication with limited resources and flying qubits. Existing SPF methods and their solvers are too slow to study large stabiliser codes, limiting both theoretical understanding and practical application.

Method: The authors generalise SPF to g-SPF, where logical information is localised onto at most g unspecified target qubits. They use analytical arguments based on percolation theory and the disjointness of stabiliser codes. Computationally, they propose two solvers: an exact integer linear program (ILP) formulation and a heuristic that casts SPF as a decoding problem.

Result: They prove that for i.i.d. qubit loss with probability p < 1/2 and sufficiently large planar surface codes, g-SPF localisation for constant g succeeds with probability converging to one, establishing a localisation threshold. Numerically, both algorithms reproduce the surface code localisation threshold, and the heuristic is considerably faster, enabling study of substantially larger codes.

Conclusion: The analytical proof of a localisation threshold and the large speed-up from the new algorithms together enable the study of localisation in much larger stabiliser codes and open the door to applications such as fault-tolerant teleportation and efficient logical fusion.

Abstract: Stabiliser path finding (SPF) has previously been introduced as a method to localise logical information in a stabiliser code undergoing loss onto a single pre-specified target qubit, using only one round of single-qubit measurements. When working with limited resources and flying qubits, this fast read-out of logical information is a helpful tool for fault-tolerant communication. In this work, we provide a broad analytical and computational study of localisation via SPF. We introduce $g$-SPF, where the task is to localise the logical information onto a set of at most $g$ unspecified target qubits. Through analytical arguments based on percolation theory and the disjointness of stabiliser codes, we prove that for i.i.d. qubit loss with probability $p<1/2$ and sufficiently large planar surface codes, localisation via $g$-SPF for constant $g$ succeeds with a probability converging to one, which establishes a localisation threshold. Furthermore, we propose and implement two algorithms to solve SPF. The first is exact and formulates SPF as an integer linear program, whereas the second is heuristic and formulates SPF as a decoding problem. We validate both algorithms by numerically reproducing the localisation threshold for the surface code and demonstrate that the heuristic algorithm is considerably faster. Our work significantly reduces the time required to solve SPF compared to current state-of-the-art algorithms, allowing us to study localisation in substantially larger stabiliser codes than previously considered in the literature. Together, these theoretical and computational results open the door to various applications, such as fault-tolerant teleportation and efficient logical fusion.

</details>


### [130] [Predicting properties of Scrooge ensembles with high accuracy and low sample complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39992)
*Yue Wu,Yuzhi Tong,Helen Propson,Yihui Quek,Liang Mao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work develops efficient methods to analyze and implement Scrooge moments via a polynomial approximation with exponentially improved error threshold, yielding a quantum algorithm that estimates observable expectation values using Õ(k³/ε⁵) copies of ρ and a block encoding of the Scrooge k-th moment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Scrooge ensemble is the maximally random ensemble with average density matrix ρ. Its higher-order moments are hard to analyze because the Haar integrand is non-polynomial, blocking standard Haar integration, and existing sampling mechanisms need costly postselection, making them inefficient at large system size.

Method: The authors introduce a polynomial approximation to the Scrooge k-th moments whose error is tunable to a threshold improving from inverse polynomial to exponential in 1/||ρ||∞. Building on it, they design a quantum algorithm estimating observable expectation values and construct a block encoding of the Scrooge k-th moment given the preparation circuit of ρ. A key subroutine efficiently implements projection onto the k-fold symmetric subspace for ρ^⊗k.

Result: The first efficient quantum algorithm estimating observable expectation values with Õ(k³/ε⁵) copies of ρ for any target error ε above the threshold. A block encoding of the Scrooge k-th moment is provided. The projection subroutine uses O(k³/ε²) samples of ρ, avoiding the naive k! postselection cost.

Conclusion: These results supply new tools for theoretical analysis and practical prediction of Scrooge ensemble properties, with potential applications to quantum many-body dynamics and quantum information processing.

Abstract: The Scrooge ensemble associated with a density matrix $ρ$ is the maximally random ensemble whose average density matrix is $ρ$. It has attracted growing interest for its applications to quantum many-body dynamics and quantum information. The intrinsic statistical properties of the Scrooge ensembles are encoded in their higher-order moments, yet they are nontrivial to analyze and prepare for they feature a non-polynomial Haar integrand, blocking direct use of Haar integration machinery. Existing physical mechanisms to sample multiple copies of states from the Scrooge ensembles, meanwhile, typically require costly postselection, rendering them inefficient for large system size. In this work, we develop efficient and accurate methods for analyzing and implementing Scrooge moments. We first introduce a polynomial approximation to the Scrooge $k$-th moments whose error is tunable down to a threshold that improves from inverse polynomial to exponential in $1/||ρ||_\infty$ over previous results. Building on our approximation, we then give the first efficient quantum algorithm that estimates observable expectation values using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(k^3/ε^5)$ copies of $ρ$ for any target error $ε$ above this threshold. We further provide a construction of a block encoding of the Scrooge $k$-th moment when the preparation circuit of $ρ$ is known. Of independent interest could be a key subroutine in our algorithms, which efficiently implements projection onto the $k$-fold symmetric subspace for $ρ^{\otimes k}$ using $\mathcal{O}(k^3/ε^2)$ samples of $ρ$, avoiding the naive $k!$ postselection cost of such a projection. These results provide new tools for both the theoretical analysis and practical prediction of properties of Scrooge ensembles, with potential applications to quantum many-body dynamics and quantum information processing.

</details>


### [131] [Quantum state learning beyond approximate unitary designs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39994)
*Gyungmin Cho,Changhun Oh,Dohun Kim*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Approximate unitary designs with logarithmic depth can replace exact Haar designs for classical-shadow observable estimation and related tasks, but only when guarantees are derived from circuit structure; arbitrarily high-order design error alone does not preserve Haar learning guarantees, and reused bases in all-to-all two-local circuits require nearly linear depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent logarithmic-depth constructions of approximate unitary designs enable shallow measurement protocols for quantum state-learning while preserving performance, raising the question of whether approximate designs can generally replace exact designs. The paper investigates whether small design error is sufficient to preserve exact-design learning guarantees, or whether guarantees must instead be derived directly from the measurement circuit structure.

Method: The authors analyze observable estimation via classical shadows. They prove that logarithmic-depth two-layer Clifford circuits give an unbiased estimator whose variance matches the global Clifford scaling for every state and Hermitian observable. They connect this bound to setting-efficient tomography, mixed-state metrology, and stabilizer structure learning, and provide a compact exact tensor-network representation of the inverse shadow channel. They also study measurement-basis reuse in approximate designs and assess the depth needed for all-to-all random two-local circuits to uniformly match Haar variance in the many-shot limit.

Result: Exponentially small design error does not necessarily preserve exact-design learning guarantees. Logarithmic-depth two-layer Clifford circuits achieve an unbiased estimator matching global Clifford variance scaling for all states and Hermitian observables, extending global Clifford guarantees to shallow measurements for several tasks. A compact exact tensor-network representation of the inverse shadow channel is obtained. With reused measurement bases, high-order approximate unitary designs need not uniformly match Haar variance in the many-shot limit; for all-to-all random two-local circuits this requires nearly linear depth.

Conclusion: Shallow quantum state learning has both capabilities and limitations. Approximating Haar randomness is distinct from reproducing its learning guarantees: design error alone is insufficient, whereas structure-derived guarantees from shallow circuits can recover global-Clifford performance for key tasks, while basis reuse imposes stringent depth requirements.

Abstract: Approximate unitary designs reproduce the statistics of Haar-random unitaries to a given order and accuracy. Recent constructions realize such designs with logarithmic-depth circuits, enabling shallow measurement protocols for various quantum state-learning tasks while preserving performance. These results raise the question of whether approximate designs can replace exact designs more generally. We show that even exponentially small design error need not preserve the learning guarantees of exact designs. This motivates deriving learning guarantees directly from the measurement circuit structure. For observable estimation with classical shadows, we prove that logarithmic-depth two-layer Clifford circuits yield an unbiased estimator matching the global Clifford variance scaling for every state and Hermitian observable. Beyond observable estimation, this bound allows shallow measurements to retain global Clifford guarantees for other tasks, including setting-efficient tomography, mixed-state metrology, and stabilizer structure learning. We complement these statistical guarantees with a compact, exact tensor-network representation of the inverse shadow channel. When measurement bases are reused, approximate unitary designs of arbitrarily high order need not uniformly match the Haar variance in the many-shot limit. For the all-to-all random two-local circuits considered, doing so requires nearly linear depth. Together, our results reveal the capabilities and limitations of shallow quantum state learning, highlighting the distinction between approximating Haar randomness and reproducing its learning guarantees.

</details>


### [132] [An exponential query advantage from access to Stinespring dilation unitaries in quantum channel learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40001)
*Manaki Arihara,Satoshi Yoshida,Philip Taranto,Mio Murao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates an exponential query-complexity separation between black-box channel access and access to a Stinespring dilation unitary (with its inverse) for estimating the optimal average gate fidelity (OAGF) of a quantum channel, and rules out a universal simulator for dilation-based algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how different access models to a quantum channel affect the query complexity of learning its intrinsic properties is a fundamental question in quantum learning theory. Prior work has not established whether joint system-environment (dilation) access can yield exponential advantages over standard black-box channel access.

Method: The authors define a learning task: estimating the OAGF of an unknown channel, i.e., its average gate fidelity maximized over all unitary channels. For constant Kraus rank and a dilation environment of dimension polylog(d), they design an algorithm using polylog(d) queries to the Stinespring dilation unitary and its inverse. They then prove a lower bound of Ω(√d) queries for the black-box model under the same constant-rank, constant-accuracy conditions, and derive a corollary excluding a universal simulator with poly(log d, q) queries.

Result: The dilation-access algorithm estimates the OAGF to fixed additive accuracy with polylog(d) queries, whereas black-box access requires at least Ω(√d) queries—an exponential separation in the number of qubits. Consequently, no universal simulator using only poly(log d, q) black-box queries can approximate arbitrary q-query algorithms that access a randomly-sampled Stinespring dilation unitary and its inverse, even to small constant error.

Conclusion: Access to the system-environment joint evolution and its inverse provides an exponential advantage for estimating an intrinsic property of a quantum channel, establishing a strict separation between black-box and dilation access models and precluding universal simulation of dilation-based algorithms from black-box queries.

Abstract: We present a learning task for quantum channels that exhibits an exponential separation in query complexity (in terms of the number of qubits) between two access models: i) black-box channel access and ii) access to a Stinespring dilation unitary of the channel along with its inverse. We consider the task of learning how close the unknown channel is to the set of unitary channels. This is characterized by the \textit{optimal average gate fidelity (OAGF)}, defined as the average gate fidelity maximized over all unitary channels. For constant Kraus rank and a dilation environment of dimension $\operatorname{polylog}(d)$, we provide an algorithm that estimates the OAGF to fixed additive accuracy using $\operatorname{polylog}(d)$ queries to the Stinespring dilation unitary and its inverse. In contradistinction, under the black-box access model, this estimation requires at least $Ω(\sqrt{d})$ queries, even for channels of constant Kraus rank and constant additive accuracy. As a corollary, we rule out a universal simulator that uses only $\operatorname{poly}(\log d,q)$ queries to the unknown channel to approximate the output of arbitrary $q$-query algorithms with access to a randomly-sampled Stinespring dilation unitary and its inverse, even to sufficiently small constant error. These results demonstrate that access to the system-environment joint evolution and its inverse can provide an exponential advantage in estimating an intrinsic property of a quantum channel.

</details>


### [133] [Technical analysis of the Resource-efficient Quantum Walkers Quantum Random Access Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40020)
*Giuseppe De Riso,Giuseppe Catalano,Seth Lloyd,Vittorio Giovannetti,Dario De Santis*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This technical follow-up presents full derivations, protocol specifications, and resource analysis for a resource-efficient qRAM architecture based on discrete-time quantum walkers, extending it with long- and short-range routing paradigms and confirming optimal O(n+m) circuit depth for the short-range approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Achieving quantum advantage in algorithms like database search and quantum machine learning requires qRAM, but leading proposals suffer major drawbacks: the "Bucket Brigade" architecture needs an exponential number of active nodes O(2^n), and previous quantum-walker schemes face spatial bottlenecks from multiple parallel trees. A more resource-efficient and experimentally realistic qRAM is needed.

Method: The paper builds on a discrete-time quantum-walker qRAM model, providing full mathematical derivations, detailed protocol specifications, and in-depth resource analysis. It employs a number of quantum walkers scaling linearly with address size n and message size m. Two algorithmic paradigms are proposed—long-range and short-range approaches—differing in the interaction length of routing gates. The short-range variant decomposes multi-target controlled gates into sequences of 2- and 3-body local gates. Routing, message-copy, and walker retrieval phases are formalized for both variants, and different walker types (bosons, dual-rail qubits, four-level qudits) are explored.

Result: The model avoids the exponential O(2^n) active nodes of Bucket Brigade by using walkers scaling linearly with n and m, and overcomes spatial bottlenecks of prior quantum-walker schemes by eliminating multiple parallel trees. The short-range approach achieves optimal O(n+m) circuit depth and improves feasibility for near-term experimental implementation.

Conclusion: The discrete-time quantum-walker qRAM resolves primary drawbacks of leading qRAM proposals, offering linear scaling in address and message sizes, optimal O(n+m) circuit depth via the short-range approach, and improved near-term experimental feasibility, while accommodating multiple walker types.

Abstract: Quantum Random Access Memory (qRAM) is a critical component for achieving quantum advantage in algorithms ranging from database search to quantum machine learning. In a recently introduced model [arXiv:2508.02855], we proposed a resource-efficient qRAM architecture based on discrete-time quantum walkers. This article serves as a comprehensive technical follow-up, providing the full mathematical derivations, detailed protocol specifications, and in-depth resource analysis. Moreover, we extend the original proposal with novel techniques for the purpose of making the qRAM implementation more realistic. Our model resolves the primary drawbacks of leading qRAM proposals: it avoids the exponential number of active nodes $\mathcal{O}(2^n)$ required by the "Bucket Brigade" architecture by employing a number of quantum walkers that scales linearly with the address and message sizes, $n$ and $m$, respectively. Simultaneously, it overcomes the spatial bottlenecks of previous quantum-walker schemes by eliminating the need for multiple parallel trees. We propose two algorithmic paradigms: the long- and the short-range approaches, which differ by the length of interaction of the main routing gates employed in the qRAM. We formalize the routing, message-copy, and walker retrieval phases for both variants and we show how, while the long-range scheme employs controlled gates having a multitude of target systems, the short-range approach decomposes these interactions into sequences of 2- and 3-body local gates, which improves the architecture's feasibility for near-term experimental implementation. Finally, our comprehensive resource analysis confirms that the short-range approach achieves the optimal $\mathcal{O}(n+m)$ circuit depth. Within these two paradigms, we explore the potential of different types of quantum walkers, namely bosons, dual-rail qubits and four-level qudits.

</details>


### [134] [CSS codes for Quantum Metrology with Discrete-time Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40022)
*Ugnė Liaubaitė,Debora Ramacciotti,Robert Raußendorf*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work develops a CSS-code-based quantum metrology protocol showing that discrete-time error correction preserves Heisenberg-like temporal scaling over a finite interrogation-time window for noise perpendicular to the sensing Hamiltonian, while post-evolution-only recovery offers no metrological advantage over the uncorrected noisy protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum metrology aims to estimate an unknown parameter with better-than-classical precision, ideally attaining Heisenberg scaling. However, realistic noise substantially reduces this quantum advantage and often restores standard-quantum-limit-like performance. Preserving the quantum enhancement therefore requires noise-robust strategies, which quantum error correction can provide.

Method: The authors develop and analyze a quantum-metrology protocol based on Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes. They build an analytical framework applicable to arbitrary CSS codes and apply it to the Steane and Shor codes, determining the discrete-time corrected dynamics, the Heisenberg-like-to-SQL-like crossover, and the optimal interrogation time.

Result: For noise perpendicular to the sensing Hamiltonian, discrete-time error correction preserves Heisenberg-like temporal scaling over a finite interrogation-time window whose duration depends on the correction frequency. Recovery applied only after the sensing evolution provides no metrological advantage over the corresponding uncorrected noisy protocol in the considered setup. Numerical analysis of the Steane and Shor codes characterizes the corrected dynamics, the Heisenberg-like-to-SQL-like crossover, and the optimal interrogation time.

Conclusion: Discrete-time error correction with CSS codes can protect Heisenberg-like sensing scaling against perpendicular noise, but only within a finite window set by correction frequency; post-evolution-only recovery is ineffective in this setup. The general CSS analytical framework enables evaluation of specific codes such as Steane and Shor.

Abstract: The goal of quantum metrology is to estimate an unknown parameter with better-than-classical precision and, ideally, to attain Heisenberg scaling. In realistic settings, however, noise can substantially reduce this advantage and, in many cases, restore standard-quantum-limit-like performance. Preserving the quantum enhancement therefore requires noise-robust strategies, which can be implemented using quantum error correction. In this work, we develop and analyze a quantum-metrology protocol based on Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes. The main result is that, for noise perpendicular to the sensing Hamiltonian, discrete-time error correction preserves Heisenberg-like temporal scaling over a finite interrogation-time window whose duration depends on the correction frequency. It is further shown that recovery applied only after the sensing evolution provides no metrological advantage over the corresponding uncorrected noisy protocol in the setup considered here. An analytical framework is developed for arbitrary CSS codes and applied to the Steane and Shor codes, for which the discrete-time corrected dynamics, the Heisenberg-like-to-SQL-like crossover, and the optimal interrogation time are determined and numerically analysed.

</details>


### [135] [Measurement-induced dynamics and emergent symmetries of particles moving in a one-dimensional lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40026)
*Salvatore Di Lorenzo,Klaus Mølmer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Weak continuous measurement of a single lattice site's occupation entangles the particles and steers their permutation symmetry toward stable bosonic, fermionic, or parastatistical sectors, even without any prior symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous measurements both reveal and perturb quantum dynamics. The authors ask whether weak continuous probing can actively select and stabilize particular system properties—parity and permutation symmetry—in a lattice tunneling setup.

Method: Theoretical/computational study of particles tunneling between sites of a one-dimensional lattice, subject to weak continuous monitoring of the occupation of a single lattice site. Simulations are performed for systems of two and three particles initially possessing no permutation symmetry.

Result: Weak continuous probing induces entanglement among the particles and selects definite values for wave function parity and permutation symmetry. The measurement steers two- and three-particle systems into stable bosonic, fermionic, and parastatistical symmetry sectors.

Conclusion: Continuous measurement acts as a symmetry-selection and stabilization mechanism, demonstrating that weak monitoring can drive a system into distinct, stable permutation-symmetry sectors relevant to statistical behavior.

Abstract: Continuous measurements simultaneously reveal and modify the dynamics of a quantum system. In this work we consider the tunneling motion of particles between the sites of a one-dimensional lattice. We investigate how weak continuous probing of the occupation of a single lattice site induces entanglement and selects definite values for system properties such as wave function parity and permutation symmetry. Our simulations show how continuous measurement can steer systems of two and three particles, with no prior permutation symmetry, into stable bosonic, fermionic and parastatistical symmetry sectors.

</details>


### [136] [Pauli instability in arbitrary states: detecting magic in physical correlators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40032)
*Tanner Jackson,Alexey Milekhin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows how to quantify non-stabilizerness (magic) of a quantum evolution using out-of-time-order correlators (OTOCs) evaluated in an arbitrary state, and proves this measure vanishes iff the evolution is Clifford for any full-rank state, including Gibbs states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating quantum evolution classically is hard when the dynamics contain "magic" (non-stabilizerness), so a key question is how to extract information about magic from physically accessible correlators, such as those at finite temperature or in the ground state.

Method: The authors construct a magic measure based on out-of-time-order correlators evaluated in an arbitrary quantum state. They establish its basic properties, relate it to the stabilizer entropy of the Choi state, and analyze its behavior with a single fixed probe operator.

Result: For every full-rank state, and in particular every Gibbs state, the measure vanishes if and only if the evolution is Clifford. Even with a single fixed probe operator, it provides a lower bound on the number of T gates required to implement the dynamics in any circuit architecture.

Conclusion: OTOC-based correlators offer a physically grounded, state-robust probe of non-stabilizerness, connecting magic to circuit complexity via a T-gate lower bound.

Abstract: Measures of non-stabilizerness (``magic'') of a quantum evolution quantify how hard it is to simulate on a classical computer. A natural question is how to extract information about magic from physical correlators, for example at finite temperature or in the ground state. In this paper we show how to measure magic using out-of-time-order correlators evaluated in an arbitrary state. For every full-rank state, and in particular for every Gibbs state, the resulting quantity vanishes if and only if the evolution is Clifford. We establish its basic properties, relate it to the stabilizer entropy of the Choi state, and prove that, even with a single fixed probe operator, it gives a lower bound on the number of $T$ gates needed to implement the dynamics in any circuit architecture.

</details>


### [137] [Experimentally realized local-global tradeoff in quantum resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40033)
*Yue Dai,Zhiyu Wang,Xinzhi Zhao,Xinglei Yu,Chengjie Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes and experimentally demonstrates a local-global tradeoff in quantum resources: the sum of local maximum coherence and quantum mutual information is bounded by a definitive upper limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interconversion among quantum resources (e.g., coherence-entanglement tradeoff) has drawn attention, but the relationship between local and global quantum resources remains largely unexplored.

Method: The authors propose and experimentally demonstrate a local-global tradeoff, quantifying local resources by local maximum coherence and global resources by quantum mutual information.

Result: Experiments show the sum of local maximum coherence and quantum mutual information is bounded by a definitive upper limit, establishing a fundamental tradeoff inequality and providing direct experimental evidence of local-global complementarity.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a constraint in quantum networks: local quantum resources at individual nodes and global quantum resources of the network are mutually exclusive, so enhancing one necessarily limits the other.

Abstract: The interconversion among different quantum resources has attracted considerable attention, exemplified by the tradeoff between coherence and entanglement. However, the relationship between local and global quantum resources remains largely unexplored. Here, we propose and experimentally demonstrate a local global tradeoff in quantum resources, quantified respectively by the local maximum coherence and the quantum mutual information. Our experiments show that the sum of the local maximum coherence and the quantum mutual information is bounded by a definitive upper limit. These results not only establish a fundamental tradeoff inequality, but also provide direct experimental evidence of the complementarity between local and global quantum resources. Moreover, our findings reveal an important constraint in quantum networks: the local quantum resources available at individual nodes and the global quantum resources of the network are mutually exclusive, implying that enhancing one necessarily limits the other.

</details>


### [138] [Cycle Codes and Decoded Quantum Interferometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40036)
*Anuj Apte,Shouvanik Chakrabarti,Andi Gu,Stephen P. Jordan,Ojas Parekh,Ruslan Shaydulin,Jacob Watkins,Noureldin Yosri,Adam Zalcman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) for MaxCut-like optimization via cycle-code decoding. It derives satisfaction guarantees under imperfect decoding, proves minimum-weight decoding of homological cycle codes is NP-hard for all fields with q>2, gives ML recovery bounds and a strong converse on Linial-Simkin graphs, and shows LP decoders recover a positive fraction of errors. New upper/lower bounds rule out quantum advantage in the analyzed regimes but identify regular Max-k-Cut instances where DQI achieves a nontrivial guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior DQI no-go results for MaxCut relied on minimum-distance assumptions that do not reflect true decodability thresholds for common noise channels, thereby underestimating actual DQI performance. The aim is to estimate true performance under realistic imperfect decoding.

Method: The authors derive DQI satisfaction guarantees under imperfect decoding for fixed instances and generalize prior random-instance results. They study homological cycle codes over arbitrary finite fields, analyzing minimum-weight decoding complexity and, on the Linial-Simkin ensemble of regular graphs with logarithmic girth, establish maximum-likelihood recovery bounds and a strong converse for a family of non-uniform additive noise channels. They further use LP decoders for polynomial-time recovery of randomly located errors.

Result: Minimum-weight decoding of homological cycle codes is NP-hard for every field of size q>2, unlike binary cycle codes. On Linial-Simkin graphs, ML recovery bounds and a strong converse hold for non-uniform additive noise. LP decoders polynomially recover a positive fraction of randomly located errors with arbitrary values. These yield upper and lower bounds on DQI's approximate optima under classical decoders, ruling out quantum advantage in the analyzed regimes while identifying regular Max-k-Cut instances where DQI efficiently achieves a nontrivial satisfaction fraction guarantee.

Conclusion: Under realistic decodability thresholds, DQI shows no quantum advantage in the regimes analyzed, though it can still efficiently guarantee nontrivial satisfaction fractions for a specific family of regular Max-k-Cut instances.

Abstract: Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) reduces optimization problems with two-variable constraints to decoding cycle codes. For one such problem, namely MaxCut, prior work showed that DQI achieves a nontrivial satisfaction fraction guarantee only on linear-girth graphs, for which MaxCut is classically easy. However, these no-go results rely on minimum distance assumptions that do not represent true decodability thresholds for common noise channels, thus underestimating actual DQI performance. To estimate the true performance, we derive DQI satisfaction guarantees in the presence of imperfect decoding for fixed instances and generalize prior results for random instances. We then study homological cycle codes over arbitrary finite fields, and show that minimum-weight decoding is NP-hard for every field of size $q>2$, in contrast with known results for binary cycle codes. On the Linial--Simkin ensemble of regular graphs with logarithmic girth, we prove maximum-likelihood recovery bounds and a strong converse for a family of non-uniform additive noise channels. Moreover, LP decoders provide polynomial-time recovery of a positive fraction of randomly located errors with arbitrary values. These results allow us to prove upper and lower bounds on the approximate optima achievable by DQI when restricted to classical decoders. These bounds rule out quantum advantage in the regimes we analyze but also identify a family of regular Max-$k$-Cut instances on which DQI efficiently achieves a nontrivial satisfaction fraction guarantee.

</details>


### [139] [Finite-Bandwidth Protection of a Three-Level Quantum Heat Engine Against Parasitic Heat Leaks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40038)
*Gilberto Aparecido Prataviera,Marcos César de Oliveira*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Spectral filtering can suppress a parasitic transition in a three-level quantum heat engine, but the filter's finite response time imposes a trade-off: while a Lorentzian filter removes the unwanted hot-to-cold short circuit, excessive narrowing reduces useful energy throughput, yielding a finite optimal bandwidth that depends on the model used.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In quantum thermal machines, a physical (finite-bandwidth) spectral filter is desirable to suppress unwanted transitions, but any real filter also has a finite response time. This paper studies how this competition between spectral selectivity and dynamical response time affects the performance of a continuous three-level heat engine in which the hot environment parasitically couples to the cold transition.

Method: The authors model a continuous three-level heat engine whose hot bath leaks into the cold transition. They first solve the corresponding three-state rate network exactly, treating the parasitic coupling as a thermodynamic short circuit, and derive a closed-form threshold for the loss of positive-power operation. They then explicitly retain a damped auxiliary mode as a physical spectral filter with a Lorentzian response. Two independent open-system approaches—local-GKSL and nonsecular Bloch–Redfield—are used to compute the engine's performance, and results are compared with a frequency-resolved Lorentzian-rate reduction.

Result: The exact rate-network solution shows the parasitic transition creates a hot-to-cold short circuit and yields a closed-form threshold for the breakdown of positive power output. Including the damped auxiliary mode as a Lorentzian filter suppresses the detuned parasitic transition, but excessive narrowing curtails useful energy throughput. Both local-GKSL and nonsecular Bloch–Redfield calculations reduce to the no-leak Markovian engine in the weak-coupling limit and predict a finite maximum-power bandwidth, though its exact location is model dependent. The frequency-resolved Lorentzian-rate reduction, in contrast, exhibits no interior optimum.

Conclusion: Spectral selectivity can protect the useful thermodynamic cycle from parasitic leakage, but a physically realizable filter cannot be narrowed indefinitely without incurring a dynamical throughput cost. This establishes a design principle: optimal filter bandwidth for quantum thermal machines emerges from balancing parasitic-transition suppression against loss of useful energy throughput, and the optimum is sensitive to the chosen modeling framework.

Abstract: Finite-bandwidth reservoir engineering can suppress unwanted transitions in a quantum thermal machine, but a physical filter also introduces a finite response time. We study this competition in a continuous three-level heat engine whose hot environment couples parasitically to the cold transition. The corresponding three-state rate network is solved exactly, showing that the parasitic transition produces a hot-to-cold thermodynamic short circuit and yielding a closed threshold for the loss of positive-power operation. We then retain a damped auxiliary mode explicitly as a physical spectral filter. Its Lorentzian response suppresses the detuned parasitic transition, whereas excessive narrowing limits the useful energy throughput. Independent local-GKSL and nonsecular Bloch--Redfield calculations both recover the no-leak Markovian engine in the weak-coupling limit and predict a finite maximum-power bandwidth, although its precise location is model dependent. A frequency-resolved Lorentzian-rate reduction, by contrast, has no interior optimum. The resulting design principle is that spectral selectivity can protect the useful thermodynamic cycle, but a real filter cannot be narrowed without a dynamical throughput cost.

</details>


### [140] [Unconditional quantum advantage with noisy planar architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40050)
*Libor Caha,Robert Koenig,Louis Paletta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: 2D-local quantum circuits with bounded local stochastic noise are shown to be strictly more powerful than AC0, giving the first unconditional, noise-resilient, complexity-theoretic separation for planar quantum devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior quantum-advantage separations typically either assume ideal (noise-free) circuits, rely on unproven complexity assumptions, or ignore geometric locality (2D constraint). This work aims to establish an advantage that simultaneously applies to planar devices, tolerates local stochastic noise below a constant threshold, and is unconditional—moving experimental quantum advantage closer to realistic settings.

Method: A computational problem is constructed and analyzed against two models: (i) a geometrically 2D-local quantum circuit (restricted to local operations in 2D) subject to local stochastic noise below a constant threshold, and (ii) polynomial-size AC0-circuits (constant-depth, unbounded fan-in AND/OR/NOT, noise-free, geometrically unconstrained). A separation is proven by showing the quantum circuit solves the problem with high probability while any AC0-circuit fails on certain instances with constant probability.

Result: The paper exhibits a problem such that any instance is solved with high probability by the noisy 2D-local quantum circuit, yet any polynomial-size AC0-circuit fails on certain instances with constant probability. This yields an unconditional separation of noisy 2D-local quantum circuits from AC0.

Conclusion: This is the first complexity-theoretic separation that applies to planar quantum devices, incorporates noise-resilience, and is unconditional (no complexity-theoretic assumptions). It brings the experimental demonstration of unconditional quantum advantage closer to experimental realities.

Abstract: We consider quantum devices restricted to local operations in 2D and subject to local stochastic noise below a constant threshold. We show that such circuits are computationally more powerful than AC0-circuits, i.e., noise-free, geometrically-unconstrained constant-depth classical circuits with unbounded fan-in AND, OR and NOT gates. To this end, we exhibit a computational problem with the following properties: (i) Any instance of the problem is correctly solved with high probability by a certain geometrically 2D-local quantum circuit even if the latter is imperfectly implemented, but (ii) any polynomial-size AC0-circuit fails to solve certain instances of the problem with constant probability. To our knowledge, this isthe first complexity-theoretic separation which applies to planar quantum devices, incorporates noise-resilience and is unconditional, i.e., does not rely on complexity-theoretic assumptions. This brings the experimental demonstration of an unconditional quantum advantage closer to experimental realities.

</details>


### [141] [Transmitting algebras through quantum channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40054)
*Robert Salzmann,Satvik Singh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a theory of exact transmission of finite-dimensional C*-algebras through quantum channels, showing that a dominating transmittable algebra can fail to exist even in small dimensions, and characterizing transmission via hybrid capacities and minimal forbidden algebra types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-dimensional C*-algebras model hybrid classical-quantum information where the quantum system's dimension may depend on the classical message. The authors seek to unify zero-error information theory with operator-algebraic error correction by studying which algebra types can be transmitted exactly through a quantum channel under arbitrary encodings and decodings.

Method: The transmission set of algebra types, ordered by embedding, is analyzed. The authors ask whether a dominating algebra exists into which every transmittable algebra embeds. They introduce hybrid capacities that reconstruct the only possible dominating algebra type, provide a finite characterization of domination via minimal forbidden algebra types, and study tensor products and n-fold tensor powers of channels.

Result: Domination can fail even in small dimensions, yielding several incomparable maximal algebras corresponding to different optimal channel uses. Dominating algebras are identified for channels whose operator systems are graph-isomorphic to *-algebras (including highly divisible channels) and for channels with zero one-shot zero-error quantum capacity. Tensor products can produce new algebra types, an algebraic analogue of superadditivity. Domination can fail for joint use of two channels even when each separately admits a dominating algebra. A channel is constructed whose n-fold tensor powers have doubly exponentially many maximal algebra types, the largest possible growth scaling for finite-dimensional channels.

Conclusion: The full transmission structure of certain channels cannot be generated from any finite collection of bounded-block-length codes, since new transmittable algebra types appear at arbitrarily large block-lengths. In the absence of domination, users must select among incomparable maximal algebras depending on the operational task and the type of information to be preserved.

Abstract: We develop a theory of exact transmission of finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras through quantum channels. These algebras describe hybrid classical-quantum information, allowing the dimension of the quantum system to depend on the classical message. Allowing arbitrary encodings and decodings yields a transmission set of algebra types, ordered by embedding, that unifies zero-error information theory with operator-algebraic error correction. We ask if this set admits a dominating algebra into which every transmittable algebra embeds. Our central finding is that domination can fail even in small dimensions. In its absence, several incomparable maximal algebras can describe different optimal uses of the same channel, forcing the user to select one depending on the operational task and the type of information to be preserved. We introduce hybrid capacities that reconstruct the only possible dominating algebra type and present a complete finite characterization of domination using minimal forbidden algebra types. We identify dominating algebras for channels whose operator systems are graph-isomorphic to $*$-algebras, including highly divisible channels, and for channels with zero one-shot zero-error quantum capacity. Under tensor products, we prove that joint coding can produce new algebra types, giving an algebraic analogue of superadditivity from Shannon theory. Domination can fail for the joint use of two channels, even when both channels separately admit dominating algebras. Finally, we construct a channel whose $n$-fold tensor powers have doubly exponentially many maximal algebra types, attaining the largest possible growth scaling for finite-dimensional channels. Consequently, new transmittable algebra types appear at arbitrarily large block-lengths for this channel, so its full transmission structure cannot be generated from any finite collection of bounded-block-length codes.

</details>


### [142] [Equality in the Bosonic Quantum Entropy Power Inequality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40061)
*Yonglong Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper fully characterizes the equality class of the bosonic quantum entropy power inequality (QEPI): for independent finite-mean-photon-number inputs, equality in either linear or exponential form holds iff the inputs are Gaussian with identical covariance matrices (arbitrary displacements allowed), for any number of modes and any transmissivity strictly between 0 and 1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The bosonic QEPI bounds the entropy of a beam-splitter output via the input entropies, but its complete equality class was previously unknown. Determining when equality holds is essential for understanding the inequality's tightness and the structure of entropy-optimal bosonic states.

Method: The key step shows equality for one output forces independence of the two outputs. A thermal auxiliary converts equality into mutual-information preservation by a fixed noisy channel, and a two-outcome instrument shows this preservation requires a product state with the reference. The quantum Darmois--Skitovich theorem then yields Gaussianity, and a central-limit argument reduces exponential equality to linear equality.

Result: Equality in either the linear or exponential form of the bosonic QEPI holds if and only if the inputs are Gaussian sharing the same covariance matrix, with arbitrary displacements, for any number of modes and any transmissivity strictly between zero and one.

Conclusion: The complete equality class of the bosonic QEPI is Gaussian inputs with identical covariance matrices (arbitrary displacements), establishing tightness and the structure of entropy-optimal beam-splitter inputs.

Abstract: The bosonic quantum entropy power inequality bounds the entropy of a beam-splitter output in terms of the input entropies. We determine its complete equality class among independent inputs of finite mean photon number: for any number of modes and any transmissivity strictly between zero and one, equality in either the linear or the exponential form holds if and only if the inputs are Gaussian with the same covariance matrix, allowing arbitrary displacements. The main step shows that equality for one output forces the two outputs to be independent: a thermal auxiliary converts equality into preservation of mutual information by a fixed noisy channel, and a two-outcome instrument shows that preservation of mutual information requires a product state with the reference. The quantum Darmois--Skitovich theorem then gives Gaussianity, while a central-limit argument reduces exponential equality to linear equality.

</details>


### [143] [A Fourier-Label Information-Loss Barrier for Dihedral Coset Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40062)
*Aparna Gupte,Seyoon Ragavan,Mark Zhandry*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves a no-go theorem showing that any DCP quantum algorithm following the Fourier-sampling/subset-sum-measurement template and discarding ω(log n) bits from each Fourier label cannot succeed; it then applies this to show that the recent Simon algorithm fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dihedral coset problem (DCP) is central to quantum cryptanalysis, and the Fourier-sampling plus subset-sum-measurement template is a leading approach. There is a need to determine how the Fourier labels must be used, or an algorithm cannot succeed, to guide future DCP algorithm design and to verify claimed solutions.

Method: The authors analyze the Regev template: after measuring the lower n-1 bits of the subset sum, the algorithm discards any ω(log n) bits from each Fourier label. They prove a general no-go theorem for this class of algorithms. They then apply it to Simon's recent algorithm by showing it can be implemented, up to exponentially-small error, using only the most-significant third of the Fourier labels after the subset-sum measurement.
