Result: Any algorithm in this template that discards ω(log n) bits from each Fourier label cannot solve DCP. Simon's algorithm (IACR ePrint:2026/1591) falls into this class and therefore does not solve DCP. Lean 4 code is released for verifiability.

Conclusion: Algorithms following this template must make extensive use of the Fourier labels; discarding ω(log n) bits per label is fatal. This provides a useful design guide for DCP algorithms and refutes the recent Simon algorithm as a DCP solver.

Abstract: We establish a no-go theorem for a broad class of quantum algorithms for the dihedral coset problem (DCP). We consider the Fourier-sampling and subset-sum-measurement template proposed by Regev (SIAM Journal on Computing, 2004), which is one of the main approaches to solving DCP. Suppose that, after measuring the lower $n-1$ bits of the subset sum, the algorithm discards any $ω(\log n)$ bits from each of the Fourier labels. Then we prove that the algorithm cannot succeed in solving DCP. This shows that any algorithm following this template must make extensive use of the Fourier labels, and thus serves as a useful guide for developing algorithms for DCP.
  As a main application, we show that the recent algorithm by Simon (IACR ePrint:2026/1591, August 11 2026) does not solve DCP. We show that after the subset-sum measurement, this algorithm can be implemented (up to exponentially-small error) using only the most-significant third of the Fourier labels, and is therefore subject to our general no-go theorem. To help with verifiability, we release Lean 4 code for our results.

</details>


### [144] [Transducer-based linear combination of unitaries: theory and applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40073)
*Dong An,Dekuan Dong,Changpeng Shao,Yuxin Zhang,Chenhao Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a transducer-based LCU framework that implements block-encodings of linear combinations of unitaries with query cost depending on a weighted average rather than the worst-case cost, yielding improved block-encodings of sparse matrices and near-optimal algorithms for sparse Hamiltonian simulation and QLSP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LCU has a cost governed by the most expensive unitary in the combination (worst-case dependence). The authors aim to reduce this worst-case dependence to a weighted average query complexity when the unitaries share access to common primitive oracles, especially when costly unitaries carry small weights.

Method: The authors build on the transducer framework of Belovs, Jeffery, and Yolcu, developing a complementary operator-level theory tailored to block-encodings. For A = sum_j c_j U_j with each U_j using C_j primitive queries, they implement a block-encoding of A/alpha (alpha = O(a), a >= ||A||) using O~(C_max + C_bar * lambda / a) primitive queries. They identify the resolvent norm K as a key complexity measure beyond catalyst complexity and convert transducer action into an epsilon-approximate block-encoding with O(K log(1/epsilon)) queries.

Result: The algorithm uses O~(C_max + C_bar * lambda / a) primitive queries, versus O~(C_max * lambda / a) for standard LCU, where lambda = sum_j c_j, C_max = max_j C_j, C_bar = sum_j c_j C_j / lambda. Improvement is substantial when costly unitaries have small weights and lambda/a is large. Applications include improved block-encodings of sparse matrices, leading to sparse Hamiltonian simulation and quantum linear systems algorithms with near-optimal query complexity up to polylogarithmic factors. Precision dependence improves from polynomial to logarithmic in 1/epsilon.

Conclusion: A transducer-based LCU framework achieves weighted-average query complexity, improving over worst-case standard LCU, with applications to sparse matrix block-encodings and near-optimal quantum algorithms for sparse Hamiltonian simulation and QLSP.

Abstract: Linear combination of unitaries (LCU) is a fundamental primitive in quantum algorithms, whose cost is typically governed by the most expensive unitary appearing in the combination. We develop a transducer-based LCU framework that reduces this worst-case dependence to a weighted average query complexity, when the constituent unitaries share access to a common set of primitive oracles.
  Consider $A=\sum_j c_j U_j$, where $c_j>0$ and each unitary $U_j$ can be implemented using $C_j$ primitive queries. Given an upper bound $a\geq \|A\|$, our algorithm implements a block-encoding of $A/α$ with rescaling factor $α=\mathcal{O}(a)$, using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}} (C_{\max}+\overline{C} λ/{a} )$ primitive queries, where $λ=\sum_j c_j$, $C_{\max}=\max_j C_j$, and $\overline{C}= {\sum_j c_j C_j}/λ$. By comparison, the standard LCU construction requires $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}} (C_{\max} λ/{a} )$ primitive queries. The improvement can therefore be substantial when costly unitaries have small weights and $λ/a$ is large. As applications, we obtain improved block-encodings of sparse matrices, leading to quantum algorithms for sparse Hamiltonian simulation and quantum linear systems with near-optimal query complexity up to polylogarithmic factors in all relevant parameters.
  Our main technique is the transducer framework developed by Belovs, Jeffery, and Yolcu [Quantum, 8:1444 (2024)]. Here we develop a complementary operator-level theory tailored to block-encodings. We identify the resolvent norm $K$ as a key complexity measure for transducer implementation besides the existing catalyst complexity. We show that the transducer action can be converted into an $ε$-approximate block-encoding using only $\mathcal{O}\left(K \log(1/ε)\right)$ queries to the transducer, improving the precision dependence from polynomial to logarithmic.

</details>


### [145] [Oscillator-Qubit Primitives for a Molecular Quantum Dynamics Simulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40078)
*Jungsoo Hong,Joonsuk Huh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes near-optimal quantum primitives for molecular quantum dynamics, synthesizing nonlinear and multimode bosonic phase gates via generalized Jacobi-Anger expansions within QSP, achieving ~one-third shorter benchmark circuits and identifying a concrete hardware checkpoint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating molecular quantum dynamics with both electrons and nuclei treated quantum mechanically is essential for predicting chemical reactions, but classical computation scales exponentially. Oscillator-qubit processors (trapped-ion, circuit QED) can represent electronic states with qubits and vibrations with oscillators, but sequential gate application accumulates cost and error, limiting simulation duration. Existing product-formula and direct QSP approaches are not cost-optimal.

Method: The authors develop near-optimal quantum primitives covering representations of both particle types and their interactions. They synthesize nonlinear and multimode bosonic phase gates using generalized Jacobi-Anger (GJA) expansions within quantum signal processing (QSP), with cost growing near-optimally in both interaction strength and precision. The interaction model and gate synthesis each carry separate accuracy settings, tuned jointly.

Result: Tuned accuracy settings make the multimode benchmark circuits about one-third shorter than the previous direct QSP approach. Simulating energy exchange between stretching and bending vibrations of a simplified CO2 model under circuit-QED noise, a short simulation narrowly misses accuracy criteria at qubit and oscillator lifetimes near 2 ms and 5 ms.

Conclusion: The GJA-based QSP primitives provide near-optimal cost scaling for anharmonic multimode molecular quantum dynamics. The identified qubit and oscillator lifetimes mark a concrete checkpoint toward simulating such dynamics on near-term oscillator-qubit processors.

Abstract: Molecular quantum dynamics simulations that treat both electrons and nuclei quantum mechanically are crucial for predicting chemical reactions. With classical computation, a full wave-function representation of both types of particles requires resources that grow exponentially with their number. Oscillator-qubit processors, including trapped-ion and circuit QED devices, represent electronic states with qubits and nuclear vibrations with oscillators. Their native operations produce linear vibronic interactions, from which programmable anharmonic interactions can be built. Product-formula approaches build the corresponding phase gates by repeating small noncommuting phase steps, and quantum signal processing (QSP) approaches approximate each gate directly with a Fourier series. Simulations apply many such gates in sequence, so accumulated cost and error limit how long the dynamics can be followed. We propose near-optimal quantum primitives for molecular quantum dynamics simulation, covering the representations of both types of particles and their interactions. We synthesize nonlinear and multimode bosonic phase gates using generalized Jacobi-Anger (GJA) expansions within QSP, with a cost that grows near-optimally in both interaction strength and precision. Our interaction model and gate synthesis each have their own accuracy setting. Chosen together, these settings make our multimode benchmark circuits about one-third shorter than those of the previous direct QSP approach. We then simulate energy exchange between the stretching and bending vibrations of a simplified carbon dioxide model. With circuit-QED noise, a short simulation narrowly misses our accuracy criteria at qubit and oscillator lifetimes near 2 ms and 5 ms. These lifetimes mark a concrete checkpoint on the way to simulating anharmonic multimode molecular quantum dynamics on near-term oscillator-qubit processors.

</details>


### [146] [Universal Entanglement Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40080)
*Salvatore Tirone,Francesco Anna Mele,Vittorio Giovannetti,Ludovico Lami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A universal protocol distills entanglement from any unknown bipartite state at the optimal asymptotic rate (the state's distillable entanglement), with no rate penalty for not knowing the state. It combines LOCC tomography with adaptive distillation and a local robustness lemma; the worst-case optimal uniform rate over a family equals the minimum distillable entanglement on the family's closure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard entanglement distillation assumes the bipartite state is known. In practice the state is unknown, so a natural question arises: does ignorance of the state force an asymptotic rate penalty relative to the known-state optimum (the distillable entanglement)? The paper aims to eliminate this gap and also address worst-case distillation over families of states.

Method: A universal protocol for unknown bipartite states in fixed finite dimension: (1) tomography of the state via local operations and classical communication (LOCC); (2) an adaptive distillation routine that updates based on estimates; (3) a local robustness lemma to control finite-sample estimation errors so that near-optimal rates are maintained despite statistical uncertainty. Worst-case analysis over a family is derived via the closure of the family and the distillable entanglement functional.

Result: The protocol asymptotically attains every ebit rate below the true distillable entanglement of any unknown state, with vanishing trace-distance error—i.e., no asymptotic rate penalty for ignorance. The optimal uniform rate for a family of states equals the minimum distillable entanglement over the closure of that family.

Conclusion: Ignorance of a bipartite state does not incur an asymptotic rate penalty: universal, state-agnostic distillation matches the known-state optimum. Worst-case uniform distillation is characterized by the minimum distillable entanglement on the family's closure.

Abstract: We present a universal entanglement distillation protocol that asymptotically achieves the distillable entanglement of any unknown bipartite state in a fixed finite dimension. That is, its ebit yield attains every rate below the optimum that would be available if the state were known, with vanishing trace-distance error. Our approach combines tomography by local operations and classical communication with an adaptive distillation routine, using a local robustness lemma to control finite-sample estimation errors. We also characterize worst-case distillation from a family of states: the optimal uniform rate is the minimum distillable entanglement on the family's closure. These results show that ignorance of the state need not incur an asymptotic rate penalty.

</details>


### [147] [Super-Quadratic Quantum Speedups for Combinatorial Optimization via Tilted Walks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40088)
*Guneykan Ozgul,Shouvanik Chakrabarti*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "quantum tilted walks," a framework that averages powers of a tilted Hamiltonian biasing a mixer's discriminant matrix with the objective function, achieving super-quadratic speedups over the classical conditioning-and-search baseline for weighted MAX-Ek-LIN2 and MAX-k-CSP, without requiring ground-state preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantum short-path algorithms achieve super-quadratic speedups over exhaustive search but only sub-quadratic speedups over the stronger classical conditioning-and-search algorithm of Le Gall and Tamaki. This weakens the claimed quantum advantage, motivating a framework that outperforms the stronger classical baseline.

Method: The framework applies an average of powers of a tilted Hamiltonian that biases the discriminant matrix of a base Markov chain (mixer) with the objective function. For maximization problems, conditions are derived under which tilted walks amplify the amplitude on a high-objective target state when initialized from a lower-objective state.

Result: Under the same assumptions, tilted walks yield super-quadratic speedups over conditioning-and-search for weighted MAX-Ek-LIN2 and weighted MAX-k-CSP. The framework recovers quantum short-path algorithms as a special case and is demonstrated on a synthetic problem where tilted walks achieve a super-quadratic speedup but short-path algorithms do not.

Conclusion: Quantum tilted walks provide a more general and powerful algorithmic framework that bypasses the limitations of short-path algorithms—requiring neither ground-state preparation nor ground-state initialization in the base mixer—while establishing genuine super-quadratic quantum advantage over the strongest known classical baseline.

Abstract: We introduce quantum tilted walks, a quantum algorithmic framework for solving exact combinatorial optimization problems. The framework applies an average of powers of a tilted Hamiltonian that biases the discriminant matrix of a base Markov chain (mixer) with the objective function. Our starting point is quantum short-path algorithms, which prepare the ground state of such a Hamiltonian and obtain super-quadratic speedups over exhaustive search for certain combinatorial optimization problems. Recently, Le Gall and Tamaki~(arXiv:2604.12131) developed a classical conditioning-and-search algorithm for weighted MAX-E$k$-LIN2 and weighted MAX-$k$-CSP. Under the same assumptions, their algorithm is only sub-quadratically slower than quantum short-path algorithms. Consequently, existing short-path algorithms do not establish a super-quadratic speedup over this stronger classical baseline. For maximization problems, we give conditions under which tilted walks increase the amplitude on the target state with high objective value when initialized from a starting state with lower objective value. This framework captures conditioning-and-search and yields super-quadratic speedups over it for the same problems. While our framework recovers quantum short-path algorithms as a special case, it neither requires ground-state preparation nor initialization in the ground state of the base mixer. We demonstrate these advantages on a synthetic optimization problem for which tilted walks achieve a super-quadratic speedup whereas the short-path algorithms do not.

</details>


### [148] [Accelerating Quantum Dense-Output Simulation through Locality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40094)
*Songqinghao Yang,Chang Liu,Junkai Wang,Jingxuan Zhang,Jin-Peng Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a locality-based truncation framework for computing time-accumulated (dense) observables in lattice Hamiltonian simulations, replacing Hamiltonian-norm-dependent query costs with local-simulation bounds tightened via Lieb-Robinson bounds, applicable to fermions, bosons, and Lindbladians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most quantum-simulation algorithms target a final state or single-time expectation value, but many applications instead require the integrated signal J_H(O)=∫_0^T ⟨ψ(t)|O(t)|ψ(t)⟩dt (a dense output). Prior Hamiltonian-simulation-based dense-output algorithms have query costs scaling with the norm of the full Hamiltonian, which grows with system size, making them inefficient. Since simulation can be optimized when system structure is known, there is a need for a structure-aware approach.

Method: The authors develop a locality-based truncation framework for the dense-output problem on lattice Hamiltonians. They truncate the Hamiltonian and tighten the resulting truncation error using Lieb-Robinson bounds, showing that a local simulation suffices to recover the dense output. They bound the quantum simulation error arising from spatial truncation and translate it into local-simulation query bounds. The analysis covers free and interacting fermionic and bosonic systems, and open quantum systems governed by Lindbladians.

Result: The truncation-based algorithm yields query-cost improvements relative to norm-dependent approaches that range from constant to unbounded as system size grows. The error from spatial truncation is explicitly bounded and mapped to local-simulation query complexity. Illustrative applications and numerical experiments corroborate the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: Exploiting known lattice structure through locality-based truncation enables efficient dense-output quantum simulation with improved, system-size-scaling query bounds across closed and open quantum systems, offering a practical route to estimating time-accumulated observables.

Abstract: Estimating time-accumulated observables, or dense outputs, is central to extracting classical information from quantum simulations. Whereas most quantum-simulation algorithms aim to reproduce a final state or a single-time expectation value, many applications require the integrated signal $J_H(O)=\int_0^T \langle ψ(t)|O(t)|ψ(t)\rangle\,dt$. Previous Hamiltonian-simulation-based dense-output algorithms have query costs that depend on the norm of the full Hamiltonian, which can grow with system size. However, one can naturally optimize the simulation algorithm when the system structure is known. Consequently, we develop a locality-based truncation framework for the dense-output problem on lattice Hamiltonians. By truncating the Hamiltonian and tightening the resulting error with Lieb-Robinson bounds, we show that a local simulation can recover the dense output. We bound the quantum simulation error from spatial truncation and translate it into local-simulation query bounds. Our analysis includes free and interacting systems for both fermions and bosons, as well as open quantum systems governed by Lindbladians. The improvement from our truncation algorithm varies from constant to unbounded as the system size grows. We also give example applications and conduct relevant numerical experiments to support our findings.

</details>


### [149] [Classical Shadows with Selective GHZ Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40095)
*Nicolas Faroß,Marc Wanner,Pingal Pratyush Nath,Akshay Gaikwad,Anton Frisk Kockum,Devdatt Dubhashi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: SGM shadows generalize classical shadows by using GHZ-basis measurements on random qubit subsets plus computational-basis measurements on the complement, with CNOT cost linear in qubit number, exact shadow channel/inverse characterization, and polynomial shadow-norm scaling for fixed X/Y-locality, outperforming local Pauli and global Clifford shadows in certain regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical shadow efficiency depends critically on the measurement ensemble. Global Clifford circuits enable strong guarantees but are generally hard to implement, while local Pauli shadows are practical but weaker. A practically feasible ensemble is needed that interpolates between these extremes and can be tuned to observable structure.

Method: The paper introduces selective GHZ measurement (SGM) shadows: GHZ-basis measurements are performed on randomly selected subsets of qubits and computational-basis measurements on the complement. The number of CNOT gates scales linearly with the number of qubits. The authors tune the protocol via the subset distribution, derive the exact shadow channel and its inverse for arbitrary subset distributions, and prove a shadow-norm bound using discrete Fourier analysis and Markov semigroup theory.

Result: For natural subset distributions, the shadow norm scales polynomially for observables with fixed X/Y-locality. Numerical simulations up to 50 qubits confirm improvements for nonlocal observables and long-range systems, and show that subset distributions can be tailored to settings such as fermionic modes under the Jordan-Wigner transformation, outperforming local Pauli and global Clifford shadows in certain scenarios.

Conclusion: SGM shadows provide a practically implementable, tunable measurement ensemble that bridges local Pauli and global Clifford shadows, with rigorous guarantees and demonstrated advantages for structured nonlocal observables and long-range systems.

Abstract: Classical shadows allow the prediction of many quantum-state properties from measurement data, but their efficiency strongly depends on the underlying measurement ensemble. We introduce selective GHZ measurement (SGM) shadows, which perform Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ)-basis measurements on randomly selected subsets of qubits and computational-basis measurements on their complement. The corresponding quantum circuits are practically feasible, with their number of CNOT gates scaling linearly with the number of qubits. This is in stark contrast to global Clifford circuits, which are generally difficult to implement. Varying the subset distribution allows tuning the SGM shadow protocol for different classes of observables with prescribed X/Y support, allowing us to outperform local Pauli as well as global Clifford shadows in certain scenarios. We determine the exact shadow channel and its inverse for arbitrary subset distributions and prove an explicit bound on the shadow norm using methods from discrete Fourier analysis and Markov semigroup theory. For natural choices of subset distributions, this gives a polynomial scaling for observables with fixed X/Y-locality. Numerical simulations of up to 50 qubits support the predicted improvements for nonlocal observables and long-range systems, and demonstrate the practical benefits of our protocol's generality by showing how the subset distributions can be tailored to settings such as fermionic modes under the Jordan-Wigner transformation.

</details>


### [150] [Finding gflow on unlabelled open graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40099)
*Miriam Backens,Victor Gasse,Piotr Mitosek,Robert Raussendorf*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an O(n^3) algorithm that, given an unlabelled open graph state, finds a measurement labelling and a compatible gflow for one-way quantum computing, extending prior gflow-finding methods to incompletely specified computations and constrained measurement orders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In the one-way (measurement-based) quantum computing model, gflow is necessary and sufficient for deterministic computations and is central to efficient translation into quantum circuits. Prior algorithms assume the computation is fully specified (measurements already fixed) and only need to find a gflow. Practical and theoretical scenarios—especially photonics and optimisation—often present an incompletely specified computation where the resource graph state, inputs, and outputs are known but the measurement labelling is not. A method to simultaneously discover a valid labelling and compatible gflow was lacking.

Method: The authors develop an algorithm operating on an unlabelled open graph (graph state with known inputs and outputs but unspecified measurements). It identifies a measurement labelling together with a compatible gflow in O(n^3) time, strictly generalising the previous gflow-only approach (which also ran in O(n^3)). The algorithm additionally accepts restrictions on the measurement order and returns only solutions compatible with those constraints. The paper also proves that an open graph with equal numbers of inputs and outputs admits at most one gflow-compatible labelling, and shows how to add extra inputs to an open graph lacking the maximal number without breaking an existing gflow. Finally, it establishes a relationship between inputs (or potential inputs) and the information flow of the computation.

Result: The proposed algorithm finds a measurement labelling and compatible gflow for an unlabelled open graph in O(n^3) time, matching the complexity of the prior gflow-finding method while solving the strictly more general problem. It handles measurement-order restrictions by returning only constraint-compatible solutions. The paper proves uniqueness of the gflow-compatible labelling when input and output counts are equal, provides a constructive method to add inputs without breaking an existing gflow, and demonstrates a link between inputs/potential inputs and information flow.

Conclusion: Gflow-finding can be extended efficiently to incompletely specified one-way computations, where both the measurement labelling and the gflow are unknown. The O(n^3) algorithm strictly generalises earlier work, supports measurement-order constraints, and is complemented by structural results: uniqueness of gflow-compatible labellings under balanced input/output counts, a technique for augmenting inputs while preserving gflow, and a characterisation of information flow via inputs and potential inputs.

Abstract: The one-way model of measurement-based quantum computing implements computations via successive adaptive single-qubit measurements on a resource graph state. This model has practical applications, particularly in photonics, and it is also useful as a theoretical tool e.g. for optimisation. Gflow is a necessary and sufficient condition for implementing certain one-way computations deterministically (in a suitable sense); it is also used in efficient translations from the one-way model to quantum circuits. For a computation on $n$ qubits, a gflow can be found in $\mathcal{O}(n^3)$ time.
  Here, we consider an incompletely specified computation given by an unlabelled open graph: the graph state as well as the input and output qubits are known, but the measurements have not yet been fixed. We give an algorithm that identifies a measurement labelling and a compatible gflow, and runs in $\mathcal{O}(n^3)$, strictly generalising the previous approach. The new algorithm can also handle restrictions on the order of the measurements and returns only solutions compatible with these constraints. We additionally prove that if an open graph has equal numbers of inputs and outputs, it has at most one labelling compatible with gflow; and show how to identify additional inputs for an open graph that does not yet have the maximal number, without breaking an existing gflow. Finally, we demonstrate a relationship between inputs or potential inputs and the information flow in the computation.

</details>


### [151] [Logarithmic-Depth Fermion Sampling: Anticoncentration and Average-Case Hardness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40100)
*Natansh Mathur,Iordanis Kerenidis*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Logarithmic-depth fermionic sampling with independent Haar two-mode gates matches the passive-Haar ensemble's anticoncentration and high-precision average-case #P-hardness, using only O(n log n) gates instead of quadratic, via a magic input that suppresses the slowest relaxation mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling problems give clear conditional quantum-classical separations; Fermion Sampling uses passive linear optics on a non-Gaussian magic input. Global Haar randomness gives anticoncentration and average-case hardness but needs linear depth and quadratically many gates. The open question is whether linear depth is actually necessary.

Method: Fresh uniform matchings with independent Haar two-mode gates; collision reaches the passive-Haar scale at a sharp threshold of log n / log(9/4) ≈ 0.855 log2 n layers, with an explicit limiting transition profile and a matching lower bound from two-particle correlations. Hard instances are built in four native layers, embedded in typical schedules, and interpolated along Cayley paths using a rational linear-program decoder; a finite 192-gate alphabet preserves the collision law exactly.

Result: Logarithmic depth suffices for both anticoncentration and hardness guarantees in the same ensemble. The mechanism is input-dependent: the magic input suppresses the extensive weight of the slowest relaxation mode, leaving the next to set the scale; an occupation-basis input retains large collision even under full Haar randomness. At larger logarithmic depth, average-case #P-hardness of estimating output probabilities to additive error 2^{-O(n log^2 n)} on any fixed fraction of instances above 3/4 is proved in the real-RAM model.

Conclusion: Both guarantees use logarithmic native fermionic depth between arbitrary mode pairs and O(n log n) gates, replacing the global-Haar construction's quadratic budget. The proved accuracy is finer than the 1/N scale, where N = C(n, n/2), required by sampling-to-counting; hardness of sampling within constant total-variation distance remains open.

Abstract: Sampling problems give some of the clearest conditional separations between quantum and classical computation. In Fermion Sampling, passive linear optics acts on a non-Gaussian magic input. Globally Haar-random transformations give anticoncentration and high-precision average-case hardness, but require linear depth and quadratically many gates. A natural open question is whether linear depth is necessary?
  We show that logarithmic depth suffices for both guarantees in the same ensemble. For fresh uniform matchings with independent Haar two-mode gates, the collision reaches the passive-Haar scale at a sharp threshold of $\log n/\log(9/4)\approx0.855\log_2 n$ layers, with an explicit limiting transition profile and a matching lower bound from two-particle correlations. The mechanism is input-dependent: the magic input suppresses the extensive weight of the slowest relaxation mode, leaving the next to set the scale. An occupation-basis input retains large collision even under full Haar randomness.
  At a larger logarithmic depth, we prove, in the real-RAM model, average-case $\#\mathsf{P}$-hardness of estimating output probabilities to additive error $2^{-O(n\log^2 n)}$ on any fixed fraction of instances above $3/4$. We construct hard instances in four native layers, embed them in typical schedules, and interpolate along Cayley paths using a rational linear-program decoder. A finite $192$-gate alphabet preserves the collision law exactly. Both guarantees use logarithmic native fermionic depth between arbitrary mode pairs and $O(n\log n)$ gates, replacing the global-Haar construction's quadratic budget. The accuracy proved is finer than the $1/N$ scale, where $N=\binom{n}{n/2}$, required by sampling-to-counting; hardness of sampling within constant total-variation distance remains open.

</details>


### [152] [Harnessing problem structure for end-to-end quantum speed-ups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40105)
*Qifan Jiang,Xiao-Ming Zhang,Debin Xiang,Xiao Yuan,Liqiang Lu,Jianwei Yin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a structure-aware quantum data encoding framework that compiles recursive descriptions of problem structure into efficient state-preparation circuits, showing that exploiting problem structure lets quantum speed-ups survive end-to-end cost accounting, including state preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum speed-ups may vanish once structure-agnostic classical data encoding costs are counted, but real problems often have rich internal structure. The key question is whether this structure can be harnessed so quantum advantages hold under full end-to-end cost accounting.

Method: A general framework for structure-aware quantum data encoding that compiles compact recursive descriptions of problem structure into efficient state-preparation circuits, translating structural information directly into reduced encoding complexity. Applied to uncapacitated facility location and extended to structured quantum states for combinatorial optimization capturing constraint relations (group balance, conflicts, synergistic rewards).

Result: For uncapacitated facility location, structure-agnostic encoding admits a classical dequantization that removes the quadratic quantum speed-up, while exploiting problem structure restores the advantage even including state-preparation costs. The framework also extends super-polynomial quantum advantages to broader classes of objectives.

Conclusion: Exploitable problem structure acts as a computational resource for quantum algorithms and offers a systematic route toward realizing quantum advantage in data-intensive problems.

Abstract: Quantum algorithms can offer substantial computational speed-ups, yet these advantages may disappear once the cost of structure-agnostic classical data encoding is taken into account. Real-world problem instances, however, often possess rich internal structure. This raises a fundamental question: can such structure be harnessed to make quantum speed-ups survive end-to-end cost accounting? Here we show that it can. We introduce a general framework for structure-aware quantum data encoding that compiles compact recursive descriptions of problem structure into efficient state-preparation circuits, translating structural information directly into reduced encoding complexity. For the uncapacitated facility-location problem, structure-agnostic encoding admits a classical dequantization that eliminates the quadratic quantum speed-up, whereas exploiting the underlying problem structure restores this advantage even when state-preparation costs are included. More broadly, the same framework prepares structured quantum states for combinatorial optimization that capture non-trivial relations among constraints, including group balance, conflicts and synergistic rewards, extending previously reported super-polynomial quantum advantages to broader classes of objectives. These results establish exploitable problem structure as a computational resource for quantum algorithms and provide a systematic route towards realizing quantum advantage in data-intensive problems.

</details>


### [153] [A Complete and Natural Rule Set for Multi-Qudit Clifford Circuits in All Odd Prime Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40106)
*Xiaoning Bian,Sarah Meng Li,Neil J. Ross,John van de Wetering,Yuming Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a complete set of 16 rewrite rules for multi-qudit Clifford circuits in all odd prime dimensions, proven complete at both the symplectic and Clifford levels and formally verified in Agda.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establishing a complete, intuitive rewrite system for multi-qudit Clifford circuits enables canonical reasoning about circuit equivalence, which is valuable for quantum circuit optimization and formal verification, but no such complete rule set existed across all odd prime dimensions.

Method: Work first at the symplectic level via the isomorphism between Sp(2n, Z_d) and the Clifford group modulo the Pauli group; construct a circuit normal form capturing the stabiliser tableau and unique up to Pauli correction; derive complete symplectic relations from this normal form; lift them to Clifford relations by adding Pauli corrections.

Result: A complete set of 16 rewrite rules, each involving at most three qudits with intuitive interpretations, sufficient to rewrite any two Clifford circuits representing the same linear map into each other; completeness verified up to global phase in the Agda proof assistant.

Conclusion: The 16 rules form a complete and intuitive rewriting system for multi-qudit Clifford circuits in all odd prime dimensions, with completeness established mathematically and formally verified in Agda.

Abstract: We present a complete set of $16$ rewrite rules for multi-qudit Clifford circuits in all odd prime dimensions. Completeness means that any two Clifford circuits representing the same linear map can be rewritten into each other using these rules. Each rule involves at most three qudits and admits an intuitive interpretation. To establish completeness, we first work at the symplectic level, using the isomorphism between the symplectic group $\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z}_d)$ and the quotient of the Clifford group by the Pauli group. We construct a circuit normal form that captures the stabiliser tableau of a Clifford operator and is unique up to Pauli correction. Using this normal form, we derive a complete set of symplectic relations, which we then lift to Clifford relations by incorporating Pauli corrections. We also formally verify completeness up to global phase in the Agda proof assistant.

</details>


### [154] [Verification Complexity and Extension of Classical Shadows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40107)
*Georgios Karaiskos,Asad Raza,Dorian Rudolph,Dax Enshan Koh,Sevag Gharibian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies two problems on classical shadows: verifying that a shadow S originates from a genuine n-qubit state (Classical Shadow Validity, CSV), and spoofing a shadow capturing 2-local properties to predict 3-local properties. CSV is efficiently solvable for permutation-invariant shadows, QMA-hard for real/fermionic/bosonic shadows, and both coNP-hard and QMA-hard for Pauli-string observables. Spoofing 2-locals into 3-locals is intractable unless QCMA ⊆ BPP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical shadows compress copies of a quantum state into classical data, enabling prediction of many state properties from few copies. Two natural questions arise: (1) when can one efficiently verify that a given shadow S truly came from a genuine n-qubit state (Classical Shadow Validity, introduced by Karaiskos et al., ICALP 2026)? (2) given a shadow capturing 2-local properties of a state, can one fake a shadow that predicts 3-local properties? The paper aims to characterize the computational complexity of these verification and spoofing tasks.

Method: Complexity-theoretic analysis. For CSV, results are established across different shadow families: permutation-invariant, real, fermionic, and bosonic shadows, and the family of all n-qubit Pauli strings. A key technical ingredient is a new upper bound qc-Σ2 ⊆ P^PP, where qc-Σ2 is a quantum analogue of the second level of the polynomial hierarchy with a quantum first proof. For the spoofing question, a reduction relates estimating 3-local marginals from 2-local marginals to a hardness assumption against QCMA.

Result: For CSV: it is efficiently solvable for permutation-invariant shadows; QMA-hard for real, fermionic, and bosonic shadows; and both coNP-hard and QMA-hard when the observable family is all n-qubit Pauli strings. An independent result is the upper bound qc-Σ2 ⊆ P^PP. For spoofing: estimating the 3-local marginals of ρ from its 2-local marginals S is intractable unless QCMA ⊆ BPP, even when ρ is the unique state consistent with S.

Conclusion: Classical Shadow Validity has sharply varying complexity depending on the shadow family: tractable for permutation-invariant shadows, but QMA-hard (and coNP-hard for Pauli strings) in general. Spoofing 2-local information to infer 3-local properties is computationally intractable under standard complexity assumptions (QCMA ⊆ BPP), even under uniqueness of the consistent state. The new bound qc-Σ2 ⊆ P^PP is of independent interest for quantum complexity theory.

Abstract: Classical shadows are an influential framework for compressing copies of a given quantum state $ρ$ into classical data $S$, enabling many properties of $ρ$ to be predicted from relatively few copies. In this work, we study two natural questions involving shadows: (1) Given $S$, when can one efficiently verify that $S$ came from a genuine $n$-qubit state? This is called the Classical Shadow Validity (CSV) problem, introduced by Karaiskos, Rudolph, Meyer, Eisert, and Gharibian [ICALP 2026]. (2) Given $S$ that allows one to capture 2-local properties of $ρ$, can one fake or spoof a shadow $S'$ which predicts 3-local properties of some state? For (1), we show CSV is efficiently solvable for permutation-invariant shadows, QMA-hard for real, fermionic, and bosonic shadows, and both coNP-hard and QMA-hard when the observable family consists of all $n$-qubit Pauli strings. A result of independent interest along the way is a new upper bound qc-$Σ_2$ $\subseteq$ $\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{PP}}$, where qc-$Σ_2$ is a quantum analogue of the second level of the polynomial hierarchy in which the first proof is quantum. For (2), we show intractability: Given the 2-local marginals $S$ of a quantum state $ρ$, estimating the 3-local marginals of $ρ$ is intractable unless QCMA $\subseteq$ BPP, even if the state $ρ$ is the unique state consistent with $S$.

</details>


### [155] [Quantum Advantage of Permutation-Invariant Functions in Communication Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40116)
*Yunqi Huang,Zekun Ye*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Symmetry in two-party communication complexity is shown to sharply constrain quantum advantage: for permutation-invariant partial functions over any fixed alphabet, public-coin randomized complexity is at most quadratic (up to log factors) in quantum complexity, while growing alphabets and graph symmetries allow exponential separations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates how symmetry limits quantum advantage in two-party communication complexity, aiming to generalize prior binary-alphabet results for permutation-invariant functions and to determine when large separations between quantum and randomized communication are possible.

Method: For partial functions over any fixed alphabet of size q invariant under simultaneous coordinate permutations, the authors prove an upper bound relating public-coin randomized communication to entanglement-assisted quantum communication, and characterize quantum communication complexity via a combinatorial parameter up to logarithmic factors. They also construct permutation-invariant functions over growing alphabets and graph-invariant functions exhibiting exponential separations, using protocols without prior entanglement or shared randomness.

Result: For any fixed alphabet size q, permutation-invariant partial functions satisfy R^pub(f) = O_q(Q*(f)^2 log n). Input-length dependence is necessary: for every fixed ε∈(0,1), binary permutation-invariant partial functions can have quantum complexity O_ε(log log n) and randomized complexity Ω_ε((log n)^{1-ε}). With growing alphabets, length-n strings over an n-symbol alphabet yield quantum complexity O_ε(log n) versus randomized complexity Ω_ε(n^{1-ε}); graph-invariant functions on v-vertex graphs yield quantum complexity O_ε(log v) versus randomized complexity Ω_ε(v^{2-ε}).

Conclusion: Symmetry imposes strong constraints on quantum advantage for fixed-alphabet permutation-invariant problems, extending and improving prior binary-alphabet bounds, but exponential quantum-versus-randomized separations arise when alphabet size grows or graph symmetries are considered.

Abstract: We study how symmetry constrains quantum advantage in two-party communication complexity. For partial functions over any fixed alphabet of size $q$ that are invariant under simultaneous coordinate permutations, we prove that public-coin randomized and entanglement-assisted quantum communication complexities satisfy $R^{\mathrm{pub}}(f)=O_q(Q^{\ast}(f)^2\log n)$, where $n$ is the input length. We also characterize quantum communication complexity by a combinatorial parameter up to a logarithmic factor. These results extend the binary-alphabet result of Guan et al. and improve its logarithmic overhead. Input-length dependence is necessary: for every fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, binary permutation-invariant partial functions can have quantum complexity $O_\varepsilon(\log\log n)$ and randomized complexity $Ω_\varepsilon((\log n)^{1-\varepsilon})$.
  Growing alphabets and graph symmetries permit exponential separations. For every fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, we construct permutation-invariant partial functions on length-$n$ strings over an $n$-symbol alphabet with quantum complexity $O_\varepsilon(\log n)$ and randomized complexity $Ω_\varepsilon(n^{1-\varepsilon})$. We also construct graph-invariant partial functions on $v$-vertex graphs with quantum complexity $O_\varepsilon(\log v)$ and randomized complexity $Ω_\varepsilon(v^{2-\varepsilon})$. All separation protocols use neither prior entanglement nor shared randomness.

</details>


### [156] [Optimal Resource Scaling for Early Fault Tolerant Iterative Quantum Phase Estimation under Cost Error Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40128)
*Mrudula A Mahindrakar,Avhishek Chatterjee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives closed-form optimal shot allocations N_k* per iteration for iterative quantum phase estimation (IPEA) under a total resource budget, maximizing the probability all L phase bits are correct. Solutions are obtained via surrogate concentration bounds and yield simple resource-allocation rules of thumb.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IPEA is preferable to the original QPE for NISQ and early fault-tolerant hardware since it avoids the inverse quantum Fourier transform. However, error mitigation and correction make unitaries imperfect, causing erroneous phase feedback. Reliability is improved by repeating the k-th iteration 2^k-power unitary N_k* times with majority voting, but iterations differ in circuit complexity, giving varying resource costs w_k and per-shot error probabilities q_k. Under a tight total budget, the optimal per-bit shot counts are unclear.

Method: The problem is framed as maximizing P(all L bits correct) subject to sum_k w_k N_k <= W. The authors construct tractable upper and lower surrogate problems using tight concentration and anti-concentration bounds, solve them, and show their solutions converge when q_k << 1. Closed-form expressions for N_k* are derived under conditions W >> sum_k w_k, w_k increasing in k, small q_k, and large L.

Result: When W >> sum_k w_k ln(sum_k w_k), the optimal N_k* is proportional to ln(sum_k w_k)/c_k and is independent of individual w_k, where c_k = -ln(2 sqrt((1-q_k/2) q_k/2)) ∝ ln(1/q_k). When sum_k w_k << W << sum_k w_k ln(sum_k w_k), the allocation is additionally influenced (additively) by (1/c_k) ln(1/w_k) for larger k, i.e., higher powers of the unitary. These results translate into simple rules of thumb.

Conclusion: Optimal per-iteration shot allocation in IPEA admits closed-form solutions that depend primarily on per-shot error probabilities via c_k ∝ ln(1/q_k), with a secondary dependence on resource costs w_k at large k in the intermediate budget regime. The derived rules of thumb can guide practical resource allocation in IPEA-based systems.

Abstract: The iterative quantum phase estimation algorithm (IPEA) is better suited for NISQ and early fault-tolerant hardware compared to the original algorithm, which requires the inverse quantum Fourier transform. However, current error mitigation and correction implementations lead to imperfect unitaries, causing erroneous phase feedback in IPEA. To improve the reliability of phase bits is to repeat the k^th iteration for the 2^k-th power of the unitary $N_k^*$ times, followed by a majority decision. However, due to variations in their circuit complexities, different iterations incur varying resource costs ($w_k$) and different per-shot error probabilities ($q_k$). Given tight resource constraints, we ask what the optimal number $\{N_k^*: 1\le k \le L\}$ of shots per bit is: $$\arg \max_{\{N_k\}} \mathbb{P}(\text{all } L \text{ bits correct}) \text{ s.t.} \sum_{k=1}^L w_k N_k \le W$$ We obtain closed-form expressions for $N^*_k$ under the conditions: $W \gg \sum_k w_k $(enough for at least one shot per iteration), $w_k$ increases with $k$, $q_k$ are small enough and $L$ is large. We do so by analyzing tractable upper and lower surrogate problems derived via tight concentration and anti-concentration bounds and showing that their solutions converge when $q_k\ll 1$.
  We observe that when $W\gg \sum_k w_k \ln\! \left(\sum_k w_k\right)$, the optimal $N_k^*$ is proportional to $\frac{\ln\! \left(\sum_k w_k\right)}{c_k}$ and does not depend on individual $w_k$, where $c_k=-\ln\left(2\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{q_k}{2} \right) \frac{q_k}{2}}\right) \propto \ln\frac{1}{q_k}$. However, when $\sum_k w_k \ll W\ll\sum_k w_k \ln\! \left(\sum_k w_k\right)$, the allocation is also (additively) influenced by $\frac{1}{c_k}\ln\frac{1}{w_k}$ for larger values of $k$, i.e., higher powers of unitary. These results lead to simple rules of thumb for resource allocation that may benefit the design of practical systems.

</details>


### [157] [Fast Cliffords When Your Quantum Memory Is Full](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40144)
*Marten Folkertsma,Ian Mertz,Sergii Strelchuk,Sathyawageeswar Subramanian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies catalytic implementations of quantum circuits, where dirty qubits can be used as workspace as long as their joint state (including entanglement) is restored exactly. It shows that Clifford circuits and higher Clifford-hierarchy gates admit shallow catalytic implementations matching clean-workspace depth asymptotically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Additional qubits can reduce quantum circuit depth by enabling parallelism, but standard constructions require this workspace to be initialized in a known (clean) state. This work asks whether dirty qubits can instead be used catalytically, i.e. with their joint state and entanglement restored exactly at the end, avoiding the need for clean workspace.

Method: The authors construct catalytic implementations of quantum circuits. They show every n-qubit Clifford circuit has a catalytic implementation of depth O(log n) using O(n^2/log^2 n) catalytic qubits and no clean qubits. They extend the approach to diagonal elements of any fixed level C_k of the Clifford hierarchy, and to semi-Clifford gates.

Result: Every n-qubit Clifford circuit admits a catalytic implementation of depth O(log n) with O(n^2/log^2 n) catalytic qubits and no clean qubits, matching the asymptotic depth achievable with clean workspace. For diagonal elements of any fixed level C_k of the Clifford hierarchy, catalytic implementations achieve depth O(log(n+1)) with O(n^k/log n) gates and O(n^k/log^2 n) catalytic qubits. The approach also extends to semi-Clifford gates.

Conclusion: Dirty qubits can serve as catalytic workspace, restoring their joint state and entanglement exactly, to implement Clifford circuits and higher Clifford-hierarchy gates at depths asymptotically matching clean-workspace constructions, without requiring any clean qubits.

Abstract: Additional qubits can reduce the depth of a quantum circuit by providing workspace for parallel computation, but standard constructions assume that this workspace is initialized in a known state. In this work we study catalytic implementations, i.e. asking whether dirty qubits can instead be used provided that their joint state including any entanglement with other registers is restored exactly at the end of the computation. We show that every $n$-qubit Clifford circuit has a catalytic implementation of depth $O(\log n)$ using $O(n^2/\log^2 n)$ catalytic qubits and no clean qubits, matching the asymptotic depth achievable when clean workspace is available. We extend this approach to diagonal elements of any fixed level $C_k$ of the Clifford hierarchy, which admit catalytic implementations of depth $O(\log(n+1))$ with $O(n^k/\log(n))$ gates and $O(n^k/\log^2(n))$ catalytic qubits, as well as to semi-Clifford Gates.

</details>


### [158] [QuLoC: Photonic Quantum-Assisted Low-Rank LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40146)
*Xiao-Hui Ni,Yu-Han Yao,Yu-Ze Zhu,Hang Song,Xiang Zhao,Lin Yang,Xian-Min Jin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QuLoC, a photonic quantum-assisted SVD-based LLM compression method, gates retained low-rank components with quantum circuit outputs, recovers performance via local functional reconstruction and end-to-end knowledge distillation, then absorbs gating into low-rank factors for classical inference, achieving a 9.73% relative average accuracy gain over SOTA baselines on Qwen3.5-4B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs grow in size, compression is increasingly important for efficient deployment. SVD-based low-rank compression reduces parameter counts but can degrade downstream task performance, so a method is needed to improve post-compression performance.

Method: QuLoC uses quantum circuit outputs to gate the retained low-rank components during training. Model performance is recovered through local functional reconstruction followed by end-to-end knowledge distillation. After training, the gating coefficients are absorbed into the low-rank factors, so the compressed model runs on classical hardware without executing quantum circuits at inference.

Result: On Qwen3.5-4B, QuLoC achieves a 9.73% relative improvement in average accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines across multiple downstream tasks. On LLaMA-7B at different compression ratios, it consistently achieves higher average downstream accuracy than SOTA baselines. Experiments on photonic quantum hardware retain these benefits with only a small accuracy loss relative to simulation, indicating robustness to hardware noise.

Conclusion: QuLoC is effective for SVD-based LLM compression, generalizes across models and compression ratios, and is robust to photonic quantum hardware noise. These results motivate further exploration of photonic quantum-assisted compression for larger models and more complex agentic tasks.

Abstract: As LLMs grow in size, compression becomes increasingly important for efficient deployment. SVD-based low-rank compression reduces parameter counts but can degrade downstream performance. To improve performance after compression, we introduce QuLoC, a photonic quantum-assisted LLM compression algorithm that uses quantum circuit outputs to gate the retained low-rank components during training. Model performance is recovered through local functional reconstruction followed by end-to-end knowledge distillation. After training, the gating coefficients are absorbed into the low-rank factors, allowing the compressed model to run on classical hardware without executing quantum circuits during inference. Experiments on Qwen3.5-4B show that QuLoC achieves a 9.73\% relative improvement in average accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines across multiple downstream tasks, demonstrating its effectiveness. We further evaluate QuLoC on LLaMA-7B at different parameter compression ratios. It consistently achieves higher average downstream accuracy than state-of-the-art baselines, supporting its applicability to a larger model across different compression settings. Notably, experiments using the photonic quantum hardware retain these benefits with a small accuracy loss relative to simulation, suggesting robustness to hardware noise. These results motivate further exploration of photonic quantum-assisted compression for larger models and more complex agentic tasks.

</details>


### [159] [Quantum Černý complexity of binary words](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40154)
*Pui Hang Lee,Pui-Yee Lee,Bjørn Kjos-Hanssen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces quantum Černý complexity qc(w), the minimal dimension of quantum channels synchronizing a binary word, shows a quadratic saving over the classical analogue (2 ≤ qc(w) ≤ ⌈√(|w|+1)⌉), proves constant words are extremal, demonstrates no quantum analogue of the Černý function exists, and studies a pure-target variant qcp, showing purity costs exactly one dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical Černý conjecture concerns the maximum length of a shortest synchronizing word for deterministic finite automata. The authors seek a quantum analogue by defining a quantum Černý complexity, motivated by understanding whether quantum channels can synchronize binary words more efficiently than classical automata and whether intuitive notions of descriptive complexity (like word length or structure) predict this complexity.

Method: They define qc(w) as the least dimension d for which quantum channels A_0, A_1 on d×d density matrices and a start state ρ_0 exist such that w is the unique shortest word whose channel is constant on the reachable set. They bound qc(w), construct explicit instances (e.g., a qubit rotation counter for qc(01^n0)=2), study the pure-target variant qcp, prove a dimension-2 impossibility result, exhibit an explicit qutrit construction combining coherent rotation with a measure-and-funnel channel, and show computability of qc via reduction to the first-order theory of the reals.

Result: They prove 2 ≤ qc(w) ≤ ⌈√(|w|+1)⌉ for every nonempty w, a quadratic saving over the classical analogue, with constant words extremal: qc(0^m)=⌈√(m+1)⌉. They show qc(01^n0)=2 for all n≥1, realized by a single qubit rotation counter, implying no quantum analogue of the Černý function and strong anti-correlation of qc with intuitive descriptive complexity. For qcp, they prove no word of length ≥2 in dimension 2 can be a unique shortest synchronizing word with pure target, and exhibit a qutrit instance achieving qcp(01^n0)=3 with a computational basis target and universal synchronization over all input states, showing purity costs exactly one dimension on this family. They also observe qc is computable via reduction to the first-order theory of the reals.

Conclusion: Quantum synchronization complexity exhibits a quadratic advantage over classical Černý complexity and lacks a well-defined quantum Černý function, with qc strongly anti-correlated with intuitive descriptive complexity. Requiring the synchronization target to be pure costs exactly one dimension for the family 01^n0, and qc is computable by reduction to the first-order theory of the reals.

Abstract: We introduce the quantum Černý complexity $\mathrm{qc}(w)$ of a binary word $w$: the least dimension $d$ for which there exist quantum channels $A_0,A_1$ on $d\times d$ density matrices and a start state $ρ_0$ such that $w$ is the unique shortest word whose associated channel is constant on the reachable set. We show that $2\le\mathrm{qc}(w)\le\lceil\sqrt{|w|+1}\,\rceil$ for every nonempty $w$, a quadratic saving over the classical analogue, and that constant words are extremal: $\mathrm{qc}(0^m)=\lceil\sqrt{m+1}\,\rceil$. In contrast, $\mathrm{qc}(01^n0)=2$ for every $n\ge 1$, realized by a single qubit whose rotation angle acts as a counter; consequently there is no quantum analogue of the Černý function, and $\mathrm{qc}$ is strongly anti-correlated with intuitive notions of descriptive complexity. We further study the variant $\mathrm{qcp}$ in which the synchronization target is required to be a pure state. We prove that in dimension $2$ no word of length at least $2$ can be a unique shortest synchronizing word with pure target, and we exhibit an explicit qutrit instance, combining a coherent rotation with a measure-and-funnel channel, achieving $\mathrm{qcp}(01^n0)=3$ with target a computational basis state and with synchronization holding universally over all input states. Thus purity of the reset state costs exactly one dimension on this family. We also observe that $\mathrm{qc}$ is computable, by reduction to the first-order theory of the reals.

</details>


### [160] [Classical and Quantum Simulation of All-to-All Quantum Dynamics in Logarithmic Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40162)
*Maximilian Lutz,Rahul Trivedi,J. Ignacio Cirac*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies classical and quantum complexity of estimating disorder-averaged quench dynamics for all-to-all spin Hamiltonians (quantum Hopfield, SK, Dicke-type). It gives a polynomial-time, O(log N)-space quantum algorithm for permutation-invariant k-local dynamics, shows a classical match would imply BQL ⊆ SC, provides short-time classical algorithms, and extends results to Hopfield models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disorder averaging restores permutation invariance, allowing N-spin states to be stored in O(log N) qubits. Log-space quantum computation is classically simulatable in poly time or polylog space, but whether one algorithm achieves both is open. This gap raises the possibility of simultaneous quantum advantage in both time and space for these models.

Method: Quantum algorithm for permutation-invariant k-local dynamics without disorder using polynomial time and O(log N) quantum and classical space. Complexity-theoretic reduction showing classical matching resources imply BQL ⊆ SC. For short times, explicit classical algorithms, including for SK models. For Hopfield models, disorder is dilated to ancilla qubits, reducing the problem to the disorder-free case on an enlarged system.

Result: A quantum algorithm achieving poly time with O(log N) space for permutation-invariant k-local dynamics; a conditional separation result: classical algorithms with the same resources would imply BQL ⊆ SC. Short-time classical algorithms exist, including for SK models. Hopfield models reduce to the disorder-free case via ancilla dilation, extending both classical and quantum results. Evidence suggests Hopfield long-time dynamics are non-trivial.

Conclusion: All-to-all spin models emerge as a candidate for a simultaneous time–space advantage in quantum simulation, since a single classical algorithm achieving both polynomial time and logarithmic space would have significant complexity-theoretic consequences.

Abstract: We study both the classical and the quantum complexity of the problem of estimating disorder-averaged quench dynamics for all-to-all spin Hamiltonians such as quantum Hopfield, Sherrington--Kirkpatrick (SK) or Dicke-type models. Disorder averaging restores permutation invariance in this setting, so states of $N$ spins can be stored in $\mathcal O(\log N)$ qubits. While logarithmic-space quantum computations are known to be simulatable classically in polynomial time or in polylogarithmic space, whether a single algorithm can achieve both is open, raising the possibility of simultaneous quantum advantage. To this end, we give a quantum algorithm for permutation-invariant $k$-local dynamics without disorder using polynomial time and $\mathcal O(\log N)$ quantum and classical space. We show that any classical algorithm matching these resources would imply $\mathsf{BQL}\subseteq \mathsf{SC}$. For short times, however, we provide such algorithms, including for SK models. For Hopfield models, we discuss how dilating the disorder to ancilla qubits reduces the problem to the disorder-free case on an enlarged system, thus extending both classical and quantum results to them. We give some evidence that their long-time dynamics are non-trivial. They thus emerge as a candidate for a simultaneous time--space advantage in quantum simulation.

</details>


### [161] [Exact spectral gaps for random Pauli rotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40164)
*Ziyuan Dong,Xiang Fan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors resolve the spectral-gap problem of Baer and Haah for random Pauli rotations: on SU(d) the gap is (d-8)/(8(d-1)) for n≥4 (disproving the conjectured formula), while the conjectured value d(d-3)/(8(d²-1)) is exactly the gap on PU(d) for n≥3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work addresses the spectral-gap problem posed by Baer and Haah, which concerns the gap of the random walk generated by random nonidentity Pauli rotations e^{iθP} on the special unitary and projective unitary groups. Resolving this clarifies a conjectured formula and the representation-theoretic structure that governs the gap.

Method: A random nonidentity n-qubit Pauli P and angle θ mod 2π are chosen uniformly and independently, with step e^{iθP} and d=2^n. The gap is analyzed on SU(d) and PU(d). Key ingredients: the spectrum of the graph of anticommuting Pauli operators, a minimum-weight bound for binary polynomials, and a local inequality on a single-qubit Clifford-fixed subspace. The lower bounds hold uniformly over all finite-dimensional unitary representations, and the proof is analytic without computational verification.

Result: On SU(d) the gap is (d-8)/(8(d-1)) for n≥4, disproving the conjectured full-special-unitary formula. The conjectured value d(d-3)/(8(d²-1)) is instead exactly the gap on PU(d) for n≥3. The SU gap is attained by an explicit vector in ⋀⁸ℂ^d built from affine three-dimensional subspaces of F₂^n, and its nontrivial central action explains why balanced tensor representations fail to detect the smaller gap. On the projective group the gap is attained in U ↦ U^{⊗4} ⊗ Ū^{⊗4}.

Conclusion: The Baer–Haah conjectured gap does not hold on the full special unitary group; it characterizes rather the projective unitary group. The two gaps differ and are witnessed by distinct representations, with the SU(d) gap governed by an explicit wedge-product vector carrying nontrivial central action.

Abstract: We resolve the spectral-gap problem posed by Baer and Haah for random Pauli rotations. In this walk, a nonidentity $n$-qubit Pauli operator $P$ and an angle $θ$ modulo $2π$ are chosen independently and uniformly, and the step is $e^{\mathrm{i}θP}$. Writing $d=2^n$, we prove that the gap on $\mathsf{SU}(d)$ is $(d-8)/(8(d-1))$ for $n\ge4$. This disproves their conjectured formula on the full special unitary group. We also prove that the conjectured value, $d(d-3)/(8(d^2-1))$, is exactly the gap on $\mathsf{PU}(d)$ for $n\ge3$. The special-unitary gap is attained by an explicit vector in $\bigwedge^8\mathbb C^d$, constructed from affine three-dimensional subspaces of $\mathbb F_2^n$. Its nontrivial central action explains why balanced tensor representations do not detect this smaller gap. On the projective group, the gap is attained in $U\mapsto U^{\otimes4}\otimes\bar U^{\otimes4}$. The matching lower bounds hold uniformly over all finite-dimensional unitary representations. They combine the spectrum of the graph of anticommuting Pauli operators, a minimum-weight bound for binary polynomials, and a local inequality on a single-qubit Clifford-fixed subspace. The proof is analytic and requires no computational verification.

</details>


### [162] [Shadow Quantum Singular Value Transformation with Shallow Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40167)
*Nai-Hui Chia,Hyunseong Kim,Chia-Ying Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Shadow QSVT estimates observable expectations ⟨ψ|f(H)†O_j f(H)|ψ⟩ without building a full block-encoding, using structure in the state and observables to reduce circuit depth, with three algorithms for state-aware, observable-aware, and classical-shadow settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard QSVT is resource-intensive because it constructs a unitary block-encoding of f(H). However, many applications only require estimates of observables rather than the full transformed unitary. Shadow QSVT exploits structure in the input state and observables to reduce the quantum resources, particularly circuit depth, needed for these computations.

Method: The paper formalizes Shadow QSVT: given |ψ⟩, Hermitian H, polynomial f, and observables {O_1,...,O_m}, estimate ⟨ψ|f(H)†O_j f(H)|ψ⟩. It presents three algorithms: (1) a state-aware QSVT algorithm that prepares the target state at low circuit depth when the Krylov subspace of H and |ψ⟩ is low-dimensional or has an accurate low-dimensional approximation; (2) an observable-aware Shadow QSVT algorithm combining a new observable-aware Krylov subspace with history states; and (3) Classical Shadow QSVT, which builds a classical representation from H, f, and |ψ⟩ without prior knowledge of the observables or explicit target-state preparation.

Result: The three algorithms reduce quantum circuit depth and gate complexity in different regimes: the state-aware method leverages low-dimensional (or approximately low-dimensional) Krylov subspaces; the observable-aware method further reduces depth and gate complexity via observable-aware Krylov subspaces and history states; and Classical Shadow QSVT enables estimation of observables specified after the quantum computation, without explicit target-state preparation.

Conclusion: Shadow QSVT provides a systematic framework for lowering the circuit depth of QSVT-based computations. Its three algorithms offer tools for resource reduction across a range of settings, from state-structured and observable-aware quantum computations to classical-shadow representations supporting post hoc observable specification.

Abstract: We introduce shadow quantum singular value transformation (Shadow QSVT): given an initial state $|ψ\rangle$, a Hermitian matrix $H$, a polynomial $f$, and a set of observables $\{O_1,\dots,O_m\}$, the goal is to estimate $\langleψ|f(H)^{\dagger}O_j f(H)|ψ\rangle$ for all $j\in\{1,\dots,m\}$. Shadow QSVT provides a systematic route to reduce the quantum resources required by standard QSVT, which constructs a unitary block-encoding of $f(H)$. It uses structure in the input state and observables, together with the fact that many applications require only observable estimates rather than synthesizing the full unitary.
  We present three algorithms that exploit structure in the initial state and observables to reduce quantum circuit depth. First, we develop a state-aware QSVT algorithm that prepares the target state with low circuit depth when the Krylov subspace associated with $H$ and $|ψ\rangle$ is low-dimensional or admits an accurate low-dimensional approximation. Second, we introduce an observable-aware Shadow QSVT algorithm that combines a new observable-aware Krylov subspace with history states to further reduce circuit depth and gate complexity. Finally, we develop Classical Shadow QSVT, which constructs a classical representation from $H$, $f$, and $|ψ\rangle$ without prior knowledge of the observables or explicit preparation of the target state proportional to $f(H)|ψ\rangle$. This representation enables estimation of the target quantities for observables specified after the quantum computation. Together, these three algorithms provide tools for reducing the circuit depth of QSVT-based computations across a range of settings.

</details>


### [163] [Non-Markovian dissipation as a resource for quantum reservoir computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40172)
*Luca Nigro,Francesco Monzani,Enrico Prati*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a quantum reservoir computing framework in which non-Markovianity is explicitly modeled and regulated via fractional derivatives, using fractional time subordination to generate tunable heavy-tailed relaxation and information backflow, showing that optimal fractional non-Markovianity improves short-delay linear memory and nonlinear prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum reservoir computing typically relies on Markovian dissipation for sequential data processing, leaving the computational role of non-Markovian memory effects largely unexplored. The authors aim to establish non-Markovianity as an active computational resource rather than a mere environmental requirement.

Method: They develop a quantum reservoir computing framework in which non-Markovianity is explicitly modeled and regulated using fractional derivatives. Fractional time subordination is employed to generate tunable, heavy-tailed relaxation dynamics that govern information backflow between the system and its environment.

Result: Non-Markovian information backflow regulates the system's overall temporal retention, maximizing short-term linear memory capacity. Within an optimal operating regime, fractional non-Markovianity redistributes reservoir memory toward recent inputs, improving both linear memory capacity and nonlinear prediction accuracy at short delays, at the expense of long-range temporal retention.

Conclusion: System-environment interaction is not merely a requirement for quantum reservoir computing but an active resource that embeds memory retention directly into the quantum evolution.

Abstract: The control of open-system dynamics provides a powerful mechanism for using quantum information to process sequential tasks. While quantum reservoir computing typically relies on Markovian dissipation to process sequential data, the computational role of non-Markovian memory effects remains largely unexplored. We introduce a framework for quantum reservoir computing where non-Markovianity is explicitly modeled and regulated using fractional derivatives. By employing fractional time subordination, we generate tunable, heavy-tailed relaxation dynamics that govern the information backflow between the system and its environment. Non-Markovian information backflow regulates the overall temporal retention of the system, maximizing short-term linear memory capacity. Within an optimal operating regime, fractional non-Markovianity redistributes the reservoir memory towards recent inputs, improving both linear memory capacity and nonlinear prediction accuracy at short delays, at the expense of long-range temporal retention. System-environment interaction proves being not merely a requirement for quantum reservoir computing, but an active resource that embeds memory retention directly into the quantum evolution.

</details>


### [164] [Practical fermionic shadows enabled by improved sample-complexity bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40173)
*Maxwell West,Su Yeon Chang,Luke Coffman,Martin Larocca,M. Cerezo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Fermionic (matchgate) shadow tomography sample-complexity is improved from O(n^{2k}‖O‖²) to O(n^k‖O‖²), which is asymptotically tight and reduces measurement shots by ~99.98% in a practical Hubbard-chain example.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical shadow tomography offers polynomial sample complexity, but even polynomial scaling is prohibitive for currently available quantum computers with hundreds of qubits. This creates a practical need for tighter sample-complexity bounds, specifically for fermionic shadows.

Method: The paper analyzes fermionic (matchgate) shadow tomography and derives an improved sample-complexity bound for estimating an arbitrary observable O of Majorana degree 2k.

Result: The sample-complexity bound for fermionic shadows is improved from O(n^{2k}‖O‖²) to O(n^k‖O‖²), which is asymptotically tight. In a concrete case—estimating energy per mode of a 50-site open fermionic Hubbard chain (t=1, V=4) to additive precision 0.1—the required shots drop from ~10^9 to ~10^5, a ~99.98% reduction.

Conclusion: The asymptotically tight O(n^k‖O‖²) bound makes fermionic shadow tomography substantially more practical, cutting measurement requirements by orders of magnitude in realistic scenarios.

Abstract: Classical shadow tomography is widely touted as supplying a family of methods for extracting information from quantum systems with polynomially scaling sample-complexities. In the current era of quantum computers possessing on the order of hundreds of qubits, however, polynomial scaling can nonetheless be prohibitive. Thus, there is a strong practical need for obtaining sample-complexity bounds which are as tight as possible. Here we address this in the case of fermionic (matchgate) shadows. For an arbitrary observable $O$ of Majorana degree $2k$, we improve the previously known sample-complexity bound of $\mathcal{O}(n^{2k}\|O\|_\infty^2)$ to $\mathcal{O}(n^{k}\|O\|_\infty^2)$, which is asymptotically tight. For example, for the estimation of the energy per mode of an open fermionic 50-site Hubbard chain with hopping and on site strenghts respectively given by $t=1$, $V=4$, and for a target additive precision of 0.1, this reduces the number of required shots from $\sim 10^9$ to $\sim 10^5$. That is, the new bound reduces the required number of shots by approximately $99.98\%$.

</details>


### [165] [Dissipation accelerates quantum and classical simulation of open-system dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40174)
*Armando Angrisani,Ricard Puig,Yanting Teng,Zoë Holmes*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Pauli noise (e.g., depolarization) exponentially suppresses high-weight components of Heisenberg-evolved observables, enabling system-size-independent Trotter steps for bounded-degree Lindblad dynamics and efficient estimation of local observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissipative channels on quantum hardware are typically viewed as adding operations and increasing cost. This work challenges that view by showing that a broad class of Pauli noise can actually ease quantum and classical simulation of open quantum systems.

Method: The authors exploit the exponential suppression of high-weight components of Heisenberg-evolved observables under Pauli noise (including depolarization). For bounded-degree Lindblad dynamics, they combine this compression with Richardson extrapolation to derive classical and quantum error bounds with respect to the 2-norm of the propagated observable, yielding high-probability expectation-value guarantees for random input states, random Hamiltonians on a fixed state, and out-of-time-order correlators. They also analyze sparse Pauli propagation classically.

Result: Local observable expectation values at time t can be estimated to accuracy ε with circuit depth O([1+(t/γ)^{3/2}] log^2(1/ε)), independent of system size n. Classically, sparse Pauli propagation runs in time polynomial in n, t, and 1/ε for every fixed γ>0, with polynomial degree O(1/γ) in the weak dissipation limit. The gap between quantum and classical upper bounds suggests substantial polynomial quantum advantage as dissipation weakens, supported by numerical results.

Conclusion: Dissipation modeled as Pauli noise can be a resource rather than a cost, enabling size-independent quantum circuits for local observable estimation and leaving room for substantial polynomial quantum advantage over classical simulation in the weak-dissipation regime.
