Abstract: Simulating open quantum systems reveals how environmental coupling shapes relaxation, excitation transport, and the dynamics of quantum correlations. On quantum hardware, dissipative channels add operations and might seem to increase cost. However, we show that a broad class of Pauli noise, including depolarization, can ease quantum and classical simulation by exponentially suppressing high-weight components of Heisenberg-evolved observables. For bounded-degree Lindblad dynamics, this permits system-size-independent Trotter steps when estimating local observables. Combining this compression with Richardson extrapolation, we provide classical and quantum error bounds with respect to the 2-norm of the propagated observable. These give high-probability expectation-value guarantees for random input states or random Hamiltonians acting on a fixed state and control (out-of-time-order) correlators. Concretely, we show that expectation values of local observables at time $t$ can be estimated to accuracy $\varepsilon$ using circuits of maximum depth $O\!\left(\left[1+\left(t/γ\right)^{3/2}\right]\log^2(1/\varepsilon)\right)$, independent of the system size $n$. Classically, we show that sparse Pauli propagation runs in time polynomial in $n$, $t$ and $1/\varepsilon$ for every fixed $γ>0$, with polynomial degree $O(1/γ)$ in the weak dissipation limit. The gap between our quantum and classical upper bounds leaves room for a substantial polynomial quantum advantage as dissipation weakens, an observation further supported by our numerical results.

</details>


### [166] [Breaking the Multiplicative Overhead in Quantum Entropy Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40179)
*Junxiang Huang,Chenyang Li,Lu-Fan Zhang,Yusen Wu,Yukun Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces multi-level quantum algorithms with local normalization and precision allocation for estimating von Neumann, Tsallis, and Rényi entropies, achieving near-optimal rank-independent query complexities and improving prior bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard entropy estimation multiplies worst-case spectral transformation and statistical readout costs, leaving gaps to query lower bounds. The goal is to reduce this overhead and approach optimal query complexity for quantum spectral estimation.

Method: Multi-level algorithms using local normalization and precision allocation, plus variable-time estimation accounting for the probability of reaching expensive spectral tests. Assumes controlled purified access and its inverse; derives functional-estimation theorem replacing global product with a sum of local costs weighted by function magnitudes and spectral masses.

Result: Tsallis entropy: near-optimal rank-independent query complexity Õ(ε^{-1/(α-1)}) for 1<α<2 and Õ(1/ε) for α≥2. Von Neumann entropy improved from Õ(R/ε²) to Õ(R/ε). Noninteger Rényi orders 1<α<3 yield Õ(R/ε). Additionally, fixed logarithmic moments and entropy variance estimated in Õ(R/ε) queries.

Conclusion: The framework extends beyond entropy to a broad class of density-matrix functionals, offering a systematic route toward optimal query complexity in quantum spectral estimation and enabling efficient access to fluctuation parameters relevant to finite-blocklength quantum compression and pure-state entanglement conversion.

Abstract: The von Neumann, Tsallis, and Rényi entropies are fundamental measures of quantum information. A common estimation strategy multiplies the worst-case costs of spectral transformation and statistical readout, leaving gaps to query lower bounds. We reduce this overhead with multi-level algorithms that use local normalization and precision allocation, while variable-time estimation accounts for the probability of reaching expensive spectral tests. With controlled purified access and its inverse, we obtain bounds for additive error $\varepsilon$, rank upper bound $R$, and fixed order $α$. Tsallis entropy estimation has near-optimal rank-independent query complexity $\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-1/(α-1)})$ for $1<α<2$, when the allowed dimension and rank accommodate the lower-bound instances, and $\widetilde O(1/\varepsilon)$ for $α\ge2$, where $\widetilde O$ suppresses logarithmic factors. The first saves a factor $1/\varepsilon$; the second extends known integer-order scaling to noninteger orders. We also improve the von Neumann entropy bound from $\widetilde O(R/\varepsilon^2)$ to $\widetilde O(R/\varepsilon)$ and obtain $\widetilde O(R/\varepsilon)$ for noninteger Rényi orders $1<α<3$, with further bounds at other orders. Our functional-estimation theorem replaces the global product by a sum of local costs weighted by function magnitudes and spectral masses. We further estimate fixed logarithmic moments and entropy variance using $\widetilde O(R/\varepsilon)$ queries, providing efficient access to fluctuation parameters that enter finite-blocklength quantum compression and pure-state entanglement conversion. More broadly, our framework extends beyond entropy to a broad class of density-matrix functionals, offering a systematic approach toward optimal query complexity in quantum spectral estimation.

</details>


### [167] [Improved Quantum Query Bounds for Boolean Matrix Product Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40180)
*Amin Shiraz Gilani,François Le Gall,Xingyu Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes the first non-trivial upper bound for quantum query complexity of Boolean Matrix Product Verification, via a connection to the Orthogonal Vectors problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Boolean Matrix Product Verification (BMPV) had a longstanding gap between its best upper bound (O(n^{3/2})) and lower bound (Ω̃(n^{19/18})); improving these bounds was an open question in quantum query complexity.

Method: The authors establish equivalences between the Orthogonal Vectors (OV) problem and BMPV, and derive bounds for BMPV by proving corresponding bounds for OV. They also analyze a variant of BMPV asking whether the product contains a given row vector.

Result: An upper bound of Õ(n^{17/12}) improving the standard O(n^{3/2}) bound, a lower bound of Ω(n^{5/4}) improving the previous Ω̃(n^{19/18}), and a tight Θ̃(n^{3/2}) bound for the row-vector variant. These imply a polynomial separation between quantum query complexities of deciding radius-at-most-two versus diameter-at-most-two in graphs.

Conclusion: The connection between OV and BMPV yields the first non-trivial upper bound and an improved lower bound for BMPV, and separates the quantum query complexities of two related graph problems.

Abstract: We prove the first non-trivial upper bound for the quantum query complexity of Boolean Matrix Product Verification ($\mathsf{BMPV}$), answering a longstanding open question in quantum query complexity. For $n\times n$ matrices, our upper bound is $\widetilde O(n^{17/12})$, improving on the standard $O(n^{3/2})$ bound obtained using Grover search by Buhrman and Špalek (SODA 2006). We complement this result by showing an $Ω(n^{5/4})$ lower bound, which improves over the previous best known lower bound of $\widetildeΩ(n^{19/18})$ by Childs, Kimmel, and Kothari (ESA 2012).
  Our approach centers on a connection with Orthogonal Vectors ($\mathsf{OV}$), which asks whether an indexed list of $n$ Boolean vectors of dimension $n$ contains two vectors with disjoint supports. In particular, we prove equivalences between $\mathsf{OV}$ and $\mathsf{BMPV}$ and establish the above bounds for $\mathsf{OV}$. We also prove a tight $\widetilde Θ(n^{3/2})$ bound for a variant of $\mathsf{BMPV}$ that asks whether the product contains a given row vector. Together, these results imply a polynomial separation between the quantum query complexities of deciding whether a graph has radius at most two and whether it has diameter at most two.

</details>


### [168] [A mixing time method for estimating the sample complexity of quantum state discrimination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40182)
*Juntai Zhou,Felix Leditzky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a mixing-time method to estimate sample complexity of quantum state discrimination, reducing quantum problems to classical mixing times via representation theory and answering several open problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating the sample complexity of quantum state discrimination lacks tight, unifying tools; classical mixing times and Dobrushin coefficients have been powerful in classical settings, motivating a quantum analogue that can exploit symmetry.

Method: For minimum-error discrimination of geometrically uniform pure state ensembles, the authors bound sample complexity by a quantum homogeneous mixing time plus a quantum generalized Dobrushin coefficient. When (G,H) is a Gelfand pair the quantum mixing time reduces to a classical one and the Dobrushin coefficient is expressed through representation-theoretic data of the commutative Hecke algebra End_G(C[G/H]). They extend to arbitrary mixed ensembles with uniform priors via a quantum weakly mixing time and a sandwiched bound, and use strengthened data processing inequalities.

Result: Tight sample-complexity estimates are obtained; the framework reduces learning quantum coupon collector states and phase states to classical mixing time problems, answers open problems on degree-d phase states over F_q and generalized Boolean phase states over Z_q, applies to hypergraph state ensembles with estimates in hypergraph data (recovering graph state results), and gives a tight estimate for minimax discrimination via a Dobrushin-type coefficient plus an upper bound from a strengthened data processing inequality constant.

Conclusion: A unified mixing-time framework tightly characterizes quantum state discrimination sample complexity, bridges quantum and classical mixing via Gelfand pairs and Hecke algebras, resolves open learning problems, and extends to mixed ensembles and minimax discrimination.

Abstract: We develop a mixing time method for estimating the sample complexity of quantum state discrimination. We start with considering the minimum-error discrimination of geometrically uniform pure state ensembles, and prove that its sample complexity has a tight estimate given by a quantum homogeneous mixing time [George et al., 2026] and a quantum version of the generalized Dobrushin coefficient [Wolfer, 2020]. This quantum mixing time further reduces to a classical one when the generating group $G$ forms a Gelfand pair with the stabilizer subgroup $H$ of the generator state. In this case the generalized Dobrushin coefficient can be fully expressed by representation-theoretic quantities of the commutative Hecke algebra $\operatorname{End}_G(\mathbb C[G/H])$. In particular, this method reduces the sample complexity estimation of learning quantum coupon collector states [Arunachalam et al., 2020] and learning phase states to classical mixing time problems. We apply this framework to answer the open problems of learning degree-$d$ phase states over $\mathbb F_q$ in [Alrabiah et al., 2026] and generalized Boolean phase states over $\mathbb Z_q$ [Arunachalam et al., 2023]. The framework also applies to hypergraph state ensembles, giving estimates expressed fully in terms of hypergraph data and recovering estimates for graph state ensembles in [Montanaro and Shao, 2022]. Finally, we extend the discussion to arbitrary mixed state ensembles with uniform priors, prove a sandwiched bound for minimum-error discrimination sample complexity by a quantum weakly mixing time, and provide a tight estimate for the minimax discrimination sample complexity from [D'Ariano et al., 2005] by a Dobrushin-type coefficient. We also discuss the method of strengthened data processing inequality [Gao and Rouz{é}, 2022] and give an upper bound in terms of a strengthened data processing inequality constant.

</details>


### [169] [More is Less:Optimal Security for Haar Quantum Money and More](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40184)
*Zihan Hao,Xingjian Li,Qipeng Liu,Wei Zhan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that a quantum money scheme based on an n-qubit Haar-random state with a reflection oracle achieves optimal query security: holding fewer than Ω(2^n) banknotes gives no asymptotic counterfeiting advantage, proven via a compressed-oracle framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum cryptography uses unclonability to enable classically impossible applications like quantum-protected digital currency, but existing security bounds for quantum money degrade as the number of banknotes a user holds increases. This motivates seeking constructions whose query security does not deteriorate below the state-tomography scale.

Method: The authors study a construction of quantum money based on an n-qubit Haar-random state and a reflection oracle. They develop a framework based on the compressed-oracle technique to define and analyze progress measures for quantum tasks such as Haar state cloning, and prove a tight lower bound for generating r additional copies with a matching attack.

Result: The construction achieves optimal query security: unless a user holds Ω(2^n) banknotes, their existing banknotes cannot asymptotically speed up counterfeiting, and their best possible attack is the same as having no banknotes. The paper also provides a tight lower bound for generating r additional copies, a matching attack, and applies the results to quantum copy-protection.

Conclusion: Quantum money can retain optimal query security against counterfeiting as long as the number of banknotes held by a user remains below the state-tomography scale, demonstrating robustness of unclonability-based security and offering tools (compressed-oracle framework) with applications to quantum copy-protection.

Abstract: Quantum cryptography leverages unclonability to enable a wide range of cryptographic applications that are impossible classically, such as digital currency protected against counterfeiting by quantum mechanics. Security requires that no efficient user can produce even one additional valid banknote beyond those already in their possession. However, existing security bounds weaken as the number of banknotes available to a user increases.
  In this paper, we study a construction of quantum money whose asymptotic query security does not deteriorate as long as the number of banknotes available to a user remains below the scale required for state tomography. In particular, we show that the construction based on an $n$-qubit Haar-random state and a reflection oracle achieves optimal query security: unless a user holds $Ω(2^n)$ banknotes, their existing banknotes cannot asymptotically speed up counterfeiting --- their best possible attack is the same as if they do not have any banknotes. To establish this result, we develop a framework based on the compressed-oracle technique for defining and analyzing progress measures for quantum tasks such as Haar state cloning. Furthermore, we prove a tight lower bound for generating $r$ additional copies, give a matching attack, and apply our results to quantum copy-protection.

</details>


### [170] [The Structure of Higher-Order Quantum Resources with an Application to Coherence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40186)
*Felix Ahnefeld,Thomas Theurer,Martin B. Plenio*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified framework for higher-order quantum resources covering states, channels, and supermaps, defining supermap resource monotones, distillation, and dilution with several notions of free supermaps, and applies it to dynamical coherence with one-shot distillation/dilution results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resource theories of states and channels are well developed, but a unified treatment of higher-order resources (supermaps/processes) is lacking. The authors aim to extend resource-theoretic concepts to the supermap level to compare and quantify resourcefulness of states, channels, and supermaps within one framework.

Method: Generalize concepts from state and channel resource theories to define supermap resource monotones and supermap distillation/dilution. Introduce three types of free supermaps: free networks (implementable with free channels), compatible supermaps (sets closed under arbitrary composition), and maximally free superchannels. Analyze approximate channel-to-channel conversions, and apply the framework to dynamical coherence, characterizing largest compatible sets via semidefinite constraints.

Result: For approximate channel-to-channel conversions under mild assumptions, the conversion error with respect to diamond distance coincides with worst-case infidelity. In dynamical coherence, largest sets of compatible supermaps exist, are characterized by semidefinite constraints, are strictly larger than the corresponding free networks, yet do not contain all maximally free superchannels. Operational differences appear in one-shot channel distillation and dilution, with solutions given in the parallel setting for both tasks and for dilution in the adaptive setting.

Conclusion: The framework unifies resourcefulness across states, channels, and supermaps and yields new monotones, conversion-error equivalences, and characterizations of compatible supermaps. The distinct behaviors of the proposed free supermap sets in one-shot tasks highlight genuine higher-order operational differences, motivating further study of adaptive distillation and general supermap resource theories.

Abstract: We develop a general framework for higher-order quantum resources that treats the resourcefulness of quantum states, channels, and supermaps (also called processes) in a unified manner. By generalizing well-established concepts from resource theories of states and channels, we introduce and study supermap resource monotones and supermap distillation and dilution. As free supermaps, we consider free networks (supermaps that can be implemented with free channels), compatible supermaps (sets of supermaps closed under arbitrary composition), and maximally free superchannels. Specializing to approximate channel-to-channel conversions, we show that under mild assumptions, the conversion error with respect to the diamond distance and the worst-case infidelity coincide. We then apply the general framework to resource theories of dynamical coherence, where we establish the existence of largest sets of compatible supermaps. By characterizing these sets with semidefinite constraints, we show that they are strictly larger than the corresponding free networks but do not contain all maximally free superchannels. We finish by investigating the operational differences of these sets in one-shot channel distillation and dilution, providing solutions for both tasks in the parallel setting and for dilution in the adaptive setting.

</details>


### [171] [Fast classical simulation algorithms for free-fermion dynamics with magic input](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40187)
*Jiwon Heo,Oliver Reardon-Smith,Michał Oszmaniec,Zoltán Zimborás,Changhun Oh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops classical simulation algorithms for free-fermion (matchgate) circuits with non-Gaussian inputs, covering exact sampling, additive-error and exact expectation estimation, and exact Majorana-monomial expectation values, providing classical benchmarks for fermionic quantum simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical cost of simulating free-fermion circuits depends on both input state and task, but prior work largely focused on Gaussian inputs and restricted tasks. There is a need for classical algorithms handling non-Gaussian inputs across a broad range of tasks, including exact sampling and expectation estimation, to benchmark fermionic quantum simulations such as trapped-ion experiments.

Method: For inputs of n copies of the four-mode magic state, exact sampling algorithms generalize the Clifford-Clifford Boson sampling algorithm for passive and active free-fermion dynamics with worst-case arithmetic cost O(2^n) per sample, without multiplicative polynomial prefactors. Additive-error estimation of number correlators and observables is extended from passive to active dynamics using a per-state-adapted Gaussian-state decomposition to control the second moment. For even-parity product inputs with constant-size blocks, Majorana monomial expectations of logarithmic weight are computed exactly via dynamic programming that shares calculations across subsets of Majorana factors.

Result: Exact sampling runs at O(2^n) per sample with no multiplicative polynomial prefactor; the passive algorithm exactly simulates the non-interacting regime of a recent trapped-ion experiment. Additive-error estimation runs in time polynomial in n and inverse additive-error, including individual output probability estimation. Exact Majorana-monomial expectations for constant-size-block even-parity inputs are computed in polynomial time, improving prior quasi-polynomial Majorana propagation algorithms.

Conclusion: The suite of algorithms provides classical benchmarks for fermionic quantum simulations across a broad spectrum of computational tasks, handling non-Gaussian inputs and both passive and active dynamics.

Abstract: The classical cost of simulating free-fermion (matchgate) circuits depends on both the input state and the computational task. We develop classical algorithms for circuits with non-Gaussian inputs, addressing exact sampling and both additive-error and exact estimation of expectation values. For inputs consisting of $n$ copies of the four-mode magic state, we give exact sampling algorithms, generalizing the Clifford and Clifford algorithm for Boson sampling, for passive and active free-fermion dynamics with worst-case arithmetic cost $O(2^n)$ per sample, without any multiplicative polynomial prefactor. The passive algorithm enables an exact simulation of the non-interacting regime of a recent trapped-ion experiment. We also extend recently developed additive-error estimation of number correlators and related observables from passive to active dynamics with runtime polynomial in $n$ and the inverse additive-error, for input states formed by products of $n$ four-mode even parity states. Our estimator uses a Gaussian-state decomposition adapted to each sampled state to control its second moment. This includes estimation of individual output probabilities. Finally, we show that for even-parity product inputs composed of constant-size blocks, expectation values of Majorana monomials of logarithmic weight can be computed exactly in polynomial time, improving on prior Majorana propagation-style algorithms with quasi-polynomial runtime. The exact algorithm processes the input blocks by dynamic programming, sharing calculations across subsets of Majorana factors. Our suite of algorithms provide classical benchmarks for fermionic quantum simulations across a broad spectrum of computational tasks.

</details>


### [172] [Query-Limited RAM Programs and their Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40194)
*Jiahui Liu,Justin Raizes,Bhaskar Roberts,Omri Shmueli*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces query-limited RAM programs (QLPs), a stateful, RAM-generalized version of quantum one-time programs, constructed generically from a new primitive called one-shot programs that unifies one-shot signatures with single effective queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard quantum one-time programs (QOTPs) are inherently stateless and require a freshly issued quantum token for every single circuit evaluation, which limits their applicability. The authors ask whether the one-time computation paradigm can be extended to richer, stateful forms of controlled access, and whether such an extension provides inherent advantages beyond standard OTPs.

Method: The authors define query-limited RAM programs (QLPs), supporting structured, stateful computation under bounded or policy-driven access while preventing adversarial forking or rollback of computational state. They construct QLPs via a new primitive, one-shot programs, which unifies one-shot signatures (Amos et al., STOC 2020) with the single effective query paradigm (Gupte et al., STOC 2025), and prove that one-shot programs generically imply query-limited programs. They also clarify the relationship between signature-token primitives and quantum one-time programs through generic constructions.

Result: QLPs enable new applications beyond stateless one-time programs: quantum tokens for Turing Machines whose size depends only on code length (not runtime), transferable k-time or budget-limited programs, and low-communication mechanisms for delegating computation in settings such as Software-as-a-Service. The strengthened unclonability guarantees of one-shot signatures are shown to translate into enhanced functionality.

Conclusion: The one-time computation paradigm can be extended to stateful, RAM-based controlled access via QLPs, generically constructed from one-shot programs. This shows that one-time signing programs imply one-time general computation and that unclonability guarantees from one-shot signatures yield richer, more powerful cryptographic functionality.

Abstract: Quantum one-time programs (Broadbent, Gutoski and Stebila, CRYPTO 2013) or OTPs for short, enable a functionality to be encoded into a quantum token that can be evaluated on a single chosen input and then becomes unusable. While powerful, this primitive is inherently stateless and tied to a setting in which a quantum token needs to be issued and distributed for every single evaluation of a circuit. This raises a natural question: can the one-time computation paradigm be extended to richer, stateful forms of controlled access, and would such an extension offer inherent advantages beyond standard OTPs?
  We introduce $\textit{query-limited RAM programs}$ (QLPs), a RAM-generalization of QOTPs that supports structured, stateful computation under bounded or policy-driven access. QLPs allow controlled sequences of evaluations while preventing adversarial forking or rollback of computational state. This enables new applications beyond stateless one-time programs, including quantum tokens for Turing Machines whose size depends only on $\textit{code length}$ (and not runtime), transferable $k$-time or budget-limited programs, and low-communication mechanisms for delegating computation in settings such as Software-as-a-Service.
  To construct QLPs, we introduce $\textit{one-shot programs}$, unifying one-shot signatures (Amos, Georgiou, Kiayias and Zhandry, STOC 2020) with the single effective query paradigm (Gupte, Liu, Raizes, Roberts and Vaikuntanathan, STOC 2025). We prove that one-shot programs generically imply query-limited programs, demonstrating that the strengthened unclonability guarantees of one-shot signatures translate into enhanced functionality. Along the way, we clarify the relationship between signature-token primitives and quantum one-time programs via generic constructions, essentially showing that one-time signing programs imply one-time general computation.

</details>


### [173] [Generalized Reimpell-Werner Iteration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40203)
*Shihao Ru,Bikun Li,Weibo Gao,Liang Jiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The Reimpell-Werner iteration for quantum measurements and channels is generalized to linear objectives with arbitrary Hermitian cost matrices, with proven global convergence and an asymptotic O(1/ε) iteration complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum measurements and channels govern information extraction, encoding, and transmission, and optimizing their performance typically requires numerical methods that remain practical as Hilbert space dimensions grow. The Reimpell-Werner iteration is one such practical method, but its applicability and convergence guarantees have been limited.

Method: The authors generalize the Reimpell-Werner iteration—repeated matrix updates respecting problem constraints—to linear objectives defined by arbitrary Hermitian cost matrices. Convergence to a global optimum is proven under suitable support-overlap conditions on the initialization, and the asymptotic iteration complexity is analyzed for each fixed problem, parameter choice, and admissible initialization.

Result: The iterates converge to a global optimum whenever the initialization satisfies appropriate support overlap conditions. For each fixed problem, choice of iteration parameters, and admissible initialization, O(1/ε) iterations suffice asymptotically to bring the objective value within ε of the optimum.

Conclusion: The work places the Reimpell-Werner iteration on a rigorous footing and broadens the class of optimization problems covered by its convergence guarantees.

Abstract: Quantum measurements and channels determine how information is extracted, encoded, and transmitted in quantum protocols. Optimizing their performance often requires numerical methods that remain practical as Hilbert space dimensions increase. The Reimpell-Werner iteration offers a practical approach to these tasks through repeated matrix updates that respect the constraints. Here, we generalize this iteration to linear objectives with arbitrary Hermitian cost matrices. We prove that the iterates converge to a global optimum whenever the initialization satisfies suitable support overlap conditions. For each fixed problem, choice of iteration parameters, and admissible initialization, $\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon)$ iterations suffice asymptotically to bring the objective value within $\varepsilon$ of the optimum. These results provide a rigorous foundation for the iteration and broaden the class of optimization problems to which its convergence guarantees apply.

</details>


### [174] [Submodularity of entropy under quantum convolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40211)
*Milad M. Goodarzi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper builds a submodular framework for the von Neumann entropy of discrete quantum convolutions, establishing a polymatroidal structure of entropy growth and deriving several quantum entropic inequalities (strong subadditivity, Ruzsa triangle, Plünnecke–Ruzsa) as corollaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Entropic additive combinatorics has a well-developed "direct side" built on submodularity and polymatroidal methods, but no noncommutative (quantum) counterpart existed. The paper aims to import these submodular techniques into the quantum setting to obtain broad families of convolutional entropy inequalities.

Method: The authors introduce globally weighted quantum convolutions, forming compatible families indexed by admissible subsets of a fixed input collection. For a fixed admissible input block, they show that relative entropy gains admit a normalized, monotone, submodular extension to all subsets of the remaining inputs, revealing a polymatroidal geometry of entropy growth.

Result: The main theorem yields convolutional strong subadditivity, a quantum Ruzsa triangle inequality, and quantum entropic Plünnecke–Ruzsa inequalities for arbitrary input states. For repeated inputs, it provides sharp entropy-growth comparisons across admissible scales, with the quantum doubling constant controlling all higher admissible convolution entropies with optimal exponents.

Conclusion: The work brings submodular methods from additive combinatorics into the quantum domain, offering a systematic route to wide families of convolutional entropy inequalities and a noncommutative analogue of the direct side of entropic additive combinatorics.

Abstract: We develop a submodular framework for the von Neumann entropy of discrete quantum convolutions, providing a noncommutative counterpart to the direct side of entropic additive combinatorics. We first introduce globally weighted quantum convolutions, which form compatible families indexed by admissible subsets of a fixed collection of inputs. Our main theorem reveals a polymatroidal geometry underlying their entropy growth: relative to any fixed admissible input block, the entropy gains admit a normalized, monotone, submodular extension to all subsets of the remaining inputs. The theorem yields convolutional strong subadditivity, quantum Ruzsa triangle inequality, and quantum entropic Plünnecke--Ruzsa inequalities for arbitrary input states. For repeated inputs, it gives sharp comparisons of entropy growth across admissible scales; in particular, the quantum doubling constant controls all higher admissible convolution entropies with optimal exponents. Together, these results bring submodular methods from additive combinatorics into the quantum setting and provide a systematic route to broad families of convolutional entropy inequalities.

</details>


### [175] [Quantum Markov State Models for Metastable Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40214)
*Hao-En Li,Lin Lin,Michael Ragone*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs quantum Markov state models (QMSMs) that compress open quantum system dynamics onto a small state space retaining both classical sectors and quantum coherence, achieving optimal diamond-norm error bounds of O(η^{1/3}) generally and O(η^{1/2}) on metastable states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open quantum systems rapidly lose microscopic information, leaving few degrees of freedom governing long-time dynamics. Classical Markov state models capture metastable dynamics as transitions between representative phases, but phase labels alone fail to capture quantum coherence persisting between metastable states. Moreover, even when slow modes are known, their spectral projection need not yield valid quantum states. This motivates a quantum generalization addressing validity and coherence, and answers Kitaev's exact-rounding question.

Method: The authors construct QMSMs describing surviving information and its evolution on a small physical state space comprising classical sectors and quantum matrix blocks. They assume the evolution channel C changes little on second application with small defect η = ‖C² − C‖◇, and that the number of slow degrees of freedom is bounded independently of full system size. Under these assumptions they construct compression and reconstruction channels whose composition exactly recovers every reduced state, while the reverse composition yields an exactly idempotent channel approximating C.

Result: The constructions yield an optimal diamond-norm bound of O(η^{1/3}) on all input states and an improved bound of O(η^{1/2}) on metastable states prepared by C, with constants depending only on the slow dimension. The reduced transition channel can be iterated to predict microscopic dynamics with controlled error. The QMSM is illustrated on a weakly driven dissipative spin chain supporting either a metastable logical qubit or long-lived classical phases.

Conclusion: QMSMs provide a rigorous framework for compressing metastable open quantum dynamics onto small state spaces that retain quantum coherence, with provably optimal error scaling and exact state recovery, generalizing classical MSMs and answering Kitaev's exact-rounding question.

Abstract: Open quantum systems can rapidly lose most microscopic information, leaving only a few degrees of freedom to govern their long-time dynamics. Classical Markov state models (MSMs) describe such metastable dynamics as transitions between a few representative phases and are a well-established tool in condensed matter and chemical physics. However, phase labels alone cannot capture quantum coherence that may persist between metastable states. Even when the slow modes are known, their spectral projection need not produce valid quantum states. We construct quantum Markov state models (QMSMs) that describe the surviving information and its evolution on a small physical state space containing classical sectors and quantum matrix blocks. We assume that the evolution channel $\mathcal C$ changes little when applied a second time, with sufficiently small defect $η=\|\mathcal C^2-\mathcal C\|_\diamond$, and that the number of slow degrees of freedom is bounded independently of the full system size. Under these assumptions, we construct compression and reconstruction channels whose composition recovers every reduced state exactly, while the reverse composition gives an exactly idempotent channel approximating $\mathcal C$, answering Kitaev's exact-rounding question \cite{Kitaev2025}. Our constructions give an optimal diamond-norm bound of $\mathcal{O}(η^{1/3})$ on all input states, as well as an improved bound of $\mathcal{O}(η^{1/2})$ on metastable states prepared by $\mathcal C$, with constants depending only on the slow dimension. The reduced transition channel can be iterated to predict the microscopic dynamics with controlled error. We illustrate the QMSM through a weakly driven dissipative spin chain supporting either a metastable logical qubit or long-lived classical phases.

</details>


### [176] [Universal distillation of quantum entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40215)
*Ryuji Takagi,Kaito Watanabe,Takaya Matsuura,Hayato Arai,Masahito Hayashi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that distillable entanglement is achievable by a universal LOCC protocol that does not depend on the input state, removing the standard assumption that parties know the input state description.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distillable entanglement is central to characterizing the operational value of entangled states, but its operational meaning relied on the assumption that both parties know the input state and can tailor their protocol accordingly. This assumption does not reflect natural operational settings.

Method: The authors construct a universal LOCC protocol whose description is independent of the input state, based on state merging for compound states combined with the uniform convergence property of the finite-block quantity toward distillable entanglement.

Result: The universal protocol achieves the distillable entanglement of an arbitrary unknown input state, establishing that input-state knowledge is not necessary to attain the optimal entanglement distillation rate.

Conclusion: Distillable entanglement is elevated to a robust, operationally meaningful quantity valid even without input-state knowledge.

Abstract: Distillable entanglement---the maximum rate of Bell states extractable from the given noisy entangled states via local operations and classical communication (LOCC)---has been a central quantity to characterize the operational value of entangled states. However, this operational meaning had been known to be valid only under a crucial assumption: both parties know the description of the input state and can choose the protocol tailored to it, which does not necessarily reflect the natural operational setting. Here, we show that the distillable entanglement is the fundamental operational quantity even without this assumption. We present a universal LOCC protocol---whose description does not depend on the input state and thus works universally for any input---that achieves the distillable entanglement of an arbitrary unknown input state. Our result therefore lifts the distillable entanglement to a robust notion of an operationally meaningful quantity and clarifies that input-state knowledge is not necessary to achieve the optimal rate of entanglement distillation. Our protocol is based on state merging for compound states, together with the uniform convergence property of the finite-block quantity toward distillable entanglement.

</details>


### [177] [Quantum Log-Determinant Methods for Torsion-Sensitive Topological Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40216)
*Dimitrios Thanos,Caesnan Leditto,Adam Wesolowski,Andreea-Iulia Lefterovici,Mahtab Yaghubi Rad*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces the first provably correct quantum estimator of integral-homology torsion order, using alternating combinations of log pseudodeterminants of boundary Laplacians to access torsion information beyond Betti numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Betti numbers, central to topological data analysis, fail to capture torsion in integral homology. Torsion can distinguish topological structures with identical Betti numbers and has practical applications in graph-based machine learning, including biomedical domains. The authors investigate whether quantum spectral methods can access this additional torsion information.

Method: Log pseudodeterminants (sums of logarithms of nonzero boundary-Laplacian eigenvalues) summarize spectral information of a simplicial complex. Under explicit topological assumptions, an alternating combination yields the size of a higher critical group (analogue of the graph sandpile group); with stronger certification, a related construction recovers the torsion subgroup size in homology. A new quantum algorithm estimates these quantities with explicit dependence on query access, accuracy, and spectral gaps, avoiding explicit construction of exponentially large boundary matrices via compact query access. A tailored amplitude-estimation routine is used for a restricted torsion-bearing family.

Result: Exact spectra for balanced multipartite clique complexes provide favorable spectral gaps, and a related construction yields an explicit torsion-bearing benchmark. For a restricted torsion-bearing family, the tailored amplitude-estimation routine uses quadratically fewer queries than any randomized classical algorithm with comparable access when estimating the log pseudodeterminant. Estimating the full unnormalized quantity to fixed additive error still scales with the candidate-space dimension D.

Conclusion: This work provides the first provably correct quantum estimator of integral-homology torsion order, demonstrating that quantum spectral methods can access torsion information beyond Betti numbers. While query advantages are achievable for restricted families, the cost of estimating full unnormalized quantities to fixed additive error remains dependent on the candidate-space dimension D.

Abstract: Betti numbers are central to topological data analysis but fail to capture torsion in integral homology. Torsion can distinguish topological structures with identical Betti numbers, and examples from classical topological data analysis show that it can affect the topology inferred from data. Related torsion-sensitive quantities are also known to have practical applications in graph-based machine learning, including biomedical applications. We investigate how quantum spectral methods can access this additional information.
  Log pseudodeterminants, obtained by summing the logarithms of the nonzero boundary-Laplacian eigenvalues, summarize spectral information about a simplicial complex. Under explicit topological assumptions, an alternating combination of these quantities gives the size of a higher critical group, the higher-dimensional analogue of a graph sandpile group. With stronger certification, a related construction recovers the size of the torsion subgroup in homology itself. This is, to our knowledge, the first provably correct quantum estimator of integral-homology torsion order.
  We give a new quantum algorithm for estimating these quantities, with explicit dependence on how the input is queried, the required accuracy, and the spectral gaps. Exact spectra for balanced multipartite clique complexes provide favorable gaps, and a related construction gives an explicit torsion-bearing benchmark. With compact query access, the algorithm need not construct the exponentially large boundary matrices explicitly. For a restricted torsion-bearing family, a tailored amplitude-estimation routine uses quadratically fewer queries than any randomized classical algorithm given comparable access when estimating the log pseudodeterminant. Estimating the full, unnormalized quantity to fixed additive error still costs in proportion to the candidate-space dimension $D$.

</details>


### [178] [Improved quantum volume estimation with transducers and amortized quantum walks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40217)
*Arjan Cornelissen,Simon Apers,Sander Gribling*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work quantizes the state-of-the-art randomized volume estimation algorithm, achieving a quantum algorithm with query complexity O(d^3.5 + d^1.75/ε), improving on the previous best quantum bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Volume estimation is a classic computational geometry problem whose randomized algorithms drove key developments in MCMC and simulated annealing, connecting to results like the KLS conjecture. Quantum speedups for this task are valuable but face a barrier: the amortized analysis of the ball walk could not be quantum-implemented previously.

Method: The authors quantize the O(d^3.5 + d^3/ε^2)-query randomized algorithm of Cousins and Vempala. Their key technical contribution is a framework for amortizing the cost of a quantum walk, based on the transducer toolkit of Belovs, Jeffery, and Yolcu.

Result: They obtain a quantum algorithm with query complexity O(d^3.5 + d^1.75/ε), improving over the state-of-the-art quantum bound of O(d^3.5 + d^2.25/ε).

Conclusion: The amortized quantum walk framework enables exploitation of the amortized ball-walk analysis of Cousins and Vempala, overcoming the key barrier that previously blocked its quantum implementation.

Abstract: The volume estimation problem is a classic task in computational geometry. The development of randomized algorithms for this problem spurred the development of many influential algorithmic techniques related to Markov Chain Monte Carlo and simulated annealing, and the problem connects to several important geometrical results, like the recently-resolved KLS conjecture. In this work, we quantize the state-of-the-art $\widetilde{O}(d^{3.5}+d^3/\varepsilon^2)$-query randomized algorithm developed by Cousins and Vempala, and obtain a $\widetilde{O}(d^{3.5} + d^{1.75}/\varepsilon)$-query quantum algorithm, improving over the $\widetilde{O}(d^{3.5} + d^{2.25}/\varepsilon)$ state-of-the-art bound. Our key technical contribution is a framework for amortizing the cost of a quantum walk. The framework is based on the recent transducer toolkit introduced by Belovs, Jeffery and Yolcu. It is this amortized quantum walk framework that allows us to exploit the amortized analysis of the ball walk by Cousins and Vempala, thus overcoming the key barrier that previously barred its quantum implementation.

</details>


### [179] [Purification brings advantages in sequential quantum channel discrimination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40218)
*Zoe G. del Toro,Marco Túlio Quintino,Jessica Bavaresco*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Purifying degrees of freedom and their environment correlations can be exploited as an operational resource in sequential quantum channel discrimination, yielding a strict advantage over bare channel access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Purifications are typically viewed merely as a convenient mathematical representation (a change of representation) of quantum states and channels. The paper challenges this view by asking whether the purifying degrees of freedom constitute a genuine operational resource, particularly in sequential channel discrimination where the environmental reference frame may be unknown or averaged.

Method: The authors study sequential quantum channel discrimination under two access models: bare channel queries versus averaged-purification queries. They construct pairs of families of qubit-qubit channels and analyze query complexity (two-query and three-query settings) to establish separations, comparing sequential versus parallel strategies and examining whether bare queries can be transformed into averaged-purification queries.

Result: Unlike state discrimination and parallel channel discrimination (where bare and averaged-purification access are equivalent), sequential access to purified resources shows a strict advantage in success probability. Two main results: (1) a pair of families of qubit-qubit channels with strict separation in the two-query case; (2) a pair of qubit-qubit channels whose purifications are perfectly distinguishable with three queries, while no finite number of bare queries suffices for perfect discrimination.

Conclusion: These separations imply an advantage of sequential over parallel strategies for channel discrimination. They also establish the impossibility of converting bare channel queries into averaged-purification queries via transformations allowing arbitrary interventions between queries, strengthening previous results.

Abstract: Purifications are often used as a convenient mathematical representation of quantum states and channels when constructing protocols for quantum information processing tasks. Although powerful, this perspective can suggest that the purifying degrees of freedom and its correlations with an environment are just a useful change of representation. Here we show how they can instead be exploited as an operational resource in sequential quantum channel discrimination, even when the environmental reference frame is unknown or averaged. In contrast to state discrimination and parallel channel discrimination, for which bare and averaged-purification access are equivalent, sequential access to purified resources exhibits a strict advantage in probability of success. We prove this strict separation via two main results. In the first, we construct a pair of families of qubit-qubit channels with a strict separation in the two-query case, and in the second, we show an example of a pair of qubit-qubit channels whose purifications are perfectly distinguishable with three queries while no finite number of bare (non-purified) queries suffices for perfect discrimination. These separations imply an advantage of sequential over parallel strategies for channel discrimination and establish the impossibility of converting bare channel queries into averaged-purification queries via transformations that allow arbitrary interventions between queries, strengthening previous results.

</details>


### [180] [NLTM Hamiltonians from gauged sheaf quantum locally testable codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40220)
*Fuchuan Wei,Zhengyi Han,Zimu Li,Zi-Wen Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves the no low-energy trivial magic (NLTM) conjecture by constructing a family of local Hamiltonians where all states in a positive energy density window require circuit depth Ω(log n) to prepare from arbitrary (possibly highly entangled) stabilizer states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the complexity of low-energy quantum states is central to quantum complexity theory and many-body physics. The NLTM conjecture strengthens prior results like NLTS by ruling out shallow stabilizer-based preparations at low energy, which is relevant to the quantum PCP problem.

Method: The construction builds on non-Abelian gauged sheaf codes derived from the cup product structure on good quantum locally testable codes. The authors establish constant operator soundness for the associated Hamiltonians, enabling the protected logical structure to constrain all low-energy states.

Result: The NLTM conjecture is proven. The constructed Hamiltonian family yields intrinsic guarantees: the same Hamiltonian and energy density threshold work for arbitrary qudit stabilizer inputs with any bounded local dimensions, and every state within the positive energy density window needs Ω(log n) circuit depth to prepare from any stabilizer state.

Conclusion: The result substantially strengthens NLTS by excluding shallow stabilizer-based classical witnesses and advances understanding of low-energy complexity relevant to quantum PCP.

Abstract: Understanding the complexity of low-energy quantum states is a central problem in quantum complexity theory and many-body physics. Here we prove the no low-energy trivial magic (NLTM) conjecture, constructing a family of local Hamiltonians for which every state within a positive energy density window requires circuit depth $Ω(\log n)$ to prepare from arbitrary stabilizer state that may themselves be highly entangled. Notably, our result is intrinsic in the sense that the same Hamiltonian family and energy density threshold apply to arbitrary qudit stabilizer inputs with any bounded local dimensions. Our construction builds on non-Abelian gauged sheaf codes obtained using the cup product structure on good quantum locally testable codes. We establish constant operator soundness for the associated Hamiltonians, enabling their protected logical structure to constrain all states at sufficiently low energy density. By excluding shallow stabilizer-based classical witnesses, Our result substantially strengthens NLTS and advances our understanding of low-energy complexity relevant to quantum PCP.

</details>


### [181] [Causal inequalities witness non-stabilizerness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40223)
*Leonardo Vaglini,Nasra Daher Ahmed,Ravi Kunjwal*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work characterizes "nonstabilizerness without magic" (NSWM)—the phenomenon where a stabilizer basis can be perfectly discriminated by CSPO but not by stabilizer operations alone—by deriving necessary and sufficient conditions, including a link to causal inequality violations via process functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stabilizer operations (SO) define the classically simulable fragment of quantum theory, while completely stabilizer preserving operations (CSPO) form a strictly larger free set in the axiomatic resource theory of magic. A three-qubit stabilizer product basis exhibits this separation (NSWM), but a principled understanding of when and why this occurs was lacking.

Method: The authors derive a simple criterion to decide whether a given stabilizer basis's states can be perfectly discriminated using stabilizer operations alone. For stabilizer bases consisting only of product states, they exploit the connection to process functions—classical models of paradox-free causal loops—to establish an equivalence between the need for nonstabilizerness and the violation of a causal inequality.

Result: Necessary and sufficient conditions for NSWM are proved. Specifically, states in a stabilizer product basis require nonstabilizerness for perfect discrimination if and only if the corresponding process function violates a causal inequality.

Conclusion: Causal inequality violations gain a new operational interpretation as witnesses of nonstabilizerness, a form of computational nonclassicality. This links causal structure to the resource theory of magic and clarifies the SO-versus-CSPO separation.

Abstract: Stabilizer operations describe a fragment of quantum theory that is known to be efficiently classically simulable, thanks to the Gottesman-Knill theorem. For this reason, nonstabilizer resources such as magic states are necessary for universal quantum computation. Interestingly, the operational and axiomatic approaches to the resource theory of magic differ: the set of free operations in the former, namely, stabilizer operations (SO), is strictly smaller than that in the latter, namely, completely stabilizer preserving operations (CSPO). A simple example showing the separation is given by a three-qubit stabilizer product basis whose states cannot be perfectly discriminated using SO, but which do admit perfect discrimination using CSPO. Such an ensemble of states is said to exhibit nonstabilizerness without magic (NSWM). Here we obtain a principled understanding of this phenomenon, proving necessary and sufficient conditions for its existence. We first derive a simple criterion to decide whether, given a stabilizer basis, its states can be perfectly discriminated using stabilizer operations alone. We then consider the case where the stabilizer basis contains only product states and use its link with process functions---classical models of paradox-free causal loops---to prove the following: the states in a stabilizer product basis require nonstabilizerness for perfect discrimination if and only if the corresponding process function violates a causal inequality. This provides a new operational meaning to causal inequality violations as witnesses of nonstabilizerness, a form of computational nonclassicality.

</details>


### [182] [A physical and universal model of bosonic computations with Solovay-Kitaev theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40226)
*Dorian Rudolph,Arsalan Motamedi,Dhruva Sambrani,Hamid Reza Naeij,Ulysse Chabaud,Sevag Gharibian,Saeed Mehraban*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces BEQC, an energy-preserving model of bosonic quantum computation where energy enters only via input coherent states and all gates come from energy-preserving Hamiltonians, preventing unphysical energy blow-up while retaining universality and robust complexity-theoretic and gate-synthesis properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard bosonic models (Lloyd-Braunstein, hybrid oscillator-qubit) allow dramatic energy growth, yielding unphysical computational power and breaking fundamental tools like universal and efficient compilation. The authors seek a physically grounded model with controlled energy.

Method: They define Bosonic Energy-Preserving Quantum Computation (BEQC): energy is supplied solely through input coherent states and all gates are generated by energy-preserving Hamiltonians, so large energy growth is impossible by construction. They analyze its complexity-theoretic behavior and construct gate sets and synthesis procedures.

Result: BEQC efficiently simulates all polynomial-energy computations of existing models and exactly recovers BQP in the polynomial-energy regime, with several upper bounds across energy, precision, and space parameters. It has natural universal gate sets from linear optics and Kerr interactions, a Solovay-Kitaev theorem circumventing prior no-go results, and applications: rigorous GKP-state engineering, Fock-state preparation to exponential precision, and native Fock-space simulation of any qubit-based unitary.

Conclusion: BEQC is a computationally robust, physically grounded, and universal model of bosonic quantum computation that tames energy growth while preserving key power and synthesis guarantees.

Abstract: Bosonic quantum systems are among the leading architectures for quantum information processing, offering continuous-variable degrees of freedom with strong error-correction capabilities. However, standard bosonic quantum computation models such as the Lloyd-Braunstein [Lloyd and Braunstein, 1999] and hybrid oscillator-qubit models [Brenner, Dias, and Koenig, 2025; Liu et al., 2026] permit dramatic energy growth, leading to unphysical computational power and the breakdown of fundamental algorithmic tools such as universal and efficient compilation [Brenner et al., 2026; Rudolph et al., 2025]. To address this, we introduce a new model of bosonic quantum computation, Bosonic Energy-Preserving Quantum Computation (BEQC), in which energy is treated as a computational resource. Namely, energy is supplied solely through input coherent states, and all gates are generated by energy-preserving Hamiltonians. Thus, by construction, dramatic energy growth is impossible, making the model physically grounded.
  We next show that BEQC is a computationally robust and universal model in many respects, including: (1. Computational power) BEQC efficiently simulates all polynomial-energy computations in existing models, and exactly recovers BQP in the polynomial energy setting. It further admits several complexity-theoretic upper bounds when varying the energy, precision, and space parameters of the model. (2. Universal gate sets and state synthesis) BEQC has natural universal gate sets based on linear optics and Kerr interactions. In particular, we obtain a Solovay-Kitaev theorem which circumvents previous no-go results. We give various applications, including (a) a protocol for engineering GKP states with rigorous preparation guarantees, (b) Fock state preparation to exponential precision, and (c) native Fock space simulation of any qubit-based unitary.

</details>


### [183] [Randomness of exact unitary designs under symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40227)
*Christopher Vairogs,Felix Leditzky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "symmetric design strength" for finite unitary ensembles, showing that in high dimensions certain ensembles can have symmetric design strength strictly greater than their unitary design strength, and derives bounds for group-structured ensembles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical symmetries restrict which operators in a unitary ensemble are symmetry-compatible, so standard unitary design strength does not capture randomization performance under symmetry constraints. The authors aim to characterize a symmetry-aware notion of design strength and understand when finite ensembles can outperform their standard unitary design strength in symmetric settings.

Method: They define unitary design strength as the maximal t for which the ensemble's t-th moment equals that of the unitary group, and symmetric design strength as the analogous maximal t when restricted to symmetry-compatible operators and compared with symmetry-compatible unitaries. They analyze finite unitary ensembles in sufficiently high dimension across a broad family of symmetries, including global on-site U(1) and SU(d), and separately study ensembles whose operators form a finite group, deriving bounds and structural results.

Result: For sufficiently high dimension and a broad family of symmetries including global on-site U(1) and SU(d), there exist finite unitary ensembles of arbitrarily high design strength whose symmetric design strength is strictly higher. This extends prior work showing the Clifford group's symmetric design strength is strictly upper bounded by its unitary design strength for nontrivial symmetries. For group-structured ensembles, they derive bounds, prove that any finite-group ensemble has symmetric design strength at most two under global on-site U(1) and SU(d), and show uniformly weighted groups of arbitrarily high symmetric design strength exist in arbitrary dimension.

Conclusion: Symmetry-aware design strength can exceed ordinary unitary design strength for suitable finite ensembles, making them potentially more versatile than expected for unitary-randomization protocols under symmetry constraints. Group-structured ensembles face stronger limitations, with symmetric design strength at most two for global on-site U(1) and SU(d), though uniformly weighted groups with arbitrarily high symmetric design strength can still be constructed in arbitrary dimension.

Abstract: We define the unitary design strength of a finite unitary ensemble to be the maximal integer $t$ for which its $t$-th statistical moment is identical to that of the unitary group. In the presence of physical symmetry, not all operators of an ensemble are symmetry-compatible. This motivates us to define the symmetric design strength of a finite unitary ensemble to be the maximal integer $t$ for which the $t$-th moments of its symmetry-compatible sub-ensemble match those of the group of symmetry-compatible unitaries. We show that in sufficiently high dimension and for a broad family of symmetries, which includes the global on-site $\mathrm{U}(1)$ and $\mathrm{SU}(d)$ symmetries, there exist finite unitary ensembles of arbitrarily high design strength whose symmetric design strength is strictly higher. Ensembles with this property may be more versatile under symmetry constraints for protocols that rely on unitary randomization than they are without symmetry. Our results extend the previous work [Mitsuhashi and Yoshioka, PRX Quantum 4.4 (Nov. 2023)] showing that the symmetric design strength of the Clifford group is strictly upper bounded by its unitary design strength for symmetries that do not trivialize the symmetry-compatible unitaries. Due to the special significance of unitary designs with a group structure, we also derive a variety of bounds on the unitary and symmetric design strengths of finite unitary ensembles whose operators form a group. In particular, we provide a simple proof that an arbitrary ensemble of operators forming a finite group must have a symmetric design strength of at most two under the global on-site $\mathrm{U}(1)$ and $\mathrm{SU}(d)$ symmetries. Along the way, we show that there exist uniformly weighted groups of arbitrarily high symmetric design strength in arbitrary dimension and analyze their structure.

</details>


### [184] [Perfect non-local quantum computation is impossible](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40228)
*Marten Folkertsma,Dmitry Grinko,Gina Muuss,Florian Speelman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Haar-almost every two-qubit unitary admits no exact non-local quantum computation protocol with finite-dimensional resources; exact protocols reach only countably many local-unitary orbits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NLQC asks two parties to apply a joint operation using shared entanglement and one round of simultaneous quantum communication, with applications to quantum position verification, communication complexity, and quantum gravity. Approximate protocols with finite entanglement exist for every bipartite unitary, and exact protocols are known for structured families, but whether every fixed unitary admits an exact finite-resource protocol remained open.

Method: The proof shows smooth deformations of an exact protocol change its target only by local unitaries. For a fixed architecture, semialgebraic geometry restricts exact protocols to finitely many connected families, so each architecture reaches finitely many local-unitary orbits; the union over all architectures yields countably many orbits. Complementarily, they show the controlled phase diag(1,1,1,e^{iθ}) admits no exact finite-dimensional protocol when e^{iθ} is transcendental, e.g. θ=1. The framework is also extended to localizable quantum measurements.

Result: Even for two qubits, Haar-almost every unitary admits no exact NLQC protocol with finite-dimensional resource state and local operations, even when those resources are tailored to the target. Some natural explicit targets, such as controlled phase with transcendental phase, also fail. For localizable measurements, a rank-one projective measurement in a Haar-random basis is almost surely not localizable with finite-dimensional resource state and finitely many local outcomes.

Conclusion: Exact finite-resource NLQC is highly non-generic: the exactly implementable unitaries form only countably many local-unitary orbits, so generic unitaries and generic rank-one measurements cannot be realized exactly, though approximate protocols always exist.

Abstract: Non-local quantum computation (NLQC) asks two parties to apply a joint operation to their quantum inputs using an entangled resource state and one round of simultaneous quantum communication. NLQC has applications across quantum information, including attacks on quantum position verification, communication complexity, and quantum gravity.
  Every bipartite unitary has an approximate NLQC protocol with finite entanglement, and exact protocols are known for several structured families. However, it has remained open whether every fixed unitary admits an exact protocol with finite-dimensional resources. We show that, even for two qubits, Haar-almost every unitary does not admit such a protocol, even when the resource state and local operations are tailored to the target.
  The proof first shows that smooth deformations of an exact protocol can only change its target by local unitaries. For a fixed architecture, semialgebraic geometry limits the exact protocols to finitely many connected families, so each architecture reaches only finitely many local-unitary orbits. Taking the union over all architectures, the exactly implementable unitaries form countably many local-unitary orbits. We also show that some natural targets do not admit a protocol: the controlled phase $\mathrm{diag}(1,1,1,e^{iθ})$ has no exact protocol with finite-dimensional resources whenever $e^{iθ}$ is transcendental, for instance for $θ= 1$.
  Our results also apply to localizable quantum measurements, whose outcome is obtained from local measurements on inputs and a shared entangled resource state without communication. A rank-one projective measurement in a Haar-random basis is almost surely not localizable with a finite-dimensional resource state and finitely many local outcomes.

</details>


### [185] [A Quantum Algorithm for $st$-Transport on Flat Connection Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40231)
*Stacey Jeffery,Tobias J. Osborne,Galina Pass*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper generalizes undirected st-connectivity to graphs with unitary edge labels forming a flat (pure gauge) connection, defining the st-transport problem of deciding connectivity and estimating the squared overlap between transported and target states, and gives a bounded-error quantum algorithm running in time Õ(n/ε) with O(log n + log k + log(1/ε)) space, matched by an Ω(n) quantum query lower bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Undirected st-connectivity is a fundamental problem, and the authors aim to extend it to a quantum setting where edges carry unitary operations forming a flat connection. This generalization connects to consistent connection graphs and noiseless group synchronization, and is motivated by the goal of understanding connectivity when state transport involves coherent quantum operations.

Method: The problem is formalized as st-transport: given local states at s and t and an oracle that returns neighbours while coherently applying edge unitaries, decide connectivity and estimate the squared overlap between U_s(t)|ψ_s⟩ and |ψ_t⟩ to additive error ε. The authors design a transducer and apply a Metropolis-Hastings reweighting to the input graph to obtain the quantum algorithm.

Result: A bounded-error quantum algorithm for st-transport runs in time Õ(n/ε) using O(log n + log k + log(1/ε)) space. An Ω(n) quantum query lower bound is proved, holding even when s and t are promised connected, implying optimality up to polylogarithmic factors for constant ε.

Conclusion: The work generalizes classical undirected st-connectivity to graphs with quantum edge operations under a flat connection, provides an efficient space-bounded quantum algorithm via transducer design and Metropolis-Hastings reweighting, and establishes near-optimality through a matching quantum query lower bound.

Abstract: We study a generalization of undirected $st$-connectivity to graphs whose edges carry quantum operations. Let $G=(V,E)$ be an undirected graph on $n$ vertices in which each edge $\{u,v\}$ is labeled by a unitary $U_{uv}\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times k}$, with $U_{vu}=U_{uv}^\dagger$. We assume the labels form a \emph{flat} connection: the ordered product of labels along any path between a pair of vertices $u$ and $v$ is independent of the path. Equivalently, the connection is pure gauge, i.e., gauge-equivalent to the trivial connection; such graphs are exactly the consistent connection graphs of spectral graph theory and the noiseless instances of group synchronization. Consequently, whenever $s$ and $t$ are connected, transporting a state from $s$ to $t$ defines a unique unitary $U_s(t)$. Given states $|ψ_s\rangle,|ψ_t\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^k$ and an oracle that returns the neighbours of a vertex while coherently applying the corresponding edge unitaries, the \emph{$st$-transport problem} is to decide whether $s$ and $t$ are connected and, if so, to estimate the squared overlap between $U_s(t)|ψ_s\rangle$ and $|ψ_t\rangle$ to additive error $\varepsilon$. When $k=1$ and all labels are trivial, this is exactly undirected $st$-connectivity. We give a bounded-error quantum algorithm for $st$-transport that runs in time $\widetilde{O}(n/\varepsilon)$ and uses $O(\log n+\log k+\log(1/\varepsilon))$ space. We do this by designing a transducer and applying a Metropolis-Hastings reweighting to the input graph. We also prove an $Ω(n)$ quantum query lower bound that holds even when $s$ and $t$ are promised to be connected, so for constant $\varepsilon$ our algorithm is optimal up to polylogarithmic factors.

</details>


### [186] [Optimal Purity Estimation with Incoherent Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40248)
*Junseo Lee,Chirag Wadhwa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies purity estimation of an unknown quantum state under multiplicative error, characterizing the copy complexity achievable with incoherent measurements (both non-adaptive and adaptive).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating purity within multiplicative error ε is well understood for general collective measurements, requiring Θ(√d/ε² + d/ε) copies. However, collective measurements on many copies are experimentally demanding. The copy complexity under incoherent measurements was largely open, with only a non-adaptive algorithm using O(d/ε² + d²/ε) copies [PTTW26], polynomially larger than the best-known lower bound. The authors aim to close this gap.

Method: The authors design (1) a new non-adaptive incoherent measurement algorithm achieving O(d/ε² + d^{3/2}/ε) copies, and (2) a new adaptive estimator that improves complexity in the high-precision regime ε = o(1/d). They also establish matching lower bounds for non-adaptive algorithms and combine the two estimators to derive the overall optimal complexity for incoherent purity estimation.

Result: The non-adaptive algorithm uses O(d/ε² + d^{3/2}/ε) copies, improving the d²/ε term of prior work, and this is proven optimal among non-adaptive algorithms. The adaptive estimator improves complexity for ε = o(1/d). Combining both yields the optimal incoherent copy complexity Θ(min{d/ε² + d^{3/2}/ε, d²/ε + √d/ε²}).

Conclusion: The paper tightens the copy complexity of incoherent purity estimation, providing matching upper and lower bounds. It shows a separation between non-adaptive and adaptive strategies, with adaptivity helping in the high-precision regime, and fully characterizes the optimal complexity via the min of the two trade-offs.

Abstract: In this work, we consider the fundamental task of estimating the purity of an unknown state to within $\textit{multiplicative}$ error $\varepsilon$. When one can perform general collective measurements, $Θ\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon^2} + \frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)$ copies are known to be necessary and sufficient for this problem [AISW20]. However, implementing collective measurements on such a large number of copies can be experimentally demanding, and we thus aim to characterize the copy complexity of this problem with incoherent measurements.
  In this setting, the only non-trivial result is a non-adaptive algorithm that uses $O\left(
  \frac{d}{\varepsilon^2} + \frac{d^2}{\varepsilon}
  \right)$ copies [PTTW26], which is polynomially larger than the best-known lower bound. Our first result is a new algorithm performing non-adaptive incoherent measurements that succeeds using $O\left(
  \frac{d}{\varepsilon^2} + \frac{d^{3/2}}{\varepsilon}
  \right)$ copies, improving on the latter term in the copy complexity. Moreover, we show that for any algorithm restricted to non-adaptive measurements, the above copy complexity is optimal.
  We also develop a new adaptive estimator for the purity of a state that improves on the above complexity in the high-precision regime, i.e., for $\varepsilon = o(1/d)$. We also show that the non-adaptive and adaptive estimators together yield the optimal complexity for incoherent purity estimation; in particular, we show that the copy complexity of this problem is $$
  Θ\left(\min\left\{
  \frac{d}{\varepsilon^2} + \frac{d^{3/2}}{\varepsilon}, \frac{d^2}{\varepsilon} + \frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon^2}\right\}
  \right).
  $$

</details>


### [187] [An (almost) efficient classical algorithm for sampling from typical Gibbs states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40250)
*Saleh Naghdi,Eric R. Anschuetz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper provides strong evidence against exponential quantum advantage in Gibbs sampling for the quantum p-spin model by giving a classical sampling algorithm based on algorithmic stochastic localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gibbs sampling underlies Boltzmann machine-based quantum learning and quantum supremacy proposals, but its classical average-case complexity for physically relevant models is unresolved. While quantum advantage is known for worst-case Gibbs state properties and efficient quantum preparation exists at high temperatures, prior results show classical quasi-polynomial algorithms match quantum for local expectation values—leaving the stronger sampling task open.

Method: The authors design a classical algorithm for sampling from the Gibbs state to polynomially small total variation error, based on algorithmic stochastic localization (ASL). Their main technical contribution is a generalized convergence theorem for ASL that reduces approximate sampling on the hypercube to estimating the mean of a related tilted distribution to low additive error. They extend known quasi-polynomial algorithms for local expectation values of a related disordered model to compute these tilted means for the quantum p-spin model, supported by a non-rigorous physics argument that the mean-estimation works down to the spin glass transition temperature.

Result: A classical algorithm achieves Gibbs sampling for the quantum p-spin model to any polynomially small error in total variation distance. The generalized ASL convergence theorem and the extension of quasi-polynomial tilted-mean estimation constitute the key technical results; the physics argument indicates the mean-estimation algorithm operates down to the spin glass transition temperature.

Conclusion: Since efficient quantum algorithms also fail at the spin glass transition, the results suggest quantum algorithms cannot Gibbs sample the quantum p-spin model at temperatures below those at which classical algorithms are also (almost) efficient—providing strong evidence against exponential quantum advantage in Gibbs sampling for this ensemble.

Abstract: Gibbs state preparation has emerged as a potential avenue for quantum advantage in the study of physical systems. Although there is known advantage for computing local properties of worst-case Gibbs states, recent results suggest a different picture for average-case local systems: at temperatures where their Gibbs states can be efficiently prepared quantumly, quasi-polynomial time classical algorithms can also compute local expectation values. Yet this leaves open the possibility of an advantage under a stronger notion of simulation: sampling. Gibbs sampling underlies Boltzmann machine-based quantum learning algorithms and quantum supremacy proposals, but its classical average-case complexity for physically relevant models remains unresolved. Here, we give strong evidence against an exponential quantum advantage in Gibbs sampling for an ensemble of local systems known as the quantum $p$-spin model.
