We describe a classical algorithm for sampling from the Gibbs state to any polynomially small error in total variation distance. Our algorithm is based on a meta-algorithm known as algorithmic stochastic localization (ASL). Our main technical result is a generalized convergence theorem for ASL which reduces approximate sampling from any distribution on the hypercube to estimating the mean of a related "tilted distribution" to sufficiently low additive error. We then extend known quasi-polynomial time algorithms for computing local expectation values of a related disordered model to compute these "tilted means" for the quantum $p$-spin model. Finally, we give a non-rigorous physics argument that this mean-estimation algorithm works down to the spin glass transition temperature. As efficient quantum algorithms also fail at this transition, this suggests they cannot Gibbs sample the quantum $p$-spin model at temperatures below that for which classical algorithms are also (almost) efficient.

</details>


### [188] [Quantum oblique eigenprojection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40254)
*Alexander M. Dalzell,Yuan Su*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quantum algorithm performs oblique (nonorthogonal) eigenprojections given block-encoding access to a matrix, with near-linear query complexity in the inverse gap, enabling complex-spectrum eigenstate preparation, continuous-time algebraic Riccati equations, and Sylvester equations with speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard quantum linear-algebra routines handle orthogonal eigenprojections, but general square matrices decompose into generalized, nonorthogonal eigensubspaces. Existing spectral methods (e.g., quantum eigenvalue transformation) are largely restricted to real or normal spectra, leaving oblique projections and nonanalytic matrix functions unaddressed. The authors aim to extend quantum spectral processing to general (non-normal, complex-spectrum) matrices.

Method: Under block-encoding access to the input matrix, the algorithm computes the oblique eigenprojection Π. It employs a two-sided block preconditioning that uses a discrete Fourier transform of the matrix resolvent, attaining query complexity nearly linear in the inverse gap and a normalization factor close to ||Π||, contingent on a spectral-set condition on the input (subsuming numeric-range or diagonalizability assumptions).

Result: The method matches known results for orthogonal eigenprojections. Applications demonstrated: (i) preparing eigenstates of matrices with complex eigenvalues, extending Low and Su's quantum eigenvalue transformation beyond real spectra; (ii) solving continuous-time algebraic Riccati equations, giving a cubic speedup over the prior solver of Rodenas-Ruiz, Zhao, and Lee; (iii) solving ordinary Sylvester equations, giving a quadratic improvement over the direct augmented method of Wang and Liu.

Conclusion: Quantum computers can efficiently perform oblique eigenprojections of general square matrices, covering non-normal and complex-spectrum cases. This points to a promising route for applying nonanalytic matrix functions on quantum computers, with broad implications for spectral and equation-solving tasks.

Abstract: Every square matrix decomposes its underlying Hilbert space into generalized, nonorthogonal eigensubspaces. We show that a quantum computer can perform such an oblique eigenprojection $Π$ given block encoding access to the input matrix. Our approach has a query complexity nearly linear in the inverse gap and a normalization factor close to $\lVertΠ\rVert$ under a spectral-set condition on the input. This covers common assumptions on the numerical range or diagonalizability and matches known results for orthogonal eigenprojections. We achieve this with a two-sided block preconditioning that uses a discrete Fourier transform of the matrix resolvent. We describe applications to: (i) preparing eigenstates of matrices with complex eigenvalues, extending the quantum eigenvalue transformation algorithm of Low and Su beyond real spectra; (ii) solving continuous-time algebraic Riccati equations, cubically speeding up a prior solver of Rodenas-Ruiz, Zhao, and Lee; and (iii) solving ordinary Sylvester equations, quadratically improving a direct augmented method of Wang and Liu. Our result suggests a promising route to applying nonanalytic matrix functions on quantum computers.

</details>


### [189] [A General Theory of Multi-Resource Theories Involving Finite-Group Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40255)
*Yosuke Mitsuhashi,Hiroyasu Tajima*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a unified theory of combining finite-group asymmetry with other quantum resource theories, showing that the conversion rate of the combined theory typically equals the minimum of the two constituent rates, governed by the accessibility of symmetry information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realistic quantum settings impose several constraints simultaneously, making it important to study multi-resource theories. Combinations with asymmetry are especially natural because symmetry is fundamental and ubiquitous in physics, yet a general framework for such combinations was lacking.

Method: The authors treat combinations of finite-group asymmetry with a broad range of resource theories in a unified manner, analyzing how the effect of combining two constraints depends on the accessibility of symmetry information under the underlying resource theory—whether the information needed for conversion can be extracted from the input and used to control free operations. They also analyze symmetry imposed on elementary operations rather than only on the resulting channel.

Result: When symmetry information can be accessed with asymptotically vanishing error, the conversion rate of the combined theory equals the smaller of the rates of the two constituent theories, so combining incurs no additional cost. This holds for LOCC on arbitrary mixed multipartite states, magic-state conversion of multiqubit systems under Clifford symmetries, and Gibbs-preserving operations. Thermal operations, being time-translation covariant, access only limited symmetry information, yielding an additional compatibility condition between the two symmetries. Analogous results are established for LOCC and thermal operations under suitable assumptions, and for magic when symmetry is represented by Pauli operators. Finite-group symmetry does not reduce the asymptotic ergotropy per copy and introduces no new completely passive states.

Conclusion: Combining finite-group asymmetry with a resource theory generally costs nothing in asymptotic conversion rate, provided symmetry information is accessible; when it is not (e.g., thermal operations), an extra compatibility condition between symmetries arises. Finite-group symmetry also leaves asymptotic ergotropy per copy unchanged.

Abstract: Resource theories provide a universal framework for quantifying properties of quantum states, such as entanglement, magic, athermality, and asymmetry, as resources under various constraints encountered in quantum science. Since realistic settings typically impose several constraints at once, it is important to study multi-resource theories that combine them. Among these, combinations with asymmetry are especially natural, as symmetry is fundamental and ubiquitous in physics. Here we develop a general theory that treats combinations of finite-group asymmetry with a broad range of resource theories in a unified manner. Our key observation is that the effect of combining the two constraints is governed by the accessibility of symmetry information under the underlying resource theory: whether the information needed for the conversion can be extracted from the input and used to control free operations. If this can be done with asymptotically vanishing error, we show that the conversion rate of the combined theory equals the smaller of the rates of the two constituent theories, so combining them incurs no additional cost. This condition holds for LOCC on arbitrary mixed multipartite states, magic-state conversion of multiqubit systems under Clifford symmetries, and Gibbs-preserving operations. Thermal operations, being time-translation covariant, can access only limited symmetry information, giving an additional compatibility condition between the two symmetries. We also analyze symmetry imposed on elementary operations rather than only on the resulting channel. We establish analogous results for LOCC and thermal operations under suitable assumptions, and for magic when the symmetry is represented by Pauli operators. Finally, we show that finite-group symmetry does not reduce the asymptotic ergotropy per copy and hence introduces no new completely passive states.

</details>


### [190] [Self-testing the toric code against classical communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40259)
*Gustavo Fróes,Amanda Wei,Carlos de Gois,Denis Rochette,Marc-Olivier Renou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a relaxed device-independent self-testing framework for quantum networks where untrusted devices may secretly exchange classical messages within a bounded distance. It shows that the toric code's code space can be noise-robustly self-tested against communication up to distances proportional to the network diameter, and extends results to the surface code and cluster states robust to one round of nearest-neighbor communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing device-independent self-tests break down as soon as parties can exchange messages. There is a need for certification protocols that remain sound even when untrusted devices can communicate, which is a realistic scenario in quantum networks.

Method: The authors consider a quantum network setting where honest parties never communicate, but untrusted devices may secretly exchange classical messages within a bounded distance. They show the toric code's code space, distributed over a torus-geometry network, can be self-tested in this setting, and develop an algorithmic method for constructing such protocols.

Result: The toric code's code space can be self-tested noise-robustly against communication up to distances proportional to the network diameter. The results are extended to the surface code and cluster states, robust to one round of nearest-neighbor classical communication.

Conclusion: Device-independent self-testing can be made sound even in the presence of bounded classical communication between untrusted devices in a quantum network, demonstrated for the toric code, surface code, and cluster states.

Abstract: Device-independent self-testing certifies a multipartite quantum state from the correlations it produces alone, without assumptions on the inner workings of the devices. Most existing self-tests, however, break down as soon as the parties can exchange messages. We consider a relaxed setting in a quantum network, where in the honest version of the protocol, the parties never communicate, yet certification remains sound even in an untrusted case where the untrusted devices secretly exchange classical messages within a bounded distance in the network. We show that the code space of the toric code, distributed over a network with the geometry of a torus, can be self-tested in this setting in a noise-robust way, against communication up to distances proportional to the network diameter. We also develop an algorithmic method for constructing such protocols and extend our results to the surface code and cluster states, robust to one round of nearest-neighbor classical communication

</details>


### [191] [Quantum Algorithms for Minimum Generating Set](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40260)
*Bireswar Das,Udit Kumar,Kavita Samant,Dhara Thakkar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents polynomial-time quantum algorithms for computing minimum generating sets of solvable black-box groups and black-box groups in the class Γ_d, and shows that the general minimum generating set problem lies in NP ∩ coAM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The minimum generating set problem is a fundamental computational problem in group theory. Computing such sets efficiently, especially for black-box groups, is challenging; quantum computation offers a promising avenue. Prior work largely addressed solvable or specific classes, leaving broader classes and structural decompositions (e.g., chief series, factor group decompositions) less explored. The paper aims to extend efficient quantum algorithms to solvable groups and to the class Γ_d, and to characterize the complexity of the general problem.

Method: The authors develop polynomial-time quantum algorithms. First, they compute a minimum-sized generating set of solvable black-box groups. Then, for the class Γ_d (where every non-abelian composition factor embeds in S_d for fixed d), they design quantum algorithms to compute the direct product decomposition of abelian factor groups and to solve the constructive membership problem for factor groups. Leveraging these, they compute a chief series of Γ_d groups, and using that series, they construct minimum generating sets. Finally, they establish a complexity upper bound by placing the general minimum generating set problem in NP ∩ coAM.

Result: The paper yields polynomial-time quantum algorithms for: (1) computing a minimum-sized generating set of solvable black-box groups; (2) computing the direct product decomposition of abelian factor groups and solving the constructive membership problem for factor groups of Γ_d; (3) computing a chief series of groups in Γ_d; and (4) computing minimum generating sets of groups in Γ_d. Additionally, it proves that the minimum generating set problem for general black-box groups is in NP ∩ coAM.

Conclusion: Minimum generating sets can be computed efficiently by quantum algorithms for solvable black-box groups and for the class Γ_d, with chief series and factor-group decompositions serving as key structural tools. The general black-box minimum generating set problem is contained in NP ∩ coAM, providing a complexity-theoretic characterization.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for computing a minimum-sized generating set of solvable black-box groups. Next, we consider the class $Γ_d$ of black-box groups, where every non-abelian composition factor is isomorphic to a subgroup of the symmetric group $S_d$ for a fixed $d$. We design polynomial-time quantum algorithms to compute the direct product decomposition of abelian factor groups and solve the constructive membership problem for factor groups of groups from $Γ_d$. With the help of these algorithms, we design a quantum algorithm for computing a chief series of black-box groups from $Γ_d$. Using the chief series, we construct a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for computing minimum generating sets of black-box groups from $Γ_d$. Finally, we show that the minimum generating set problem for general black-box groups is in $\textrm{NP} \cap \textrm{coAM}$.

</details>


### [192] [Quantum List Recovery and Decoding: Achievability and Limitations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40262)
*Fernando Granha Jeronimo,Xiaojuan Ma*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes combinatorial upper and lower bounds for quantum list recovery (QLR) and quantum list decoding (QLD) of balanced folded quantum Reed–Solomon codes and balanced random CSS codes, characterizing exactly how list sizes scale as the radius approaches the quantum Singleton bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QLR and QLD require short lists of logically distinct Pauli corrections consistent with a syndrome and error constraints. For CSS codes, two problems arise: separating X- and Z-sectors can multiply output list sizes, and distinct classical candidates may collapse after stabilizer quotienting. The work aims to resolve these issues and pin down the correct list-size and radius tradeoffs near the quantum Singleton bound.

Method: The authors derive combinatorial bounds for balanced folded quantum Reed–Solomon (FQRS) codes and balanced random CSS codes. Achievability uses a pairing lemma for joint X/Z candidate lists (building on Brakensiek–Chen–Dhar–Zhang) that preserves the one-sector coefficient and avoids product list-size loss. The QLD converse uses a quantum generalized Singleton bound via classical projection-and-patching with stabilizer distinctness. QLR lower bounds adapt the folded Reed–Solomon construction of Chen–Zhang to keep candidates stabilizer distinct. For random CSS codes, they show classical bad lists survive the stabilizer quotient w.h.p.

Result: With quantum rate R∈(0,1) and component rate R1=(1+R)/2, as the radius ρ=(1−R)/2−γ approaches the quantum Singleton bound, L*_QLR = ℓ^{Θ(R1/γ)} and L*_QLD = Θ((1−R)/γ) as γ↓0 (deterministically for FQRS codes, w.h.p. for balanced random CSS codes). The asymptotically exact QLD radius tradeoff is ρ*_L = (L/(L+1))·(1−R)/2. These results extend to the average-radius setting.

Conclusion: The paper gives tight combinatorial characterizations of QLR and QLD list sizes and the exact QLD radius tradeoff near the quantum Singleton bound for FQRS and balanced random CSS codes, resolving the X/Z product loss and stabilizer-quotient collapse issues, with extensions to the average-radius setting.

Abstract: Quantum list recovery (QLR) and quantum list decoding (QLD) seek short lists of logically distinct Pauli corrections consistent with a syndrome and prescribed error constraints. For CSS codes, two issues arise: treating the \(X\)- and \(Z\)-sectors separately can multiply their output list sizes, while distinct classical candidates can collapse after stabilizer quotienting.
  We study combinatorial upper and lower bounds for balanced folded quantum Reed--Solomon (FQRS) codes and balanced random CSS codes, both studied by Bergamaschi, Golowich, and Gunn (STOC 24). Let \(R\in(0,1)\) be the quantum rate and \(R_1=(1+R)/2\) the common component rate. As the radius \(ρ=(1-R)/2-γ\) approaches the quantum Singleton bound, we have, deterministically for FQRS codes and w.h.p. for balanced random CSS codes, \[
  L^\star_{\rm QLR}
  =
  \ell^{Θ(R_1/γ)},
  \qquad
  L^\star_{\rm QLD}
  =
  Θ\!\left(\frac{1-R}γ\right)
  \qquad (γ\downarrow0). \] The asymptotically exact QLD radius tradeoff is \[
  ρ_L^\star
  =
  \frac{L}{L+1}\frac{1-R}{2}. \] These conclusions extend to the average-radius setting.
  For achievability, building on the work of Brakensiek, Chen, Dhar, and Zhang (STOC 2026), we establish a pairing lemma for joint \(X/Z\) candidate lists that preserves the one-sector coefficient and avoids a product loss in list size. For the QLD converse, we prove a quantum generalized Singleton bound based on the classical projection-and-patching argument with stabilizer distinctness. For the QLR lower bounds, we adapt the folded Reed--Solomon construction of Chen and Zhang (STOC 2025) so that the candidates remain stabilizer distinct. For random CSS codes, we show classical bad lists survive the stabilizer quotient with high probability.

</details>


### [193] [Superlinear Quantum Query Lower Bounds for Subgraph Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40263)
*Amin Shiraz Gilani,Xingyu Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves the first unconditional superlinear lower bounds on the bounded-error quantum query complexity of subgraph detection, resolving a longstanding open question. It shows detecting $K_r$ requires $n^{\lambda_r-o(1)}$ queries (with $\lambda_4=19/18$), generalizes to all connected $H$ with chromatic number $c\ge 4$, and demonstrates that chromatic number alone does not characterize complexity via the bipartite case $K_{r,r}$.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subgraph detection is a fundamental problem, but no unconditional superlinear quantum query lower bounds were known. Standard techniques fail because a copy of $H$ is a constant-size certificate, so the adversary method with nonnegative weights cannot yield superlinear bounds.

Method: The main technical ingredient is a lower bound for finding an all-ones certificate from a known family when input bits are sampled independently. The proof combines Zhandry's compressed oracle with conditioning on a randomly planted certificate, adapting an argument of Belovs. Hard instances are constructed from graphs containing many copies of the target subgraph with limited overlap: Gowers–Janzer constructions for cliques and a random construction for complete bipartite graphs.

Result: For every fixed $r\ge 4$, detecting $K_r$ requires $n^{\lambda_r-o(1)}$ queries, where $\lambda_4=19/18$, the exponents $\lambda_r$ increase strictly, and $\lambda_r\ge 2-4\sqrt{2/r}+O(1/r)$. Superlinear lower bounds hold for every fixed connected $H$ with chromatic number $c\ge 4$, approaching quadratic as $c$ grows: $n^{2-O(\sqrt{\log\log c/c})-o(1)}$. Detecting $K_{r,r}$ requires $n^{\beta_r-o(1)}$ queries, with $\beta_{10}=181/180$ and $\beta_r\ge 2-O(1/\sqrt{r})$.

Conclusion: The paper establishes the first unconditional superlinear quantum query lower bounds for subgraph detection. It shows chromatic number alone does not characterize the quantum query complexity of subgraph detection, and that the bounds approach quadratic as the chromatic number grows.

Abstract: Subgraph detection asks whether an $n$-vertex graph, accessed through queries to its adjacency matrix, contains a copy of a fixed graph $H$. We prove the first unconditional superlinear lower bounds on the bounded-error quantum query complexity of this problem, answering a longstanding open question. A copy of $H$ is a certificate of constant size, so the adversary method with nonnegative weights cannot prove superlinear lower bounds.
  For every fixed $r\ge 4$, detecting the clique $K_r$ requires $n^{λ_r-o(1)}$ queries, where $λ_4=19/18$, the exponents $λ_r$ increase strictly with $r$, and $λ_r\ge 2-4\sqrt{2/r}+O(1/r)$. More generally, we prove superlinear lower bounds for detecting every fixed connected graph $H$ with chromatic number $c\ge 4$. These bounds approach quadratic as $c$ grows: for sufficiently large $c$, detection requires $n^{2-O(\sqrt{\log\log c/c})-o(1)}$ queries. Chromatic number alone does not characterize the quantum query complexity of subgraph detection: we show that detecting the complete bipartite graph $K_{r,r}$ requires $n^{β_r-o(1)}$ queries, where $β_{10}=181/180$ and $β_r\ge 2-O(1/\sqrt{r})$.
  Our main technical result is a lower bound for finding an all-ones certificate from a known family when the input bits are sampled independently. Its proof combines Zhandry's compressed oracle (CRYPTO 2019) with conditioning on a randomly planted certificate, adapting an argument of Belovs (FOCS 2026). Our hard instances are built from graphs containing many copies of the desired subgraph with limited overlap. For cliques, we use a construction of Gowers and Janzer (CPC 2021); for complete bipartite graphs, we use a random construction.

</details>


### [194] [Two-Stage Quantum-Classical Distribution Network Reconfiguration via Cycle-Edge Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40264)
*Nowar Alashkar,Cade Kennedy,Phillip C. Lotshaw,Shaked Regev,Miguel Angel Lopez-Ruiz,Ananth Kaushik,Paul Smith,Claudio Girotto,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A two-stage quantum-classical method for distribution network reconfiguration (DNR) combines a linear-ramp QAOA (LR-QAOA) search over cycle-edge-encoded spanning-tree subspaces with an MISOCP solver guided by the resulting sample distribution, achieving near-best-known solutions faster than an unguided solver on 33–417 bus networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DNR is a combinatorial optimization problem seeking a low-loss network topology under topological and electrical operating constraints. Solving it at scale is challenging, motivating a hybrid approach that leverages quantum search to guide classical optimization.

Method: First, an iterative LR-QAOA searches topologically feasible subspaces built via a novel cycle-edge encoding that guarantees every represented configuration is a spanning tree while permitting subspace size and coverage to be tuned to the available qubit budget. Second, the resulting sample distribution guides a mixed-integer second-order-cone programming (MISOCP) solver.

Result: Evaluated on six distribution networks from 33 to 417 buses via simulation and trapped-ion quantum hardware execution. Across all six systems, the MISOCP solver guided by hardware-derived distributions reaches an incumbent within 1% of the best-known solution in less median solver time than the unguided solver.

Conclusion: The two-stage quantum-classical framework yields high-quality DNR solutions with reduced median solve time, and the cycle-edge encoding together with hardware-derived distributions effectively bridges quantum search and classical optimization.

Abstract: Distribution network reconfiguration (DNR) is a combinatorial optimization problem that seeks a low-loss network topology subject to topological and electrical operating constraints. In this work, we propose a two-stage quantum-classical method for DNR. First, an iterative linear-ramp Quantum Alternating Operator Ansatz (LR-QAOA) searches topologically feasible subspaces constructed using a novel cycle-edge encoding. The encoding guarantees that every represented configuration is a spanning tree while allowing the size and coverage of each subspace to be adjusted to the available qubit budget. Second, the resulting sample distribution is used to guide a mixed-integer second-order-cone programming (MISOCP) solver. The method is evaluated on six distribution networks ranging from 33 to 417 buses through simulation and execution on trapped-ion quantum hardware. Across all six test systems, the MISOCP solver guided by hardware-derived distributions reaches an incumbent within 1\% of the best-known solution in less median solver time than the corresponding unguided solver.

</details>


### [195] [Compatibility of quantum instruments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40266)
*Chloe Kim,Yujie Zhang,Eric Chitambar,Marius Junge,Felix Leditzky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper generalizes measurement compatibility to d-compatibility for measurements, channels, and instruments, formulates it as a common linear factorization through a d-dimensional register, defines a compatibility functional to certify d-incompatibility, and applies it to quantum information tasks such as Schmidt number certification and memory-bounded preparation games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Measurement compatibility concerns whether a family of measurements can be simulated by one parent measurement plus classical post-processing. The authors aim to extend this notion to settings where the parent passes only a d-dimensional quantum system while still allowing unlimited classical information, thereby interpolating between full compatibility and incompatibility and enabling quantitative certification of limited quantum resources.

Method: The authors formulate d-compatibility as a common linear factorization of operator spaces and operator systems, showing that d-compatible families form a closed set even with infinitely many settings and separable infinite-dimensional input. They define a compatibility functional as the smallest product of completely bounded norms over common factorizations through a d-dimensional register, and use factorization theory of operator spaces to establish d-incompatibility results. They also connect d-compatibility of multi-instruments to the preparability of Choi assemblages via channel-state duality.

Result: Using factorization theory of operator spaces, the authors show that measure-and-prepare channels defined via mutually unbiased bases and pinching channels defined via Heisenberg-Weyl operators are d-incompatible in certain regimes of d. They link d-compatibility of multi-instruments to the preparability of Choi assemblages, and use this connection to certify Schmidt numbers of bipartite states and the implementability of instruments using one-way LOCC protocols. The compatibility functional also bounds the generalized robustness of compatibility.

Conclusion: The paper establishes d-compatibility as a unifying framework for limited quantum memory in simulating families of quantum operations, provides quantitative certification tools via the compatibility functional, and connects these ideas to entanglement certification and one-way LOCC implementability. The compatibility functional is interpreted through a memory-bounded nontransient preparation game, bounding the generalized robustness of compatibility.

Abstract: Measurement compatibility asks whether a family of quantum measurements can be simulated by one parent measurement followed by classical post-processing. We extend this measurement compatibility to the notion of \emph{$d$-compatibility} for measurements, channels, and instruments whose parent passes a $d$-dimensional quantum system and unlimited classical information to the post-processing. We formulate $d$-compatibility as a common linear factorization of operator spaces and operator systems and show that $d$-compatible families form a closed set, even with infinitely many settings and a separable infinite-dimensional input. To certify $d$-incompatibility, we define the compatibility functional as the smallest product of completely bounded norms over common factorizations through a $d$-dimensional register. Using factorization theory of operator spaces, we show that measure-and-prepare channels defined in terms of mutually unbiased bases and pinching channels defined in terms of Heisenberg-Weyl operators are $d$-incompatible in certain regimes of $d$. We also link $d$-compatibility of multi-instruments to the preparability of Choi assemblages using channel-state duality, and use this connection to show that for bipartite states the (in)compatibility of instruments can be used to certify Schmidt numbers and the implementability of the instrument using one-way LOCC protocols. Finally, our compatibility functional also bounds the generalized robustness of compatibility, which we interpret through a memory-bounded nontransient preparation game.

</details>


### [196] [Testing quantum Gaussianity with constant sample complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40270)
*Mahtab Yaghubi Rad,Ricard Puig,Saksham Hassanandani,Luke Coffman,Jens Eisert,Carlos Bravo-Prieto,Antonio Anna Mele*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work provides optimal and robust algorithms for testing fermionic and bosonic Gaussian states, with sample complexity independent of system size, particle number, and energy, using experimentally feasible measurements and a unified "gradient-flow method."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Testing whether a quantum state possesses a given structure (e.g., being Gaussian) is a fundamental and practical task in quantum information, but optimal tests of Gaussianity were previously unknown despite Gaussian states being central to quantum optics, many-body physics, and efficient classical simulation.

Method: The authors develop optimal and robust Gaussianity testers using experimentally feasible measurements tied to exact symmetry characterizations: Bell sampling on two copies for fermions and passive interferometry with photon counting on three copies for bosons. Tolerant testers use joint measurements on a larger but still constant number of copies. The core analytical tool is a unified "gradient-flow method," where representation theory converts approximate satisfaction of the defining symmetry into quantitative control of the gradient of the rejection probability, enabling a continuous descent from the input state to the target family.

Result: Optimal and robust algorithms for testing both fermionic and bosonic Gaussian states, achieving sample complexity independent of system size, particle number, and energy, along with tolerant testers requiring a constant number of copies.

Conclusion: The proposed gradient-flow method unifies the analysis across structured families and yields optimal Gaussianity testers, establishing benchmarks for testing structured quantum states.

Abstract: Efficiently testing whether a quantum state possesses a given structure is both a fundamental and a practical task in quantum information. Among the most important structured families are Gaussian states, which underpin quantum optics and many-body physics while defining paradigmatic regimes of efficient classical simulation. Despite the central role of Gaussian states, optimal tests of Gaussianity have remained unknown. Here we give optimal and robust algorithms for testing fermionic and bosonic Gaussian states, with sample complexity independent of system size, particle number, and energy. Remarkably, the protocols implement experimentally feasible measurements associated with known exact symmetry characterizations of Gaussianity, using Bell sampling on two copies for fermions and passive interferometry with photon counting on three copies for bosons. We further obtain tolerant testers requiring joint measurements on a larger, but still constant, number of copies. At the heart of our analysis is a unified approach---which we call the gradient-flow method---that applies across all structured families considered here and underlies our optimal testers: representation theory turns approximate satisfaction of the defining symmetry into quantitative control of the gradient of the rejection probability, allowing a continuous descent from the input state to the target family whose length bounds their distance.

</details>


### [197] [Refined sample complexities from the tomographic rate function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40271)
*Arick Grootveld,Alexander Maloney,Jason Pollack,Peixue Wu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a framework for analyzing covariant quantum state tomography protocols by their tomographic rate function rather than sample complexity, showing that rate functions obey strict orderings even when all protocols are sample-order-optimal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tomography protocols are usually compared by sample complexity, but this coarse metric cannot distinguish protocols that are all order-optimal. A finer performance indicator is needed to differentiate their large-deviation behavior.

Method: The authors establish an analysis framework for covariant protocols and derive their rate functions, including random purification-based protocols and the sample-optimal protocol of Haah et al. The rate functions are expressed via a new family of divergences subsuming the reverse sandwiched Rényi divergence and Keyl's annealed quantum relative entropy. A Wasserstein-distance-based sample complexity is also introduced for comparison.

Result: The derived rate functions obey a strict ordering despite every protocol being sample-order-optimal. This ordering translates into analogous strict inequalities for the Wasserstein-type sample complexity.

Conclusion: The tomographic rate function is a more fine-grained and discriminating indicator of tomography protocol performance than conventional sample complexity.

Abstract: Protocols for quantum state tomography can be characterized either by their sample complexity or by the tomographic rate function that governs the large deviation behaviour of error estimates. We establish a framework for the analysis of covariant protocols which allows us to compare these measures. First, we derive the rate functions for several protocols, including random purification-based protocols and the sample-optimal protocol of Haah et al. These rate functions are expressed in terms of a new family of divergences which includes the reverse sandwiched Rényi divergence and Keyl's annealed quantum relative entropy as special cases. We show that these rate functions obey a strict ordering, even though the sample complexity of each protocol is order optimal. We consider a different version of sample complexity based on Wasserstein distance rather than trace distance, and show that the ordering of the rate functions implies analogous strict inequalities for the Wasserstein-type sample complexity. Thus the tomographic rate function is a more fine-grained indicator of the performance of a tomography protocol than the usual sample complexity.

</details>


### [198] [Conditioning-Free Non-Uniform Quantum Fourier and Chebyshev Transforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40277)
*Chaowen Guan,Akshit Katiyar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient quantum algorithm for the non-uniform Chebyshev transform (projection onto Chebyshev polynomials sampled at nodes uniform in x∈[-1,1], non-uniform in θ=arccos x), building on an improved Non-uniform Quantum Fourier Transform (NUQFT) that removes geometry-dependent conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QFT-based quantum Chebyshev transforms cannot handle the non-uniform node sampling setting where nodes are uniform in x but non-uniform in angle θ=arccos x. Prior NUQFT work had error bounds depending on a geometry-dependent parameter κ, which the authors aim to eliminate.

Method: The construction improves an existing NUQFT by removing conditioning from non-uniform node sampling, making error bounds independent of the geometry-dependent parameter κ. It exploits the fact that the Chebyshev transform matrix is the average of two Type-II non-uniform DFTs, implemented with a single controlled NUQFT circuit. Explicit oracle constructions are provided, including the previously assumed row-access oracle.

Result: The resulting ε-accurate block encoding has O(1) normalization, uses O(L) qubits and Õ(L²) gates, where L = log N + log(1/ε). An end-to-end implementation with complexity analysis is given, including success probability and output-state error.

Conclusion: The paper delivers an efficient quantum algorithm for the non-uniform Chebyshev transform with a block encoding of O(1) normalization, achieving explicit oracle constructions and a full complexity analysis including success probability and error bounds.

Abstract: We present an efficient quantum algorithm for the non-uniform Chebyshev transform. It is defined as the projection of a function onto Chebyshev polynomials sampled at given nodes that are uniform in $x\in[-1,1]$, and hence non-uniform in the angle $θ=\arccos x$, a setting that QFT-based quantum Chebyshev transforms cannot handle. Our construction is based on an improvement of an existing Non-uniform Quantum Fourier Transform (NUQFT) whereby we remove the conditioning from non-uniform node sampling. Hence, error bounds are independent of the geometry-dependent parameter $κ$ of prior work. We use the fact that Chebyshev transform matrix is the average of two Type-II non-uniform DFTs, which we implement with a single controlled NUQFT circuit. We provide explicit oracle constructions, including the row-access oracle previously left as an assumption. The resulting $\varepsilon$-accurate block encoding has $O(1)$ normalization and uses $O(L)$ qubits and $\widetilde O(L^2)$ gates, where $L=\log N+\log(1/\varepsilon)$. We give an end-to-end implementation with complexity analysis, including the success probability and output-state error.

</details>


### [199] [Computational Bounds for $f$-Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40278)
*Oren Renard,Nicholas Spooner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves unconditional resource lower bounds for the f-routing quantum position verification protocol, showing that any uniformly generated low-resource attacking strategy succeeding on all inputs forces the target function f into restricted complexity classes, yielding explicit functions secure against polynomial and quasipolynomial qubit attacks under computational restrictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: f-routing is a leading quantum position verification candidate, but security guarantees for explicit functions remain limited. Prior works relied on communication-complexity bounds, which are inherently at most linear in the input length, capping achievable lower bounds (e.g., q ≤ log n for the inner product function). The paper aims to bypass these limitations and establish stronger resource lower bounds.

Method: The authors prove unconditional resource lower bounds for uniform attackers, with new proof techniques that circumvent communication-complexity arguments. They analyze what computational complexity f must have if a uniformly generated strategy using q qubits with polynomial description length succeeds with probability at least 1-ε on every input. Three adversarial computation restrictions are considered: arbitrary quantum channels, explicit Pauli-sparse unitaries with at most s nonzero Pauli coefficients, and Clifford+T circuits with at most t magic gates.

Result: Under success probability ≥ 1-ε for all inputs: (1) arbitrary quantum channel strategies imply f ∈ QSZK(poly(nq)); (2) explicit Pauli-sparse unitary strategies with ≤ s nonzero Pauli coefficients imply f ∈ DTIME(poly(nqs)); (3) Clifford+T strategies with ≤ t magic gates imply f ∈ DTIME(poly(nq2^t)).

Conclusion: Using time and space hierarchies, the results yield explicit functions secure against polynomial and even quasipolynomial qubits q under the stated computational restrictions. These bounds exceed the q ≤ log n bound of Bluhm, Christandl, and Speelman (2022) for f = IP, at the cost of restricting adversarial computation and increasing honest evaluation complexity.

Abstract: The $f$-routing protocol is a leading candidate for quantum position verification (Kent, Munro, and Spiller, 2011), but security guarantees for explicit functions remain limited. We prove unconditional resource lower bounds for uniform attackers; our new techniques bypass communication-complexity bounds central to previous works, which are inherently at most linear in the input length.
  We show that, for input length $n$ and sufficiently small constant $ε>0$, a uniformly generated strategy using $q$ qubits and having description length $\mathrm{poly}(q)$, with success probability at least $1-ε$ on every input, implies the following computational bounds on $f$:
  1. If the strategies are arbitrary quantum channels, then $f\in\mathrm{QSZK}(\mathrm{poly}(nq))$, where $\mathrm{QSZK}(T)$ is the class of languages having quantum statistical zero knowledge proofs in which the verifier runs in time $T$ (and the simulator in time $\mathrm{poly}(T)$).
  2. If the strategies are explicit Pauli-sparse unitaries on $q$ qubits that have at most $s$ nonzero Pauli coefficients, then $f\in\mathrm{DTIME}(\mathrm{poly}(nqs))$.
  3. If the strategies are Clifford+T circuits using at most $t$ magic gates, then $f\in\mathrm{DTIME}(\mathrm{poly}(nq2^t))$.
  Time and space hierarchies then yield explicit functions secure against polynomial and even quasipolynomial qubits $q$ under our computational restrictions. These bounds exceed the $q\le\log n$ bound of Bluhm, Christandl, and Speelman (2022) for inner product function $f=\mathrm{IP}$, at the cost of restricting adversarial computation and increasing honest evaluation complexity.

</details>


### [200] [Classical simulation of coherent crosstalk in surface codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40279)
*Andrew S. Darmawan,Yelyzaveta Kolesnyk,Robert Koenig*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a polynomial-time classical algorithm for sampling the syndrome distribution of a surface code under coherent nearest-neighbor ZZ crosstalk, and proves that efficient simulation becomes impossible (under standard complexity assumptions) when both coherent single-qubit noise and coherent crosstalk are present.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient classical simulation of surface codes is known for certain noise models (e.g., coherent single-qubit errors), but it is unclear how adding coherent two-qubit crosstalk affects simulability. Understanding where the boundary between efficient and intractable simulation lies matters for assessing the power of these noisy quantum systems.

Method: The authors construct a polynomial-time classical algorithm that samples from the syndrome distribution of a surface code state corrupted by coherent nearest-neighbor ZZ crosstalk. For the combined case of coherent single-qubit noise and coherent crosstalk, they establish a complexity-theoretic hardness result by mapping the syndrome distribution to the output distribution of IQP circuits defined on a non-planar graph.

Result: Sampling the syndrome distribution under coherent nearest-neighbor ZZ crosstalk alone is classically efficient, complementing known efficient simulation algorithms for coherent single-qubit errors. However, in the presence of both single-qubit coherent noise and coherent crosstalk, there is no efficient classical algorithm for sampling from the syndrome distribution even up to constant multiplicative error, unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses.

Conclusion: Coherent crosstalk by itself remains classically simulable, but its combination with coherent single-qubit noise leads to a complexity-theoretic obstruction to efficient simulation. This delineates a sharp boundary of classical simulability for surface codes under coherent noise models.

Abstract: We give a polynomial-time classical algorithm which samples from the syndrome distribution of a surface code state corrupted by coherent nearest-neighbor $ZZ$ crosstalk. Our algorithm complements the known efficient simulation algorithms for coherent single-qubit errors in the surface code. When both single-qubit coherent noise and coherent crosstalk are present, we find a complexity-theoretic obstruction to efficient simulation: unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses, there is no efficient classical algorithm for sampling from the syndrome distribution, even up to a constant multiplicative error. This is obtained by connecting the syndrome distribution under coherent noise to the output distribution of certain IQP circuits associated with a non-planar graph.

</details>


### [201] [Memory dimension detection in open quantum dynamics via pseudo-control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40281)
*Chang Liu,Alexander Yosifov,Ximing Wang,Zhenhuan Liu,Jinzhao Sun*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a pseudo-control protocol that encodes the memory dimension of an open quantum system into an experimentally accessible interference matrix, whose rank yields a lower bound on the memory dimension without requiring environment access or full process-tensor tomography, and applies it to quantum many-body environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterising open quantum dynamics requires identifying the environmental degrees of freedom that shape the system's evolution. The memory dimension—the size of the smallest effective memory needed to reproduce the environment's influence—quantifies dynamically relevant environmental resources and offers a common basis for comparing different many-body environments. However, existing methods typically require direct environment access or full process-tensor tomography.

Method: The authors introduce a pseudo-control protocol that encodes information about the memory dimension into an interference matrix. The rank of this matrix certifies a lower bound on the memory dimension. The protocol flexibly adjusts interaction and interval times, enabling study of how these parameters, environmental dynamics, and system-environment interactions shape detectable memory, without direct environment access or full process-tensor tomography.

Result: Applied to quantum many-body environments, the protocol reveals how interaction times, interval times, environmental dynamics, and system-environment interactions shape detectable memory.

Conclusion: The approach offers a new operational technique for characterising open quantum dynamics and probing the structure of system-environment interactions through their memory signatures.

Abstract: Characterising open quantum dynamics requires identifying the environmental degrees of freedom that shape the system's evolution. The memory dimension, defined as the size of the smallest effective memory needed to reproduce this influence, quantifies the dynamically relevant environmental resources and provides a common basis for comparing different many-body environments. Here we introduce a pseudo-control protocol that encodes information about the memory dimension in an experimentally accessible interference matrix, whose rank certifies a lower bound on this dimension without direct access to the environment or full process-tensor tomography. Applied to quantum many-body environments, the protocol allows flexible adjustment of the interaction and interval times, revealing how these parameters, environmental dynamics, and system-environment interactions shape detectable memory. Our approach provides a new operational technique for characterising open quantum dynamics and probing the structure of system-environment interactions through their memory signatures.

</details>


### [202] [Distinguishing Coherent Crosstalk from Calibration Drift via Pauli-Transfer Signatures and Quantum Edge Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40283)
*Syed Emad Uddin Shubha,Tasnuva Farheen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A residual Pauli-transfer-matrix (PTM) framework verifies and detects coherent crosstalk in multi-tenant quantum processors by exploiting a weight-preserving structural invariant and an antisymmetric cross-weight feature that is locally observable near the identity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coherent crosstalk in multi-tenant quantum processors can masquerade as benign calibration drift when average gate infidelity matches, making standard fidelity-based monitoring blind to a pulse-level attack surface. The paper seeks a structural verification and detection method that distinguishes coherent crosstalk from benign drift.

Method: The authors use the residual Pauli transfer matrix (PTM). They prove that products of local unital, trace-preserving channels cannot mix weight-1 and weight-2 Pauli operators under coherent drift of arbitrary strength or axis. Using PTM-to-Hamiltonian relations, they prove that for any qubit pair the first-order map from its nine interaction coefficients to the antisymmetric cross-weight feature is an isometry up to scale, making every interaction direction locally observable near the identity. They analyze cancellation under large local rotations, show the full cross-weight norm is invariant under local unitary composition as a structural countermeasure, and use shared randomized-Pauli measurements for periodic verification with the antisymmetric detector. Further experiments cover relaxation, depolarization, native ZZ fluctuations, and time-dependent pulse dynamics; an FRQI encoding with Pauli-graph quantum Hadamard edge detection is also tested.

Result: In simulation, 16,384 randomized settings give a detection threshold of λ_min ≈ 0.13 at a 5% calibrated false-positive target, with approximately M^{-1/2} scaling in the number of settings M. Detection is further characterized under relaxation, depolarization, native ZZ fluctuations, and time-dependent pulse dynamics. The FRQI encoding with Pauli-graph quantum Hadamard edge detection reproduces the classical structural edge score to numerical precision.

Conclusion: The residual-PTM antisymmetric cross-weight detector provides a structurally grounded, locally observable means of distinguishing coherent crosstalk from benign calibration drift, with a norm invariant serving as a countermeasure and randomized-Pauli measurements enabling periodic calibrated verification.

Abstract: Multi-tenant quantum processors expose a pulse-level attack surface in which coherent crosstalk can mimic benign calibration drift at matched average gate infidelity. We present a structural verification and detection framework based on the residual Pauli transfer matrix (PTM). We prove that products of local unital, trace-preserving channels cannot mix weight-1 and weight-2 Pauli operators, even under coherent drift of arbitrary strength or axis. Building on established PTM-to-Hamiltonian relations, we prove that, for any specified qubit pair in an arbitrarily large system, the first-order map from its nine interaction coefficients to the antisymmetric cross-weight feature is an isometry up to scale. Thus, every interaction direction is locally observable near the identity. We also exhibit cancellation of this feature under large local rotations and prove that the full cross-weight norm is invariant under local unitary composition, providing a structural countermeasure. For calibrated small local rotations, shared randomized-Pauli measurements support periodic verification using the evaluated antisymmetric detector. In simulation, $16{,}384$ randomized settings yield a detection threshold of $λ_{\min}\approx0.13$ at a $5\%$ calibrated false-positive target, with approximately $M^{-1/2}$ scaling in the number of settings $M$. Further experiments characterize detection under relaxation, depolarization, native ZZ fluctuations, and time-dependent pulse dynamics. Finally, an FRQI encoding with Pauli-graph quantum Hadamard edge detection reproduces the classical structural edge score to numerical precision.

</details>


### [203] [Verifiable Quantum Advantage and Computation via Quantum Circuit Obfuscation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40289)
*Alexandru Gheorghiu,Aparna Gupte,Vojtěch Havlíček,Yunchao Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Using quantum indistinguishability obfuscation (qiO), the authors build a publicly verifiable two-message quantum-advantage protocol and two protocols for classically verifying arbitrary BQP computations (one privately, one publicly verifiable), all working even if qiO holds only for ancilla-free unitary circuits, supported by a worst-to-average-case reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous cryptographic foundation for heuristic peaked random circuit sampling quantum advantage proposals (Aaronson-Zhang) and to give classical verification protocols for BQP under computational assumptions in the standard model.

Method: Construct protocols from quantum indistinguishability obfuscation (qiO): a two-message public-coin quantum-advantage protocol assuming qiO and a slightly stronger BQP≠BPP; a privately verifiable BQP-verification protocol from qiO alone; a publicly verifiable BQP-verification protocol adding post-quantum one-way functions. Also prove a worst-to-average-case reduction for obfuscating ancilla-free unitary circuits, extending the local-mixing framework of Canetti et al. (TCC 2024) under quantum analogues of their assumptions.

Result: An efficiently and publicly verifiable two-message quantum-advantage protocol; a privately verifiable classical BQP-verification protocol requiring only qiO (no extra hardness assumptions); the first publicly verifiable protocol for classical verification of BQP under computational assumptions in the standard model; all results hold with qiO assumed only for ancilla-free unitary circuits.

Conclusion: qiO yields a cryptographic foundation for peaked random circuit sampling quantum advantage and simple classical verification schemes for BQP, including a rare cryptographic application of iO with no additional hardness assumptions and the first publicly verifiable standard-model BQP verification, with a worst-to-average-case reduction supporting the ancilla-free unitary circuit assumption.

Abstract: We construct protocols for classically verifiable quantum advantage and classical verification of $\mathsf{BQP}$ computations using \emph{quantum indistinguishability obfuscation} (qiO). Specifically, given qiO and assuming a slightly stronger version of $\mathsf{BQP}\neq\mathsf{BPP}$, we construct a two-message quantum-advantage protocol that is efficiently and publicly verifiable. Our result can be viewed as a rigorous cryptographic foundation for the heuristic quantum advantage proposals based on \emph{peaked random circuit sampling} of Aaronson and Zhang (arXiv:2404.14493).
  We also construct two simple protocols for classically verifying arbitrary $\mathsf{BQP}$ computations. The first protocol is privately verifiable and assumes only the existence of qiO. This gives a rare example of a nontrivial cryptographic application of (quantum) iO that does not make additional computational hardness assumptions. The second protocol additionally assumes post-quantum one-way functions and is \emph{publicly verifiable}. To our knowledge, this is the first publicly verifiable protocol for classical verification of $\mathsf{BQP}$ computations under computational assumptions in the standard model.
  We show that all our results hold when qiO is assumed only for ancilla-free unitary circuits. As evidence supporting this assumption, we prove a worst-to-average-case reduction for obfuscating such circuits. This reduction extends the local-mixing framework of Canetti, Chamon, Mucciolo and Ruckenstein (TCC 2024) under quantum analogues of their assumptions.

</details>


### [204] [The power of constant-depth quantum circuits of unbounded size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40294)
*Sergii Strelchuk,Sathyawageeswar Subramanian,Máté Weisz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies whether quantum circuits with unbounded fan-in and unlimited ancillas can implement arbitrary unitaries in constant depth. It gives exact constant-depth constructions for permutations, diagonal unitaries, and arbitrary state preparation, and reduces general unitary implementation to traceless involutions, while leaving constant-depth arbitrary unitary implementation open (achieving depth O(√d) via port-based teleportation).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical circuits with unbounded fan-in compute any Boolean function in constant depth when size is unrestricted. The work asks the quantum analogue: whether removing constraints on circuit size and ancillary qubits lets quantum circuits built from arbitrary single-qubit gates and generalized Toffoli gates implement every unitary in constant depth.

Method: Exact constant-depth constructions are presented for arbitrary permutations of computational basis states, diagonal unitaries, and preparation of arbitrary pure states, linking state preparation to reversible classical computation and probability-distribution preparation. With fanout, exponential gates/ancillas are used and all ancillas are returned to zero; replacing fanout by an exact circuit preserves constant depth with possibly doubly exponential size. Equivalent formulations are given for general unitaries (copying a specified orthonormal basis, extracting labels, implementing restricted unitary families), and arbitrary unitary implementation is reduced to traceless unitary involutions using one extra clean qubit. Port-based teleportation yields a depth-O(√d) unitary circuit (independent of port number M, fidelity ≥ (1−(d²−1)/(2M))²). Adaptive measurements with gate teleportation give depth proportional to a gate's Clifford hierarchy level.

Result: Exact constant-depth constructions exist for permutations, diagonal unitaries, and arbitrary pure-state preparation. A unitary circuit of depth O(√d) is constructed for input dimension d with M ≥ d²−1 ports, independent of M and including resource preparation and port selection, with entanglement fidelity at least (1−(d²−1)/(2M))², so at fixed d accuracy can be made arbitrarily high without increasing depth.

Conclusion: Constant-depth implementation of arbitrary unitaries remains open. The paper provides equivalent reformulations and a reduction to traceless unitary involutions, and shows that with adaptive measurements depth scales with the Clifford hierarchy level. At fixed d, port-based teleportation achieves arbitrarily accurate constant-depth approximation, but whether the dependence on d can be removed remains unresolved.

Abstract: Classical circuits with unbounded fan-in can compute any Boolean function in constant depth when their size is unrestricted. We ask whether removing the restrictions on circuit size and ancillary qubits also allows quantum circuits built from arbitrary single-qubit gates and generalised Toffoli gates to implement every unitary in constant depth.
  We give exact constant-depth constructions for arbitrary permutations of computational basis states, diagonal unitaries and the preparation of arbitrary pure states. These connect quantum state preparation to reversible classical computation and the preparation of probability distributions. With fanout in the gate set, they use exponentially many gates and ancillary qubits and return all ancillary qubits to zero. Replacing fanout by an exact circuit over the original gate set preserves constant depth, although the size bounds can become doubly exponential.
  The implementation of arbitrary unitaries in constant depth remains open. We give equivalent formulations in terms of copying the vectors of a specified orthonormal basis, extracting their labels and implementing restricted families of unitaries. We also reduce arbitrary unitary implementation to that of traceless unitary involutions using one additional clean qubit. With adaptive measurements, gate teleportation gives depth proportional to the level of a gate in the Clifford hierarchy.
  Towards arbitrary unitary implementation in constant depth, we use port-based teleportation: for input dimension $d$ and $M\geq d^2-1$ ports, we construct a unitary circuit of depth $O(\sqrt d)$, independent of $M$ and including resource preparation and port selection, with entanglement fidelity at least $(1-(d^2-1)/(2M))^2$. Thus, at fixed $d$, the approximation can be made arbitrarily accurate without increasing depth. Whether the dependence on $d$ can also be removed remains open.

</details>


### [205] [Need for Coherent Access in Constructing Quantum Cryptography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40301)
*Minki Hhan,Changhun Oh,Vaughn Sohn*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Quantum oracles are constructed relative to which quantum-secure one-way functions exist but superlogarithmic-output pseudorandom states do not, revealing that coherent oracle access is necessary for constructing PRSs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Known black-box constructions build PRS generators from quantum-secure OWFs, yet this paper shows such constructions cannot work under classically accessible oracles, exposing a gap that depends fundamentally on the oracle access model.

Method: Construct quantum oracles using classical-accessible random oracles that even quantum algorithms can only query classically, while keeping other oracle components unchanged; the impossibility result is shown to extend to any classically accessible classical oracle replacing the random oracle.

Result: Relative to these oracles, quantum-secure OWFs exist but superlogarithmic-output PRSs do not; however, logarithmic-output PRFSs still exist, giving an oracle separation between classically accessible logarithmic-length PRFSs and superlogarithmic-length PRSs.

Conclusion: Coherent access to the oracle is essential: fully black-box PRS length extension from logarithmic to superlogarithmic output length must use coherent access to the underlying short PRS.

Abstract: We construct quantum oracles relative to which quantum-secure one-way functions (OWFs) exist but pseudorandom states (PRSs) with superlogarithmic output length do not. At first glance, this appears to contradict the known black-box constructions of PRS generators from quantum-secure OWFs. The distinction lies in the access model to the oracles; our oracle separation uses \emph{classical-accessible} random oracles that can be accessed only classically even by quantum algorithms. In fact, our impossibility of PRSs applies to \emph{any} classically accessible classical oracle in place of the random oracle, while keeping the other oracle component unchanged, showing the need for coherent access in constructing PRSs.
  We further show that logarithmic output length pseudorandom function-like states (PRFSs) exist relative to our oracles, giving an oracle separation between classically accessible logarithmic length PRFSs and superlogarithmic length PRSs. This shows that fully black-box PRS length extension from logarithmic to superlogarithmic output length must use coherent access to the underlying short PRS.

</details>


### [206] [Solving Sparse SDPs in Sublinear Time: A Classical Algorithm Inspired by the Quantum OR Lemma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40302)
*Fernando G. S. L. Brandão,Alexander M. Dalzell,András Gilyén,Francisca Vasconcelos*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper gives the first sublinear-time classical solvers for sparse semidefinite programs in the bounded-radius regime, showing that the quantum sample-reuse mechanism (Quantum OR lemma) is classically realizable, hence no super-quadratic quantum advantage for generic sparse SDP solving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior quantum algorithms (Brandão et al. 2019; van Apeldoorn and Gilyén 2019) achieved O(√n+√m) dependence for sparse SDPs, suggesting a quartic quantum speedup over classical O(mn) methods. This separation went beyond a usual Grover speedup and relied on the Quantum OR lemma, whose sample-reuse mechanism decouples Gibbs-state preparation from constraint search. The motivating question is whether this reuse mechanism is fundamentally quantum or can be realized classically for sparse SDPs.

Method: A classical procedure for simultaneously estimating many expectation values with respect to a sparse Hamiltonian's Gibbs state, combining randomized Lánczos filtering with an efficient sampling-based estimator. Plus a stochastic online-learning framework for SDP solving that substantially improves accuracy-dependence over standard oracle-based MMWU approaches.

Result: Let s be matrix sparsity and γ:=Rr/ε capture primal (R), dual (r) radii and accuracy (ε). When γ²≤min{m, n/s}, the solver runs in time Õ(nsγ^4.5 + msγ²). For γ=O(1), this is Õ((n+m)s), sublinear in the O(mns) input size. This matches known lower bounds in m and n up to logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: The quantum sample-reuse mechanism is classically realizable for sparse SDPs. Consequently, with respect to dimensions m and n, there is no super-quadratic quantum advantage for generic sparse SDP solving.

Abstract: We give the first sublinear-time classical solvers for sparse semidefinite programs in the bounded-radius regime, without low-rank assumptions or Frobenius norm dependence on the constraint matrices. For constant precision and bounded primal and dual radii, prior quantum algorithms of Brandão et al. (2019) and van Apeldoorn and Gilyén (2019) achieved $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{m})$ dependence on matrix dimension $n$ and constraint number $m$. Compared with the $\widetilde{O}(mn)$ runtime of existing classical methods, this suggests a quartic quantum speedup when $m \approx n$. Beyond a usual Grover speedup, this separation relies on the Quantum OR lemma, whose sample-reuse mechanism decouples the cost of Gibbs-state preparation from constraint search. We show that this reuse mechanism is classically realizable for sparse SDPs.
  Our main technical contribution is a classical procedure for simultaneously estimating many expectation values with respect to a sparse Hamiltonian's Gibbs state. This combines randomized Lánczos filtering with an efficient sampling-based estimator. We also introduce a stochastic online-learning framework for SDP solving, substantially improving accuracy-dependence over standard oracle-based MMWU approaches. Let $s$ denote the the input matrix sparsity and $γ:=Rr/\varepsilon$ capture dependence on the primal $(R)$ and dual $(r)$ radii as well as target accuracy $(\varepsilon)$. When $γ^2\leq\min\{m,n/s\}$, our solver runs in time $\widetilde{O}\left(nsγ^{4.5}+msγ^2\right)$. For $γ=O(1)$, this is $\widetilde{O}\left((n+m)s\right)$ and sublinear in the $O(mns)$ input size. Similar to the quantum algorithms, this matches known lower bounds with respect to $m$ and $n$, up to logarithmic factors. This implies that, with respect to dimensions $m$ and $n$, there is no super-quadratic quantum advantage for generic sparse SDP solving.

</details>


### [207] [A fully Gaussian quantum Stein's lemma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40307)
*Filippo Girardi,Jacopo Rizzo,Leah Turner,Gerardo Adesso,Ludovico Lami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives a single-letter formula for the Stein exponent in asymptotic asymmetric hypothesis testing of multi-mode bosonic Gaussian states under collective Gaussian measurements, showing it splits into first- and second-moment contributions and that non-additivity of Gaussian-measured relative entropy only decouples these moments asymptotically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Asymptotic distinguishability of quantum states under restricted measurements is a central but poorly understood problem in quantum information. The specific case of Gaussian states under collective Gaussian measurements lacks a clear single-letter characterization of the Stein exponent.

Method: The authors study asymmetric hypothesis testing (Stein setting) for general multi-mode bosonic Gaussian states restricted to arbitrary collective Gaussian measurements. They establish sufficient conditions for additivity of the second-moment term using novel log-determinant matrix inequalities.

Result: A single-letter formula for the Stein exponent is established for many pairs of states, featuring two distinct contributions from first and second moments. The Gaussian-measured relative entropy is shown to be genuinely non-additive, yet the only asymptotic effect of additivity violations is to decouple first and second moments. Collective Gaussian measurements provide no advantage for centred Gaussian states.

Conclusion: The results provide a single-letter expression for the Gaussian Stein exponent across a wide range of practical situations and rule out any advantage from collective Gaussian measurements when considering centred Gaussian states.

Abstract: A central yet mysterious problem in quantum information is the asymptotic distinguishability of quantum states under restricted measurements. Here we look at asymptotic asymmetric hypothesis testing for general multi-mode bosonic Gaussian states restricted to arbitrary collective Gaussian measurements. We establish a single-letter formula for the Stein exponent for many pairs of states, featuring two distinct contributions, corresponding to first and second moments, respectively. Remarkably, the Gaussian-measured relative entropy exhibits genuine non-additivity, but the only effect of additivity violations is to asymptotically decouple first and second moments. We prove this by establishing sufficient conditions for additivity of the second-moment term via novel log-determinant matrix inequalities. Our results not only identify a single-letter expression for the Gaussian Stein exponent in a wide range of practical situations, but also rule out any possible advantage from collective Gaussian measurements when considering centred Gaussian states.

</details>


### [208] [Decoupling of the QAOA into independent spin-boson systems and high-depth performance on pure and mixed spin glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40309)
*Sami Boulebnane,Abid Khan,Pragna Subrahmanya,Dylan Herman,Edward Farhi,Benjamin Villalonga,Ruslan Shaydulin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces a generalized spin-boson mapping showing that spins approximately decouple in QAOA on mixed dense spin glasses, becoming exact in the infinite-size limit, enabling efficient recursive energy computation via tensor networks at depths previously intractable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QAOA is promising for near-term quantum advantage, but scalability studies are limited: exact recursive formulas for large spin glasses scale exponentially with QAOA depth, blocking study of the promising high-depth regime.

Method: The authors show that computing QAOA energy on any mixed dense spin glass approximately reduces to independent spin-boson systems, with the decoupling exact in the infinite-size limit. This spin-boson mapping yields a recursive procedure for QAOA energies computable at modest cost using tensor networks.

Result: Numerically optimizing QAOA on pure and mixed spin glasses at depths intractable to prior techniques, they find higher-degree problems need more layers for comparable approximation ratios, while angle optimization becomes harder. Decoupling allows energy evaluation at large size and depth but does not enable strong simulation; sampling the predicted-energy bitstring still requires a quantum computer.

Conclusion: The spin-boson mapping provides a natural many-body framework for QAOA, enabling scalable energy evaluation at high depth, while clarifying that it does not substitute for quantum sampling.

Abstract: The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) is regarded as a promising candidate for near-term quantum advantage in combinatorial optimization, yet our ability to study it at scale is limited. Exact recursive formulas have been introduced for predicting QAOA performance on large spin glasses, but the computational cost of evaluating them grows exponentially with the number of QAOA layers, preventing the study of QAOA in the promising high-depth regime. In this work, we show that for the task of computing QAOA energy on any mixed dense spin glass problem, spins approximately decouple into independent spin-boson systems. In the infinite-size limit, the decoupling becomes exact, establishing the spin-boson mapping as a natural framework for the many-body physics of QAOA. This generalized spin-boson mapping gives a recursive procedure for computing QAOA energies that can be executed at modest cost using tensor networks. As a numerical application, we optimize QAOA on pure and mixed spin glasses at depths intractable for prior techniques, observing that higher-degree problems require more layers for a comparable approximation ratio while angle optimization becomes more challenging. While decoupling enables the evaluations of QAOA energies at large size and depth, it does not enable strong simulation of QAOA; a quantum computer is required to sample the bitstring corresponding to the predicted energy.

</details>


### [209] [Planted Cliques and Quantum Symmetry-Adapted Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40310)
*Vojtech Havlicek,Jordan Docter,Subhash Khot*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper performs an information-theoretic and structural study of two quantum encodings of classical graphs for the planted clique problem, showing sample-complexity bounds, identifying which symmetry-adapted registers retain detection information, and deriving a conditional computational separation between coherent quantum and single classical samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The planted clique problem has a wide computational-statistical gap and strong evidence for classical hardness, making it a promising candidate for quantum advantage. It is therefore important to determine whether natural quantum encodings of classical samples preserve enough information for detection and whether they could enable algorithmic efficiency.

Method: The paper analyzes two quantum encodings of classical graph samples: a binary phase state encoding and symmetry-adapted measurements arising from the Schur transform on the full graph register. It derives copy-complexity lower and upper bounds under arbitrary joint measurements, studies weak Schur sampling and its dependence on edge count, and examines which symmetry-adapted registers—representation label and Specht register, with or without multiplicity—retain distinguishing information. It also considers a supplied coherent quantum sample and constructs an efficient quantum distinguisher.

Result: For the binary phase state encoding, constant-advantage detection requires Ω(n^{1+2ε} ln^2 n) copies even under arbitrary joint measurements, while measurements on Õ(n^2) copies suffice statistically above the logarithmic clique threshold. Weak Schur sampling depends only on edge count and fails to distinguish the distributions. In contrast, retaining the representation label and Specht register after discarding multiplicity preserves distance 1−o(1), and near-perfect distinguishability survives even when the label is discarded. The retained states are explicitly calculated. One supplied coherent quantum sample enables an efficient quantum distinguisher, yielding a conditional computational separation from one classical sample under quantum planted-clique hardness.

Conclusion: The results are structural and information-theoretic. Efficient detection from a single classical graph in the conjectured hard regime remains open. The findings clarify the information content of natural quantum encodings of planted-clique instances and identify concrete targets for efficient measurement, while also establishing a conditional separation between coherent quantum and single classical samples.

Abstract: The planted clique problem is a promising candidate for quantum advantage with a wide computational-statistical gap and substantial evidence for classical hardness. We study two quantum encodings of classical samples, a natural binary phase state encoding and symmetry-adapted measurements, and determine if they preserve enough information for planted-clique detection, as well as discuss their potential towards algorithmic efficiency. For the binary phase state encoding, we show that constant-advantage detection requires $Ω(n^{1+2\varepsilon}\ln^2 n)$ copies, even under arbitrary joint measurements. Measurements on $\tilde{O}(n^2)$ copies suffice statistically above the logarithmic clique threshold. The symmetry-adapted measurements on the full graph register arise naturally from the Schur transform. We show that the outcome distribution of weak Schur sampling depends on the sampled graph only through its edge count and fails to distinguish the distributions; whereas retaining the representation label and Specht register after discarding multiplicity preserves distance $1-o(1)$. Near-perfect distinguishability survives even if the label is also discarded. We calculate the retained states, providing concrete targets for efficient measurement. Finally, we show that one supplied coherent quantum sample enables an efficient quantum distinguisher, which yields a conditional computational separation from one classical sample under quantum planted-clique hardness. Our results are structural and information-theoretic; efficient detection from one classical graph in the conjectured hard regime remains open.

</details>


### [210] [More mutually unbiased bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40311)
*Mateo Cárdenes Wuttig,Joseph Tindall*

Main category: quant-ph
