TL;DR: The paper introduces an ansatz for constructing mutually unbiased bases (MUBs) by applying diagonal phase matrices to a fixed unitary built from tensor products of Fourier matrices and optionally real Hadamard matrices, yielding more MUBs than previously known in several dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The maximum number of mutually unbiased bases remains unknown for dimensions that are not powers of a prime, motivating the search for constructions that surpass previously known counts.

Method: An ansatz is used where each basis consists of a diagonal phase matrix applied to a fixed unitary, which is a tensor product of Fourier matrices and, optionally, any real Hadamard matrix. Paley's real Hadamard matrix is also employed to construct bases in dimension d = q(q+1).

Result: The construction yields five MUBs in dimension 12, six in dimensions 48, 96, and 192, seven in dimensions 36 and 108, and ten in dimension 648. These extend to exceed the tensor product bound asymptotically in every eighteenth dimension. Additionally, q+1 bases are constructed in dimension d = q(q+1) for every prime power q ≡ 3 (mod 4), a count growing as √d that tensor products of smaller sets cannot exceed.

Conclusion: The ansatz produces more MUBs than previously found in many dimensions and asymptotically surpasses the tensor product bound in certain cases, with the Paley-based construction achieving a growth rate of √d that cannot be matched by tensor products of smaller sets.

Abstract: Mutually unbiased bases (MUBs) describe quantum measurements for which certainty in one basis gives uniform outcomes in the others. Their maximum number remains unknown in dimensions that are not powers of a prime. We introduce an ansatz that, in many dimensions, allows us to construct more MUBs than have previously been found. Each basis in the ansatz consists of a diagonal phase matrix applied to a fixed unitary, which is a tensor product of Fourier matrices and, optionally, any real Hadamard matrix. This construction yields five MUBs in dimension 12, six in dimensions 48, 96, and 192, seven in dimensions 36 and 108, and ten in dimension 648. These bases can then be extended to exceed the tensor product bound, asymptotically, in every eighteenth dimension. Moreover, we show that Paley's real Hadamard matrix can be used to construct $q+1$ bases in dimension $d = q(q+1)$ for every prime power $q\equiv3\pmod4$. This count grows as $\sqrt d$ and cannot be exceeded by tensor products of smaller sets.

</details>


### [211] [Explicit Capacity-Achieving Quantum LDPC Codes List Decodable in Near-linear Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40313)
*William Gay,Fernando Granha Jeronimo,Abhi Shukul*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work gives the first explicit constructions of quantum LDPC codes achieving list decoding capacity, with list decoding radius approaching the quantum Singleton bound and constant list sizes, plus near-linear time list decoding algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In classical coding theory, explicit codes achieving list decoding capacity were long sought and only found in [Guruswami-Rudra, STOC 2006]. In quantum coding theory, good quantum codes were known, and LDPC good quantum codes were found only recently [Panteleev-Kalachev, STOC 2022]. A natural open question is whether one can explicitly construct quantum codes that simultaneously achieve list decoding capacity and the LDPC property.

Method: The explicit code construction is based on expander graphs via the quantum analogue of Alon-Edmonds-Luby (AEL) amplification [Bergamaschi, Golowich and Gunn, STOC 2024], which yields the LDPC property. The efficient list decoding algorithms are obtained by generalizing classical expander-based weak-regularity list decoding algorithms [Srivastava and Tulsiani, FOCS 2025; Jeronimo and Singh, 2025] to suitable instantiations of quantum AEL.

Result: The first explicit constructions of quantum LDPC codes achieving list decoding capacity are provided, with list decoding radius approaching the quantum Singleton bound and constant list sizes. Near-linear time (in the block-length) list decoding algorithms approaching capacity are also given.

Conclusion: Explicit quantum LDPC codes can achieve list decoding capacity, resolving a natural question in quantum coding theory and connecting quantum AEL amplification with expander-based weak-regularity list decoding techniques.

Abstract: In classical coding theory, the quest for explicit codes achieving list decoding capacity has been an important driving force. While random codes are easily shown to achieve capacity, an explicit construction was only discovered decades later in the seminal work [Guruswami and Rudra, STOC 2006]. In quantum coding theory, it is highly desirable that a code family be LDPC. While good quantum codes were known for decades, obtaining the additional LDPC property was elusive. In fact, only very recently that good quantum LDPC codes were discovered in a breakthrough work [Panteleev and Kalachev, STOC 2022].
  In this context, a natural question is to ask for an explicit family of quantum codes achieving list decoding capacity while also possessing the important LDPC property. In this work, we provide (to the best of our knowledge) the first explicit constructions of quantum LDPC codes achieving list decoding capacity, namely, with a list decoding radius approaching the quantum Singleton bound with constant list sizes. Furthermore, we provide near-linear time (in the block-length) list decoding algorithms approaching capacity.
  Our explicit code construction are based on expander graphs via the quantum analogue of Alon-Edmonds-Luby (AEL) amplification [Bergamaschi, Golowich and Gunn, STOC 2024], and this enables the important LDPC property. Our efficient list decoding algorithms are obtained by generalizing the classical expander-based weak-regularity list decoding algorithms [Srivastava and Tulsiani, FOCS 2025] [Jeronimo and Singh, 2025] to suitable instantiations of quantum AEL.

</details>


### [212] [Quantum Sampling of Random Spanning Trees via Amortized Data Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40314)
*Yassine Hamoudi,Adrian Tanasa,Shrinidhi Teganahally Sridhara*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A sub-linear-query quantum algorithm generates uniform superpositions (q-samples) over spanning trees, with a matching lower bound proving near-optimality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work only produced classical samples of spanning trees, including a recent quantum algorithm by Apers, Gao, Ji, and Liu (ICALP 2025). Classical optimal algorithms require O(m) preprocessing and O(n) per sample, so a quantum approach promises sub-linear query complexity.

Method: The algorithm uses quantum walk sampling over a sequence of slowly-changing Markov chains. Each chain is an isotropized up-down walk that mixes rapidly to the spanning tree distribution. A key ingredient is an amortized data structure supporting fast implementation of the associated quantum walk operators, maintaining a spectral sparsifier and a leverage score sampler that evolve with the graph.

Result: For an n-vertex, m-edge graph, preprocessing costs O(sqrt(mn) + m^{1-delta}); each q-sample then costs O(n^{1+2delta}) for any delta. Alternatively, k independent q-samples cost O(sqrt(kmn)) total. A matching lower bound (up to logarithmic factors) shows the algorithm is essentially optimal. This yields faster quantum walk-based algorithms for counting spanning trees or finding a marked one.

Conclusion: The quantum algorithm achieves sub-linear query generation of spanning-tree q-samples with provably near-optimal complexity, outperforming optimal classical preprocessing and per-sample costs, and enables faster quantum walk-based spanning-tree counting and search.

Abstract: We present a quantum algorithm that generates a uniform superposition over the spanning trees of a graph -- also known as a q-sample -- using only a sub-linear number of queries to the graph. This goes beyond previous work that focuses on generating classical samples, including a recent quantum algorithm by Apers, Gao, Ji, and Liu [ICALP 2025]. For an $n$-vertex, $m$-edge graph, our algorithm admits a pre-processing step of $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{mn} + m^{1-δ})$, after which each q-sample can be generated in $\tilde{O}(n^{1+2δ})$ for any $δ$. Alternatively, $k$ independent q-samples can be generated at a total cost of $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{kmn})$. We also prove a matching lower bound up to logarithmic factors, showing that our algorithm is essentially optimal. Further tradeoffs are given in the paper. In comparison, the optimal classical algorithms (Anari, Liu and Vuong [FOCS 2022]) need an $\tilde{O}(m)$ pre-processing step, after which each sample costs $\tilde{O}(n)$ operations. These results yield faster quantum walk-based algorithms for counting spanning trees or finding a marked one.
  Our result is obtained via quantum walk sampling over a sequence of slowly-changing Markov chains. Each chain is an isotropized up-down walk that mixes rapidly to the spanning tree distribution of the input graph. A key ingredient is an amortized data structure supporting fast implementation of the associated quantum walk operators throughout the sequence. This data structure maintains access to a spectral sparsifier and a leverage score sampler that evolve with the underlying graph.

</details>


### [213] [Local Automorphism-Aware Syndrome Compilation for General Quantum LDPC Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40319)
*Eugenio Durazo Rocha,Olai Å. Mostad,Hsuan-Yin Lin,Eirik Rosnes*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces LocalASC, a method that reduces CSS quantum LDPC syndrome-extraction scheduling to ordered edge-coloring with quantum parity constraints, exploiting local automorphisms to find depth-optimal schedules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-depth syndrome extraction for CSS quantum LDPC codes requires valid interleaving of X- and Z-check measurements, which a proper edge-coloring alone does not guarantee. The 6-layer degree lower bound is unattainable for published weight-6 IBM bivariate bicycle codes, motivating the search for other code families where optimal depth is achievable.

Method: The problem is formulated as a proper ordered edge-coloring of the CSS Tanner graph subject to quantum parity constraints. LocalASC reduces the constraint system to edge-orbit variables under a subgroup of Tanner graph automorphisms and lifts feasible orbit assignments to the full graph. Translation subgroups are constructed for two-block group-algebra CSS codes over abelian groups, and conditions are given for square-complex symmetries to extend to Tanner graph automorphisms in quantum Tanner codes.

Result: The 6-layer degree lower bound is not universal among two-block CSS codes. LocalASC finds depth-optimal schedules for several two-block CSS codes with odd component weights (including unequal odd weights) where maximum check weight equals maximum Tanner graph degree, and yields lower-bound-saturating schedules for several quantum Tanner codes satisfying the same condition.

Conclusion: Optimal-depth syndrome extraction is achievable for broader code families than previously known, with LocalASC providing an efficient automorphism-based compilation approach.

Abstract: Low-depth syndrome extraction for Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) quantum low-density parity-check codes can be formulated as a proper ordered edge-coloring problem subject to quantum parity constraints. A proper edge-coloring of the CSS Tanner graph ensures that each data or ancilla qubit participates in at most one two-qubit gate per layer, but does not guarantee a valid interleaving of the X- and Z-check measurements as for every overlapping X/Z check pair, the number of shared data qubits on which the X interaction precedes the Z interaction must be even. The minimum number of colors in a proper ordered edge-coloring satisfying the quantum parity constraints equals the minimum two-qubit depth when each stabilizer check is measured with a single ancilla.
  We introduce local automorphism-aware syndrome compilation (LocalASC), which reduces the constraint system to edge-orbit variables under a subgroup of the Tanner graph automorphisms and lifts each feasible orbit assignment to the full graph. Although the $6$-layer degree lower bound is unattainable for the published weight-$6$ IBM bivariate bicycle codes, we show that this is not universal among two-block CSS codes. Among code instances for which the maximum check weight equals the maximum Tanner graph degree, LocalASC finds depth-optimal syndrome-extraction schedules for several two-block CSS codes with odd component weights, including instances with unequal odd weights. We also obtain lower-bound-saturating syndrome-extraction schedules for several quantum Tanner codes satisfying the same degree condition. To obtain the subgroups used by LocalASC without computing the full automorphism group of the Tanner graph, we construct translation subgroups for two-block group-algebra CSS codes over abelian groups. For quantum Tanner codes, we give conditions under which square-complex symmetries extend to Tanner graph automorphisms.

</details>


### [214] [Exponential quantum speedup for $\mathbb{F}_3^n$-Subset-Sum? Or, rigorous classical algorithms for Binary-Error LWE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40321)
*Robin Kothari,Tony Metger,Ryan O'Donnell,Noah Shutty,Kewen Wu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Using the Chen–Liu–Zhandry framework, this paper gives quantum algorithms for F_3-vector subset sum that require only m = ε·n² vectors in polynomial time, generalizing to a full sample–time tradeoff. A key ingredient is a deterministic classical algorithm for binary-error LWE with a rigorous sample–time tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem is to find a nonempty zero-summing subset among m random vectors in F_3^n. Chen–Liu–Zhandry (EUROCRYPT'22) gave an efficient quantum algorithm for m ≈ n²/2 where classical methods need exponential time. Kothari–O'Donnell–Wu (STOC'2026) then gave an efficient classical algorithm needing only m ≈ n²/3, removing the hoped-for exponential quantum advantage in that regime. The motivation is to restore the possibility of exponential quantum speedup by reducing the number of required input vectors.

Method: Build on the framework of Chen, Liu, and Zhandry. The central ingredient is a deterministic classical algorithm for the binary-error Learning-with-Errors (LWE) problem, for which the authors rigorously establish a sample–time tradeoff previously predicted by algebraic heuristics. The quantum algorithm is then constructed within the CLZ framework to exploit this improved classical subroutine. They also develop improved classical algorithms for vector subset sums over larger fields.

Result: For any fixed ε > 0, the quantum algorithm solves F_3-subset sum in polynomial time with only m = ε·n² vectors. More generally, a full sample–time tradeoff is established that interpolates between exponential and polynomial runtime. Additionally, classical algorithms for vector subset sums over larger fields are significantly improved relative to Kothari, O'Donnell, and Wu (STOC'2026).

Conclusion: The work renews the prospect of exponential quantum speedup for F_3-vector subset sum by achieving polynomial-time solution with substantially fewer vectors (m = ε·n²) than prior quantum and classical results. A deterministic classical binary-error LWE algorithm with a rigorous sample–time tradeoff is a contribution of independent cryptographic interest, and the classical algorithms for larger fields are significantly improved.

Abstract: We study vector subset sum over $\mathbb{F}_3^n$: given $m$ random vectors from $\mathbb{F}_3^n$, find a nonempty subset that sums to zero; the smaller $m$, the more difficult it is to find such a subset. Chen, Liu, and Zhandry (EUROCRYPT'22) introduced an efficient quantum algorithm that solves this problem when $m\approx n^2/2$, where a naive classical algorithm would require exponential time. Subsequently, Kothari, O'Donnell, and Wu (STOC'2026) gave an efficient classical algorithm that only requires $m \approx n^2/3$ vectors, thus removing the hope for an exponential quantum advantage in this parameter regime.
  Using the framework of Chen, Liu, and Zhandry, we give quantum algorithms that require much fewer input vectors, renewing the possibility of an exponential quantum speedup: for any fixed $ε>0$, our quantum algorithm solves $\mathbb{F}_3$-subset sum in polynomial time with $m=ε\cdot n^2$ vectors. More generally, we establish a full sample--time tradeoff that interpolates between exponential and polynomial runtime.
  The main ingredient is a deterministic classical algorithm for the binary-error Learning-with-Errors problem, which is of independent cryptographic interest. For this, we rigorously establish a sample--time tradeoff that was predicted by earlier algebraic heuristics. For vector subset sums over larger fields, we also significantly improve classical algorithms in Kothari, O'Donnell, and Wu (STOC'2026).

</details>


### [215] [Computational Work Extraction: The Complexity of Catalysts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40323)
*Atul Singh Arora,Shantanav Chakraborty,Alexandru Cojocaru,Sreyas Saminathan,Uttam Singh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves maximal separations between the ergotropy (extractable work) present in n-qubit systems and what efficient processes can actually extract, and connects ergotropy to the complexity of catalytic computation, showing catalysts enable full Θ(n) ergotropy extraction where non-catalytic processes extract negligible work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ergotropy quantifies the maximum work extractable from a quantum state, but it is unclear whether this theoretical maximum is achievable by efficient processes, and how auxiliary (catalytic) resources affect extractability. The paper aims to characterize the gap between information-theoretic ergotropy and computationally efficient extraction, and to link this gap to computational complexity.

Method: The authors prove maximal separations showing n-qubit systems can have Θ(n) ergotropy while every efficient process extracts negligible work, even for single-qubit-term Hamiltonians. They establish an unconditional existential separation and give an explicit construction in the random oracle model, extending to the plain model under quantum-secure pseudorandom functions. They connect ergotropy to catalytic computation and prove query lower bounds for quantum-space bounded algorithms, establishing relational and decision problems solvable with λ catalysts but not with cλ catalysts for any c<1 (relative to a random oracle).

Result: The paper shows catalysts are surprisingly powerful: there exist Hamiltonians and states where catalysts enable efficient extraction of full Θ(n) ergotropy while every efficient non-catalytic process extracts negligible work. It introduces and instantiates pseudoergotropy (analogous to pseudorandomness), while proving catalysts do not change information-theoretic ergotropy. For classical ergotropy, most constructions rely on classical states and Hamiltonians, implying analogous results, and certain proof of quantumness protocols generically separate classical and quantum catalytic ergotropy.

Conclusion: The work uncovers a deep connection between ergotropy and the complexity of catalytic computation, establishing maximal separations between theoretical and efficiently extractable work. Catalysts are shown to be powerful resources for computational ergotropy, enabling full extraction where non-catalytic processes fail, and the notion of pseudoergotropy is introduced. The results also extend to classical ergotropy, with proof of quantumness protocols separating classical and quantum catalytic ergotropy.

Abstract: We prove maximal separations: $n$-qubit systems can have $Θ(n)$ ergotropy, while every efficient process extracts negligible work, even for Hamiltonians consisting of single-qubit terms. We establish an unconditional existential separation and give an explicit construction in the random oracle model. Assuming the existence of quantum-secure pseudorandom functions, this separation extends to the plain model.
  This work uncovers an important connection between ergotropy and the complexity of catalytic computation---computation where auxiliary qubits must be finally restored to their initial state. Relative to a random oracle, we establish relational and decision problems that: (i) can be solved efficiently with $λ$ catalysts; but (ii) cannot be solved by any algorithm with $cλ$ catalysts, for any $c<1$. We show this by proving query lower bounds for quantum-space bounded algorithms.
  As a consequence, for computational ergotropy, catalysts prove to be surprisingly powerful---there is a family of Hamiltonians and states for which catalysts enable efficient extraction of the full $Θ(n)$ ergotropy, while every efficient non-catalytic process extracts negligible work. Furthermore, catalysts also allow us to introduce and instantiate the notion of pseudoergotropy---analogous to pseudorandomness. On the other hand, we show catalysts do not change (information-theoretic) ergotropy.
  Finally, our work also sheds light on the classical aspect of the problem. First, most of our constructions rely on classical states and Hamiltonians and therefore imply analogous results for classical ergotropy. Second, we show that certain proof of quantumness protocols can be used to generically separate classical and quantum catalytic ergotropy.

</details>


### [216] [Quantum Enhanced Deep Reinforcement Learning for Distribution Network Reconfiguration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40327)
*Daniel Germain,Mason Blanchard,Audrey Versteegen,Nora Bauer,Jonathan Mei,Claudio Girotto,Paul Smith,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a deep reinforcement learning framework for static distribution network reconfiguration (DNR), isolating and comparing different Q-network architectures—including a quantum-enhanced variant—to study their impact on solution quality and learning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern smart grids produce rich operational data and deploy many remotely controlled switching assets, enabling data-driven distribution network reconfiguration. However, finding optimal switch configurations is a computationally demanding combinatorial problem, motivating a systematic study of how Q-network architecture affects DRL-based DNR performance.

Method: A deep reinforcement learning (DRL) framework for static DNR is proposed. Multiple Q-network architectures are evaluated within a controlled DRL setup, varying only the Q-network to isolate the contribution of each architectural choice, including a quantum-enhanced Q-network compared against a classical counterpart.

Result: Quantum-enhanced Q-networks achieve higher rewards with fewer parameters than their classical counterpart.

Conclusion: Quantum-enhanced Q-network architectures are a promising direction for combinatorial optimization in power systems.

Abstract: Modern smart grids generate increasingly rich operational data and deploy greater numbers of remotely controlled switching assets than ever before, creating new opportunities for data-driven distribution network reconfiguration (DNR). DNR optimizes grid topology to improve efficiency, reduce power losses, and maintain operational constraints, but finding optimal switch configurations remains a computationally demanding combinatorial problem. In this paper, we propose a deep reinforcement learning (DRL) framework for static DNR to systematically investigate how the Q-network architecture affects both solution quality and learning performance. We evaluate multiple architectures within a controlled DRL framework, varying only the Q-network to isolate the contribution of each architectural choice. Our results show that quantum-enhanced Q-networks can achieve higher rewards with fewer parameters than their classical counterpart, suggesting that such architectures are a promising direction for combinatorial power systems optimization.

</details>


### [217] [Bath-assisted cooling without resets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40328)
*Xie-Hang Yu,Zherui Chen,Lin Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a cooling mechanism that transfers input information into decoupling bath degrees of freedom, allowing a single coherent bath contact (no repeated resets) to prepare the interacting ground state of weakly interacting, gapped fermionic systems from any mixed state in polylogarithmic time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooling an arbitrary mixed state to a pure ground state requires transferring entropy to an environment. Standard approaches typically need repeated bath resets or modified Hamiltonians; the work asks whether a single coherent bath contact can accomplish this without resets.

Method: The protocol transfers input information into bath degrees of freedom that subsequently decouple, while the system and remaining bath follow a ground-state path. It uses two initially empty bath modes per system mode and a single shared pulse across all onsite system-bath couplings; the bath is discarded only at the end, with the system Hamiltonian kept on and unmodified. Guarantees derive from non-Markovian system-bath dynamics without an effective Lindbladian description.

Result: For a class of weakly interacting, gapped fermionic systems with local interactions, starting from any mixed state, the protocol prepares the interacting ground state in total physical time polylogarithmic in system size and inverse global trace-norm error. The same pulse prepares each admissible system's own ground state without knowledge of its microscopic parameters.

Conclusion: A single coherent bath contact suffices for ground-state preparation of certain gapped fermionic systems without repeated bath resets, using a parameter-agnostic shared pulse and relying on non-Markovian dynamics.

Abstract: Cooling an arbitrary mixed state to a pure ground state requires transferring its entropy to an environment. Can a single coherent bath contact accomplish this without repeated bath resets? We develop a new cooling mechanism that transfers the input information into bath degrees of freedom that subsequently decouple, while the system and the remaining bath follow a ground-state path. The bath is discarded only at the end, and the system Hamiltonian remains on and unmodified throughout. We rigorously realize this mechanism for a class of weakly interacting, gapped fermionic systems with local interactions. Starting from any mixed state, our protocol prepares the interacting ground state in total physical time polylogarithmic in the system size and inverse global trace-norm error. It uses two initially empty bath modes per system mode and a single pulse shared across all onsite system--bath couplings. The same pulse prepares each admissible system's own ground state without knowledge of its microscopic parameters. The guarantees follow directly from a non-Markovian system--bath dynamics without an effective Lindbladian description.

</details>


### [218] [Fast Quantum Algorithms for Learning Linear Threshold Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40331)
*Aleksandrs Krivcenko,Tuyen Nguyen,Ronald de Wolf*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents three quantum algorithms for learning linear threshold functions (LTFs), achieving significant query complexity improvements over classical methods: learning real-domain LTFs up to Euclidean error ε with O(log(n/ε)) membership queries, identifying homogeneous LTFs with k nonzero entries using O(log k) queries (an exponential improvement), and learning homogeneous LTFs under Gaussian measure from quantum examples with O(n^{1/4}/√ε) applications of the example unitary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear threshold functions are fundamental objects in machine learning for linearly discriminating positive and negative examples. Classical algorithms for learning LTFs require substantial numbers of queries (e.g., Ω(n log(1/ε)) for real-domain queries), motivating the search for quantum speedups across different access models and function classes.

Method: The paper develops quantum algorithms for three LTF learning settings: (1) learning general LTFs f_{w,θ} under real-domain membership queries via a quantum algorithm; (2) learning homogeneous LTFs f_w with k equal-valued nonzero entries on {-1,1}^n by identifying the support; and (3) learning homogeneous LTFs under Gaussian measure from quantum examples produced by a unitary U, exploiting an efficient Hermite transform technique (of Jain et al.) in a weaker access model than membership queries.

Result: 1. Learning f_{w,θ} up to Euclidean error ε requires O(log(n/ε)) membership queries and Õ(n) other gates, with generalization to Gaussian inputs at error O(ε), versus Ω(n log(1/ε)) classical queries. 2. Identifying the support of a homogeneous LTF with k equal-valued nonzero entries requires O(log k) queries, exponentially improving on Belovs' O(k^{1/4}). 3. Learning homogeneous LTFs under Gaussian measure from quantum examples requires O(n^{1/4}/√ε) applications of U and U† and Õ(n²/ε⁴) other gates.

Conclusion: The paper establishes three quantum advantages for learning linear threshold functions across varying access models and structural assumptions, demonstrating exponential and substantial polynomial improvements in query complexity over classical and prior quantum algorithms.

Abstract: Linear threshold functions are $f_{w,θ}(x)=\text{sign}(\langle x,w \rangle -θ)$, where the weight vector $w\in\mathbb{R}^n$ is a unit vector, $θ\in\mathbb R$ is a threshold, and typically $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$ or $x\in\{-1,1\}^n$. When $θ=0$, the LTF is called homogeneous, and we write $f_w:=f_{w,0}$. Such functions are among the most important objects in machine learning, since they serve to linearly discriminate positive and negative examples. We give three positive results about learning LTFs: 1. Suppose we can make real-domain queries, meaning we can compute $f_{w,θ}(x)$ at any $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$ of our choice. We give a quantum algorithm that learns $f_{w,θ}$ up to Euclidean error~$ε$ using $O(\log(n/ε))$ membership queries and $\widetilde{O}(n)$ other gates. Then we have also learned $f_{w,θ}$ up to error $O(ε)$ when $x$ is Gaussian. Classical algorithms need $Ω(n\log(1/ε))$ queries. 2. A homogeneous LTF $f_w$ on domain $\{-1,1\}^n$ where $w$ has only $k$ nonzero entries of the same value, is the Majority function on the support of $w$. Belovs gave a bounded-error quantum algorithm that identifies the hidden support exactly (and hence learns $f_w$) using $O(k^{1/4})$ queries. We give an exponential improvement, using $O(\log k)$ queries. 3. Suppose we have a unitary $U$ that can produce (discretized) \emph{quantum examples} under Gaussian measure, corresponding to $\int_x \sqrt{γ_n(x)}|x\rangle |f_w(x)\rangle dx$. This is a weaker access model than membership queries. We give a quantum algorithm based on the efficient \emph{Hermite transform} of Jain et al.\ to learn homogeneous LTFs~$f_w$ with error~$ε$ under the Gaussian distribution, using $O(n^{1/4}/\sqrtε)$ applications of $U$ and $U^\dagger$ and $\widetilde{O}(n^2/ε^4)$ other gates.

</details>


### [219] [Tight Universal Bounds on Quantum Data Hiding with Multipartite Werner States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40332)
*Oren Akresh,Jacob Beckey,Felix Leditzky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper resolves the optimal dependence of the Werner-state quantum data hiding scheme on the number of parties n and local dimension d, showing the PPT-restricted distinguishing bias is Θ(n²/d), improving the certified hiding regime from n=O(d^{1/4}) to n=O(√d), with implications for quantum property testing lower bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eggeling and Werner introduced a multipartite data hiding scheme based on Werner states over two decades ago, but the optimal dependence of its uniform security guarantee on the number of parties and local dimension remained unknown. The paper aims to close this long-standing gap by pinning down the exact scaling of the distinguishing bias under local operations and classical communication (LOCC), as well as its PPT relaxation.

Method: The authors reduce the distinguishing bias of any pair of n-qudit Werner states to trace norms of partially transposed operators, and analyze these using mixed Schur-Weyl duality. They demonstrate that the PPT relaxation admits the same optimal dependence on both n and d by bounding the bias as O(n²/d) for PPT measurements and constructing an explicit pair attaining this scaling via nonadaptive local measurements.

Result: The distinguishing bias of any pair of n-qudit Werner states under PPT measurements across every bipartition is O(n²/d), and an explicit pair achieves this scaling using only nonadaptive local measurements, establishing worst-case optimality. This shows the PPT relaxation preserves the optimal dependence on both n and d, and at fixed security extends the certified hiding regime from n=O(d^{1/4}) to n=O(√d).

Conclusion: The same bound implies that testing any nontrivial unitarily invariant property with adaptive single-copy measurements requires Ω(√d) copies, yielding separations for any property with dimension-independent sample complexity under collective measurements. The work demonstrates that representation-theoretic methods developed for port-based teleportation are useful for data hiding and quantum property testing.

Abstract: Quantum data hiding concerns pairs of states which are highly distinguishable with global measurements, yet nearly indistinguishable when restricted to local operations and classical communication. More than two decades after Eggeling and Werner introduced a multipartite hiding scheme based on Werner states, the optimal dependence of its uniform security guarantee on the number of parties and local dimension remained unknown. We resolve this problem by showing that the distinguishing bias of any pair of $n$-qudit Werner states is $O(n^2/d)$ under measurements whose effects remain positive under partial transposition (PPT) across every bipartition. An explicit pair attains this scaling using only nonadaptive local measurements, establishing worst-case optimality and showing that the PPT relaxation preserves the optimal dependence on both the number of parties and the local dimension. At fixed security, this extends the certified hiding regime from $n=O(d^{1/4})$ to $n=O(\sqrt d)$. Beyond data hiding, the same bound implies that testing any nontrivial unitarily invariant property with adaptive single-copy measurements requires $Ω(\sqrt d)$ copies, yielding separations for any property with dimension-independent sample complexity under collective measurements. Our proof reduces the distinguishing bias to trace norms of partially transposed operators and analyzes them using mixed Schur-Weyl duality, demonstrating the utility of representation theoretic methods developed for port-based teleportation to data hiding and quantum property testing.

</details>


### [220] [Local Relaxation Hierarchies for Quantum Ground State Energies: Convergence Guarantees and Message Passing Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40336)
*Sheng-Ku Lin,Ricardo Rivera Cardoso,Roberto Bondesan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces local convex relaxation hierarchies with highly parallelizable message passing algorithms to estimate ground state energies of quantum many-body systems, proving exactness on trees and exponential convergence for perturbed separable chains, with O(n/ε²) and O(n/ε) runtimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Convex relaxation hierarchies yield polynomial-time computable lower bounds on quantum many-body ground state energies at any fixed level, but scaling to large systems and achieving accurate approximations is hindered by the high computational cost of traditional solvers and the lack of efficiency guarantees.

Method: The authors develop local relaxation hierarchies and efficient, highly parallelizable message passing algorithms. These are based on the subgradient method and a Nesterov-type accelerated gradient descent applied to an entropy-smoothed objective. The first hierarchy level uses local consistency of pairwise reduced density matrices; a second hierarchy uses consistent intervals.

Result: The first hierarchy level is exact for commuting Hamiltonians on trees. The consistent-interval hierarchy converges exponentially fast in interval size to the ground state energy for weak perturbations of separable Hamiltonians on a chain, giving an efficient classical algorithm. Two message passing variants run in O(n/ε²) and O(n/ε) time at any fixed hierarchy level on bounded-degree graphs (assuming O(1) norm optimal messages). Benchmarks across Hamiltonians, lattice geometries, and relaxation levels validate the theory and show potential to surpass standard convex optimization solvers.

Conclusion: Local relaxation hierarchies combined with message passing provide efficient, parallelizable classical algorithms for estimating relaxed ground state energies, with provable guarantees in structured settings and strong empirical performance. The resulting library is released at github.com/rick1924/gse-message-passing.

Abstract: Convex relaxation hierarchies provide lower bounds to the ground state energy of quantum many-body systems that can be computed in polynomial time on a classical computer, at any fixed hierarchy level. However, scaling these methods to large systems and accurate approximations remains challenging due to the computational cost of traditional solvers and the scarcity of efficiency guarantees. In this work, we develop local relaxation hierarchies and efficient, highly parallelisable message passing algorithms for estimating the relaxed ground state energies. We show that the first level of the hierarchy---based on local consistency of pairwise reduced density matrices---is exact for commuting Hamiltonians on trees. We further establish that another hierarchy, based on consistent intervals, converges exponentially fast in the interval size to the ground state energy for weak perturbations of separable Hamiltonians on a chain, thereby providing an efficient classical algorithm for these systems. Then, we introduce two variants of message passing algorithms that run in $\mathcal{O}(n/ε^2)$ and $\mathcal{O}(n/ε)$ time for any fixed level of the local hierarchy on bounded-degree graphs, where $ε$ is the precision for the relaxed ground state energy per site. This assumes that the optimal messages have $\mathcal{O}(1)$ norm---a condition we observe in practical settings in our experiments. These algorithms are based on the subgradient method and the Nesterov-type accelerated gradient descent method applied to an entropy-smoothed objective. Finally, we benchmark the message passing algorithms across different quantum Hamiltonians, lattice geometries, and relaxation levels, validating the theoretical predictions and their potential to surpass standard convex optimisation solvers for this problem. We release the resulting library at github.com/rick1924/gse-message-passing.

</details>


### [221] [The Single-Copy Quantum Bandit Is Classical: An Exact Spectral Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40339)
*Siu Hin Ng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that for multi-armed bandits with quantum-state arms and reward defined by a known effect F, adaptivity and randomization in measurement cannot beat spectral measurement plus classical KL-UCB; the leading regret coefficient is instance-optimal among consistent single-copy policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum bandit arms return unknown quantum states, and the mean reward is determined by a known effect F, with each copy measurable by an adaptively chosen POVM. The central question is whether adaptive or randomized quantum measurement design can reduce regret compared to simple fixed measurements, and what the fundamental limits of single-copy and quantum-memory policies are.

Method: The paper formulates a measurement game and proves an exact collapse: the measured relative entropy from an arm state to its confusing reward half-space equals the classical Burnetas-Katehakis functional of F's spectral statistics. It shows the spectral measurement of F together with an explicit least-favorable state constructed from the derivative of the matrix logarithm forms a saddle point. A self-contained finite-time bound is derived for general effects and boundary distributions. For policies with arbitrary quantum memory, an amortized relative-entropy argument yields a converse using the Umegaki half-space divergence, and the per-arm regret floors.

Result: Spectral measurement followed by classical KL-UCB is asymptotically instance-optimal among all consistent single-copy policies in every finite dimension and for every reward effect under nondegeneracy assumptions. Adaptive and randomized measurement design cannot improve the leading logarithmic regret coefficient, nor can storing copies for later single-copy measurement. For consistent policies with arbitrary quantum memory, the two per-arm regret floors 1/K_inf and 1/D_inf coincide if and only if the arm commutes with F. Whether the Umegaki floor is attainable is posed as an open problem with two-copy numerical evidence.

Conclusion: Adaptive and randomized measurement cannot improve the leading regret coefficient; any further improvement must come from measurements outside the single-copy class. The Umegaki half-space divergence governs the quantum-memory converse, and attainability of the Umegaki floor remains open, linked to a composite quantum Stein problem and adaptive regret.

Abstract: We study multi-armed bandits whose arms supply unknown quantum states and whose mean rewards are defined by a known effect $F$. Each fresh copy may be measured by an arbitrary, adaptively chosen POVM. We prove an exact collapse: the measured relative entropy from an arm state to its confusing reward half-space equals the classical Burnetas-Katehakis functional of $F$'s spectral statistics, and the spectral measurement of $F$ together with an explicit least-favorable state, built from the derivative of the matrix logarithm, forms a saddle point of the underlying measurement game. Consequently, spectral measurement followed by classical KL-UCB is asymptotically instance-optimal among all consistent single-copy policies, in every finite dimension and for every reward effect under the stated nondegeneracy assumptions: adaptive and randomized measurement design cannot improve the leading logarithmic regret coefficient, and neither can storing copies for later single-copy measurement. A self-contained finite-time bound covers general effects and boundary distributions. Any further improvement must come from measurements outside the single-copy class. For consistent policies with arbitrary quantum memory, an amortized relative-entropy argument gives a converse with the Umegaki half-space divergence, and the two per-arm regret floors, $1/K_{\mathrm{inf}}$ and $1/D_{\mathrm{inf}}$, coincide if and only if the arm commutes with $F$. Whether the Umegaki floor is attainable involves a composite quantum Stein problem and a separate reduction to adaptive regret; we pose it as an open problem, with two-copy numerical evidence.

</details>


### [222] [Quantum de Finetti theorems for states and channels in any distance measure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40343)
*Liuhang Ye,Bjarne Bergh,Nilanjana Datta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes standard and Renner exponential quantum de Finetti theorems in the stronger form of operator inequalities, yielding bounds in every Schatten norm and for all data-processing quantum Rényi divergences, and extends these to a channel de Finetti representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard finite de Finetti theorems approximate k-system marginals of permutation-invariant states via iid mixtures, typically in trace distance. A stronger, operator-level formulation is desirable because operator order between Choi states translates directly into complete positivity order between channels, enabling channel-level de Finetti representations; this also addresses an open question of Berta et al. on permutation-covariant channels.

Method: The authors prove standard and Renner exponential de Finetti theorems as operator inequalities. Operator inequalities imply bounds in every Schatten norm and for every quantum Rényi divergence satisfying data processing. They exploit the equivalence between operator order of Choi states and CP order of channels to derive channel de Finetti representations for permutation-covariant channels, and, under a no-signalling condition, an exponential channel de Finetti theorem whose approximating mixture consists of Choi-almost-iid channels.

Result: For fixed local dimension, the standard-case k/n error bound in max-relative entropy improves the prior best k^2/n scaling, even classically. For permutation-covariant channels N^(n): A^⊗n → B^⊗n with d_A = dim A, the k-system reduced channel is CP-dominated by a tensor-power mixture with error O(k/√n) and polynomial dependence on local dimensions. Under no-signalling, the exponential channel de Finetti theorem approximates via Choi-almost-iid channels; with r defects the representation error is at most poly(n)(2d_A^4 k^3/(n r^2))^((r+1)/2) and decays exponentially in n for suitable r and k.

Conclusion: Formulating de Finetti theorems as operator inequalities unifies and strengthens state- and channel-level approximation guarantees, delivering bounds in arbitrary Schatten norms and data-processing Rényi divergences. It improves known max-relative-entropy rates, resolves the Berta et al. question with an O(k/√n) CP-dominance bound, and provides an exponential channel de Finetti theorem with exponentially decaying error via Choi-almost-iid mixtures.

Abstract: Standard finite quantum de Finetti theorems approximate the $k$-system marginals of permutation-invariant states of $n$-systems by mixtures of independent and identically distributed (iid) states, usually in trace distance. We prove both standard and Renner's exponential de Finetti theorems in the stronger form of operator inequalities, implying bounds in every Schatten norm and for every quantum Rényi divergence satisfying data processing. In the standard case, for fixed local dimension, our $k/n$ error bound in max-relative entropy improves on the previously best known $k^2/n$ scaling, even in the classical setting. The operator-inequality approach is particularly suited to study channel de Finetti representations because operator order between Choi states is equivalent to completely positive (CP) order between the underlying channels. For permutation-covariant channels $N^{(n)}:A^{\otimes n}\to B^{\otimes n}$, where $d_A=\dim A$, we prove that the $k$-system reduced channel is CP-dominated by a mixture of tensor-power channels with error $O(k/\sqrt{n})$ and polynomial dependence on the local dimensions, addressing a question raised by Berta et al. [Math. Program. 194, 781-829 (2022)]. Under the no-signalling condition, we also prove an exponential channel de Finetti theorem where the approximating mixture consists of Choi-almost-iid channels, whose normalized Choi states are almost-iid in the sense of Mazzola-Sutter-Renner. In the case of $r$ defects, the representation error is at most $\mathrm{poly}(n)\bigl(2d_A^4k^3/(nr^2)\bigr)^{(r+1)/2}$ and decays exponentially in $n$ for a suitable choice of parameters $r$ and $k$.

</details>


### [223] [Efficient Calculation of Equilibrium Correlation Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40349)
*Yizhi Shen,Roel Van Beeumen,Wibe A. de Jong,Efekan Kökcü*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a quantum algorithm that stores time as a quantum variable using O(log N_t) ancilla qubits, enabling parallel computation of equilibrium correlation functions over all time points with a single circuit run only O(log N_t) times, achieving O(N_t log N_t) total quantum resources—proven optimal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional quantum algorithms (Hadamard test, variational methods, linear-response-based algorithms) compute equilibrium correlation functions G_AB(t-t') by using a separate circuit for each time point t-t', requiring at least O(N_t) total shots and O(N_t^2) total quantum gates, which is inefficient.

Method: The authors encode time as a quantum variable stored in O(log N_t) ancilla qubits, allowing the computation of an equilibrium correlation function to be effectively parallelized. Only a single quantum circuit is needed for all time points, and this circuit is run only O(log N_t) times.

Result: The algorithm requires O(N_t log N_t) total quantum resources in the large system-size limit, which is proven to be optimal scaling. The method is demonstrated by calculating the Green's function of a one-dimensional spinless Hubbard model.

Conclusion: Storing time as a quantum variable enables efficient, parallelized quantum computation of equilibrium correlation functions with proven optimal resource scaling, overcoming the per-time-point circuit overhead of conventional approaches.

Abstract: A majority of measurable dynamic quantities of a physical system is described as an equilibrium correlation function of the form $G_{AB}(t-t') = \left<A(t)B(t')\right>$, where $t'$ represents the impact time and $t$ represents the response time. Conventional quantum algorithms to compute such quantities via Hamiltonian simulation such as the Hadamard test, variational methods, or linear-response-based algorithms share one feature in common: each time point $t-t'$ is calculated by a different quantum circuit, leading to at least $O(N_t)$ total shots, and at least $O(N_t^2)$ total quantum gates. Here, we provide a different approach: utilizing $O(\log N_t)$ ancilla qubits to store time as a quantum variable, and effectively parallelize the computation of an equilibrium correlation function by requiring only a single quantum circuit for all time points. We show that this circuit needs to be run only $O(\log N_t)$ times, which in total requires $O(N_t \log N_t)$ quantum resources in the large system size limits. We prove that this scaling is optimal, and demonstrate our algorithm calculating the Green's function of a one-dimensional spinless Hubbard model.

</details>


### [224] [On The Simplest Quantum-Secure Block Cipher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40350)
*Gorjan Alagic,Joseph Carolan,Christian Majenz,Saliha Tokat*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that the two-round key-alternating Even-Mansour cipher is information-theoretically secure in the ideal permutation model against polynomially-many adaptive quantum queries (both forward and inverse), and shows this round count is minimal since any single-public-permutation construction is quantumly insecure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudorandom permutations secure against quantum adversaries are increasingly important, e.g., for constructing pseudorandom unitaries and separating SZK from BQP. While the key-alternating Even-Mansour framework is well understood classically, its quantum-query security status was unclear: one round is broken by a Simon's-algorithm-based attack, and for two or more rounds only non-adaptive security was known.

Method: The authors analyze the two-round Even-Mansour cipher in the ideal permutation model (IPM) against adaptive adversaries making polynomially-many forward and inverse quantum queries to all oracles. Their proof employs compressed permutation oracles and a specially crafted isometry relating the ideal and real experiments. They also establish a minimality result via an attack on any cipher using a single call to a public permutation.

Result: The two-round Even-Mansour cipher is shown to be information-theoretically secure in the IPM against polynomially-many adaptive forward and inverse quantum queries. Additionally, the construction is minimal: essentially any cipher built from a single call to a public permutation is quantumly insecure.

Conclusion: Two rounds suffice for quantum-query security of key-alternating Even-Mansour in the IPM, resolving the previously open adaptive-security question, and this round complexity is optimal.

Abstract: Pseudorandom permutations are ubiquitous in theoretical and applied cryptography. PRPs that offer security even against adversaries making quantum queries are of increasing interest, and used in applications ranging from constructing pseudorandom unitaries to separating SZK from BQP.
  A successful framework for constructing classically-secure PRPs is the key-alternating Even-Mansour approach, which interleaves applications of public permutations with additions of round keys. The single-round construction is already classically secure in the ideal permutation model (IPM), with added rounds offering improved concrete security. However, in the quantum-query setting, the status of this framework is presently unclear. A simple quantum-query attack based on Simon's algorithm breaks the one-round cipher. For two or more rounds, security is only known against non-adaptive adversaries who must prepare all queries in advance.
  In this work, we show that the two-round Even-Mansour cipher is information theoretically secure in the IPM against adversaries making polynomially-many adaptive forward and inverse quantum queries to all available oracles. Our proof uses compressed permutation oracles and a specially crafted isometry relating the ideal and real experiments. We also show that this construction is minimal, in the sense that essentially any cipher constructed via a single call to a public permutation is quantumly insecure.

</details>


### [225] [All Unitaries Have Constant Depth Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40351)
*Barak Nehoran,Henry Yuen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Every n-qubit unitary can be approximated to operator norm ε by a one- and two-qubit gate circuit of depth poly(n, log 1/ε) using 2^{O(n)} ancilla qubits, showing exponential depth is not necessary. With unbounded fan-out gates, depth reduces to constant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It was well-known that every n-qubit unitary admits a 2^{O(n)}-depth circuit, but whether exponential depth is necessary for general unitaries remained an open question, even with unlimited ancilla qubits. The paper aims to resolve this question.

Method: The construction connects the unitary synthesis problem of Aaronson and Kuperberg to locally-decodable codes and private information retrieval (PIR) from complexity theory and cryptography, leveraging these to parallelize general unitaries.

Result: All n-qubit unitaries can be approximated to operator norm ε by a circuit of one- and two-qubit gates of depth poly(n, log 1/ε), using 2^{O(n)} ancilla qubits. If unbounded fan-out gates are allowed, the circuits reduce further to constant depth.

Conclusion: Exponential depth is not necessary for implementing general n-qubit unitaries: every such unitary can be parallelized to polynomial depth, and even to constant depth with unbounded fan-out.

Abstract: It is well-known that every $n$-qubit unitary can be implemented by a $2^{O(n)}$-depth quantum circuit using single- and two-qubit gates. It has been open whether exponential depth is \emph{necessary} for general unitaries, even when allowing for unlimited number of ancilla qubits. We show, perhaps surprisingly, that all unitaries can be approximated to operator norm $ε$ by a circuit of one- and two-qubit gates of depth $\poly(n,\log 1/ε)$ with $2^{O(n)}$ ancilla qubits. In other words, every $n$-qubit unitary can be parallelized to polynomial depth. Moreover, if we allow unbounded fan-out gates, these circuits can be reduced further to \emph{constant} depth. Our construction takes advantage of a novel relationship connecting the unitary synthesis problem of Aaronson and Kuperberg to locally-decodable codes and private information retrieval from complexity theory and cryptography.

</details>


### [226] [Quantum hypothesis testing of non-mixed-unitarity: A multifaceted hierarchy of quantum channel discrimination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40355)
*Pratik Ghosal,Pritam Halder,Ayan Patra,Aditi Sen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper formulates the discrimination between a specified non-mixed-unitary channel and the set of mixed-unitary channels as a composite channel hypothesis testing problem and characterizes the achievable Stein exponents under various probe-state and auxiliary-memory restrictions, establishing several strict separations in a resulting hierarchy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the need to determine, given multiple uses of an unknown quantum channel, whether it is a specified non-mixed-unitary channel or belongs to the set of mixed-unitary channels, and to understand how achievable Stein exponents depend on the resources available, such as probe-state correlations and auxiliary memory.

Method: The problem is cast as composite channel hypothesis testing. The authors consider parallel strategies under progressively weaker restrictions on probe states and auxiliary memory, including block-i.i.d. probes with arbitrary intra-block correlations but i.i.d. across blocks, and derive finite-letter expressions for the corresponding Stein exponents. They also analyze intra-block correlation restrictions, auxiliary memory, and input-reference entanglement, and prove strict separations across the resulting hierarchy.

Result: Without auxiliary memory, fully i.i.d. probes yield a vanishing Stein exponent for every unital non-mixed-unitary channel, while three product probes suffice for a strictly positive exponent for the qutrit Werner-Holevo channel. For block size two, product probes appear insufficient for O(3)-covariant channels, but an entangled probe achieves a strictly positive exponent for the qutrit Werner-Holevo channel, even though every maximally entangled probe fails. With auxiliary memory, fully i.i.d. probes become sufficient, yielding a strictly positive exponent for every non-mixed-unitary channel without input-reference entanglement, bounded below by the diamond-norm distance from the mixed-unitary set; for odd-dimensional Werner-Holevo channels this exponent is infinite, with every full-Schmidt-rank pure state optimal.

Conclusion: The achievable Stein exponents form a multifaceted hierarchy shaped by probe-state correlations, block size, intra-block correlation constraints, and auxiliary memory. Strict separations exist within this hierarchy, and auxiliary memory fundamentally improves discrimination, making fully i.i.d. probes sufficient and, for odd-dimensional Werner-Holevo channels, yielding an infinite exponent.

Abstract: Given multiple uses of an unknown quantum channel, we determine whether it is a specified non-mixed-unitary channel or belongs to the set of mixed-unitary channels. Formulating this as a composite channel hypothesis testing problem, we characterize the Stein exponents achievable by parallel strategies under progressively weaker restrictions on probe states and auxiliary memory. In particular, we consider block-i.i.d. probes, which allow arbitrary correlations within blocks of fixed size while remaining i.i.d. across blocks, and derive finite-letter expressions of the corresponding Stein exponents. Varying the block size, which interpolates between fully i.i.d. and arbitrary probes, together with further restrictions on intra-block correlations and access to auxiliary memory, results in a multifaceted hierarchy. We establish several strict separations within this hierarchy. Without auxiliary memory, we prove that fully i.i.d. probes yield a vanishing Stein exponent for every unital non-mixed-unitary channel, while blocks of three product probes suffice to obtain a strictly positive exponent for the qutrit Werner-Holevo channel. For block size two, although product probes seem to be insufficient for O(3)-covariant channels, an entangled probe achieves a strictly positive exponent for the qutrit Werner-Holevo channel, even though every maximally entangled probe fails. In contrast, auxiliary memory makes fully i.i.d. probes sufficient by yielding a strictly positive exponent for every non-mixed-unitary channel, even without requiring input-reference entanglement, and this exponent admits a lower bound determined by the diamond-norm distance from the mixed-unitary set. Moreover, for odd-dimensional Werner-Holevo channels, we find that this exponent is infinite, with every full-Schmidt-rank pure state being optimal.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [227] [Synchronous Monte Carlo method and its application to a mean-field spin glass model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38790)
*Yoshiyuki Kabashima*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper introduces a parallel Monte Carlo method for pairwise-interacting systems where all variables update simultaneously via auxiliary Gaussian fields, preserving detailed balance. Applied to the random orthogonal model, DMFT analysis and simulations show a clear FDT violation below the dynamical transition, with a two-slope generalized FDT and quantitative theory-simulation agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MCMC updates variables one at a time to satisfy detailed balance, which prevents full exploitation of massively parallel hardware such as GPUs. This limits computational efficiency for large pairwise-interacting systems, motivating a parallelizable update scheme that still guarantees convergence to the canonical distribution.

Method: A Monte Carlo method for systems with pairwise interactions is developed in which all variables are updated simultaneously. Auxiliary Gaussian fields, introduced through the Gaussian integral identity, enable this parallel update while maintaining detailed balance and thus guaranteed convergence to the canonical distribution. For mean-field spin glasses, the dynamics are analyzed exactly via dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT). The method and DMFT are applied to the random orthogonal model, which exhibits a random first-order transition.

Result: The fluctuation-dissipation theorem (FDT) is clearly violated below the dynamical transition temperature. The relation between response and correlation takes the two-slope form of a generalized FDT. Simulations and theory are in quantitative agreement.

Conclusion: The proposed simultaneous-update Monte Carlo method satisfies detailed balance and is well suited to parallel hardware. Its combination with DMFT provides an exact dynamical analysis for mean-field spin glasses, and application to the random orthogonal model reveals a generalized two-slope FDT with quantitative agreement between simulation and theory.

Abstract: Standard Markov chain Monte Carlo methods update variables one at a time to satisfy detailed balance, which prevents them from fully exploiting massively parallel hardware such as graphics processing units (GPUs). We study a Monte Carlo method for systems with pairwise interactions in which all variables are updated simultaneously, made possible by auxiliary Gaussian fields introduced through the Gaussian integral identity. The method satisfies detailed balance and is therefore guaranteed to converge to the canonical distribution. For mean-field spin glasses, its dynamics can be analyzed exactly by dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT). Applying the method and the DMFT to the random orthogonal model, which exhibits a random first-order transition, we find that the fluctuation-dissipation theorem (FDT) is clearly violated below the dynamical transition temperature, and that the relation between response and correlation takes the two-slope form of a generalized FDT, with simulations and theory in quantitative agreement.

</details>


### [228] [On the Relation Between the Boson Peak and the Vibrational Phases in Glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39541)
*Philip Rasmussen,Søren S. Sørensen*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The boson peak in glasses is shown to emerge at a crossover from localized to extended non-wave-like vibrations, coinciding with a maximum in phase coherence and spatial periodicity, offering a microscopic explanation across diverse glasses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The boson peak—a deviation from Debye scaling (g(E)∝E²) in glassy and disordered systems—is widely observed, yet its microscopic vibrational nature has only been discussed anecdotally. A microscopic origin remains unresolved.

Method: Analysis of the vibrational modes associated with the boson peak, examining the crossover between localized and extended (non-wave-like) vibrations and quantifying phase coherence and spatial periodicity.

Result: The boson peak appears in conjunction with the crossover from localized to extended but non-wave-like vibrations, and coincides with a maximum in phase coherence and spatial periodicity.

Conclusion: These analyses provide new insights into the microscopic origin of the boson peak applicable across a wide range of glasses.

Abstract: The boson peak is a vibrational feature commonly found in glassy and disordered systems described as the deviation from the scaling of the Debye model ($g({E}){\propto}{E}^2$). Despite much interest, the microscopic nature of the vibrations is only anecdotally discussed. Here we report how the boson peak appears in conjunction with a crossover from localized to extended but non-wave-like and coincide with a maximum in phase coherence and spatial periodicity. Our analyses provide new insights into the microscopic origin of the boson peak across a wide range of glasses.

</details>
